# level-design

## clue and object placement
- [model] Build a chain where each found object gives the player a next location to query, and an NPC there confirms the item's owner or history. When: Structuring a sequence of investigation locations. Why: Each confirmation creates the next lead, keeping the player moving forward.
- [definition] Circumstantial evidence supports an inference from facts that could have other explanations, so it points toward a conclusion without proving it. Why: Direct proof is needed to close the gap between a strong inference and certainty.
- [mechanism] A decisive clue planted early among mundane objects or in an item description becomes meaningful when context or attention changes, so the player can re-read it then. When: Designing a clue for later recognition or a delayed-payoff clue. Why: The object is ignored until its relevance is triggered; changed context gives an earlier fact new meaning.
- [mechanism] An ordinary object, such as a cord or knot, can hide a use or second object that only handling and testing reveals, since interaction exposes what visual inspection misses. When: An ordinary item can plausibly conceal something and the player can interact with it. Why: A testable use turns a detail into a clue; interaction reveals concealed parts that visual inspection misses.
- [mechanism] Use a per-room checklist of hiding spots, including objects players likely ignore, each with its own chance to conceal an item, covered in order. When: Designing hidden clues or a search mechanic.
- [mechanism] Evidence the player hides rather than presents can stay in inventory and later change how NPCs react to the player.
- [mechanism] A critical clue can sit inside a visible object while the surrounding puzzle directs the player toward a different category of evidence. When: Designing a fair-play mystery. Why: Visible but unexpected placement rewards a shift in perspective.
- [mechanism] An object invisible to bystanders becomes visible to everyone present once a character physically seizes it, and it remains the same object afterward. When: a hidden item is grabbed by a character.
- [mechanism] Placing cryptic messages at increasing proximity to a key location, with timed gaps, lets the player react and collect clues between them. When: Generating a sequence of hints tied to a location. Why: Timed spacing gives time to decode and approach the source.
- [mechanism] Hidden objects can be moved between hiding spots as danger grows, so the first cache found should lead the player to a second, more dangerous one. When: Designing a concealment chain for a dungeon or vault puzzle. Why: A relocation chain rewards searching beyond the first find and escalates tension.
- [mechanism] When an NPC describes an intruder and a dropped item such as a hair or fibre contradicts it, the contradiction should trigger a new lead for the player. When: When designing witness descriptions and physical clues. Why: Contradiction shifts suspicion and gives the player a next step.
- [mechanism] A new item placed in a container stands out to attentive players when an NPC knows the container's usual contents and the item does not fit them.
- [mechanism] A clue can be hidden in a medium that shows only under a specific condition, attached to an item the searcher will naturally handle. Why: The searcher finds the item but not its concealed message.
- [mechanism] Residue samples tagged with map coordinates let a player who collects them compare them against locations later. When: Designing collectible environmental evidence. Why: Coordinates make collected traces usable for location matching.
- [mechanism] Tidying or removing bodies does not erase a crime's traces; impressions, holes and drag marks persist and can reveal how the original scene was arranged. When: A scene has been moved or cleaned after an event. Why: Physical changes leave residual marks that can be used to reconstruct prior state.
- [mechanism] Giving objects hidden properties that stay unresolved until examined lets an apparently junk item later unlock a path or expose a culprit. When: In puzzle systems where the player can inspect objects. Why: Delayed payoff makes thorough examination rewarding.
- [mechanism] A puzzle in which the player checks a claimed effect against an in-game clock or known mechanism, rather than an NPC's word, rewards verification. When: Designing deduction puzzles. Why: Verifiable mechanisms let players test claims themselves.
- [mechanism] A concealed release can hold a fastening shut while it still looks nailed or bolted, so appearance alone does not prove a room is sealed. Why: the visible fixture hides a working mechanism behind it.
- [mechanism] Unlit areas can hide a character from the player's view, so the player stays uncertain whether someone remains inside after an apparent departure. Why: Visibility limits create doubt about presence, which keeps an assumed-empty room open to suspicion.
- [mechanism] Let objects in passages serve as traces that imply a route, while sound cues stay unlocated until the player finds their source.
- [mechanism] An item placed only after a character's route passes through a room later reveals that character's visit. When: items are dropped as a character moves through a level. Why: the item's presence records that the character came through.
- [mechanism] When an expected item is missing from its routine spot, the gap signals that someone has been there, so players can infer recent access from the absence. When: NPCs keep keys or journals in predictable spots.
- [behavior] Arrangements made to create an alibi or empty location can later become evidence that an investigator examines. When: When a scheme was prepared in advance. Why: Planning leaves a trail of schedules, requests and travel records.
- [behavior] Traces left in haste, such as footprints, fingerprints and misplaced items, reveal a staging that did not follow its intended pattern. When: A scene is staged quickly under pressure. Why: Haste produces placement errors that careful planning would avoid.
- [behavior] When a clue spreads before anyone understands it, a crowd of competing theories forms, each reading the same object in a different way. When: a physical clue is shared publicly early. Why: One clue can support many explanations.
- [behavior] An animal that wanders with its track unbroken and no handler at its head suggests its driver was absent, which a player can read as a missing person. When: Placing animal tracks as a narrative clue. Why: The absence of a handler's trail implies the handler left or was never present.
- [behavior] Searchers skip positions they cannot reach and check them only after obtaining a tool such as a chair or ladder. When: When a hiding place sits above normal reach. Why: Reaching the spot costs extra effort, so players defer or ignore it until a tool is available.
- [rule] Place objects carrying an NPC's identity, such as an image matching its appearance, in rooms visited before the reveal so players can connect them to a location and time. When: A hidden identity should become recognizable after the reveal. Why: A visible match reads as harmless until the identity is known. (x2)
- [rule] Hide a key clue inside a long stack of in-game documents so the player must scan them and spot a repeated name or amount. When: Designing in-game documents that carry a puzzle clue. Why: Scanning rewards attention and makes the discovery feel earned.
- [rule] Hide the key fact in an unrelated in-game document, such as a receipt or ledger found during other activity, so the player must find it while doing something else. When: When designing a lost-records puzzle. Why: Incidental discovery rewards exploration and makes the clue feel earned.
- [rule] Assign each clue only what it proves, such as ownership, location or time; a single small clue carrying the whole argument risks coincidence. When: Interpreting physical evidence. Why: Clues often prove less than they seem, and a lone clue may be coincidence.
- [rule] Separate facts that bear on the timeline or solution from unverifiable detail; confirmed peripheral facts do not prove the central claim, so verdicts rest on the verified core. When: Some claims are checkable and others are not, or a story is mostly confirmed but its core claim is unverified. Why: Only checked claims bearing on the question support a conclusion; confirmed peripheral facts lend false credibility.
- [rule] Seed every clue needed for the final deduction earlier in the level, then hold the answer for a formal reveal; new facts at the end read as a trick. When: Designing a mystery with a late solution or a level with a deduction payoff. Why: Conclusions depending on unseen information feel arbitrary; a recap should only reorder facts the player already held.
- [rule] Plant a motive or key fact in casual talk, jokes or routine objects, stated plainly but not marked significant, so players recall it later in a confrontation. When: Writing dialogue or clues that will later support an accusation or pay off. Why: Casual remarks are discounted when heard, so recalled facts feel discovered rather than told.
- [rule] Place environmental traces such as broken branches or foliage, scuffed dust, stains, drag marks, tyre tracks and grate gaps so players can reconstruct the sequence of events. Why: Physical traces let players infer what happened.
- [rule] Make each link in a clue chain checkable by independent evidence, such as a writing tool that matches a message or a tool that matches a mark. Why: Testable links let players verify conclusions instead of accepting them blindly.
- [rule] Give clue items distinctive marks or attributes, such as a maker's mark or unusual shape, identifying an NPC or origin, but require a second confirming clue before the player acts. When: Designing clue items that identify an NPC or point to an origin. Why: A single mark is weak; a second source makes the link fair but not trivial.
- [rule] Design each found item as a partial clue, such as a postmark or stamp, that narrows nothing alone and gains value only when combined with other items. Why: Requiring combination rewards synthesis and prevents a single item from solving the mystery.
- [rule] Hide recoverable items in one of several ordinary searchable props such as chests, barrels or boxes, so the player must sweep them in an organized way. When: Placing quest items. Why: Plain props reward attention.
- [rule] Plant an object whose location or checkable fact, such as a bottle, ticket or item in a fuel bin, contradicts an NPC's stated account. When: When designing NPC dialogue that can be tested. Why: Contradiction rewards inspection and reasoning; a conflict invites the player to test the account.
- [rule] Show each clue in play before explaining it; a long post-event explanation of unseen clues reads as justification or summary. When: A delayed reveal is used or clues are explained. Why: The audience can check the reasoning against clues already seen.
- [rule] Plant each small checkable detail the solution needs early, then recall it later so it pays off when combined with a later discovery. When: Building a puzzle or mystery the audience must solve. Why: A clue is recognised later only if seen earlier; recall makes the solution feel earned.
- [rule] Place an out-of-place object, such as a moved item or a dropped container on a path, so players can infer who was there. When: when generating a scene that should imply a past visitor. Why: players reconstruct events from physical clues.
- [rule] Recheck hiding spots such as pockets behind flaps, since concealed items can escape a first search.
- [rule] Hide a puzzle's code word in an ordinary conversation, so the player can recall it when a second clue prompts them.
- [rule] Write puzzle clues in an in-game script or mythology, and let players learn the key symbol from a lore entry or NPC, so the solution is earned rather than guessed. When: Designing a cryptic in-game message. Why: Players can only decode a symbol they have been given a way to learn.
- [rule] An NPC can hold back one clue, revealing the rest only when the player offers the matching part they already have. When: Designing dialogue that gates a clue behind player knowledge.
- [rule] Let one crafted clue imply its tools and source, and let a found tool in a room link the clue to a location. When: Designing in-world clue items that encode their creation. Why: Players connect clues to places by matching the tools and materials they imply.
- [rule] Let one artifact support two readings so the player must weigh them. When: Designing a clue the player should interpret. Why: Weighing competing readings engages deduction more than a single fixed answer.
- [rule] Let a damaged note carry only part of a name or location, and let the player complete it by matching it against NPC rosters or map records. When: Designing a clue that requires assembly. Why: Players assemble a complete answer by combining fragments with world data.
- [rule] Spread a hidden figure's identifying traits across several locations so the player must join them to unmask the figure. When: designing a hidden character mystery. Why: Joining clues rewards exploration and gives the reveal weight.
- [rule] Plant several weak clues that each look incriminating, and make every link checkable so the player can expose a wrong suspect.
- [rule] Plant a recognisable silhouette or gait early as a fair clue, and confirm it when the disguise is later revealed.
- [rule] Build NPC dialogue that leaks clues when players ask about unrelated topics. When: designing NPC conversation. Why: Off-topic talk rewards curiosity.
- [rule] Place a clue inside a routine setting where its use looks like ordinary housekeeping rather than evidence.
- [rule] Examine blotters and waste-paper baskets early, before they are cleared, since their traces are lost once discarded.
- [rule] Plant a phrase or line of dialogue early that echoes a later document or NPC statement, so players connect them on their own.
- [rule] Hide anachronistic physical details in in-world documents, such as ink or a seal that postdates the claimed date, so an attentive player can expose the forgery. When: designing forged in-game documents. Why: Players reward attention when physical contradictions are discoverable.
- [rule] Make every clue point only to a character already on the board; a hidden or late-introduced actor breaks fair play.
- [rule] Keep a hidden answer in the game and check the player's named suspect against it, giving a clear confirm-or-deny moment without spoiling the rest of the deduction.
- [rule] To let players trace information, plant an unusual detail in one NPC and spread it through dialogue in a known order so players can reconstruct the chain. When: designing NPC dialogue that reveals a source.
- [rule] Scatter a suspect's small offenses across several rooms or days, and reveal the pattern only once three or more events share one motive. When: Designing a multi-event accusation clue. Why: Three matching events give the player enough repetition to notice a pattern without a single decisive clue.
- [rule] Place objects such as a named item left in a swamp, kennel remains, chains and a discarded tin so the level's story can be deduced without dialogue. When: when designing a level's environmental narrative. Why: the player infers purpose from the objects.
- [rule] Record which traces each scene should leave and which conditions erase them, so the player is not shown a false negative.
- [rule] Place a clue in a room the player passes through, and let an NPC react to it so the player notices it through them.
- [rule] Include a few real clues among many useless items on an investigation board, since too much junk makes the search tedious.
- [rule] Hide the means of a crime until a second check, prompted by a list of open questions, reveals it. When: when designing a crime puzzle.
- [rule] Place traces such as dropped items, marks or prints so the player can prove an intruder passed through even when the exit is unknown. When: Designing a sealed space with an unknown route. Why: Physical traces establish that the intruder existed, so the puzzle centers on the exit.
- [rule] Place small items such as a torn scrap or discarded tool that tie an NPC to a route or hidden door. When: when designing environmental clues.
- [rule] Store each physical trace with a position and time, then check the sequence obeys physical rules before revealing the clue to the player. When: generating a reconstructable scene. Why: keeps clues consistent with one order of events.
- [rule] Give a common prop a small physical flaw, such as a hole or scorch mark, whose meaning changes once the player learns the event. When: generating props for a scene. Why: the flaw becomes a readable trace.
- [rule] Place a weapon as an apparent unplanned find in a room, so players can judge whether the enemy chose it or grabbed what was nearby. When: Placing weapons or tools in an authored or generated room. Why: Placement context signals planning versus impulse.
- [rule] Place large, obvious props whose function, such as blocking a door or crushing a shelf, tells the player what happened in the room. When: Dressing a room with evidence of past events. Why: Function-based props communicate events without needing small hidden clues.
- [rule] Hide a second part of a document in a plausible place so that gathering the full set becomes a task. When: Designing collectible evidence. Why: Completing a partial clue rewards exploration.
- [rule] Make a key item, such as a missing page, required to complete a plan, so the player must find it before the solution works.
- [rule] Place environmental traces, such as a ring-shaped depression in a rug or spilled dust, that players reveal by changing the light or inspecting closely. When: when designing hidden clues in a room.
- [rule] Keep the rendering of a changed or hidden detail consistent under each lighting or angle variation, so the player can confirm it through testing. When: When a game object reveals different details under different lighting or viewing angles. Why: Inconsistent rendering prevents the player from distinguishing a real detail from a rendering glitch.
- [rule] Plant a message containing details only the culprit could know, and let players check its timestamp, typeface or paper against suspects. Why: checkable traces turn the message into a deduction.
- [rule] Place a clue in a closet or chest that is empty in a telling way, such as a missing pair. When: When placing environmental clues in containers. Why: Empty containers convey information when expectations are set.
- [rule] Hide a link such as a watermark or maker's mark inside an item's details so players must inspect it closely to find the connection. When: when generating clue items for investigation.
- [rule] Give each room a plausible set of traces for the event it claims, so a body in the wrong place with mismatched blood or light prompts the player to reconstruct the real sequence. When: When designing crime scenes for players to investigate.
- [rule] In a procedural case generator, place the decisive clue where a random roll can complete a chain of reasoning that depends on player skill.
- [rule] Let furniture and item descriptions hint at past events, such as an indentation or worn surface, that the player can use as evidence. When: Populating rooms in a story-driven level.
- [rule] Place a dropped item such as a card, key, or note under debris so players must search for it; clutter can reduce legibility without destroying it.
- [rule] Let the player fake incapacity in a trap room while a hidden NPC reveals plans and facts through dialogue. When: Designing a stealth or deception encounter. Why: The player's apparent helplessness draws out information the NPC would otherwise hide.
- [rule] Check random-looking dungeon events for a scripted cause, and make the staged solution the likely one so probability-minded players reach it.
- [rule] Include one timestamped event that breaks the claimed sequence once times are corrected, so players must find the inconsistency. When: designing timeline-based puzzles. Why: A single contradiction gives players a clear test of their reconstruction.
- [rule] When two suspects match the same checklist, provide one clue that separates them so the player must find it. When: When designing ambiguous suspects. Why: A single distinguishing clue resolves the ambiguity fairly.
- [rule] Design clue chains where each deduction yields a checkable fact stored in a different place, so only the full set confirms the suspect. When: Designing a multi-step mystery. Why: Distributed checks require the player to gather and combine evidence.
- [rule] Place a concealed alcove with sight and sound of a target, such as a stair, so players learn guard routines, passwords, and conversations by watching and listening. When: Designing a stealth area where information is gathered by observation. Why: A concealed vantage point turns passive watching into a source of useful knowledge.
- [rule] Place key items and small checkable traces, such as a hair in a dish or stain in a crack, in mundane containers hurried searchers skip, so careful examination finds them. When: Designing search-based clue placement. Why: Careful examination of every container rewards effort.
- [rule] Place incidental traces, such as discarded material or fragile props, that record an NPC's path or activity so players infer an entrance or movement without witnessing it. When: When designing environmental storytelling that reveals past movement. Why: Players can reconstruct unseen movement from traces, which rewards observation.
- [rule] A hidden clue or spot must be findable by a stated method and shown in the room description, with a reason to check it; otherwise it feels like cheating. When: Introducing a hidden clue or a surprise observer late in a scene. Why: Players cannot anticipate a spot they were never shown.
- [rule] Store clues inside containers like a cushion, lamp base or book, revealed only through feeling, cutting, forcing, or using a tool or mechanism, so inspection is rewarded. When: Designing hidden evidence in props or containers. Why: Effortful access makes discovery feel earned.
- [rule] Let players compare several physical clues against each suspect and confirm they converge before an accusation is accepted. When: the game uses physical evidence as its main proof. Why: players can test convergence instead of guessing.
- [rule] Hide carried items from the character model while stealth is active, so what the player sees matches the concealment. When: A stealth state conceals an inventory item. Why: Visible items would contradict the concealment the player is relying on.
- [rule] Place hints at the edge of the player's view, such as a faint draft, a peripheral mark, or a sound, so they are easy to miss when staring at one object. When: designing subtle environmental hints. Why: edge-of-view hints reward a player who surveys the scene.
- [rule] Hide key items by construction, such as behind a sprung panel in furniture, and hint the trigger through how the furniture looks or moves. When: when a container or room should conceal information physically. Why: secrets hidden by construction reward close inspection.
- [rule] Build a hidden alcove with windows over a key object, such as a grave, lever or chest, and let the player wait there for an NPC to return.
- [rule] Place an odd, unexplained detail in one room or conversation that points toward a resolution placed in a later location.
- [rule] Place hidden items so they survive the disposal a player might assume, such as metal in a fireplace, and hint the drop location through an NPC or log. When: when designing a clue hidden in a fireplace, stove or bin. Why: Careful searching is then rewarded rather than leading to a dead end.
- [rule] Place a secondary surface on a weapon or object that still shows recent use, so a cleaned primary surface does not fully hide the evidence. When: Building an inspection puzzle around a concealed or cleaned object. Why: The clue is discoverable through inspection but not obvious, so careful players can find it.
- [rule] Hide puzzle items in decorative clutter, such as candelabra or fittings, and give the player a reason to search that scene. When: Placing collectible or clue items in a scene. Why: Clutter conceals items from a casual glance while rewarding careful inspection.
- [rule] Place clues in containers whose owner's habits explain why the clue would be there, and mark those containers with habitual-use notes. When: When designing searchable containers that hold clues players might skip. Why: Habit gives players a reason to search where the clue lies.
- [rule] Hide a cache under removable tiles in a sparsely visited, maze-like area so that it stays hidden from casual exploration. When: when designing hidden loot or evidence placement.
- [rule] Eliminate hiding places in the room and the adjacent corridor before concluding that a person used a particular route. When: A missing person or intruder must be explained. Why: A concealed person changes which routes are needed, so the search must be exhausted first.
- [rule] Let hidden items sit in a cache that becomes reachable when a guard rotation opens, so the player's own action reveals it, rather than requiring a search of every wall tile. When: designing hidden-item puzzles. Why: a timed opening makes discovery an action rather than exhaustive searching.
- [rule] Make trails that double back into a branching clue path that rewards players who look sideways.
- [rule] Place a habit-specific item, such as a branded stub or drink residue, on a route so players can infer who passed, while generic tracks stay ambiguous. When: Designing evidence along a route. Why: Specific items narrow inference; generic ones do not.
- [rule] Run two investigative paths in parallel, each yielding different clues the player can later combine into one conclusion.
- [rule] Place the target behind a corner or within foliage so the player hears the danger before gaining sight lines.
- [rule] Give a level incidental features such as weather, sounds, or a late guard, but mark which ones affect puzzle state so players learn to ignore noise.
- [rule] Place keys or tokens along a route that reveals a shortcut, so the player reconstructs the path from them.
- [rule] Place loot using constraints such as fire risk, areas already searched, and height, and define player search zones with the same rules. When: Placing concealed items in a level. Why: Shared constraints keep hiding places fair and consistent for player deduction.
- [rule] Hold back one key document or clue until the final area so the player feels the shift when it appears.
- [rule] Place a fiber, scorch mark or scratch at a trap or lever, and let players match it to a tool or item found elsewhere in the level. When: when building environmental puzzles that link a mechanism to a found item. Why: a matched trace lets players deduce the solution from world evidence.
- [rule] Place an obvious but weak clue where a guard or player is likely to find it, so attention shifts away from the real route.
- [rule] Mark one wall element as loose and interactable, so players find the cache through systematic searching rather than by chance.
- [rule] Place a numbered item, such as a coin, ticket or key, on an NPC so it leads to a shop, vault or ledger in the level. When: When designing clue chains that pass through objects. Why: Numbered items give players a concrete, traceable path to a new location.
- [rule] Hide key items inside objects the player is likely to handle, and reward squeezing, shaking or turning them. When: placing a key item such as a lockpick or map fragment. Why: Items in routinely handled objects are found through natural play rather than random searching.
- [rule] Build a clue with several valid locations, and let players narrow it with a second constraint such as travel times or a key item.
- [rule] Give a hidden-object puzzle a known set of stash locations so the player can find the object through systematic elimination. When: Designing a search puzzle. Why: A finite, visible set of options rewards methodical play over luck.
- [rule] Lead a player with no clue through a sequence of odd, plausible spaces, planting one oddity in each that the target would also notice.
- [rule] Describe the layout so the audience can place every room and knows how each connects to the entrance and exits.
- [rule] Hide a conspicuous object in a hollow or bog to remove it from observers' view, rather than leaving it in plain sight. When: a visible item would betray the target's position. Why: removing the object removes the visual clue.
- [rule] Hide key items in an ordinary container of a common kind, so players find them by comparing the room with its remembered state.
- [rule] Let players test each door themselves so answers come from observation, not from a designer's or NPC's statement.
- [rule] Store off-scene events in neutral records, such as ticket logs, that players unlock by questioning a neutral NPC rather than the suspect.
- [rule] Hide a key object behind movable furniture so the player finds it only by noticing the displaced item. When: Placing a clue that should be discovered through observation. Why: The displaced item becomes the visible signal that leads to the hidden object.
- [rule] Let one trace point at a suspect and a second trace exonerate them, so players must test each clue rather than accept the first.
- [rule] Place a hidden key item where the designer's logic would put it, such as an already searched area or a dull room, and make an in-world clue point to it. When: when designing a fair hidden item or cache. Why: Re-checking a searched area becomes a deliberate choice, and a clue keeps the puzzle fair.
- [rule] Generate NPC objections from evidence the player has not yet linked, so the objections point toward where more investigation is needed. When: Designing feedback during a deduction. Why: Objections guide players to gaps without giving the answer.
- [rule] Make a staged scene that lacks a needed tool or item a clue in itself, so the absence contradicts the staged explanation. When: a scene is staged to look like an accident. Why: a missing requirement exposes the staging.
- [rule] Store items in layers within a container so stack order tells players what was placed later and when an event happened. When: When designing container puzzles.
- [rule] Place a deliberately odd object, such as a disguise or mismatched item, that players can notice and connect to a later event. When: Designing clues that reward attentive exploration. Why: Incongruous objects stand out and invite players to form connections.
- [rule] Place objects, sounds and traces in a level as candidate clues, and give each at least one checkable relation to another item so it does not read as filler. When: populating a level with environmental details. Why: Items linked to others make the player's search rewarding rather than random.
- [rule] Vary hiding spots across a room and avoid a single obvious piece of furniture as the only hiding place. When: When populating a space with several secrets. Why: One obvious spot is found quickly and makes the rest of the room feel empty of secrets.
- [rule] Assign one team to each zone, covering floors, rear, balcony steps, lobby, exterior, alleys, lounge, restrooms and cloak-room.
- [rule] Make hidden items reachable by a full sweep, including walls, ceilings and loose fixtures, so a thorough player can find them.
- [rule] Give props a hidden stain or patch that reveals they were moved, so a tool-based inspection exposes a changed puzzle state. When: designing object-state puzzles. Why: A detectable trace lets players discover that the world changed.
- [rule] When a clue's default reading is misleading, make its true source discoverable only through a closer examination action or an expert NPC.
- [rule] Give hiding spots size or fit rules, so a small pocket suits small items and a wall gap suits objects below a set volume. When: Designing hiding spots that must accept varied items. Why: Fit rules make hiding choices meaningful and testable.
- [rule] Confirm that every clue for a solution appears in the level or journal before the reveal event, and that no editing step removes one of them. When: Verifying a fair-play mystery in a game. Why: A missing clue leaves the player unable to reach the solution.
- [rule] Hide objects inside walls or crevices that only surface during renovation, so their timing and location become clues. When: Designing a space that changes over time. Why: Discovery timing and place carry information.
- [rule] Give a hidden-compartment puzzle a memory clue from an earlier scene, and a second route if its trigger has degraded.
- [rule] Give hidden doors a visible tell, such as a gap or dark seam, that players can notice in passing.
- [rule] Let a word on a note point to a location the player must find in the world's atlas or codex before they can proceed. When: puzzle clues tied to world geography.
- [rule] Plant an object with an odd feature, such as a mismatched mark, and log it in the journal to be explained once the player gains a key. When: designing an object whose explanation is delivered later. Why: A logged anomaly gives the player a thread to follow when new information arrives.
- [heuristic] Hide a key clue inside a pointless side conversation, minigame or rambling NPC dialogue, releasing it only when the player pays attention or asks well-ordered follow-up questions. Why: Players learn the clue only by paying attention and cutting through irrelevant talk.
- [heuristic] Place key items in plain sight among a room's mundane objects, such as behind a painting; players hunt concealed caches and overlook familiar things until examining each closely. When: Players expect secret stashes and will dismiss openly placed objects as trivial. Why: Searchers focus on hidden places and overlook what is obvious; careful inspection of ordinary items pays off.
- [heuristic] Show a distinctive, valuable, odd or locked object openly early, before explaining its importance or use, and let it return later with a meaning that answers the central puzzle. When: Planning a fair-play reveal or a mystery where the object matters later. Why: Early exposure makes the later reveal recognisable rather than arbitrary; an object seen early feels earned when its meaning is revealed later.
- [heuristic] Let a trait or detail appear in casual dialogue or an early observation, so it seems like small talk yet later connects to the solution as evidence. When: Designing an opening with later payoffs, or a trait that must be planted without drawing attention. Why: A detail noticed early and revisited later reads as deduction rather than coincidence; casual talk seems unimportant and is easily overlooked.
- [heuristic] Hide clues in routine objects, such as a coat pocket, desk, bin or already-examined item, with concealed compartments if needed; set search depth to match concealment, rewarding thorough players. When: Designing findable but non-obvious clues in everyday objects. Why: Players overlook objects they judge ordinary, and containers in routine use get less thorough search than dedicated areas.
- [heuristic] Place hidden items at an NPC's habitual spots, such as where it routinely hid things, so players who learn its habits find them first. When: When the item should be findable through character knowledge or an NPC's routine. Why: Habits are predictable, so they reward attention.
- [heuristic] Among plausible hiding spots, favour rarely touched or avoided ones, such as rumoured-haunted locations, since searchers skip neglected objects and seldom search there. When: When ranking candidate locations for a hidden item or clue. Why: Searchers skip neglected objects, so rarely touched spots stay unchecked.
- [heuristic] Impressions left by an object hidden in an odd place, judged by dimensions and depth, identify it and give a measurable clue traceable to a moment in time.
- [heuristic] Plant a minor early event that later becomes the cause of a new development, so the reader can recognise the link when it pays off. When: when a plot depends on coincidence. Why: Signalled events feel earned rather than arbitrary.
- [heuristic] A small hollow dug beside a structure can mark where something was hidden, so note it before the surface is disturbed further.
- [heuristic] Plant a line in a busy area so the player hears only a fragment, then reveal fuller context later so the early misreading becomes a discovery. When: designing an NPC overheard line. Why: partial information invites a plausible but wrong reading that later context corrects.
- [heuristic] Early physical marks reported in a first account can have meanings left open until later evidence clarifies them.
- [heuristic] When a sound or breathing is heard in a room, check adjacent hidden spaces for its source before concluding someone is present inside. When: an unexplained sound seems to come from an occupied room. Why: a concealed observer in a nearby space can produce it.
- [heuristic] Place a marked object on a victim's remains that the player can match against NPCs who knew the victim. When: Generating a mystery or identification puzzle. Why: Matching a marker to known people turns identification into a player task.
- [heuristic] Place key items among ordinary room clutter and have NPCs mention their properties in passing. When: designing an item the player must recognise later. Why: passing mentions let players recognise the item when it becomes relevant.
- [heuristic] Place items at a scene that show what the culprit tried to hide, so players must ask why an identifier was left where it was. When: Designing a crime scene or evidence placement. Why: Items left behind invite questions about the culprit's intent.
- [heuristic] Place initials on clothing and marks on glass in a level so the player can match them to a hidden NPC before seeing the NPC. When: Designing environmental storytelling for generated levels. Why: Early traces let players deduce a presence before the character appears.
- [heuristic] Place the most important clue object in the best-furnished room, so room quality directs player attention. When: When placing key clues within a set of rooms. Why: Players tend to examine richer spaces more closely.
- [heuristic] Items disposed of in water or elsewhere can become reachable clues that support a later investigation.
- [heuristic] When several NPCs wear the same boot type, footprints make a weak filter and need a second clue to separate suspects. When: Multiple characters share identical footwear. Why: Identical prints cannot separate suspects without corroborating evidence.
- [heuristic] Items placed on a character to frame them can be found by the player, who must reason about who placed them and why. When: when designing a framing plot. Why: discovery forces the player to model the planter's motives.
- [heuristic] A shared maker's mark on items in separate regions can link them, bridging two areas of a dungeon. When: Designing connections between separated regions. Why: A common mark gives players a reason to connect distant places.
- [heuristic] Scene-dressing can place one half of an action, such as a spent torch, so players must locate the other half.
- [heuristic] Tag generated items such as monogrammed cases, keys, or letters with owners, so the player can present them to NPCs and log each reaction as a clue. When: Designing items for an investigation game. Why: Owner tags let the reaction of an NPC become a traceable clue.
- [heuristic] Let a missing item turn up in an unexpected area, such as outside the room, to direct the player toward a new location. When: When designing leads that move players into new areas. Why: A displaced item creates a reason to explore.
- [heuristic] Hide clue items in refuse or debris where a player's sightline from a window or outbuilding can reveal them, and gate access behind a visible permission or key. When: Placing clues in generated dungeons or levels. Why: Visible access gates keep discovery fair while sightlines reward careful observation.
- [heuristic] Make ambient crowd chatter carry clues, but tag each line with a position or time so the player can narrow down the speakers. When: Designing background dialogue that hints at events. Why: Positional or temporal tags let players infer who spoke.
- [heuristic] A puzzle whose test result contradicts the obvious answer engages players, and an accidental player action can remove the evidence unless a fallback record is provided. When: Designing a puzzle where a test result drives the solution. Why: Surprise prompts reasoning, but losing the evidence before it is recorded blocks that reasoning.
- [heuristic] Weight clues by rarity: an uncommon or peculiar trait should raise confidence more than a general trait shared by many candidates. When: Ranking how strongly a clue identifies a suspect or object. Why: Common traits match many candidates and so prove little on their own.
- [heuristic] Clue instructions that name likely obstacles, such as a blocked cord, leave the player to solve those obstacles independently. When: when a clue requires a physical retrieval.
- [heuristic] An NPC guarding a secret can offer dialogue options that each reveal one fact, letting the player chain them into a deduction.
- [heuristic] In an escape puzzle, the true exit should be a route the player has not yet checked, findable from doors, locks and items left in the room.
- [heuristic] A crafted item's components can serve as clues, each traceable to a shop, stash or NPC, so players can follow the parts back to their source.
- [heuristic] A searcher who knows exactly what to look for spots a small object, such as a photograph among other items, that a cursory search misses. When: searching cluttered containers. Why: target knowledge directs attention to details a general glance skips.
- [heuristic] A clue that seems decorative may be the key; test its meaning with one lookup or check before dismissing it. When: A clue seems meaningless at first. Why: A single check can change its meaning entirely.
- [heuristic] When everyone in a crowd dresses alike, an item worn on the head or body can pass unnoticed as part of the crowd. When: A missing item may be worn by someone in a uniform-looking crowd. Why: Uniform dress makes the item blend in.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can hide a code in ordinary objects, such as dates on pages or letters in names mapped to calendar days, to open a lock or reveal a route.
- [heuristic] Give same-named items hidden differences, such as different materials, that only a specialist NPC can reveal. When: designing item identification. Why: Players must consult the expert to learn what the shared name hides.
- [heuristic] Let a hidden item's survival hinge on a visible routine, like a character who routinely inspects one shrub.
- [heuristic] Place a key clue at a viewpoint the player must climb to reach, and gate its explanation behind a sightline or a quiet corner.
- [heuristic] Pair a biased eyewitness with a neutral record, so players can verify one account against the other. When: Designing evidence for a case. Why: A neutral source gives players a way to test the biased one.
- [heuristic] Read layers in order: a hollow may be followed by solid material and then by another hollow beneath it.
- [heuristic] A failed plan can still reward the player with partial clues, such as a fragment that names the source of the threat.
- [heuristic] An item found hidden inside another object can open a new investigation branch. When: Designing discovery paths in an investigation. Why: Unexpected containment rewards inspection and redirects the player to a new line of inquiry.
- [heuristic] Physical traces can be buried, trampled or nearly invisible, so reveal them through methodical search and careful scene protection.
- [heuristic] Give a key clue a plausible alternative source so the player must test it before accepting the obvious reading. When: Designing evidence that points to a conclusion. Why: A second possible source forces verification rather than instant acceptance.
- [heuristic] Hide clues on mundane surfaces such as floor tiles, ash or a basin, and reveal them only through a specific action like kneeling, searching or lighting a fire. When: designing a scene with subtle evidence. Why: Discovery through a deliberate action rewards attentive play.
- [heuristic] Hide a cache in a visibly disturbed room; players infer the wrong location from the mess, and an item-by-item check against a list reveals what is missing. When: a puzzle needs misdirection around a hidden object.
- [heuristic] Direct a player who finds a trace on one object to a second location where the same trace should appear; a mismatch exposes a false lead. When: when designing clue verification. Why: matching traces across places rewards careful checking.
- [heuristic] An ordinary-looking location can signal a hidden story and draw the player in, creating curiosity before any events happen. When: Opening a level or area that should invite investigation. Why: An unremarkable setting that hints at a secret prompts the player to ask questions.
- [heuristic] A claimed stronghold that sits beneath a route used by patrols or observers is not really defended, so its stated strength can be tested by its location.
- [heuristic] Tapping a surface and hearing a hollow answer where solid material should give a dull one suggests a concealed void beneath it. When: searching a room for hidden passages. Why: the sound contrast reveals what the surface hides.
- [heuristic] Structure clue sets so each fact removes a whole class of candidates, and let the final step require an NPC specialist. When: when designing a multi-step deduction. Why: staged elimination keeps the search manageable.
- [heuristic] An unused or empty adjoining space can hold the decisive trace, such as disturbed dust or a clean mark showing recent use. When: Designing or searching a sealed area with a neighbouring empty room. Why: Unused spaces are easy to dismiss and so are good hiding places for evidence.
- [heuristic] Most details in a scene are insignificant and only one or two are vital, so mark the critical clues to keep players from drowning in noise. When: Designing a scene or puzzle room with many props. Why: Minor details that gain undue importance distract from the real answer.
- [heuristic] A second investigator on an independent route, such as outside a building, can reach information that the main investigators cannot. When: case needs sightlines or access the main team lacks.
- [heuristic] In a room with little cover, a piece of furniture may be the only hiding place available to a concealed observer.
- [heuristic] Hide key objects in storage spaces the player is not told to search, rewarding thorough exploration. When: when placing clues or items in a level. Why: Players who search thoroughly find what a quick look misses.
- [heuristic] Look for a concealed release where a maker would blend it in, such as among decorative detail near the area it protects. When: Searching a room for a hidden compartment or door. Why: Makers hide mechanisms where they look like ordinary decoration.
- [heuristic] A building's history of hiding people suggests where concealed spaces are built, so a historical clue can point players to a particular wall. When: Placing secret areas in a historically themed space. Why: Past use shapes where hidden spaces would have been made.
- [heuristic] A key returned after use suggests the visitor could have made a copy, so consider whether copies were possible. When: A key is returned after a visit. Why: Returning a key does not prove it was unused.
- [heuristic] A key too large for the visible locks implies a stronger container elsewhere in the room.
- [heuristic] Seed a readable clue early in an area passed for another reason, then give it meaning later via a journal entry or later area so the player can test it. When: Designing a clue that pays off in a later area or a late reveal. Why: An incidental discovery is remembered and gains meaning later; a connected clue lets the player verify the reveal against earlier evidence.
- [heuristic] Place a clue in a mundane spot that nobody examines while passing through a room, so it is found only by a targeted search. When: the clue should reward effort.
- [heuristic] Place a broken or mismatched object early in a level and its partner later in another room, so the two areas link. When: When designing linked objects across a level. Why: A matched pair creates a connection the player discovers over time.
- [heuristic] Place a lit lantern before each entrance so its state shows whether a door was used during a time skip. When: When a level has time skips or offscreen periods. Why: A changed indicator lets the player infer activity they did not witness.
- [heuristic] Place a key item where the recurring event occurs, so the player links the object to the event. When: When designing a clue item tied to a recurring event. Why: Co-location lets the player connect cause and effect.
- [procedure] 1) Hide the key item in a concealed spot. 2) Mark the spot with a distinctive landmark or visible in-world sign the player can recognize from an earlier description. 3) Plug the opening so it blends into the terrain. When: The player should find evidence only by following clues to a specific place. Why: A recognizable landmark lets the hider return and lets the player navigate by clues or the marker.
- [procedure] 1) Arrange all known facts together. 2) Keep each small fact that does not fit. 3) Treat each misfit as significant until it is explained. When: facts seem to conflict or an explanation leaves loose ends. Why: Misfits often reveal the true sequence of events.
- [procedure] 1) List every name found in the clues. 2) For each name, record each document it appears in. 3) Present the list as an index so the player can trace a suspect across records. When: when designing a clue system for a mystery or investigation. Why: Tracing one name through several records is how players connect evidence.
- [procedure] 1) Place a scent, sound or object early in the level. 2) Let it pay off as a required clue when the player reaches a related area. 3) Log the observation in a journal so the link is visible at the end. When: when designing clue payoff across a level.
- [procedure] 1) List every clue the final puzzle requires. 2) Confirm each appears in the game before that puzzle. 3) Fix any clue that appears later or not at all. When: During level review before shipping a deduction puzzle. Why: This ensures the solution is fair to the player.
- [procedure] 1) Store the intended dimensions of rooms and corridors. 2) Compare the measured layout with the plan. 3) Flag a mismatch as a hidden-space clue. 4) Confirm the mismatch before concluding a room exists. When: A space's layout disagrees with its expected dimensions. Why: Dimension mismatches reveal concealed areas, but measurement error can create false ones.
- [procedure] 1) Bound the unknown space using known neighboring rooms to list likely entrances. 2) Test each candidate in fixed order, ruling it out before moving on. 3) Check out-of-place features first. 4) Read airflow and sound as signs of sealing. 5) If nothing opens, suspect the space was closed after it was built. When: A space is suspected to exist but has no visible access.
- [procedure] 1) List the conditions the hidden item must satisfy. 2) Eliminate locations that fail any condition. 3) Test the remaining location against the conditions and verify it by search. When: A puzzle asks where a stolen or missing item is hidden. Why: Explicit constraints narrow the possibilities to one answer that can be verified.
- [procedure] 1) List all differences among the items, such as size, price and edition. 2) Find a feature present in every item at the same place. 3) Order the dates chronologically and convert each to a weekday. 4) Compare these values with another feature, such as name letters, to test for a code. When: A set of unrelated-seeming items shares a location or handling feature. Why: Listing differences first confirms that no obvious link exists.
- [procedure] 1) Examine the immediate spot thoroughly. 2) Search the next ring outward, such as bushes and ferns. 3) Keep widening the rings until the area is covered. When: When searching an outdoor area for hidden traces.
- [procedure] 1) Divide the scene into named zones. 2) Search obvious objects in a zone, then test its structure. 3) Have a partner recheck the work. 4) Finish the zone before starting the next.
- [procedure] 1) Test candidate sites for nearby observers first. 2) Reject busy sites where any passer-by could see the act. 3) Check whether the object's properties suit the disposal method. 4) Prefer ordinary-looking sites that give cover. When: Choosing a place to conceal an object or evidence. Why: Observers, object behaviour and ordinariness decide whether a hiding spot survives.
- [procedure] 1) Place the conversation in a hub the player passes through. 2) State a concrete time and condition in it. 3) Let the player hear it only by lingering nearby. When: A time-bound fact should be delivered as a passive clue.
- [procedure] 1) Set the corpse's state (clean joints, missing item, decay level). 2) Derive the time-since-death range from that state. 3) Check the range against the in-game calendar and other witnesses. When: when building a corpse or skeleton that carries clues. Why: physical state links the object to a checkable timeline.
- [procedure] 1) Show the document early, without stressing it. 2) Give each of its questions a physical referent in the world. 3) Let an in-world tool resolve those referents into one spot. 4) Open the hidden space there.
- [tradeoff] Placing similar items together makes an anomaly stand out by contrast, yet hiding the target among duplicates keeps it unseen unless inventory is checked thoroughly. When: When deciding whether an odd item should be noticed by contrast or hidden among duplicates. Why: Players judge an item against peers more readily than in isolation, while ordinary-looking duplicates hide the target in plain sight.
- [tradeoff] A vaguely planted clue is easily forgotten, while a sharply planted one signals the solution; aim for noticeable on return but unflagged on arrival.
- [tradeoff] Let the game control which traces a player can read: clear, fair traces reward attention, while hidden ones risk unfair guesswork. When: Designing environmental clues. Why: Clues must be fair for the player to act on them.
- [tradeoff] Planted environmental details such as a light source, a book title or a scratch can prompt a false identity or verify an action, so they must be placed deliberately. When: Designing puzzle clues in a generated level. Why: The same detail can mislead or confirm depending on how the player reads it.
- [tradeoff] Highly detailed clue instructions turn solving into mere transcription, while sparser instructions keep solving but risk unfair failure. When: when writing instructions for a hidden clue.
- [pitfall] Overly heavy foreshadowing erases the question, dulls suspense, deflates surprise, makes reveals predictable and clues look planted; unmet hints erode trust, so frame hints as minor flaws or jokes. When: Planting hints about hidden trouble or future events. Why: Overt signals remove surprise, and promises not kept erode credibility.
- [pitfall] Pointing directly at or heavily planting a detail signals its importance and reduces suspense; the setup then loses its disguise.
- [pitfall] Treating a concealed document or a minor detail such as make-up or manner as proof of guilt ignores innocent causes like secrecy, fear of exposure, grief or convention. When: Interpreting a hidden document or minor detail as evidence. Why: Concealment and odd details are consistent with both guilt and innocent causes.
- [pitfall] Overlooking early quiet facts, such as a small changed object or a missing date, can miss clues or hide a pattern, since minor details often fix the order of events. When: When reconstructing a sequence of events. Why: Minor early details often fix the order of later events.
- [pitfall] A hidden clue must stay recoverable, since one hidden too well is never found, stalling the deduction chain and leaving the audience no fair chance. Why: Later reasoning has nothing to build on when a clue is unrecoverable.
- [pitfall] Once others hold the keys, or areas are blocked or searched only by household staff, gaps appear that must be recorded rather than assumed clear.
- [pitfall] A search checking only visible surfaces misses secrets hidden by construction in furniture, containers or under mats; check every out-of-place object.
- [pitfall] A list of checkable facts can still rest on hidden assumptions, so each item needs a stated source. When: When an investigator builds a list of required facts. Why: Unsourced items can look verified while remaining unsupported.
- [pitfall] A remark that never connects to a later fact is just noise and does not function as a clue.
- [pitfall] Cleaning visible stains does not remove blood soaked into hidden surfaces; an overlooked smear can contradict a staged cover story.
- [pitfall] A reveal feels arbitrary when the culprit's access was hidden so completely that the reader cannot see how the crime was possible.
- [pitfall] Detailed motive shown early can point too clearly at one person, so balance it with other reasons to suspect others.
- [pitfall] A meeting that looks like chance may have a fixed time set by someone else, so investigators should ask who set the time. When: a suspect's presence somewhere seems coincidental. Why: arranged presence can be disguised as luck.
- [pitfall] Removing clues during revision breaks fair play, so the eventual solution feels unearned. When: Editing a mystery after the solution has been fixed. Why: Cut evidence leaves the reveal unsupported by what the audience has seen.
- [pitfall] Hidden objects need a plausible reason for their location, or their discovery feels arbitrary to the player. When: placing concealed clue items. Why: without a motive for the hiding place, the find lacks logic.
- [pitfall] Later traffic can overwrite earlier marks, so evidence that changes over time must be checked for which set lies on top before its order is read. When: Placing time-sensitive tracks in an environment that updates. Why: Subsequent events erase earlier evidence and invert the apparent sequence.
- [pitfall] Giving weight to a striking physical detail, without testing it against the question, often mistakes noise for evidence. When: Evaluating a physical clue at a scene. Why: Vivid details attract attention regardless of their relevance.
- [pitfall] A sealed or hidden item is only a threat if the target believes it contains the evidence against them.
- [pitfall] One time-stamped object can clear a suspect under one theory and implicate them under another, so check which window the clue actually fits.
- [pitfall] Relying on one subtle resemblance as the only key-lock link lets most players miss the connection entirely. When: A key's only pointer to its lock is a visual resemblance.
- [pitfall] An object meant to stay hidden is found within minutes by a thorough search, so it was never really hidden and the concealment fails. When: A thorough search is available to the player. Why: Concealment only works when search takes real effort.
- [pitfall] A chance discovery can look contrived when the story depends on it too heavily. When: A clue is found only by accident. Why: Accidental discovery that carries the whole plot reads as convenient.
- [pitfall] Hidden traces that are never checked make a case unsolvable, while too many make it trivial.
- [pitfall] A clue hidden only by shadow is missed entirely if the player never moves the light, so the light change must be discoverable.
- [pitfall] A red herring that has no way to be contradicted becomes unfair rather than misleading, and players cannot recover from it. When: when designing decoy evidence. Why: Without a contradiction, the player has no fair path to the truth.
- [pitfall] A hiding spot planned for one object type fails for objects of different shape or weight, so concealment must match the hidden object. When: Hiding a mix of items with different sizes or shapes. Why: Hiding plans made for coins do not hold for bulkier valuables.
- [pitfall] A concealed panel is weak if its joins with fixed parts can be seen or felt, because a searcher who looks closely will find the seam. When: When disguising a hidden panel with decoration. Why: Decoration hides the surface but not the gap between sliding and fixed parts.
- [fact] Later damage that destroys evidence can conceal how an event began, so the earlier cause must be inferred from what still survives.
- [fact] Parcels assembled from purchases made from many earlier grantees record who sold to whom, exposing trade ties between neighbors.
- [fact] A physical clue such as a sound can indicate the route an actor took and where that actor lived.
- [fact] Small objects often show unexpected features when enlarged, so detail invisible at normal size can reward close inspection.

## sightlines and visibility
- [model] Who can see or reach a spot depends on walls, balconies, bay windows, gates, alleys and locked doors, which together set sightlines and entry points.
- [mechanism] A sightline from a fixed position defines what a watcher there could see, so a sighting depends on the doorway or viewpoint. When: when judging whether a witness could have seen an event.
- [mechanism] A window overlooking a busy area makes an outside intruder unlikely, since the entry would be seen. When: An intrusion theory is weighed against the layout. Why: Visibility to others raises the risk of entry and so lowers its likelihood.
- [mechanism] A structure such as a parapet can hide an actor from street-level observers, letting an act happen unseen from below. When: an act happens above a public space. Why: blocking sightlines removes observers.
- [mechanism] Corridors or rooms that look alike can supply a believable wrong-turn explanation for how a character reached a restricted area. When: Explaining how a character entered a restricted room. Why: Visual similarity makes a mistaken route plausible.
- [mechanism] Layout features such as a tunnel under a road and two gates shape an escape route and determine where pursuers search.
- [mechanism] A secret site stays hidden when nearer terrain blocks every outside sight line, and observers assume the object went elsewhere. When: when the site sits on a rise with blocking features between it and the usual approaches.
- [mechanism] Floor layout limits who can reach which room; a wing with no direct link to another removes that wing as an access route. When: when judging who could have entered a room.
- [mechanism] Peepholes or sightlines through a room let guards watch the player, and darkness can let the player avoid being seen. When: Designing rooms that are monitored. Why: Darkness cuts off the watcher's view.
- [mechanism] Track or wall curvature can hide most of a long moving object from witnesses in other parts of it, so visibility depends on layout. When: deciding which observers could see an event. Why: geometry blocks sightlines.
- [mechanism] Room layout, not only furniture, determines who can reach a listener and who can overhear them.
- [behavior] Observers at the same vantage can see the same scene yet draw opposite conclusions, because each brings different assumptions to what they see. When: two watchers share one observation post. Why: interpretation depends on prior assumptions.
- [behavior] Witnesses who glimpsed a scene at a distance or under stress often garble incidental details, such as a sign's wording or an animal's colour. When: reporting brief or stressful sightings. Why: attention is limited, so peripheral details are recorded weakly.
- [rule] Give each witness a bounded vantage point (window, doorway, mirror, screen, corridor) and record its duration, place and closeness so players can check claims against the layout. When: testimony depends on sight lines, such as designing NPC witnesses for a mystery level. Why: a confident account can hide a partial view; players can then judge which testimony to trust. (x2)
- [rule] Provide a second exit or hidden passage, such as a back exit no guard watches, so the player can discover a route and watch a room unseen. When: Designing a stealth space where observation is part of play. Why: A second route lets the player watch and escape unseen.
- [rule] Give stealth scenes watcher NPCs with limited sightlines and use windows, doors and corridors as sightline puzzles, so players find unobserved routes that reward route planning. When: designing a stealth or surveillance encounter. Why: Blind spots turn observation into a puzzle the player can solve.
- [rule] Provide a readable level plan showing entrances, doors, stair destinations, passages, switches, sightlines and cut-off areas before the investigation starts, so players can test routes and NPC accounts. When: opening a setting that will be investigated. Why: known routes give players a baseline for judging later movement and accounts.
- [rule] Place balconies, bay windows, windows, doorways and screening trees so players can check who can observe each key spot and confirm or exclude witnesses.
- [rule] Check the room's geometry before reasoning from a sound, since mistaking a window for a door changes the possible sources and can derail the reconstruction. When: a sound is traced to a room. Why: openings determine what could have made the noise.
- [rule] Make some traces visible only from particular vantage points and signpost those points, since a clue seen from one spot is easily missed if players never go there.
- [rule] Make each locked room's entrances and each eliminated option visible through sight lines or map features, so the player can check exclusions and see why they are closed. When: When designing a sealed room puzzle. Why: Visible, listed routes give the player a clear elimination process.
- [rule] Place a grille or window that gives a partial sight-line as a concealed vantage point from which the player can watch a target. When: when designing hidden observation spots.
- [rule] Use shared rooms and thin walls as overhearing opportunities, but treat overheard speech cautiously if the listener was distracted or had a stake in the outcome. When: When a room is shared or walls are thin.
- [rule] Let doors, vents and corridors carry sound or sight to NPCs, so players learn things they were not meant to hear. When: designing spaces where NPCs may overhear.
- [rule] When a fugitive NPC escapes, make the next lead a contact the player can tail rather than the fugitive's known location. When: Designing the lead after an NPC escapes. Why: Tailing a contact keeps the trail alive and rewards patient observation.
- [rule] Build stakeout and tail mechanics with fixed watch positions, line-of-sight checks and a risk that the target notices the player. Why: Positional checks turn surveillance into a skill test with a clear failure state.
- [rule] A reversal of who watched whom can serve as a late twist that retroactively explains a locked room. When: when designing a late-game twist.
- [rule] Record how long a guard stays away from a post, and let the player test whether a planned sequence fits that window using an in-game timer. When: Designing timed stealth sequences in a level. Why: Measurable windows let players check their plans against real timing data.
- [rule] Place light switches and connecting doors deliberately, since they set which routes, lighting and sounds are possible in a space. When: generating or hand-placing rooms. Why: These elements determine reachable routes and perception.
- [rule] Make the required viewing position itself a clue, such as a ledge that fits only one seated position, to confirm the intended vantage point.
- [rule] Use line-of-sight and sound rules to limit what the player sees and hears, and flag NPC reports that cover only part of an event as partial. When: Designing observation and eavesdropping in a level. Why: Partial information should be marked so players weigh it correctly.
- [rule] Give the player two sightlines and a visible approach path so that both stealth and direct confrontation remain viable.
- [rule] Let the player spot a following guard through a window or reflection, so the watcher works as a clue. When: A guard or spy follows the player out of a room.
- [rule] Open a level on a disorienting view of a landmark that the player understands only after reaching it. When: Designing a level opening around a landmark. Why: Seeing the landmark out of context creates curiosity.
- [rule] Route the player past named landmarks, use fog and lighting for tension, and end the approach on a reveal of the target location. When: when designing a level path toward a reveal. Why: named landmarks orient the player and the reveal rewards the journey.
- [rule] Place a mirror so a hidden observer sitting in the dark can see a door or window opposite without being seen. When: a watcher must observe unseen. Why: a reflected sightline keeps the observer in shadow.
- [rule] Make witness corroboration a puzzle: the player confirms a claim by finding a matching physical trace in the environment.
- [rule] Use line-of-sight checks to show where an enemy could watch the player without being seen, and make those unseen views discoverable. When: designing enemy placement in a search or stealth area. Why: players can then deduce hidden watchers from the layout.
- [rule] A capture tool that records a scene from a fixed list of viewpoints gives players a reusable, checkable record of the space. When: Designing an in-game photo or scan mechanic. Why: Fixed views let players compare and recall the layout later.
- [rule] Make reach constraints explicit, such as a key on a high ledge, so players can infer a character's height or ability; add furniture like a step that changes the estimate. When: designing objects placed at set heights.
- [rule] Render distant crowd members as grouped silhouettes and reveal individual tells only up close, since visual resolution falls with distance. When: Designing crowds or enemy groups seen from afar, or where an observer views a group from different distances. Why: Distance limits what the eye can resolve, so individual detail disappears at long range.
- [rule] Use eye-height line-of-sight tests to decide which characters or positions a puzzle can include or exclude.
- [rule] Place residences with sightlines to a scene on the city map, so a canvass yields a mix of witness and null results.
- [rule] Let an unobstructed window supply testimony that players can reach only by moving through the map, so location gates the clue.
- [rule] Assign each observer a fixed post covering part of an area and mark the posts on a plan so overlaps and blind spots are visible. When: Several observers cover overlapping but imperfect views. Why: Fixed posts make coverage gaps predictable.
- [rule] A hidden listening post works best when the room beside the target is empty and locked from the target's side.
- [rule] Let a gap or crack in a wall give the player an unseen view into an adjacent room.
- [rule] Let a building's position and the roads around it limit what a witness could have seen, so the map itself works as an alibi check. When: designing witness testimony in a game. Why: players can test claims against the geography.
- [rule] Design a house layout as a crime scene map, with rooms and access routes that determine who could have been where. When: Building a mystery location.
- [rule] Keep a hidden character out of view so that the player's knowledge of their state stays partial, as the observer's is.
- [rule] Give guards or NPCs in a crowded hall sight cones, so a player-seen event may go unnoticed by others for a logical reason. When: When designing a crowded scene where NPCs react to player actions. Why: Limited sight lines make missed observation believable and fair.
- [rule] Place partial views such as a gap in a fence or a window that signal a presence without identifying it, rewarding players who wait and watch. When: Designing hidden presence clues in a level. Why: Partial information invites patient observation and inference.
- [rule] Build sightlines and blind corners into the layout so a player can find a hidden spot from which to watch or act unseen. When: when designing an area meant for stealth or surveillance. Why: Blocked views from nearby windows let a visitor proceed unobserved.
- [heuristic] Judge a claimed sighting by the witness's position, obstacles, distance, light and whether the observed looked toward them; a claim outside these sight limits is implausible. When: a witness claims to have seen someone through a door, crowd, across distance or an act. Why: A half-open door can hide a person from one observer but not from another.
- [heuristic] Let one room be seen from several vantage points and record the differences between views, since they reveal what a single view cannot.
- [heuristic] Check each alibi witness's sightline to confirm they saw the suspect continuously; a continuous, complete view strengthens a sequence more than a partial view. Why: A badly placed witness leaves room for an unseen departure.
- [heuristic] Events falling within the same short window are candidates for a causal link, but overlap only prompts investigation and is not proof. Why: Coincidence in time is common, so overlap alone does not establish a connection.
- [heuristic] A witness's account of where an item was dropped narrows the search more than the item itself does. When: when a searcher has a reliable sighting of a disposal. Why: The sighting points to the exact container or spot to examine.
- [heuristic] A sound many people heard, such as a clock chime, can anchor an hour that several witnesses can confirm independently. When: when witnesses need a common reference time. Why: several people can confirm the same sound.
- [heuristic] A witness's casual sighting fixes a point on the timeline and tests a stated alibi, but only if the time and place of the sighting are known. When: A witness recognises someone while passing by. Why: An unlocated sighting cannot anchor a timeline or contradict a claim.
- [heuristic] A witness who admits taking no notice of the people involved adds little evidence.
- [heuristic] Before accepting a witness's description of a figure's shape, check the angle, lighting, reflective surfaces and objects between observer and figure. When: when a witness reports an odd or monstrous shape.
- [heuristic] Check a witness's timing against where they stood. A view from a different floor or angle limits what they could have seen.
- [heuristic] Record why a witness was positioned to observe, since that reason is part of judging how reliable the account is. When: Assessing a witness account from a hidden position. Why: Motive for watching explains the vantage and affects how much attention was paid.
- [heuristic] Where witnesses disagree about who passed a point, check which witness could actually see that route, since honest witnesses may lack a view.
- [heuristic] Map homes, meeting places and travel distances to judge which witnesses could have seen or heard an event. When: testing a witness's account. Why: physical access limits what a person could observe.
- [heuristic] A connecting door that is never used can be made discoverable as a shortcut into a hidden area. When: Designing secret routes.
- [heuristic] A stealth encounter can use silence and a narrow sightline so the player learns the enemy's movement without alerting it. When: Designing a timed stealth encounter. Why: Limited vision rewards patient observation and builds tension through restraint.
- [heuristic] In dark stealth areas, place a trackable sound cue, such as a clock or creaking floor, that the player must follow to avoid detection. When: generating dark stealth rooms. Why: Sound cues give the player usable information when light is limited.
- [heuristic] Place a room or lodging with a view of a target's door in the level as a stakeout spot for the player. When: Designing surveillance or stakeout missions.
- [heuristic] Place a high vantage point so the whole trail is visible at once, letting players read the clue before they reach it.
- [heuristic] Place landmarks along routes so players can correct distance estimates that darkness or sealed windows have distorted.
- [heuristic] Make a question such as why a room was chosen answerable by examining the building's layout and routines.
- [heuristic] A stairway or archway with a fixed sightline makes witness positions in a level meaningful.
- [heuristic] Map a setting's isolation to the availability of witnesses and rescue, and let the player judge access from the layout. When: Designing a location where witness presence affects the story. Why: Layout-based cues let players infer access without explicit explanation.
- [heuristic] Verify a stealth rule, such as a guard not seeing a doorway, by checking line of sight from the guard's actual position in the layout. When: when a layout must hide an area from an agent. Why: geometry, not intent, decides what the agent sees.
- [heuristic] Let a player on a tower or roof see into zones hidden from ground level, rewarding height and exploration. When: when designing vertical level layouts. Why: height reveals areas that ground movement cannot.
- [heuristic] Choose exit routes that avoid windows and open ground under observation, and use roofs or pipes as hidden traversal paths. When: when designing or planning a route past watchers. Why: Exposed open ground and watched windows make detection likely, while vertical routes stay out of sight.
- [heuristic] Store positions a witness describes as data and check them against level geometry, so testimony the layout makes impossible can be rejected. When: in games with NPC witnesses. Why: geometry determines what a sight line allows.
- [heuristic] A plain building layout can decide what is possible, since limited entrances and passages define the routes a culprit could take. When: Route of access is central to the puzzle. Why: Physical constraints of the layout determine which paths are feasible.
- [heuristic] Place a witness at a deliberate sightline and time so the player's own timing check becomes a test of whether a claimed account holds. When: An NPC claims to have been somewhere or seen something.
- [heuristic] Use a concealed watch point with partial cover, and remember that the watched may look up and see the watcher. When: Designing a hidden observation position in a street or building. Why: Concealment is reciprocal; the observed can spot the observer.
- [heuristic] Show only part of a room, such as through a door or cutaway, and follow it with a character's reaction to build tension before the clue appears. When: Designing a reveal in a room the player is about to enter. Why: A reaction without a visible cause raises curiosity.
