- [heuristic] Use a reflector or window across a shaft to let a player see a hidden space, so the sightline itself becomes the clue. When: designing a hidden room visible from elsewhere. Why: The player discovers the room through the geometry rather than through text.
- [heuristic] A person hidden from one watcher may be visible to another watcher at a different angle, so each sightline needs checking. When: Placing NPCs who can observe a meeting area. Why: Coverage differs by position, so one blind spot does not guarantee privacy.
- [heuristic] Give familiar props or NPCs an alternate state visible from a particular angle, rewarding players who inspect from new positions. When: Designing hidden detail in a known environment. Why: Changing position reveals information that default viewing hides.
- [procedure] 1) Stand where the witness claims to have been, or locate their window or room. 2) Check what they could see or hear from there, including whether any door is visible. When: A witness claims to have seen, not seen, or heard something, or claims a sight line or shot position. Why: Position limits what could be seen, so standing there tests the claim.
- [procedure] 1) Establish the distance. 2) Establish the lighting. 3) Establish the angle and any obstructions. 4) Establish the time of observation. 5) Compare the account to the resulting sightline. When: when testimony concerns an event seen through a barrier, at night or from a distance. Why: a claim that cannot physically be seen should be doubted until these conditions are checked.
- [procedure] 1) Take the witness's stated sequence of events. 2) For each step, ask where the person was heading, not only what they did. 3) Compare the direction of travel with the scene of the event. 4) Probe the unobserved gap after parting from companions and before the next known point. 5) Ask about things the witness did not volunteer. When: A witness's account includes movement or times that can be measured against a map or clock.
- [procedure] A level can be restored to a saved state, and replayed player routes can check whether a trap, pickup, or enemy still gives the same result. When: Testing whether a placement behaves consistently across runs. Why: Replaying the same conditions isolates the effect of a single placement.
- [procedure] 1) Pick two distant landmarks. 2) Stand at a fixed spot from which they appear in one line. 3) Use that line as a precise bearing to the hidden position. When: for placing a hidden object that is visible from a fixed point. Why: A line of sight through two landmarks gives a bearing that a reader can follow exactly.
- [procedure] 1) Name each landmark passed on the route. 2) Keep a mental map of direction from the sequence of names. 3) Note where orientation is lost, such as in fog. When: when a traveller must track an unknown route. Why: named landmarks let a traveller keep bearings that fog or darkness would erase.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the vulnerable points of the building. 2) Assign one watcher to the exit the suspects would use. 3) Place others behind cover to close in together. 4) Cover each remaining exit with a separate group. When: setting an ambush before suspects arrive. Why: every escape route is blocked before the suspects act.
- [procedure] 1) From the observer's position, check whether one fixed light or point hides another behind it. 2) If they line up, read the alignment as a danger signal. When: Testing whether a signal or hazard is aligned to a spot.
- [procedure] 1) Sketch the room and any adjoining room as a plan, marking doors, windows and furniture. 2) Stand at the position a witness describes. 3) Look through each opening and record what is visible. 4) Confirm fittings work before concluding. When: when a witness's account depends on what could be seen from a location. Why: claims about visibility are only checkable against the actual layout.
- [procedure] 1) Map walls, gates and entry points. 2) Mark which windows and balconies have a clear view. 3) Note obstructions such as foliage. 4) Keep the diagram as a permanent record when it bears on the solution. When: question is who could see or reach a location. Why: obstructions change the answer.
- [procedure] 1) Position the observer behind a narrow gap, such as a half-open window or a gap in a blind. 2) Observe the subject without alerting it. 3) Record only what the gap reveals. When: The subject must be learned about without being warned. Why: A narrow sightline limits what the observer sees and keeps the observer hidden.
- [procedure] 1) List every exit of the building. 2) For each exit, record which witness or sight line could observe it. 3) Mark exits with no observer as candidate routes for an unseen departure. When: when someone's absence must be explained by an unobserved route, or when designing a hidden exit. Why: gaps in observation reveal where an unseen exit may lie; observer positions give a fair, observable basis for deduction, rewarding careful observation over guesswork.
- [procedure] 1) Place windows, glass partitions and lamps so each hidden spot gives a specific view. 2) Let the player test each sightline on a map. When: Designing hiding spots and observation points in a level. Why: Defined sightlines make hidden positions readable and testable by the player.
- [procedure] 1) Fix the vantage point from which an immobilised player views the room. 2) Place layout, patrol routes and key items within that view. 3) Set critical enemy dialogue or planning where the sightline is clear. When: A player is immobilised by a trap, cutscene, or sleep effect but must still learn the situation. Why: A deliberate vantage point lets the player learn the threat before they can respond.
- [tradeoff] A vantage point that reveals more should carry detection risk: a larger concealed peephole shows more but attracts attention. When: when placing a hidden observation spot. Why: unpunished vantage points make information gathering trivially safe.
- [tradeoff] A distant location lowers exposure to observers but lengthens travel time, increasing the absence that may be noticed. When: Choosing where a player or NPC must go under a deadline. Why: Safety from observation and time spent away pull in opposite directions.
- [pitfall] A conclusion drawn from one short window of observation can miss a route used at another time.
- [pitfall] A short unexplained absence can look sinister or be innocent, so gaps need checking rather than assumption.
- [pitfall] Relying on sight alone misses differences that a magnifier would reveal. When: inspecting matched objects. Why: Small details fall below ordinary visual detection.
- [pitfall] Agreement among people in one room is not independent corroboration when all of them accept the same unverifiable report.
- [pitfall] A single check shows only the state at the moment it was made, not that the state never changes.
- [pitfall] A location clue is only as precise as its landmark; a vague feature such as a round tower leaves the exact point unclear.
- [pitfall] A single disturbance read in isolation can mislead, so test it against the rest of the scene before drawing a conclusion.
- [pitfall] A watcher who hears nothing may assume nothing happened. Ask about sounds as well as sightings. Why: Events can be heard without being seen.
- [pitfall] The observer's own noise, such as a knocked table, can be mistaken for an event. Note self-caused sounds. When: when the observer is moving or working nearby. Why: Their own actions can produce events that look external.
- [pitfall] A single watch post fails to cover a building that has several unguarded entrances.
- [pitfall] A route that avoids windows can still pass a sleeper who wakes, so sightline safety does not guarantee an undetected route. When: when relying on sightline-safe routing. Why: Unplanned events such as waking occupants can expose a route.
- [pitfall] Trusting a witness's route through a dark room without checking it against the layout can hide impossible movements. When: a route is described from memory in poor light. Why: Dark rooms make descriptions unreliable.
- [pitfall] A sweep that checks only eye-level surfaces misses what sits above or behind normal reach.
- [pitfall] A claim that a watcher would have seen an intruder is unreliable when the guarded area is dimly lit. When: when judging whether a watcher could have observed an entry. Why: low light limits what a watcher can actually perceive.
- [fact] A vantage point gives sight without sound: a watcher on a hill can see a meeting clearly yet cannot hear what is said. When: The meaning of an event depends on words. Why: Distance blocks sound, so sight alone is insufficient.
- [fact] Rooms with undrawn curtains can be seen into from the grounds, so anyone outside may have observed the interior. When: Hot weather, when curtains are often left open.
- [fact] An image can be arranged so that it reads correctly only from one viewpoint, limiting where observers can verify it.
- [fact] Elevation lets a viewer see over obstacles that hide the same ground from ground level.
- [fact] Walls and buildings block sightlines from a house, so a character can move between them without being seen by an observer inside.
- [fact] A locked store's position relative to an external opening determines who could reach it from outside and by what route. When: when assessing access from outside a building. Why: proximity to openings sets the possible approach paths.
- [fact] A pulled-out chair can hide a table and whatever sits on it from anyone entering through the door. When: Furniture stands between a doorway and an object. Why: Displaced furniture blocks sight lines to what lies behind it.
- [fact] A room corner, especially a sharp acute one, is hard to see into without strong light. When: Deciding whether an observer in a corner can be seen. Why: Lighting limits sight lines into corners.
- [fact] Thin partitions carry speech between adjoining rooms, so private conversation in one room can be overheard from the next. When: walls between rooms are flimsy.

## routes and access
- [mechanism] A known departure time limits who could have caused an event: an actor who left before it, or cannot cover the travel time, could not have been there. When: when a precise departure time, such as a fixed train, is known. Why: travel takes time.
- [mechanism] Footprints or dropped items act as directional clues that point the player along a route. When: Guiding the player between scene locations. Why: Traces oriented in a direction reveal the path an actor took.
- [mechanism] A concealed passage between rooms gives a second route the watcher does not know about, letting someone cross unseen. When: The route is the only way to cross unseen and the far exit is known.
- [mechanism] A hidden passage offers a plausible route by which an item could be swapped without anyone being seen. When: explaining how a switched item reached a location. Why: concealed routes allow unobserved movement.
- [mechanism] A hidden-room mechanic, such as a swinging bookcase, can be revealed when the player points to it at the right point in dialogue.
- [mechanism] Attackers who leave before witnesses gather go unseen, because the crowd arrives only to find the aftermath. When: An attack is followed by a gathering crowd. Why: Witnesses arriving late cannot place the attackers at the scene.
- [mechanism] Deep soft ground slows mounted pursuers, and a flooded obstacle forces the escaper to change plan, so terrain can shape a chase's outcome. When: when designing a chase across open ground. Why: terrain changes pursuit speed and route choice.
- [mechanism] A barrier can look secured while a hidden support has been weakened beforehand, so the first inspection misreads its real state.
- [mechanism] Environment tiles such as dry creeks or dust can rule out escape routes like water or footprints, letting players deduce by elimination.
- [mechanism] A hub can require the player to propose a locality first and then unlock a limited search, and a failed search can cost time and reduce NPC trust. When: When designing investigation structure in a game. Why: Limiting search scope makes deduction meaningful and gives failure real consequences.
- [mechanism] A hidden reflective panel in a corridor can make an enemy's silhouette resemble the player, so the designer controls when the misidentification is revealed.
- [mechanism] Terrain concealment defeats distant ground-level observers without optics, but fails against observers with height or optical aids. When: when choosing a hiding place for a site. Why: elevation or optics reveal what ground level hides.
- [mechanism] An outer wall that blocks sightlines from the main building hides anyone leaving through an exit on that side. When: Testing whether an escape route would be observed. Why: A blocked view removes the risk of being seen while leaving.
- [mechanism] A single-access room with a side passage to an outer court limits who can enter while still offering an escape path the player must rule out. Why: A restricted entrance narrows suspects, while the secondary exit keeps the puzzle from being closed too early.
- [mechanism] A seemingly impossible crime can be solved by a hidden route, such as a freight door or train, that the player uncovers by checking schedules. When: designing an apparently impossible scenario.
- [rule] Disguise a hidden route, sealed passage or trap as ordinary scenery, such as a drainage sluice, storeroom or solid wall, sized to match decor so it reads as ordinary. When: when designing a secret passage between rooms. Why: matching the decor hides the route from casual inspection.
- [rule] Make hidden door traces findable, such as a drilled hole, clean patch, fresh scratch or altered bolt, so close inspection can reopen a dead end. When: Designing secret entrances or a mystery where players reach a dead end. Why: traces let players infer a hidden route; a small physical clue rewards careful inspection.
- [rule] Give locked vaults, screens and windows signs of forced entry, such as a scratched lock or moved item, as physical evidence that narrows the suspect list. Why: physical damage gives a clear clue.
- [rule] For a locked room, list all exits including overhead openings and let the player mark each as checked; place a fair, non-obvious route that elimination reveals. When: Designing a sealed room or hidden-exit puzzle. Why: A hidden route that follows from elimination rewards careful observation without being arbitrary.
- [rule] Show every exit to the player and make each checkable in the world, such as through bars, bolt type or lock state; an exit hidden from the player breaks deduction. When: when building a deduction-based or sealed room puzzle. Why: Players cannot rule out routes they cannot see.
- [rule] Make hidden exits and mechanisms discoverable through visible clues; an exit or mechanism appearing only at the reveal, or leaving no trace, breaks fair play and the solution. When: when designing a secret mechanism or exit players must discover. Why: players cannot deduce a mechanism or exit they have no evidence of.
- [rule] Plant a clue to a hidden exit, such as a scuffed floor or draft in the first room or inspectable materials named early, before the trap activates. When: Designing a sealed room with a hidden exit. Why: An exit that appears only at the last moment reads as an arbitrary cheat; players need a checkable trace that leads to it.
- [rule] Give a sealed room a hidden second route, such as an old wing, cellar or passage, revealed by clues or by opening a container such as a worn safe. When: Puzzle levels with locked areas or any locked space that needs a way forward. Why: Players stop at a locked door unless clues show another way in.
- [rule] Generate each exit as sealed by a logged event or deliberately open, logging its time; a checker can compute usable exits and players can test claimed routes. When: Generating a locked-room puzzle or a level with doors that record or reveal passage. Why: A complete, logged exit list lets the game verify the solution; a logged route lets the player verify or refute a suspect's account.
- [rule] Hide a main or secret space behind terrain, mist, a turning corridor or a ring of terrain, revealing it in pieces as the player moves and climbs. When: when designing a secret area or hidden main space. Why: terrain forces exploration to reach it.
- [rule] Do not enter a hidden route whose return is uncertain, because a route you cannot exit safely can trap you.
- [rule] Build a sealed wing guarded by a keyholding NPC to create a natural hidden-captive or hidden-item puzzle. When: Designing a locked-area encounter.
- [rule] Keep a hidden captive behind a locked room, with the guard giving a false reason for the locked door.
- [rule] Let returning an item be a deliberate move that closes a line of questioning, and let the player recognize and test it as a possible bluff later.
- [rule] Hide a compartment in an NPC's pouch that requires a second search action to find.
- [rule] Give locked rooms an inspectable entry mechanism, such as a bolt that shows wear, leaves residue or works from both sides.
- [rule] Offer an apparent entry route that the player can disprove by testing the ground beneath it, such as a smooth bed beneath a tree. When: Designing entry points and route puzzles. Why: Testing an apparent route teaches players to verify routes rather than trust appearances.
- [rule] Give doors audio cues, such as a shifting foot or a touch on the handle, so the player can infer an NPC waits behind.
- [rule] Place a hidden closet or cellar that contradicts a respectable facade, so the gap between image and act becomes the discovery.
- [rule] Check who knows about a back or disused entrance and who holds its key, since such doors can serve as secret routes. When: assessing who could enter or leave unseen. Why: Hidden entrances bypass normal observation.
- [rule] Provide a hidden vantage point from which the player can watch NPC responses, rewarding attentive play. When: Designing stealth observation spaces. Why: Players gain information by observing rather than acting.
- [rule] Place a hidden NPC who watches a private action and can later alter a weapon, lock or trap.
- [rule] Give a room one exit or a single choke point, so the player can watch every NPC who passes, which suits stealth or tailing puzzles. When: when designing stealth or tailing puzzles. Why: one exit forces all traffic past a single watch point.
- [rule] Shift the active investigation to a new location when a lead moves, so the location change drives level progression.
- [rule] Design the grounds so retreat routes are visible and their exits are known, since layout defines an intruder's escape path. When: designing outdoor areas where intruders flee.
- [rule] Make the most striking anomaly the lead clue, such as a missing item or an odd tool, so the player has a thread to follow. When: Designing the opening clues of a mystery level. Why: Players need a specific thread to start investigating.
- [rule] Fix all watcher positions before any light is extinguished, and keep light sources covered so the watchers stay unseen.
- [rule] Give levels service ducts, pipe access panels or loose floor tiles as concealment spots, each with a visible but unremarkable sign. When: Designing hiding spots for items or clues. Why: A concealment spot that looks ordinary is easy to overlook, yet discoverable by a careful player.
- [rule] Give stealth exits a disguised escort NPC or a reserved path that enemies cannot easily follow. When: when designing an escape sequence. Why: Hidden allies and routes keep the player ahead of pursuit.
- [rule] Search all rooms connected to a scene, because residue such as clay or sawdust can fall in several places.
- [rule] Describe a hub's atmosphere through threat cues so its mood hints at what the player will find there. When: designing a hub area.
- [rule] Give hidden voids a measurable size and a telltale anomaly so players can detect them by reasoning rather than luck. When: designing a concealed space in a level. Why: detectable clues make discovery a matter of reasoning.
- [rule] Let a sealed vault be glimpsed through a window or from another door, creating a contradiction the player files away for later resolution. When: when a locked area should stay a mystery until later. Why: A partial view creates tension without giving away the solution.
- [rule] A partial crossing must be fair: it needs visible physical traces the player can inspect, not a casual trick.
- [rule] Design routes with hidden access points, such as service gates and paths behind hedges, so players can discover and map them.
- [rule] Give players a visible sign that points to a concealed route, and make the rule linking sign and route readable.
- [rule] Place a visible second route, such as a rope or ladder, that the player finds only after forcing or inspecting a locked door. When: Designing a sealed room with a hidden escape. Why: The route rewards investigation and explains how an escape was staged.
- [rule] Place stairs or hidden routes so players must reconstruct unseen movement and test it on the level.
- [rule] Avoid a second route where the investigator would disturb the scene or warn the suspects.
- [rule] Do not bury a lead so deeply that the player has no route to reach it; keep at least one reachable path to every key clue.
- [rule] Use fixed landmarks and sightlines in stealth spaces so that guard routes and cover points are checkable by the player.
- [rule] Record recent movement through tile-based state such as broken foliage, footprints and ground firmness that changes when a character passes.
- [rule] Gate access to a water area on a character trait, such as swimming ability, and let the player learn that trait through NPC talk or a record.
- [rule] When a witness disproves a key clue, move the player to a new lead, so the investigation keeps moving rather than stalling. When: a clue is disproved during play. Why: a fresh lead prevents a dead end.
- [rule] Hide a puzzle's mechanism in the room's structure, such as under the floor, so players inspect switches rather than hunt for a key.
- [rule] Place an exit that looks open but is locked by a mechanism the player learns about only by talking to an NPC. When: Designing exits in a locked-room puzzle. Why: Dialogue reveals the hidden constraint.
- [rule] Place a discoverable alternate route to a locked room in plain sight, such as a connecting door, spare key or forgotten lever, so a careful player finds it. When: When designing the entry path to a locked room or restricted area. Why: A visible but overlooked route rewards careful observation without hidden knowledge, and players accept a lock more readily when a discoverable alternative exists.
- [rule] Let the player see the approach to a sealed area while its contents stay hidden, adding a small clue at each step toward it. Why: the approach builds suspense before the contents are shown.
- [rule] Introduce a hidden bypass route through dialogue before the player needs it, giving directions and a time limit. When: Designing a sealed room that has a concealed second entrance. Why: A route set up in advance reads as fair, while an unexplained one feels like cheating.
- [heuristic] Ask why an object departs from ordinary practice; the departure is often a clue, but a deviation is not proof of wrongdoing, so test innocent explanations first.
- [heuristic] Level wear, damp, echoing ambient sound, rodents, drafts and wet floors can hint at a hidden room or passage without placing a map marker. When: generating areas with secrets. Why: environmental cues let players infer hidden space.
- [heuristic] Compare recent behaviour and scene states, such as shades, windows and lights, against established routine; deviations often reveal more than the routine and are worth recording. When: a timeline contains an unexpected visit or change; scene departs from habit. Why: unusual states suggest someone acted differently.
- [heuristic] Treat a secret passage as both shortcut and risk, and give the player a test, such as a sound check, before committing.
- [heuristic] A single directional mark, such as a drag, handprint or scraped gravel, suggests a route, while a trail that ends abruptly suggests escape.
- [heuristic] A trampled strip running beside a path suggests someone deliberately walked next to the path rather than on it. When: When a trodden margin is found near a path. Why: People avoid a path to avoid being seen or to avoid leaving clear tracks.
- [heuristic] A stated route that forces a large detour from the expected path is evidence against the account.
- [heuristic] Recorded departures and returns, such as leaving at six and returning at nine, bound a timeline into known intervals.
- [heuristic] Where a character flees to, not only what they say, reveals where they feel safe and who they trust. When: A character runs to someone after an incident and their account is being tested. Why: Choice of refuge is a behavioral trace that can contradict a simple story.
- [heuristic] Make hiding logic differ from search logic, since a search over a fixed list of containers and surfaces will always miss items hidden by rules off that list. When: designing hidden items and the searches that find them.
- [heuristic] Let a pipe or shaft serve as a hidden communication route, with a tug code that players can learn to signal contact. When: Designing secret routes in dungeons. Why: Hidden routes reward players who discover and decode them.
- [heuristic] Place a hidden room where a systematic player will reach it, with an ordered set of likely candidates such as walls, fireplaces and roofs. When: Generating a concealed room the player must find.
- [heuristic] Make a simple-looking scene hide a second layer that rewards players who consult a reference or notes system. When: designing an investigation scene. Why: Players who check their notes gain access to the hidden layer.
- [heuristic] Hide a transfer in a moment of distraction, such as a dropped item during a conversation, so the gap is fair and can be noticed later. When: designing a handoff the player should be able to detect afterwards. Why: a visible but easily missed moment makes the later clue fair rather than arbitrary.
- [heuristic] Seed a scripted escape earlier in the level, so a player who noticed the watcher can understand the reversal when it happens.
- [heuristic] After narrowing the possible routes, look for side passages, service stairs and side doors to identify which route was actually used. When: More than one route remains possible. Why: Secondary routes are often overlooked when attention stays on the main entrance.
- [heuristic] A feature that refuses to move was likely never designed as a door, so it should not be treated as an entrance. When: testing a sealed feature for an opening. Why: a design intended to open would respond to attempts to open it.
- [heuristic] Leave one entrance as the only possible route in and lock it, so every intrusion funnels to a known point. When: one entry is cheaper to defend than several.
- [heuristic] Position folk tales about a haunted corridor or wall noises close to actual hidden passages, so the story masks how the passage works.
- [heuristic] A confirmed sealed exit does not rule out a hidden passage elsewhere; check other walls before concluding a space is closed. When: a room's only known exit has been sealed. Why: a sealed exit may be the known route while another remains hidden.
- [heuristic] A straight, labelled channel where a natural stream meanders suggests human engineering. When: when comparing map representations of a waterway. Why: straight forms rarely occur naturally.
- [heuristic] Offer a second route into a locked room, such as a connecting door in an adjacent room, with a stiff bolt that rewards patience. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: A second route lets players solve the puzzle without the main key.
- [heuristic] A room with no tracks on a route can tell the player that nothing has entered by that route. When: Movement traces are used as level feedback.
- [heuristic] Add a fallback route, such as a slower alternative, so a failed path lets players revise their plan instead of being stopped. When: Designing timed routes with failure risk.
- [heuristic] A feature that disappears on a second look should hint at a hidden mechanic or concealed passage rather than being treated as an empty room. When: A room or object changes between visits. Why: A change in the environment usually signals something the player has not yet discovered.
- [heuristic] Hidden or rarely open passages can be signposted by an NPC remark, so the player has a reason to check the route.
- [heuristic] A level can record a last-seen entry point without a matching exit record, pointing players to where they should search. When: Designing a location clue that directs player investigation. Why: An unbalanced entry and exit record turns the location into a meaningful search target.
- [heuristic] When a window escape seems to lead only downward, check for upward routes such as sills, gutters and gable faces within reach. When: When evaluating escape routes in multi-story or elevated spaces. Why: The downward assumption is natural and hides upward options that may be reachable.
- [heuristic] A disproved alibi can unlock a new area or route, turning an investigation step into exploration.
- [heuristic] Replace an imagined hidden route with a physical route the player has already seen, so the solution is grounded in the environment. When: when an escape or access route is revealed late. Why: a route already seen feels earned, not invented.
- [procedure] 1) List every route into the scene. 2) Note the barriers and exits on each. 3) Decide who could have used each route. When: the scene is enclosed and the routes can be listed.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible route out of a sealed location, including water. 2) Test each route in turn against ownership and availability records. 3) Rule out each route that fails. When: a location appears unreachable. Why: each route is a separate exit to check.
- [procedure] 1) Place open rooms first in the search order. 2) Place locked rooms after them. 3) Add a sensory clue such as dust, odor or a displaced object to guide the player. When: generating a hidden-room puzzle. Why: Gives the player a method of search rather than random guessing.
- [procedure] 1) Test whether the candidate surface is fixed; if it is secured, rule it out. 2) Find the object that is clear of clutter, as the likeliest entrance. 3) Spread a fine layer, such as ash or dust, in front of it. 4) Leave, return and read the disturbed marks. When: the surface is likely to show handling and can be left and returned to without disturbing the layer.
- [procedure] 1) List surfaces and fittings no one has searched yet. 2) Inspect them for fresh holes, tool marks or disturbed parts. 3) Test the proposed route against the marks found. When: A room seems to have no other exit. Why: Overlooked fittings often hold the route.
- [procedure] 1) Find features whose function the design does not explain. 2) Measure them against the design. 3) Separate a true void from a chamber by size and disturbance. 4) Test whether a sealed feature was meant to open. 5) Treat unexplained damage as evidence of someone else's search. When: searching a building for a concealed space. Why: measurement and inspection separate real hidden rooms from structural features.
- [procedure] 1) Use the building's history to guess where a hidden space would be built. 2) Tap walls and note dull versus hollow sounds. 3) Pace the distance between rooms. 4) Convert paces to length to narrow the search. 5) Rule out unlikely surfaces. When: Locating a concealed passage in an existing building.
- [pitfall] A known departure does not prove absence or action: the record must also include the return trip, since a confirmed train departure shows only opportunity. Why: opportunity is not action.
- [pitfall] A place that is both eerie and vital leaves players unsure which signals matter.
- [pitfall] A thorough search of ordinary hiding places can still miss the real hidden route, so the search must also consider routes absent from the initial exit list. When: Searching a space for an escape route after ordinary checks fail. Why: The real route may lie outside the set of places the searcher expected.
- [pitfall] A seemingly secure opening may not be secure: a second door or freight entrance can contradict a claim that only one entrance exists. Why: witnesses often describe only the main route.
- [fact] Packed luggage and an open box signal that a departure is underway.
- [fact] Staff who saw someone leave can often recall the direction taken and the person's state of mind, such as a remark made in passing. When: A departure is being reconstructed.
- [fact] Families leaving a settlement and abandoned sites mark where residents chose to leave, a trace that a record of arrivals alone would miss.
- [fact] Cliffs, ravines and waterfalls can close an area as effectively as walls do.
- [fact] A detour path around a building can let a person leave without being seen from the building.
- [fact] Builders more often create a hidden space with a false inner partition than by thickening an outer wall, so inner walls are the likelier location. When: Searching a building for concealed space. Why: An inner partition is cheaper and easier to hide.

## locked room design
- [model] A scene plan keyed by letter marks each position, including the victim, empty seats, aisles, exits and staff stations, so claims about location can be checked.
- [model] A locked room with a stolen item has three clue types: the chain of custody, the list of key holders, and any preserved proof of the item. When: Designing a locked-room theft. Why: These three clues let the player reconstruct how access was possible.
- [definition] A locked-room puzzle seems impossible because its only entrance appears fastened from inside; that impossibility rests on the claim, which must be tested rather than assumed.
- [definition] A locked-room puzzle is solved by accounting for every entrance and exit of a sealed space, including minor and service routes.
- [mechanism] A locked room with a sound from inside, such as a cry, hides an event for a short time and builds suspense while its cause stays open. Why: Withholding the explanation briefly keeps the scene tense.
- [mechanism] If NPCs cannot tidy or move a scene's evidence, and the player has a clear preserve action, the evidence stays available for investigation. When: Designing scenes where NPCs might disturb clues. Why: Protecting evidence from NPC action keeps the clue accessible for the intended deduction.
- [mechanism] A locked room controls who can enter, leave, or be heard, so a trapped person has no witness and no easy exit.
- [mechanism] Hiding a key item across several containers lets a player who overlooks one still find it in another. When: Designing a hidden item in a level. Why: Redundant hiding spots reduce the chance of total failure while preserving discovery.
- [mechanism] A melting candle propping up a latch holds the lock open. When the wax softens and bends, the latch drops and seals the door.
- [mechanism] A mechanism's effect can depend on location; a bypass near a blockage can reroute the effect, so exceptions must be considered. When: when predicting the outcome of a known principle.
- [mechanism] A locked room's sealed features, such as reinforced walls, a single latched door and an unreachable window, define which escape routes the puzzle allows, since each closes one route. When: designing an enclosed space meant to resist entry or exit. Why: each listed feature removes one possible way out.
- [mechanism] Evidence can disappear during any interval when a room is left open, since everyone who enters during that window could have taken it. When: when a scene or storage room is unlocked for a period. Why: the open window lets unknown people reach the item unobserved.
- [mechanism] A door locked from both sides makes a room seem sealed, and forcing it reveals how much time an escape would take. When: A room appears inaccessible from outside. Why: Physical forcing exposes the real escape time and the traces left behind.
- [mechanism] A spring latch can be a small upward-pushing moulding, and a tug on nearby mouldings can release it, so the hidden panel opens without a visible control. When: Designing a concealed door with a non-obvious opener. Why: Hidden triggers reward careful manipulation of the surrounding detail.
- [mechanism] Locked and unlocked doors, switches hidden behind furniture, and lights that show recent use can form a locked-room puzzle with several solvable routes. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: Multiple routes let different players reach a solution by different reasoning.
- [mechanism] An apparent impossibility can dissolve once a physical remainder is found, revealing the real route an event took.
- [mechanism] Locking a key clue behind a condition, such as a later quest, keeps the player under pressure from an unknown answer while exploring. Why: Unresolved uncertainty motivates continued exploration.
- [mechanism] A door locked with a key that is then removed seals the space behind it, and the missing key's whereabouts become a question pointing to who held it. When: A space must be closed off to all but those with access. Why: The lock and the key's location together restrict entry and implicate the key holder.
- [rule] A door locked from inside rules out that exit, so a low window or detour path can be tested for physical possibility.
- [rule] Build a locked-room puzzle from candidate doors, bolts, keys and lookouts, each with an observable, checkable state; players rule them out one at a time, leaving one route open. When: When building an enclosed-space or sealed-room puzzle. Why: Discrete, observable states let players eliminate routes systematically, and a final open route rewards careful elimination.
- [rule] Map and make explicit every access route of a locked room, including doors, windows, vents, lifts, service doors and hidden stairs, so players can test each one. When: When designing a locked or sealed room. Why: Players can only solve an access puzzle if all routes are knowable.
- [rule] Keep one plausible hidden route in a locked-room puzzle so that the player's elimination process ends in a satisfying discovery. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle with a solution. Why: A reachable hidden route rewards systematic elimination.
- [rule] Track each exit's state and the last time a guard or trigger observed it, so the player's exit deduction can be checked. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: Checkable state makes deduction fair.
- [rule] List every sealed feature of a locked room, including all doors, windows and shared passages, so the solver cannot find an unlisted route. When: building a locked-room puzzle. Why: an incomplete list invites the solver to hunt for the one missing route, which makes the puzzle feel unfair.
- [rule] Treat a locked door or gate as a control point that limits who can enter or leave an area.
- [rule] Use keyed doors and hidden gates so that the key holder, not the layout alone, determines who may pass. When: designing restricted access. Why: access then depends on possession.
- [rule] Lock a key deduction behind a specific clue, so the player can only reach it after finding that clue. When: designing a mystery level with a central reasoning step. Why: gating preserves the reveal until the player has the needed evidence.
- [rule] Keep a threatened witness's clues locked until the player intervenes or removes the threat that silences her. When: a witness's knowledge is blocked by an intimidating party. Why: Releasing the clue only after the threat changes makes the cause and effect legible.
- [rule] Make a locked-room solution use a route whose use exposes a different suspect, so the solution carries its own red herring.
- [rule] A locked room whose owner refuses access creates a known gap that can be investigated later. When: designing a locked space the player cannot search at first. Why: A recorded gap gives the player a clear target for later investigation.
- [rule] A lock should have a clear in-world reason to exist; a locked room that is only a formality weakens the scene.
- [rule] Lock a puzzle room from inside with an NPC holding the key, leave neighboring rooms empty and nearby NPCs absent, so the player must find another route out.
- [rule] Allow exceptions in the rules at specific places, so a clever answer can fail there and add depth. When: when designing system rules for puzzles.
- [rule] A double-locked door signals guarded privacy and marks who controls access to the room behind it. When: a door shows extra locks. Why: Extra locking indicates restricted control.
- [rule] Give locked rooms layered access routes, such as keyhole, second door and force, with each step revealing something new to the player. When: Designing access to a locked room. Why: Layered access rewards exploration and makes each failed attempt informative.
- [rule] Build locked-room puzzles whose solution is a key copied during a small event the player can observe, such as a key dropped and returned. When: Designing a locked-access puzzle. Why: A visible custody gap gives players a fair path to the solution.
- [rule] Design locked rooms with a fixed sequence of barriers and times, so players can work out which window allows entry.
- [rule] Encode the entry method in a locked-room puzzle so players can deduce how the room was reached and who had access. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: Players can reason backward from the method to the access it required.
- [rule] Make intrusion events depend on discoverable prerequisites, such as a weakened wall, unlocked door or guard routine, that players can trace back. When: Designing an event where an intruder enters a space. Why: Traceable prerequisites turn the intrusion into a deduction task.
- [rule] Map every route into a restricted room so designers can verify which characters can reach a lever.
- [rule] Use a locked room as a constraint puzzle: the solver must find a hidden route or a reason the occupant never left. When: designing an enclosed space puzzle. Why: constraints force creative deduction.
- [rule] Place a key, exit or boss where the player's search habits do not look, but keep it reachable by a route the level has already taught.
- [rule] Give forced and keyed openings different visual wear so players can read how a container was opened. When: Designing damage states for locks and containers. Why: Distinct wear lets players infer the method and find the true key holder.
- [rule] A bolt on the inside of a door means the door can be secured only from within, or by someone with access to the bolt from inside.
- [rule] Build a puzzle whose solution hinges on a key missing from its usual hook, and include expected-location checks in the search mechanic so players can detect the gap. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle.
- [rule] Hide a physical route in a locked-room puzzle, such as a remainder in an adjacent room, that players find by searching around the apparent impossibility. When: designing impossible-looking events or locked-room puzzles.
- [rule] When a locked door hides a sound from an occupant, break it open rather than searching for a key. When: when a locked room suggests a captive or hidden person.
- [rule] Use a private room as a locked-room questioning setting, with no other characters present to overhear the conversation. When: Staging a sensitive interview.
- [rule] Offer a locked room one unusual route, such as a gap too small for a player character, so players must deduce the mechanism from the room's state. When: Designing a locked-room challenge. Why: A non-obvious route rewards deduction from fixed conditions.
- [rule] When early rules rule out destructive force, the designer must still leave at least one fair path to entry. When: Limiting methods in a locked-space puzzle. Why: Without a fair path the puzzle becomes unsolvable.
- [rule] A game lock puzzle can list its constraints in-world, such as key holders, noise rules, and sealed walls, so the player can reason toward a fitting entry method. When: Designing a lock puzzle the player must solve. Why: Visible constraints let the player reason rather than guess.
- [rule] Give a sealed room a few countable entries, such as one door, one key holder and a few hatches, so the player can check every route.
- [rule] Close an area once its central evidence is recovered, so the player cannot re-check the story. When: When designing the ending of a mystery area. Why: Locking the area preserves the conclusion without further verification.
- [heuristic] Give a locked space several admission routes, like a window, service stair, loose panel or hidden entrance, so the first deduction is not final and the lock becomes a puzzle. When: When designing locked spaces in a level. Why: A discoverable alternate route turns a barrier into a problem to solve. (x2)
- [heuristic] Before accepting a room is sealed, check its fastenings from the other side, other doors and windows, and trapdoors or fixture openings; ignoring adjacent rooms also misleads alibis. When: A victim is found in, or an alibi depends on, a room that appears locked. Why: Apparent sealing often has a hidden or overlooked route; unchecked locks and adjacent spaces are common blind spots.
- [heuristic] Use a locked room to hide an event only briefly, not as the sole source of mystery. Why: A single hidden event carries little weight if it is the only puzzle.
- [heuristic] When a sealed room seems to rule out the culprit's escape, ask whether the culprit was already inside before it was sealed. Earlier presence removes the apparent impossibility. When: a sealed or locked scene appears impossible.
- [heuristic] A locked room can hide an absent character, and a trace inside, such as a prepared bed, tells the player she left in a hurry. When: rooms the player cannot enter directly.
- [heuristic] A locked container with its key left in the lock narrows the people who could have known what it held to a small group. Why: Knowledge of the contents is limited to those with access to the container.
- [heuristic] If no key holder can be identified, the lock itself becomes the question, and the investigation shifts to how it was opened. When: No one can be identified as a key holder.
- [heuristic] Combine a physical key with a changeable code so the player must learn who knows the code and when it changed. When: Designing a locked-room or safe puzzle. Why: Two access layers force the player to gather information from NPCs and timelines.
- [heuristic] Hide items in containers with a mechanical quirk, and give an earlier hint about the quirk so the discovery feels earned. When: When placing concealed items in furniture or chests. Why: A prior hint makes the discovery fair and satisfying.
- [heuristic] When every entrance is sealed from inside, observing through a partial opening such as a window becomes the main way to gather information. When: direct entry to a space is blocked.
- [heuristic] Make a sealed room's lock obvious while leaving a second access route that looks secure but is not fully fastened. When: The room's apparent security is central to the story. Why: A visible seal convinces people that nobody can enter, so the gap goes unseen.
- [heuristic] A person who stayed inside a locked building can leave only when its doors reopen, which narrows an unknown intruder to a limited set of possibilities. When: a building has fixed hours and attended or timed exits.
- [heuristic] A fortified enclosed room suits puzzles where exits can be listed and closed one by one; it does not suit puzzles that depend on a hidden passage. When: choosing a setting for an entry-and-exit puzzle.
- [heuristic] Do not accept a claim that a key never left its holder without counting how many keys exist for the lock. When: Assessing whether a locked object could have been opened without force. Why: Unique locks still have a limited number of keys that can be accounted for.
- [heuristic] When a room is locked from outside, look for an exit route beyond the room or through a passage that staff or allowed roles can use. When: A sealed room seems to have no way out. Why: A lock blocks the obvious exit, so the solution usually lies in an allowed or overlooked passage.
- [heuristic] In a locked-room puzzle, give several access points with real and missing traces so the player learns to test each one. When: when designing a locked-room or sealed-scene puzzle. Why: Testing every access point rewards systematic investigation over a single guess.
- [heuristic] Track each door's lock state and the keys issued to each character in a locked-room puzzle so the player can check inventory against the sealing. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle with keys. Why: Checkable key and lock records let the player test theories fairly.
- [heuristic] A locked-room puzzle can state its premises openly, so the player can identify which premise is false, such as an unchecked sightline or a hidden passage. When: When designing sealed-room puzzles. Why: Stated premises give the player a fair path to the false assumption.
- [heuristic] A space built for performance likely has trapdoors, trick exits, and screens that hide performers, so presence counted by roll call may be false. When: A location is a stage or set with concealed fixtures. Why: Stage design includes features that hide movement and people.
- [procedure] 1) List every entrance and exit of a closed room, including doors, windows, vents, chimneys, ceilings, floors and hidden doors. 2) Test each route against the evidence and check its state, such as bolts or catches. 3) Record what each check rules out. 4) Keep only routes still possible until one is found or all are excluded. When: A room appears sealed from inside or on all sides, and the question is how someone entered or left unseen. Why: A complete, recorded check finds the real route and avoids overlooking an exit, instead of relying on a claim that none exists.
- [procedure] 1) Record each barrier and visible exit as open or closed. 2) Search hiding places such as under furniture, behind objects, chimney and walls for traps or panels. 3) Only after all listed exits are excluded, consider unlisted routes; accept impossibility only if none is found. When: A suspect appears to vanish from a watched or sealed space, or a puzzle requires accepting that a sealed space cannot be left. Why: Systematic elimination separates the real mechanism from apparent impossibility, and a documented search makes the impossibility fair.
- [procedure] 1) List every key, duplicate and handoff, and who holds each and where it is kept. 2) Establish how long each has been kept there and whether it ever left the owner's control. 3) Check the lock for forced-entry marks. 4) Look for timed records. 5) Ask narrow questions that close alternatives. When: A locked space has a small, known set of keyholders, or its access is tied to a limited set of keys. Why: Tracing each key's holders and history narrows suspects to those who could really open it.
- [procedure] 1) Survey the outside, including grounds, road and approaches, for any entry from outside. 2) Check each door and window against its locked state. 3) Count barriers such as dogs and household routines. 4) Once outside entry is ruled out, narrow suspects to people already inside. 5) Verify every route yourself. When: when goods are taken from a locked building and every entry point can be listed.
- [procedure] 1) List every entry to the room, including doors, keys, skylights, windows and adjacent rooms. 2) Record each lock's state. 3) Identify who held keys and who could have locked it. When: Reasoning about a room found sealed. Why: Listing every opening exposes overlooked routes, and key holders show who could have sealed the room.
- [procedure] 1) Examine the door and lock from both sides. 2) Check the key, including any found inside. 3) Check any bar or barrier. 4) Inspect furniture. 5) Inspect the body and its surroundings. When: the crime seems impossible because the room was secured. Why: A fixed order ensures each physical element is checked for traces of entry or action.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible exit, including ordinary doors, bolted entrances, service doors and staff or watchman routes. 2) Close each exit with a fact. 3) Time each closure. 4) State what remains as a hypothesis to test. When: A suspect appears to vanish from a sealed space. Why: Turning how-did-they-escape into checkable eliminations makes the answer testable.
- [procedure] 1) Place a locked container in the level. 2) Assign its key to an NPC who carries it. 3) Make the key itself a route to a puzzle. When: Designing a secret hidden in a locked container. Why: Tying access to a carried key makes the NPC a source of progression.
- [procedure] 1) Jam the bolt with a piece of metal so the door closes without locking. 2) Prepare an unseen second route for the exit. 3) Pause about ten minutes before leaving, long enough to wake and dismiss a witness. When: Designing a locked-room or alibi puzzle. Why: Each step keeps a closed-looking scene passable and removes a witness.
- [procedure] 1) Establish the physical constraints of the place. 2) List every method that remains possible. 3) Measure the layout, such as distances and heights. 4) Verify the chosen method by observation under the needed conditions. 5) Check corroborating traces. When: A crime or action seems to defy access to a location. Why: Closing alternatives and testing the method turns an improbable claim into a confirmed one.
- [procedure] 1) Map each room, its exits and window positions. 2) Mark which exits are visible from the main building. 3) List each possible route out. 4) Remove routes the layout cannot allow. 5) Keep routes that are unseen and plausible. When: A culprit has left a locked room and the exit route must be deduced. Why: Checking geometry and sightlines narrows the routes to those that fit the setting.
- [procedure] 1) Identify how the barrier is secured. 2) List every route past it, such as a key, pass-key, accomplice or hidden passage. 3) Check each route against a physical survey. When: when a crime occurred behind a secured barrier.
- [procedure] 1) Pull the handle and listen for a clank. 2) If the door clanks against a hook, the fastening was set from inside. 3) Conclude someone is still inside. 4) Act on that conclusion, such as posting a watch and waking others. When: Solving a locked-room problem through the door's fastening. Why: Testing the mechanism yields an inference about occupancy that drives action.
- [procedure] 1) List the keys and who holds them. 2) Identify open or unlocked doors. 3) Determine which guards or patrols were present and when. 4) Test each access route against independent witness accounts. When: when examining how a sealed or restricted space could have been reached. Why: Access routes are found from shared keys and open doors before assuming a room was impossible to enter.
- [procedure] 1) Establish the normal entrance and confirm it is locked. 2) Rule out that entrance. 3) Reveal the unlisted second entrance an NPC described earlier. 4) Let the player enter through it within a time window. When: A player must enter a sealed space by a route other than the door. Why: Ruling out the obvious entrance directs attention to the hidden route.
- [procedure] 1) Record the room's state: door and window locks, keys, lights, fires. 2) List every possible exit. 3) Rule out exits that are sealed, observed or too narrow. 4) Test the remaining exits against measured physical reach. When: When a room appears sealed and an escape or entry must be explained. Why: Systematic elimination and measurement find feasible routes that an assumption would miss.
- [tradeoff] A nearer target reached by a simple, hazard-free route can outweigh a farther one, but only while the defender stays unprepared at that target.
- [pitfall] A locked or bolted door or container proves little about access alone, since someone using another entrance, route or key may still have entered. When: When designing or reading a locked room. Why: Players assume a locked door blocks all routes, so an unseen second entrance surprises them. (x2)
- [pitfall] Unverified assumptions about a lock's type or state, including trusting one witness, can overturn an entire chain of deduction.
- [pitfall] A lock's state shows who would need to lock it, not who was inside, so a locked door alone does not establish the occupant's whereabouts. When: Inferring who was in a sealed room from its lock. Why: Locking can be ordinary caution by the occupant, so the lock alone proves little about presence.
- [pitfall] Treating a locked door as proof that no one left is an error, since the room may have other exits or false impressions. When: reasoning about an enclosed space. Why: constraints can be bypassed.
- [pitfall] Treating a strong lock as proof of innocence is a mistake, since it only moves the question to who held the keys and secrets. When: When a secure container is cited to clear suspects. Why: A strong lock limits the method, not the person who had access.
- [pitfall] Assuming a locked store is secure overlooks copied keys and people who have seen the key being used. When: when judging who could access a locked item. Why: locks are defeated by copies and observation.
- [pitfall] Investigators who lock onto the first premise, such as a misfired plan, read every new fact through it and miss alternatives. When: Early in a case where one assumption shapes the whole inquiry. Why: Confirmation bias makes consistent facts seem to support the first reading.
- [pitfall] A deduction about occupancy assumes how people normally lock up, so a known alternative exit can break the inference. When: Relying on routine behaviour to solve a locked-room puzzle. Why: Unusual exits invalidate assumptions about normal habits.
- [pitfall] A locked room becomes a dead end unless it offers a way out, such as a key, a second door, or a sound that carries.
- [pitfall] Measurement errors can create apparent hidden rooms, so a dimension discrepancy must be confirmed before it is treated as a concealed space. When: Inferring a hidden room from a measured discrepancy. Why: Small errors in measurement look like concealed space.
- [pitfall] Messages appearing inside a sealed structure pose an impossible-delivery puzzle the player must solve to find the source. When: Designing a sealed space with unexplained messages. Why: Sealed access makes the source a deduction challenge.
- [pitfall] Forcing a lock can damage what it guarded and destroy evidence of how the container was closed.
- [pitfall] A locked room is only impossible if its constraints are complete; a missed key holder or hidden route breaks the puzzle. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: Any unlisted access route makes the puzzle solvable in unintended ways.
- [fact] A locked drawer gives weak protection while the means of access to the room stays unguarded.
- [fact] Scratches on a lock's ward can show that a tool was pressed in to turn a key from outside, revealing the means of entry.
- [fact] If the victim had no key and the door was secured, access must have come from another person admitting him or from an unlisted route into the house. When: Reasoning about a locked-room death.
- [fact] A locked door and its keys show that a room was closed, but not who entered it or why.
- [fact] A sealed chamber whose method is visible but misread gives the player a fair puzzle.

## access control
- [mechanism] A two-part token, a spoken phrase plus a gesture, verifies identity; both must be given before an NPC reveals information. When: A contact cannot check a face or papers. Why: Two checks are harder to fake than one.
- [mechanism] A door's swing direction hides or reveals an adjoining room depending on the side someone approaches from, so the same door can block sight from one side and open it from the other. When: when a door separates two rooms with a sightline concern. Why: the door panel itself blocks the view on one side of its swing.
- [mechanism] A site reachable only on foot or by a narrow track restricts vehicles and so narrows who could arrive or leave unseen. When: A theory about movement depends on who could come and go. Why: Limited access removes routes that would otherwise let people pass unobserved.
- [mechanism] A concealed passage known to only some characters gives them unseen access, so routes can be hidden from others.
- [mechanism] When locks are rare as a world rule, access depends on social custom, so any entry becomes a question of intent rather than of physical barrier. When: A world has few physical locks. Why: Without barriers, the meaning of entry shifts to who is allowed and why.
- [mechanism] An open window or other opening that connects two rooms can serve as an access route, so its open or shut state matters to the event. When: two rooms share an opening or adjacent exterior route.
- [mechanism] A gummed flap can be opened and resealed covertly, while a wax seal resists covert opening, so breaking one leaves visible damage. When: Modelling sealed containers in a game. Why: Seal type determines how tampering can be hidden.
- [mechanism] A sensor that signals each entry, like a bell over a door, can log the order and spacing of visits, so repeat traffic becomes readable.
- [mechanism] High or hard-to-reach containers can require a specific tool, such as a ladder, before they can be searched.
- [behavior] A controlling person may deny holding a key and later produce it, so the lock works as leverage.
- [behavior] A culprit may close the entry point after using it, hiding the means of entry from occupants who then cannot see how the room was reached. When: Entering through a route left open. Why: Concealing the route delays discovery.
- [behavior] A gatekeeper may refuse visitors a known party vouches for, admitting only someone it personally recognises from an earlier event. When: The gatekeeper lacks a current guarantee. Why: Personal recognition outranks third-party assurance.
- [rule] Confirm who held keys to a locked space; key holders could enter without forcing access, and if one person held the only key, possible entrants narrow. When: Assessing who could have entered a locked room. Why: Keys remove the need to break in, shrinking the candidate list and the suspect pool.
- [rule] Model each key as an item with owner and custody history, recording every change of holder, so the game can show who could have opened a locked vault and when. When: Designing locked-area puzzles or theft investigations. Why: Recorded handoffs let the player derive who had access at each moment.
- [rule] Log each entrance's state (locked, relocked, briefly opened) as timestamped events, so the game can compute which rooms were reachable in a window and verify claims. When: Designing locked-room puzzles, alibi logic, or multi-room spaces. Why: A recorded timeline lets later checks verify claims and lets a player find the one route that was open.
- [rule] Let the key witness leave before questioning, so players rely on documents and sworn statements of uneven reliability while the planned path loses its main route. When: A central suspect or key witness is removed from play before questioning. Why: The planned path depends on that NPC, so players must rebuild from remaining evidence.
- [rule] Record a broken seal or damaged lock as persistent world state, so later NPCs notice the tampering and players infer the door was forced. When: A player opens and reseals an item, or a lock or bolt is forced. Why: Recorded tampering allows consequences in later scenes and changes what players can infer afterward.
- [rule] Make checkpoint checks depend on a stated credential; a gatekeeper under crowd pressure may mishear or skip verification, letting someone pass by claiming it. When: A checkpoint relies on a verbal claim and the gatekeeper is under pressure. Why: Busy gatekeepers may mishear or skip verification.
- [rule] Let parts, thrown items or sound cross a barrier to satisfy entry conditions, since a barrier can block whole objects while their parts pass. When: Designing a sealed area.
- [rule] Track which NPCs have access to each target, and let players verify opportunity, such as key holding, before weighing motive or clues. When: Designing mystery or deduction content with multiple suspects. Why: Access is a separate, checkable filter that narrows suspects before motive and clues are weighed.
- [rule] Ask who else knew of a relevant object or location, to establish who had access to it.
- [rule] Require players to find the human consent or opening that lets a hidden antagonist act, so it cannot cause harm alone. Why: Tying the antagonist to a human act keeps responsibility and discovery in the player's hands.
- [rule] Make a proposed escape trick follow the game's own rules, so it either works or fails in ways the player can check. When: designing a mechanism-based escape. Why: fair outcomes need checkable results.
- [rule] Eliminate locked doors and fastened windows first, then use the remaining entry point least likely to be seen. When: choosing how to enter a building covertly.
- [rule] Treat doors and servant NPCs as access gates whose state (key owned, guard present, shift hour) decides who may pass. When: designing entry control. Why: Gate state determines who gets through.
- [rule] Before committing to a building, check whether it has a second exit, since the target may leave by it.
- [rule] Leave a lock intact after a theft so the player reasons about access instead of searching for a broken door. When: When designing crime or theft scenarios in a level. Why: An intact lock directs the player toward who had access.
- [rule] Place nested doors and curtains as gates that control stealth approaches and access to a boss room.
- [rule] Plant a brief access trace, such as a guttering candle or a draught effect, that the player can check to rebuild when an opening existed. When: A locked-room puzzle relies on a seal made later.
- [rule] Include one overlooked opening, such as a sash, vent or shutter, that was used and then closed, so the room seems impossible until the timeline is rebuilt.
- [rule] Attach a physical check the world enforces, such as size, residue, or strength, so wrong solutions fail visibly.
- [rule] Track a visitor's approach through an alternate entrance that the guard logs do not record. When: designing a stealth or tracking scenario with several entry routes. Why: the player can discover a route the main log misses.
- [rule] Offer routes that avoid a noisy group as a tactical choice, letting players trade time or risk for stealth. When: Designing encounters with a group that can be bypassed. Why: A bypass gives players a meaningful decision about risk.
- [rule] Duplicate a cache or key item as a decoy, so players must recognize the fake before recovering the real item. Why: A fake that passes as real forces players to test items before trusting them.
- [rule] Establish a locked door early and make clear who controls access to it, so later access questions can be checked. When: when a locked room will matter to a later reveal. Why: Known access control makes contradictions visible.
- [rule] Use a knock-and-reply ritual as a clue-based gate: players learn the sequence by watching NPCs and must repeat it to pass. When: Designing access controls that reward observation. Why: Players can only pass if they have learned the sequence from earlier clues.
- [rule] Treat handing over an access key as an act of trust: record each use and require its return after use. When: Controlling entry to a restricted scene.
- [rule] Give enemies keys to doors the player assumes are secure, so threats can arrive by a route the player did not expect.
- [rule] Let a designer step through an NPC's route across connected rooms to check that a required path is reachable in the stated time. When: Designing scripted NPC movement that depends on timing and room connections. Why: Walking the route exposes unreachable paths or timing conflicts before players find them.
- [rule] Give doors a state, such as locked from inside, locked from outside, or unlocked, that changes as NPCs act, so players gain physical evidence.
- [rule] Make a courtyard a closed-access puzzle with several gates, one locked door, and a balcony that exposes one approach.
- [rule] Account separately for any authorized exception, such as a person allowed through an exit, before concluding that no one escaped.
- [rule] Let a doorkeeper log visits and report them to the player or to an enemy faction. When: designing staffed entrances. Why: Logged visits create evidence that others can use.
- [rule] Before following a subject into a venue, check that you can leave without drawing attention. When: Tailing a subject into a shop or room. Why: A trapped follower is exposed.
- [rule] Verify every door and lock state in a scene rather than assuming it; a debug view listing locks and their status supports this. When: A scene relies on doors or locks to control access or movement. Why: Assumed lock states can be wrong, and an unnoticed open or locked door breaks the intended access rules.
- [rule] Track custody of objects in game state so the player can see who last held each object.
- [rule] A locked cabinet or chest held by an NPC can open only when the player meets a condition, which paces disclosure. When: When designing gated information containers. Why: Conditional access controls the rate of disclosure.
- [heuristic] Use a key's inside or outside position to separate accident from deliberate act and reveal who handled the lock, since a key found moved shows someone had access.
- [heuristic] Read door, lock, bolt and light states as evidence of who had access, by what route and when; each limits who could pass, and combined they rebuild event order. When: Reconstructing how someone entered, left or moved through a room or sealed space. Why: Each state records a moment of access, and together they reveal the order of events.
- [heuristic] The force used to enter reveals intent: stealthy intruders pick locks, while intruders confident no one is inside may break the door; doors can encode this for players. When: Several people could have entered and the method suggests their goal or skill, or when designing doors and locks that show intruder type. Why: Different intruders have different goals, so the way they enter reflects what they want; readable entry signs let players deduce intruder type.
- [heuristic] A dust-free area on a knob or bolt shows recent handling; an untouched marker on a door or lock shows it was not used, separating door entry from hidden entry. When: Checking whether a door was opened recently, or designing evidence of how a space was entered. Why: Hands leave dust-free areas, and a marker that stays untouched shows the door was not used.
- [heuristic] Give a sealed room more than one plausible entrance, with at least one non-obvious, so players must search beyond the locked door and weigh explanations. When: Designing sealed rooms in a level. Why: A hidden route rewards thorough checking and avoids a single fixed answer.
- [heuristic] Treat a key's position, and a locked room's obstacles, as evidence the player can see and reason about. When: Designing locked spaces or key items in a mystery. Why: Position and obstacles constrain what could have happened.
- [heuristic] An intruder without a key to the main entrance must use another route, which can point toward people already inside the building.
- [heuristic] A key left in a door can signal carelessness or a deliberate arrangement rather than a secured room. When: Investigating a locked room.
- [heuristic] A suspect who could not have arranged the event that made the crime possible is less likely to be responsible. When: When a crime depends on a prior event that someone else controlled. Why: Whoever controls the enabling event controls the opportunity.
- [heuristic] Keying a message to an item one character always carries limits who can decode it, so only that character's possessions can unlock the message. When: designing a message puzzle tied to a character. Why: restricting the key to one carrier controls who can read the message.
- [heuristic] When a lock requires two independent conditions, such as a key and a code, narrow suspects by which condition each holds and by who can know the second. When: Determining who could have opened a secured object. Why: Two conditions held by different people imply that the act needed collusion or a route through their shared circle.
- [heuristic] An unlocked door may simply have been left open, so check the time and the habits of its users before inferring intrusion. Why: people leave doors unlatched for ordinary reasons.
- [heuristic] A player who avoids long exposure to risk is more likely to choose the access route with the shortest time at risk. When: Several access routes carry different exposure risks. Why: Longer presence inside a risky space raises the chance of detection.
- [heuristic] Hide a vault behind ordinary objects such as a wall of bones or stacked crates that the player must notice and clear. When: Designing concealed storage or secret areas. Why: Mundane facades reward careful observation.
- [heuristic] Check what lies between an entrance and its target, not only whether the door is unlocked, before assuming open access.
- [heuristic] Build each exit, such as a door, window, water route or tunnel, as a checkable route with ownership or access records the player can consult. When: designing exits from a sealed location.
- [heuristic] Access to a space can itself be a clue: an apparent vanishing often has a simple explanation based on who holds keys or knows an unlisted route. When: a character seems to disappear from a location. Why: control of access explains movement.
- [heuristic] When a direct route is sealed, look for a link through an adjacent room, such as a connecting door. When: A locked room blocks the intended path. Why: Adjacent spaces often share a connection that bypasses the lock.
- [heuristic] When a locked door resists, check easier alternatives such as a window before forcing the door. Why: A simpler route may exist that the obvious barrier hides.
- [heuristic] The absence of forced entry suggests the intruder knew the house or was admitted by someone inside. When: A locked room shows no signs of forced entry. Why: Someone with access or knowledge could enter without breaking anything.
- [heuristic] Avoid entering the target's room directly, since a sound or sudden entry gives the target time to flee. When: approaching a target who is still inside a room. Why: the target reacts to noise and surprise before the trap closes.
- [heuristic] Model doors with physical barriers that need effort as well as social barriers, and track who can open each door. When: Designing access control for rooms in a level. Why: Layered barriers make access depend on both effort and social expectation.
- [heuristic] Track lock state, key location and sightline for each door so the player can work out which routes were possible. When: Designing locked-room puzzles. Why: Recorded door states let the player reconstruct movement and reason about access.
- [procedure] 1) Enumerate every opening of a space, such as window, door, lock or catch, with its state. 2) Test each claimed barrier yourself and cross off barred or fastened openings. 3) Test remaining routes against every anomaly any explanation must account for. When: A closed space is found and the question is how a person could reach the victim, or openings can be listed. Why: Claimed barriers may not work; elimination narrows routes, and anomalies verify which remaining route is real.
- [procedure] 1) Decode the message with the candidate key; a length match alone does not confirm it. 2) If output lacks sense, discard that key. 3) Search the scene for another text the sender could have used. When: Choosing between candidate key texts, or a decoding attempt yields nonsense. Why: A single loose fit is easily produced by coincidence; nonsense output signals a wrong key rather than an unreadable message.
- [procedure] 1) List every exit and barrier limiting access. 2) Note whether each was watched, locked or open, and when it could have changed. 3) Compare each lock time with the victim's arrival, discarding exits blocked before arrival. 4) Test remaining routes against the physical scene. When: A closed building or space seems to block entry or exit, and a suspect's route must be narrowed. Why: The window of opportunity is where barriers and timing allow access; routes closed before arrival could not be used afterward.
- [procedure] 1) Locate the entry point. 2) Ask who could physically reach it. 3) Test each route against access, such as keys or ladders. 4) Keep only suspects who could use the remaining routes. When: a crime has no clear or unexplained entrance.
- [procedure] 1) List every entrance to the restricted area. 2) Note which entrances can be seen or reached from outside. 3) Ask each candidate about rules and hours. 4) Remove candidates who cannot reach the area. When: when a shared resource must be traced to a person.
- [procedure] 1) Test an insider acting alone. 2) Test an insider who holds a key. 3) Test an accomplice. 4) Test the victim leaving a window unfastened. Test each in turn against the traces.
- [procedure] 1) Establish what was known about a risk. 2) Establish what advice was given and how it was answered. 3) Establish who was told. When: when reconstructing a decision. Why: ordered questions expose who knew what.
- [procedure] 1) List every holder of a key, any master key, and who guards it. 2) Account for each key: confirm its holder had it at the time and record where it is. 3) Treat an unfound key as a sign its holder was absent or that it was taken on purpose. When: Entry or re-entry to a locked room is central to the case.
- [procedure] 1) List who holds a key to each door. 2) Check whether the door was locked at the time. 3) Study lighting and routes through the layout. 4) Look for signs of forced entry. 5) Consider what the visitor knew of the occupant's habits. When: a locked or semi-secured entrance decides who could enter. Why: access depends on keys, locks, layout and knowledge.
- [procedure] 1) Map which areas are sealed and who holds a key to each. 2) Note who lacks access. 3) Record each person's position and distance. 4) Build a timeline from sounds and events. When: narrowing the people who could have acted in a sealed or partitioned space. Why: Combining access, position and timing reduces the set of candidates.
- [tradeoff] Locking empty rooms and pocketing their keys forces any later passage through them to be deliberate, which makes movement evidence meaningful. When: A character needs to control movement through a space. Why: Deliberate access leaves a trace that investigators can use to infer intent.
- [pitfall] Test barriers from both sides: a bolt checked from one side says nothing of the far side, and a room sealed on one side may open through an adjoining room.
