When: Checking a room that appears sealed, or a connecting or communicating door. Why: The far side can be bolted or unbolted independently of the near side.
- [pitfall] Do not assume a returned key was never used: a briefly lost key can be impressed and duplicated, and a locked room can have other entrances. When: A key is out of its owner's control even briefly, or reasoning about access to a locked room. Why: A duplicate key gives access without forced entry, and keys can be copied.
- [pitfall] A closed gate or eliminated exit does not prove no one passed: a modest gate can be climbed, and someone hidden inside may leave later than expected.
- [pitfall] Entry method alone does not prove identity, since a skilled intruder can change the method deliberately and a signature needs corroboration. When: An entry method is used to name a culprit. Why: Method can be chosen to mislead, and one signature does not establish identity.
- [pitfall] An unmanned checkpoint records no witness, so passing through it leaves no evidence that can confirm a route. When: a route depends on an unattended door or gate.
- [pitfall] Omitting the exterior from a plan can hide entry or exit routes, such as terraces, ladders or outer towers, that the interior alone does not show. When: Mapping a building for movement puzzles. Why: Exterior features create routes that an interior plan does not reveal.
- [fact] Rooms that connect, such as a bedroom suite or another occupant's quarters, offer routes bypassing the main door, which overlooked connecting doors can hide. When: Analysing how a building can be entered or exited, or reconstructing access to a room. Why: Interior connections create routes invisible from the main entrance.
- [fact] A key bent or twisted at its ring suggests it was wrenched free, and a new-looking duplicate on the owner's ring suggests another person used the original in a lock.
- [fact] Walls with no waste space or hidden entrances rule out a secret-passage explanation for a sealed room.
- [fact] A credible report that a route was open at the time of the crime can collapse a conclusion that only one route existed.
- [fact] A simple bolt on a door can be worked from outside with a thin tool, so a locked door is not always sealed. When: A door's lock is a basic bolt or latch. Why: Basic fastenings can be manipulated from the other side.

## red herrings
- [definition] A red herring is a plausible false lead that draws attention from the true line of inquiry, often a suspect, clue or theory that appears to point at the answer. Why: It works when it is specific enough to act on.
- [definition] A red herring is typically built on a real fact attached to the wrong person, fits some evidence but not all, and resolves as innocent or unrelated once tested. Why: It exploits what the audience expects.
- [definition] A red herring can make an innocent person with plausible motive and access look like the obvious suspect while the real culprit stays hidden.
- [definition] Red herrings can arise from wishful thinking, public assumption, a deliberate joke, or an offhand remark or sighting with an innocent explanation; test them against facts.
- [mechanism] A hidden secret, debt, affair or mundane cause can explain a suspicious absence, lie or grotesque detail, clearing the suspect of the central crime without resolving it. When: Writing suspects with private lives, or a suspect's odd behaviour has a plausible, checkable everyday cause. Why: Ordinary or decoy motives give innocent explanations, so the player must separate them from real evidence.
- [mechanism] A vivid but unsupported suspect draws attention away from the real clue, so the audience searches there before finding the true trail elsewhere. When: A character is made to look suspicious but is tied to no actual evidence. Why: Attention follows the most striking impression rather than the most reliable clue.
- [mechanism] A red herring works because every piece of evidence against the decoy is true; only the inference drawn from that evidence is wrong.
- [mechanism] Give a suspect a documented, real grievance, but make the true culprit's reason differ, so players must check each motive against the suspect's gain. When: Designing motive-based decoys in a mystery. Why: Documented grievances look convincing and need checking against gain.
- [mechanism] A dismissive NPC's mundane or bodily explanation can serve as a red herring, which a second source such as a door or footstep log can confirm or refute.
- [mechanism] Ambiguous behaviors such as packing, idling or unusual errands can serve as red herrings when a later reveal explains them differently. When: When planning clue reveals in an investigation.
- [mechanism] A fabricated monster in a locked outbuilding can be heard and glimpsed, through noises and a door, before its artificial nature is revealed.
- [mechanism] A body resembling the target may be a disguised fugitive; a dropped item lets the player correct the false belief at that checkpoint.
- [behavior] A private secret can explain a suspicious absence so neatly that the audience stops asking about the crime itself. When: A character's disappearance has a plausible personal explanation. Why: A tidy motive for the anomaly closes the open question.
- [rule] Place a misleading prop, such as a weapon found far from the scene, that fixed room details or the crime's path contradict, so checking exposes the frame. When: Designing a red herring for a deduction puzzle, or a scene whose evidence should point to an NPC. Why: Contradictions reward players who check the prop against the rest of the room; a prop that cannot physically belong exposes the frame. (x2)
- [rule] Mark some anomalies, such as a displaced object, as accidental on purpose, so players learn one anomaly need not overturn a theory. Why: Players learn to set aside coincidences rather than treat every clue as meaningful.
- [rule] Design false leads that fail a checkable test, such as a missing track or timing mismatch, so verifying players are rewarded without ending the search. When: Designing misleading clues or false leads in a mystery level. Why: False leads that fail under testing reward careful checking; a testable alternate explanation makes players reason rather than guess.
- [rule] Pair each plausible wrong explanation with a checkable fact, such as a motive, alibi or physical detail, that undercuts it, so careful players can correct their theory. When: Designing misleading clues, a red herring pointing to the wrong suspect, or a wrong but coherent solution. Why: An uncorrected red herring is unfair to careful players; a fair undercutting fact lets players correct a wrong theory through play.
- [rule] Attach a rival explanation, such as bribery or coercion, to each clue so players must rule out alternatives and weigh evidence rather than accept the first answer. When: Designing a clue with more than one plausible cause. Why: Competing causes force players to weigh evidence.
- [rule] Plant plausible leads, such as an old relationship name or a faction-feud rumour, that players can chase, then reveal what the lead really was.
- [rule] Use vivid spectacle, such as a monstrous silhouette, open window or bloodied item, to point at a harmless NPC; a later record resolves it while mundane evidence stays overlooked. When: Designing misdirection in a level or quest. Why: Spectacle absorbs attention, so the mundane clue is easier to overlook.
- [rule] Give a red-herring character an apparent motive, false links or plausible resemblance, and suspicious item with no real tie to the crime, so players must test each claim before accusing. When: Designing a mystery where the obvious suspect should be misleading. Why: Testing forces examination of the evidence rather than accepting the first chain; verification beats quick arrests.
- [rule] Seed false-lead suspects with real but incomplete evidence, like panic, a secret or no alibi, and hide the true clue in an overlooked routine known to few. When: Designing a mystery with several suspects. Why: Partial evidence feels convincing until tested, and a fair clue remains available.
- [rule] Stage fabricated tracks or a planted item toward a false suspect, but leave a discoverable contradiction that rewards players who test the opposite explanation. When: Designing misleading clues or a deceptive staged scene in a puzzle. Why: Evidence looks authentic at first glance, yet testing the opposite explanation exposes the staging.
- [rule] Plant items or a recent note making an innocent NPC look like the target, then leave a checkable fact or contradiction that clears them. When: Designing a mystery with a misleading suspect or misdirection. Why: Planted evidence makes the obvious answer wrong without being obviously false; a plausible wrong lead rewards checking.
- [rule] Use an official theory as a visible cover story, partly self-serving and chosen for convenience, and give players a route to find evidence contradicting it.
- [rule] Place a decoy accomplice or front NPC with a cover story, plus a clue that exposes it on inspection, so players have a fair way to discover the false lead. When: Building a misleading lead or deceptive setup into a mystery. Why: Players must have a fair way to discover that the lead is false.
- [rule] Place visible bait, such as a left object, moved NPC or lit lamp, to draw a suspect or hidden trap into a predicted action players can watch. When: Designing a trap for an NPC or a bait interaction. Why: A visible object invites the target to return or act.
- [rule] Make an NPC's record match a different crime's method, so the player must compare modus operandi before concluding guilt. When: designing a red herring suspect. Why: method comparison reveals the mismatch.
- [rule] Place an item that looks like the key tool of the crime but is not, so a careful player can rule it out by checking.
- [rule] Make a red herring plausible enough to hold attention, but not so obvious that it invites disbelief.
- [rule] Mark which leads are red herrings so the player can drop them without losing progress. When: Designing a mystery with several plausible leads. Why: Players can abandon false leads without feeling their progress was wasted.
- [rule] Stage a ransacked room with missing loot that points to a plausible wrong culprit, while the true cause lies elsewhere. When: When generating a crime scene for players to investigate. Why: A credible surface explanation delays discovery of the true cause.
- [rule] Show a short, alarming note with no stated cause, and let players name a suspect they can later check. When: Designing mysteries.
- [rule] Make some partial traces deliberately ambiguous so the player must combine several traces to identify a person. When: Designing multi-clue identification puzzles. Why: Combining incomplete traces rewards synthesis over a single lucky find.
- [rule] Give lure items a checkable mismatch so players who verify details before trusting an offer are rewarded. When: A lure item is offered to the player as a shortcut. Why: Verification then pays off and careless trust is punished.
- [rule] Check an object's ordinary explanation against records before reading it as incriminating; smears or missing prints do not prove guilt. When: An object in a private space looks suspicious. Why: Legitimate items, such as valid prescriptions, can look sinister until their source is confirmed.
- [rule] Make a benign-looking item become evidence once the player links it to the scene, creating an item-interaction puzzle. When: Designing investigation puzzles. Why: Recontextualizing ordinary objects rewards connecting clues.
- [rule] Give a rival detective NPC a false theory that the player can disprove. When: When designing rival characters who pursue wrong leads. Why: A wrong theory gives the player a case to test and overturn.
- [rule] Give a plausible NPC a recent visit to a room, so the player's first conclusion is wrong and a later clue corrects it.
- [rule] Make chasing a red herring cost time without blocking the true path.
- [rule] Give each planted disturbance a plausible motive that the player can infer.
- [rule] Give a pattern an innocent explanation as a red herring; testing it teaches something without revealing the answer.
- [rule] Plant false evidence implicating a suspect, such as a rival's objects or a staged intruder trail, that a careful check exposes via material, handwriting, or inside-made footprints. When: when designing misdirection in a crime scene. Why: A false trail that can be exposed rewards careful checking without being unfair.
- [rule] Change the cost of each failed escape attempt, since repeating the same reversal in the same way flattens pacing.
- [rule] Let a non-hostile group be caught doing something suspicious but harmless, so the player's suspicion is tested rather than rewarded. When: When placing neutral NPCs in a level. Why: Players should learn to test suspicion, not only confirm it.
- [heuristic] Hide the solving object or detail, such as furniture, an NPC, or an early minor clue, behind a dramatic threat like a boss or curse that draws attention away. When: When hiding a solution in plain view or under a major encounter. Why: Players spend attention on the dramatic threat and skip familiar or early minor details. (x2)
- [heuristic] Design a misleading suspect or theory to fit what the investigator already suspects, such as a convenient suspect with an obvious motive, since confirmation is accepted with little testing. When: A character already has a suspicion that a misleading clue matches. Why: Confirmation feels like insight and reduces scrutiny; investigators examine expected fits less critically.
- [heuristic] Give each clue two or more readings or suspects, such as a sound meaning arrival or departure, and reveal the correct one through a further clue. When: Designing investigative clues in a game or mystery. Why: Multiple readings keep players reasoning and keep suspicion open rather than accepting the first conclusion.
- [heuristic] A red herring works best when its innocent explanation arrives after suspicion has already settled. When: Reader should share the investigator's wrong assumption for part of the story.
- [heuristic] Incidental details can act as red herrings: they look significant, draw attention, and leave the true solution intact. When: a mystery needs plausible details that appear significant but are irrelevant.
- [heuristic] Give the correct reading of a clue the same visual prominence as the wrong one, and reveal the true purpose of red herrings later. When: Placing red herrings or ambiguous clues. Why: Equal prominence lets players recover the correct interpretation.
- [heuristic] A red-herring event similar to the main case can seem to explain it, letting players test whether the link actually holds.
- [heuristic] Plant a plausible wrong match, such as a second NPC with the same item, to act as a red herring.
- [heuristic] A brief, uncertain sighting can serve as a red herring that the player must confirm or discard.
- [heuristic] Leave one trait of the culprit ambiguous so that a rushed player accuses too early.
- [heuristic] Scatter an ominous but misleading detail, and place a contradicting object nearby so a careful player can expose the cover.
- [heuristic] Plant an arrival with no witness, so the player must work out how the character got back into the area. When: Designing a clue that points to an unseen movement. Why: Unexplained arrivals prompt players to reconstruct the route.
- [heuristic] Plant a red herring with a prepared fallback, so a lesser clue can draw a probing NPC away from the true objective.
- [heuristic] Build a common shortcut that seems to rule out a threat, then let later clues show it fails, rewarding players who test their conclusions. When: when designing misdirection in a level. Why: players trust a plausible safe conclusion until evidence contradicts it.
- [heuristic] Use shifting shadows as deliberate red herrings, giving them a mechanical tell that players can find by looking from a second viewpoint. When: when designing misleading visual cues.
- [heuristic] Include a scapegoat NPC, such as a guard or gatekeeper, whose apparent guilt fails when timing or evidence is checked. When: Designing false leads in an investigation. Why: A false lead that fails under checking rewards careful play.
- [heuristic] Give eerie sounds and drafts a mundane, testable source, such as a hidden switch, and reveal a contradicting fact later to drive the next step. When: When designing haunted locations or eerie sounds in a level. Why: Testable explanations keep the supernatural option open until they fail, and teach players that the cause is mechanical.
- [heuristic] Give an unexplained event or several suspects plausible explanations, such as supernatural and human, each leaving a distinct checkable mark, so a visible detail rules out the wrong one. When: When designing a spooky or ambiguous event, or a mystery with several suspects or causes. Why: Competing checkable explanations turn atmosphere into an investigation; distinct marks give the player a fair way to eliminate options.
- [heuristic] Make a haunted-looking room a red herring by placing ordinary physical clues inside it that explain its eeriness. When: When designing spooky locations in a mystery. Why: Players can resolve the eerie feeling through verifiable human evidence.
- [heuristic] Keep spooky cues such as legends or odd sounds separate from verifiable evidence, so the eventual reveal is human and checkable. When: When designing investigation content with eerie elements. Why: Distinct cues and evidence let players test explanations independently.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC offer a ghost story, then reveal a real person behind the legend. When: When designing NPC rumors in a mystery. Why: The reveal rewards investigation and reframes the legend as human action.
- [heuristic] Plant a red herring, such as a blackmail-like payment, that explains part of the evidence and invites a wrong conclusion. When: Designing a misleading clue for a mystery. Why: A clue that explains part of the evidence feels convincing enough to pull the player toward a wrong answer.
- [heuristic] A red-herring explanation can be a fake mechanism that the player disproves with an in-world experiment. When: Designing a mystery with a misleading explanation. Why: A testable false mechanism rewards the player for experimentation.
- [heuristic] Include a shared detail in each record that the natural explanations do not cover, and make that detail the key to the puzzle. When: Designing a set of records or inscriptions as clues. Why: A detail that no explanation covers keeps the pattern from reading as a mere list.
- [heuristic] Give a level a rival faction with a clear reason to sabotage the player, and make its traces verifiable so the real threat stays hidden. When: When designing faction-based suspicion in a level. Why: Verifiable traces make the false lead believable while the true threat remains open.
- [heuristic] Build an environmental misdirect, such as a bright landmark that is really a sign of altitude, and let the player correct it through observation.
- [heuristic] Clear a false suspect first, then reveal the real culprit through a clue that appears only after the first accusation. When: when structuring a multi-stage mystery. Why: A clue gated behind the first accusation keeps the investigation advancing after the initial answer fails.
- [heuristic] Place environmental anomalies, such as a stopped clock or an out-of-place delivery, that the player can ignore, with an optional reward for noticing them. When: Adding detail to a level that rewards attentive play. Why: Optional anomalies reward observation without forcing the player to investigate.
- [procedure] When closing a mystery, explain each misleading element in turn, resolving every red herring and decoy through a reachable, testable explanation; untested or unexplained misdirection makes readers feel cheated. When: when closing a mystery. Why: Unresolved secrets and figures given attention without payoff frustrate players.
- [procedure] 1) Plant items, such as a note or photograph, that implicate a wrong faction or person. 2) Place later evidence that exposes the false cue or shows it proves a different point. 3) Confirm the player can reach that evidence. When: Building a mystery or investigation sequence that misleads before resolving. Why: A red herring stays fair only if the correcting evidence is available; the reversal rewards attention.
- [procedure] 1) Build a chain of zones. 2) Give each zone a plausible false solution. 3) Let the next zone refute that solution with new evidence. When: Designing a multi-zone investigation level. Why: Each refuted false solution moves the player forward and keeps the chain of suspicion moving.
- [tradeoff] Red herrings build misdirection and suspense, but a misleading clue that cannot be verified or dismissed later, or is dropped without explanation, feels like a cheat and frustrates. When: The story must steer players toward a wrong suspect or cause; choosing whether to plant a diversion.
- [pitfall] Accepting a convenient explanation that conflicts with the scene, or a reassuring one placed so the investigator stops looking, leads to a wrong conclusion and an early halt. Why: A quick reassuring resolution removes the worry that would keep suspicion on the main case.
- [pitfall] Chasing a red herring can erode an investigator's confidence in their own judgment.
- [pitfall] A red herring can be built from a true fact that is attached to the wrong person, so the evidence is real but the conclusion misleads. When: Designing misleading clues for a mystery. Why: Players check facts for accuracy, so a true fact misdirects more effectively than a false one.
- [pitfall] Overusing red herrings exhausts audience trust in the story's clues. When: Scattering misleading clues across a narrative. Why: Too many false leads make players distrust all evidence.
- [pitfall] A pattern found in a small set can be coincidence, so test whether it predicts something else before trusting it.
- [pitfall] Taking a deterrent rumor as a supernatural explanation can hide a human explanation that fits the evidence better. When: An unexplained phenomenon or rumor is being investigated. Why: A false myth can mask ordinary human activity.

## timed events
- [mechanism] A guard's or partner's timed departure, such as a patrol leaving a room, opens a short window to search or act, which closes when they return. When: stealth sections with leaving or patrolling NPCs.
- [mechanism] In a timed investigation, delay is costly: evidence fades, suspects flee and leads close, so the quest should change or fail if the player waits too long. When: designing time-sensitive investigations. Why: Time pressure makes the player prioritize questions and prevents indefinite stalling.
- [mechanism] NPC timetables showing who is absent or where, and quiet periods of low traffic, reveal windows in which a concealed act can run uninterrupted, findable by checking routes. When: a crime or action depends on an absence, routine or deadline, or on NPC routes in an investigation. Why: Timing both enables the action and exposes it to someone who reconstructs the schedule; reduced observation in quiet periods opens the only undetected opportunity.
- [mechanism] A timed staged event can hold one occupant's attention for a set period, opening a window for an accomplice to act in another room. When: A crime depends on removing a guard or occupant for a limited time. Why: The occupant's attention is committed to the event for its duration.
- [mechanism] Accomplices can cover a decoy's movements and trigger small visible acts, such as a gate bolt pushed shut, as signals in a timed sequence.
- [mechanism] A sedative timed to the victim's expected sleep lets a plot proceed during the victim's unconsciousness, so the timeline must be rebuilt around that window. When: A crime depends on the victim being unable to wake. Why: Timing ties the method to the intended moment of the crime.
- [mechanism] A timed trigger works like an alarm clock: it starts a playback at a set moment with no one present. When: a device might have run unattended. Why: the trigger separates the sound from the person who made it.
- [mechanism] In a timed negotiation the NPC's tone shifts as the clock runs down, adding pressure on the player to accept terms.
- [mechanism] Timed world events, such as a debt falling due or a festival, can draw a hidden NPC to a place where the player can watch.
- [mechanism] Overlapping scheduled movements of several NPCs let a player who tracks them order events and uncover meetings the player did not see directly. When: a level schedules NPC movements that intersect in time. Why: Players who track multiple schedules can reconstruct events that occurred off-screen.
- [mechanism] Place a shared timed event, such as a clock striking, that two characters must meet at, and reward players who track it.
- [mechanism] A location that closes on a timer can cut off a conversation mid-answer and send the player back for the rest of the information. When: Designing time-limited locations with information.
- [mechanism] Timed doors and guard routines placed in a level let players learn which exits were open at which times by checking logs. When: designing locked-room or closed-building puzzles.
- [mechanism] A scheduled mechanism that releases evidence at a fixed moment makes the release time itself a clue, since it can show when the evidence was placed.
- [mechanism] A timestamped quest log lets players reconstruct which NPC learned what first, and a chain of messages where each reply triggers the next lets a delayed or skipped message change the state the player finds. When: Designing NPC information flow and quest state. Why: Timestamps preserve order, and each message in the chain conditions the next state.
- [mechanism] A timed requirement, such as needing a key or warrant before entry, forces a choice between waiting and acting.
- [mechanism] Replaying a timed sequence, such as an exit followed by a wait and a knock, lets a player confirm a clue's timing before acting on it. When: a clue depends on the order and interval of actions. Why: Timing claims are easier to trust when the player can rerun the sequence and observe it.
- [mechanism] Periodic events such as recurring attacks can serve as a level clock the player reconstructs by eliminating dates against external records. When: a sequence of events repeats on a schedule. Why: regular intervals let the player order and test events.
- [mechanism] A degrading object such as browning fruit, a burning candle or a torch works as a timer, since its state at discovery gives the elapsed time since a second person was present.
- [mechanism] Player arrival close to a scheduled event, such as the day before, creates near-miss tension. When: Designing scheduled events the player must reach in time. Why: Arriving with almost no margin raises stakes without ending the chance to act.
- [rule] Build deadlines and timed events, such as a scheduled knock, into puzzles with checkable intervals that narrow possible solutions and make the deadline fair. When: Designing puzzles with deadlines or timing clues. Why: Checkable intervals give a verifiable constraint and make the deadline solvable. (x2)
- [rule] A timed distraction, such as a helper's false errand, fetch quest or dropped object, can clear the room and open a window to inspect a hidden item unobserved.
- [rule] Give world events such as guard patrols and chimes fixed timestamps the player can reconstruct from clocks, bells or NPC routines, letting conflicting times become a puzzle. When: Designing a world where the player must order events. Why: Reconstructable times reward attention and make conflicts meaningful.
- [rule] Give NPCs timestamped scheduled movements so players can check who was present at a moment and which witnesses could have seen a crime window. When: Players must reconstruct what happened at a given time. Why: Known timed schedules let players test who was present or positioned to observe an event.
- [rule] Use in-game time to gate events, such as a guard shift, merchant's closing hour or secret meeting window, so players must find the exact window an NPC could act. When: Designing scheduled content or time-gated scenes.
- [rule] Deliver clues via letters, field-agent reports or scheduled check-ins timed to player progress, pacing discovery across locations and bridging gaps the player cannot reach. When: The player must receive information away from the main scene or across several locations. Why: Timed delivery paces discovery; timing controls pacing and forces prioritisation.
- [rule] Use timetables and fixed-time anchors, such as a set departure hour or a vehicle's arrival, to eliminate escape routes and narrow where a suspect could have gone. When: Designing a suspect's possible escape in a timed world. Why: Schedules constrain where a suspect could have gone.
- [rule] Build time-window puzzles from overlapping NPC schedules and events, letting players narrow an interval with several constraints until only one window fits. When: Designing timed events in a mystery. Why: Overlapping schedules let players deduce the time of an event.
- [rule] Gate an NPC's departure or a plot event on a timer, such as a wedding or a train, so the player must decide when to act.
- [rule] A timed trap or poison with variable delay should be reasoned from the player's route and the likely delay, so the trigger time is inferred. When: Designing a delayed hazard that a player must reconstruct. Why: Players can then work backward from their route to find the trigger.
- [rule] Fix a time and place for a confrontation so the characters must plan around it, creating a ticking clock.
- [rule] Give a doubting NPC a chance to speak; if no one voices doubt, let the panic escalate on a timer. When: when modelling mass accusation in a game town.
- [rule] In a timed quest, let a key NPC arrive at the last moment if the player was told where to look, so the arrival reads as a payoff. Why: A signposted arrival turns a late event into a planted payoff rather than a surprise.
- [rule] Make in-world event timers vague at first, and narrow the player's estimate as more clues arrive.
- [rule] Schedule a locked clue to become readable at a chosen in-game turn or event, using timed reveals to pace content.
- [rule] Give a timed window a fixed duration and mark the target room clearly on a map, so the player knows where to go and how long they have. When: a timed infiltration window is designed. Why: a clear target and known time keep the window fair and readable.
- [rule] In a timed level, set a clock that closes off options and make the missing evidence the goal.
- [rule] Stage two events in a level, one real and one staged or misread, and require the player to assign each trace to the correct event.
- [rule] Create timed movement puzzles where the gap between a log entry and the physical route reveals a hidden step, such as a secret passage used in that interval. When: when designing route-based clues. Why: Mismatched time and distance signals a concealed route.
- [rule] Give a timed NPC rendezvous a visible countdown or a shadowing agent who reports the NPC's location, so the player feels the pressure of the meeting. When: Designing a meeting the player must reach. Why: Visible time and location cues build tension.
- [rule] Use regular intervals as a timer the player can predict and exploit. When: Designing recurring events or schedules in a level. Why: Predictable timing lets players plan actions around it.
- [rule] Set a promised confrontation behind a time gate, so the reveal waits until the player returns to the location. When: A reveal depends on the player's schedule or revisit.
- [rule] In a timed-clue puzzle, place clues across locations so players must check whether a suspect could cover the distance in the time available. When: Designing an alibi that the player can test. Why: Travel times expose a claimed alibi that looks plausible on paper.
- [rule] Make timed puzzle routes depend on fixed-time events, and let a mistimed action break the plan visibly; timestamp each event in a log so players can find the conflict. When: Designing timed puzzles with guard shifts, scheduled doors or similar events. Why: Visible failure and a readable event log let players reconstruct the sequence and locate the error.
- [rule] A journal or diary found early can set the schedule for a later timed event, such as a trigger or a timed door.
- [rule] Trigger timed events from a specific observable cue, such as a phrase or light change, so players know exactly when to act. When: Designing timed events in stealth scenes.
- [heuristic] Use a timed object such as a clock strike, sundial or bell as a fixed point to place uncertain times on a timeline. When: building a timeline from unsure observations. Why: an exact time gives a reliable reference. (x2)
- [heuristic] Stage timed NPC arrivals, such as stakeouts or reveals, with readable approach cues like footsteps so players build suspense while they wait.
- [heuristic] Use timed interruptions, such as patrols passing, an accident or an alarm, to shape confrontations, forcing the player to decide whether to act now or later. When: designing confrontation scenes. Why: Interruptions create time pressure and let the suspect escape.
- [heuristic] Use the tide or fading light as a deadline, since they can erase traces before the investigation is finished. When: Traces are on a surface that the environment will erase. Why: Natural erasure sets a limit on how long evidence remains available.
- [heuristic] Give a climax a clock, such as a head start or a closing window, so that each lost moment raises the stakes. When: when designing a chase or final action. Why: A visible deadline makes every delay feel costly.
- [heuristic] Schedule an arrival so the target is still in place and the timing looks ordinary to them, not earlier or later than a normal visit would be. When: A target's movements depend on a short window of opportunity. Why: Unusual timing can make the target suspicious and prompt them to leave or react.
- [heuristic] Use unseen messages and timed appointments as quest triggers that open new areas or scenes.
- [heuristic] An NPC with a timed daily schedule gives the player a baseline, and missed appointments can trigger alerts or reveal someone's removal from the scene. When: When designing NPC schedules that feed game events. Why: A deviation from a known baseline is detectable and can drive consequences.
- [heuristic] A short, repeated, timed sequence of events can explain a situation more fully than its final outcome alone. When: when the order and timing of visits matter. Why: the sequence carries the story.
- [heuristic] A timed world event, such as a flooded chamber draining at a set hour, can prompt an NPC to move a hidden item only at that moment. When: when designing NPCs that hide items.
- [heuristic] A sudden event such as a scream or flicker of light can draw the player's attention while a real change happens elsewhere in the scene. When: designing a scene where a hidden change must go unnoticed. Why: Sudden stimuli capture attention, leaving other changes in the scene less likely to be perceived.
- [heuristic] Give timed events short, stated intervals so the player can reconstruct the order of a trap sequence or collapse.
- [heuristic] Use a timed countdown with a short open window as an event, and check NPC timelines and alibis against it. When: when designing a time-limited event. Why: timelines reveal who could have acted in the window.
- [heuristic] Give a timed window, such as a tide, patrol or guard rotation, some slack so the arrival window is achievable but still demanding. When: when designing a time-limited opportunity. Why: a window with no slack is effectively impossible.
- [heuristic] A level can run a timed schedule of events that the player orders from witness accounts, with gaps the player closes by finding traces.
- [heuristic] Pair a traveling key character with an announced deadline, so the player must reach them or defend the route in time. When: A key character is away from the player's location and an outcome depends on their arrival. Why: A visible deadline converts waiting into an active objective.
- [heuristic] Schedule timed events, such as guard rotations or departures, and let players work backward from fixed times to find a safe window. When: Designing timed events the player must plan around.
- [heuristic] Let the time of day change an NPC's alertness, so an early or late encounter yields different answers from the same character. When: designing NPC responses across a day. Why: fatigue and timing change how guarded they are.
- [heuristic] Choose the moment for a risky entry when the target's attention is engaged elsewhere, and avoid times when an alarm makes entry impractical. When: Entry into a guarded room is planned.
- [heuristic] Divide a watch into timed shifts with agreed handover times, so the post is never left unattended.
- [heuristic] Weather changes can serve as timers, and a stopped or skipping clock can serve as level dressing that signals an unreliable world. When: Designing time pressure or atmosphere in a level. Why: Environmental timers create urgency without interrupting play.
- [heuristic] Signal a timed event such as a patrol or messenger with a sound cue before its source is visible to the player. When: When designing approaching NPCs or timed events. Why: A sound cue builds anticipation and lets the player prepare.
- [heuristic] Use an external deadline, such as an escape window, as a level timer that rewards the player for tracking the right suspect early.
- [procedure] 1) Arrange an ordinary-looking reason to draw the occupant away. 2) Wait until the agreed time. 3) Enter the marked room and act quickly while the occupant is absent. When: a person must be absent for a search or confrontation. Why: an ordinary reason draws the occupant away without alerting them.
- [procedure] 1) List who will be present, away or incapacitated at the planned time. 2) Identify anyone who could raise an alarm. 3) Neutralise those people. 4) Set an arrival window. 5) Recheck their states before entering. When: the next action must wait for a known window and witnesses could raise the alarm. Why: Planning around the actual state of people reduces the risk of discovery.
- [procedure] 1) Walk or run the route between two points. 2) Time it against a watch at each checkpoint. 3) Compare the measured time with the stated timing before accepting the route. When: A route between two points matters to the case. Why: Measured timing tests whether the stated route is physically possible.
- [procedure] 1) Leave a suspect alone with the object for a fixed, timed interval. 2) Time the wait from outside. 3) Examine the result of the interval as the key evidence. When: testing a suspect's actions during a short unobserved period. Why: A fixed, checkable interval makes the result interpretable.
- [procedure] 1) Test the passage both ways before committing, going down and back. 2) Place a marker for the return route and note where the steps begin. 3) Advance and, when the watcher approaches, leave by the far end. 4) Return by the same path, timed to stay out of the watcher's sightline. When: Using a hidden route to avoid a watcher.
- [procedure] 1) Observe in timed blocks at different hours. 2) Log every block, including empty ones. 3) Treat repeated visits, not a single empty session, as the basis for a pattern. When: Building a routine model of a moving subject. Why: Empty sessions are informative, and one session can mislead.
- [procedure] 1) Let an enemy or hazard act on a timer across several visits. 2) Make each act slightly stronger or closer. 3) Show a visible sign that the threat is still active during quiet periods. When: Designing pressure that builds between encounters. Why: Visible persistence lets silence read as danger rather than safety.
- [pitfall] A role-based disguise cannot be kept up for long, so the scheme must be timed tightly around the event it serves. When: Planning a disguise that must hold through a limited window. Why: Prolonged exposure increases the chance of being recognised.
- [pitfall] Time pressure forces a search to end early, and the unsearched space can hide the target item. When: When a search is interrupted by a deadline or an arriving person.
- [fact] A timepiece that stopped and was never reset keeps its last set time, which can serve as a fixed point to build a timeline from. When: The object was wound or set at a known moment and not adjusted afterward. Why: A stopped mechanism freezes the time it last showed, so it stays readable long after the event.

## hiding spots and concealment
- [mechanism] Each added precaution creates a new witness who can contradict the story, so more precautions add exposure rather than safety.
- [mechanism] Removing a postmark or similar marker erases where a message came from and how it travelled. When: A message's origin must be hidden. Why: Postmarks are the physical record of origin and route.
- [mechanism] Moving through lit, open spaces can draw less suspicion than hiding in dim corners, since observers search hidden places first. When: Designing a stealth or disguise path. Why: Suspicion tends to focus on concealed spots.
- [mechanism] Items left in an open shared container, such as a bag in an open hall, can be reached by anyone with access during a diversion window. When: when a diversion leaves a space unattended. Why: The target is absent, so the container is unguarded.
- [mechanism] A player who moves alone is more vulnerable to ambush, so solo movement carries a visible risk. When: A player separates from companions in an area with hidden threats. Why: A lone target gives a watcher an opening.
- [mechanism] Restraining a concealing person's arms forces their hands forward, lifting the coat and revealing what they hide.
- [mechanism] A building with a second exit lets a moving target lose a follower, who is left stranded at the first entrance.
- [mechanism] A stealth or stash mechanic can score candidate spots by observer count and sight lines, and the scores can shift as NPC routines change. When: Designing a system that lets an agent choose hiding spots. Why: Observer counts and sight lines change with NPC movement, so the best spot changes too.
- [behavior] Hiders exploit searchers' reluctance to recheck places already dismissed or finished, choosing a spot judged unlikely or a site where a search has ended. When: a disappearance is planned, or a hidden item must survive a completed search. Why: searchers rarely re-check places they dismissed and treat a finished search as exhaustive.
- [behavior] An anonymous writer who omits names and signature, disguises the hand, and delivers in person expects to be recognised by the recipient. When: An anonymous note is delivered in person.
- [behavior] A person disposing of an object often picks a mundane spot that looks like it will destroy it, such as a fire or a pile of fuel. When: when concealing an item from searchers. Why: An apparent destruction discourages a closer look at the spot.
- [behavior] Owners tend to hide valuables in places they consider safer than the obvious container, so the obvious container may be a decoy. When: when a theft involves an obvious safe or chest. Why: owners treat the obvious hiding place as the one thieves check first.
- [behavior] Elaborate precautions can also serve to draw attention to one expected route and make observers overlook another. When: A suspect's protections focus on one obvious route. Why: Attention concentrates on the most visible safeguard, hiding an overlooked route.
- [behavior] A person who fears exposure may give only a partial name in a record, keeping others unsure of where they are or what they look like. When: a written identity could expose the person to strangers. Why: a fully traceable record creates risk.
- [behavior] A figure who hides and keeps distance is read as a stalker, though the same behavior can be protective. When: A character follows another unseen. Why: Concealment is interpreted as hostile intent.
- [behavior] A person who has hidden something tends to add a second obstacle at each access point, because the secret seems obvious to them and they assume others will find it too. When: when concealing a secret the hider already knows well. Why: Having found the secret easily, the hider overestimates how easily others will find it.
- [behavior] A person who believes they are hidden tends to stay still and wait for a passerby to leave, rather than act. When: a hidden person sees someone approaching. Why: they assume they have not been noticed.
- [behavior] A person behind a closed door may also be listening, so they can react to sounds made outside. When: a listener is outside a closed door.
- [behavior] People and animals avoid isolated, dangerous places, so anyone seen going there stands out. When: In a remote location with a known hazard. Why: Avoidance makes visitors conspicuous.
- [rule] Make hiding spots finite and checkable: state traffic patterns, sightlines, exits, open-plan layout and features such as furniture height or drawn curtains, so players eliminate spots by logic. When: Designing a space where something or someone is hidden and players must search or deduce its location. Why: Explicit information lets deduction replace luck, and controlled sightlines keep the set of possible hiding places small enough to verify. (x2)
- [rule] Check who could have shared a holding cell with a suspect, since placement can allow contact with the culprit. When: suspects are held in shared or nearby cells.
- [rule] Place occupancy traces in a hideout to signal that a character is nearby, and use a planted note to show the hidden NPC knows of the player.
- [rule] Keep suspicion of a leak hidden where possible, because a visible reaction alerts the culprit, who will then change the plan. When: a leak is suspected. Why: the culprit adapts once they believe discovery is possible.
- [rule] Do not treat an over-careful setup as proof of guilt, since an innocent person may also take precautions. When: Weighing a suspect's elaborate safeguards. Why: Caution has many innocent causes.
- [rule] After observing, remove any sign of approach, such as a ladder, and hide in cover so the observer is not noticed. When: leaving an observation point.
- [rule] Treat avoided locations as private meeting places, so their presence on the map becomes a clue. When: When placing locations.
- [rule] Place cover positions that give a full view of the target, so the player can watch a route without being exposed. When: The target is in an open area.
- [rule] Make a culprit's concealing action leave a trail, such as a vent opened after a death, so players who notice the unnecessary change can find the route. When: Designing a puzzle in which the intruder hides the method. Why: The act of covering tracks becomes the clue that reveals the method.
- [rule] Define a route as an ordered sequence of doors and occluders so the game can answer whether a path was observable from a given vantage point.
- [rule] Use an unexplained danger to deter exploration and protect hidden content, such as an NPC or smuggler, in a cursed zone. When: Designing a level with a secret area that should stay hidden. Why: Players avoid the feared zone, so the hidden content goes unnoticed.
- [rule] Hide a key item inside an unexpected container, such as an animal or bag, and let the player find it through examination or inventory. Why: Unexpected hiding places reward inspection and make the item's location a discovery.
- [rule] Use a physical puzzle, such as a measured spot, to trigger a hidden cellar or pit as a late-level reveal.
- [rule] Hide a key item in a trusted NPC's private room, so the player finds it by tailing that NPC at night.
- [rule] Hide a timed sound source behind furniture so it can be found only by a specific check, not by casual inspection.
- [heuristic] A hiding place fails in full view of a main entrance or constant foot traffic, but evidence in a confusing, rarely visited area stays concealed because few pass through. When: judging whether a concealment was feasible, or placing hidden evidence in a level. Why: observers and passersby would notice movement in busy spots, and low traffic lowers the chance of discovery.
- [heuristic] Blend a clue among ordinary or similar items rather than letting it stand out; players then find it by comparison, and conspicuous clues trigger suspicion and reduce fair surprise. When: Placing clues for later discovery in a collection of objects. Why: Similarity disguises the one item that differs, and conspicuous clues trigger suspicion.
- [heuristic] A hiding place works best among features people do not examine, such as service access points or loose boards left for routine maintenance. When: Choosing or searching for a hiding spot in a building. Why: Ordinary structure is rarely inspected, so a concealed item there looks unremarkable.
- [heuristic] Rarely worn clothing and seldom-opened containers are good places for small items to be hidden or forgotten. When: A small item is missing and some garments or containers have not been used recently. Why: The owner is less likely to check places they have not handled for a while.
- [heuristic] A hunted party can stay unseen by settling in a crowded, poor district, where strangers draw little notice. When: a party must disappear among ordinary people and cannot rely on authorities. Why: Few idle watchers and busy residents mean strangers go unremarked.
- [heuristic] Ask whether a hiding place fits the effort spent obtaining the item; a careless spot for a hard-won object can count against the theory. When: An item was concealed and its concealment is part of the explanation. Why: Concealment that does not match the value of the item is less likely.
- [heuristic] Strong light on a space can exclude it as a hiding place for a person. Why: a hidden person would be exposed in strong light.
- [heuristic] To make a clue harder to notice, remove it rather than alter it, so the player must search for the absence itself. When: designing a concealed clue. Why: A changed object invites comparison with its earlier state, while a removed one does not.
- [heuristic] A small hole sealed with matching material shows where a substance was inserted, and it is easy to miss on the surface. Why: the repair hides the entry point from casual inspection.
- [heuristic] Plant movement patterns, such as use of a back door, as readable tells that signal concealment. When: designing NPC routes and behaviour. Why: players learn to read movement.
- [heuristic] Hidden items are often placed in an ordinary routine setting, such as a repair shop, where they look normal rather than in hard-to-reach spots. When: when designing or searching for a concealed object. Why: Legitimate-looking settings draw less suspicion than unusual hiding places.
- [heuristic] A hiding spot that projects from a wall can give a view of the room while keeping the observer concealed.
- [heuristic] Hiding places are usually where searchers do not look first: high above the floor, inside tall or decorative furniture, or behind ornament. When: When placing hidden compartments or secrets in a room. Why: Searchers check eye-level and obvious surfaces first, so unusual positions go unexamined.
- [heuristic] Place hidden listening spots behind thin walls adjacent to unused rooms, such as an empty, locked-off or to-let room, and show the layout early. When: Designing a concealed observer or eavesdropping position. Why: An unused adjoining room gives an unseen place to stand and listen.
- [heuristic] Placing a hidden target in the last-checked, most enclosed space creates a natural climax for a search. When: Designing a search sequence that builds to a finale.
- [heuristic] Add a route with recognisable points where the player can be spotted, so stealth and disguise choices matter. When: designing tailing routes. Why: known detection points give stealth meaning.
- [heuristic] Define a location by the things it could conceal, such as loose floorboards, cramped gaps between floors or screened shrubbery. When: a location must hide something or record a past action.
- [heuristic] Hide key proof in a location only one character knows, so the hiding place is itself a secret clue the player must discover. When: When designing a clue that must be reached through knowledge of a specific place. Why: Restricting knowledge of the hiding spot makes the location part of the puzzle.
- [heuristic] A search tool that gives a sound cue on contact helps the player find hidden items out of sight. When: When designing hidden items in a game world. Why: Audio feedback locates objects the player cannot see.
- [heuristic] Hide a container's key on a routine item the owner carried, so a search of personal belongings reveals what a room search misses. When: When placing the key to a locked container in a mystery space. Why: Players search habitual items and effects, rewarding attention to character routine.
- [heuristic] Hide a disguised entity in plain sight, such as an ordinary-looking mount or an NPC's equipment, so a recognition check exposes it later. When: Designing an enemy or threat disguised as something common. Why: A disguise visible in the scene rewards observant players and makes the reveal fair.
- [procedure] 1) List every concrete place something could be concealed. 2) Rule each place in or out with a specific reason, such as fire risk or areas already searched. 3) Keep the open places and check them against the hider's likely habits. When: Locating a concealed object whose owner could reach it but a casual searcher could not, or deciding who could have been concealed and where. Why: Constraint elimination narrows the search to the most plausible place.
- [procedure] 1) List who visits the place on weekdays and weekends. 2) Estimate how often each group visits. 3) Compare the visit rate with the claimed hiding period. When: a theory depends on how long an object sat in a place before discovery.
- [procedure] 1) Rule out unusable hiding places by elimination. 2) Choose the most sensible remaining cover. 3) Copy the look of the crew so you pass as one of them. 4) Move quietly and stay still until needed. When: movement is confined to a vehicle and a cover identity exists.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the trace the suspect's action was meant to remove. 2) Check whether an existing protection already covered that trace. 3) Judge whether the stated reason fits the action. When: A suspect's action can be explained by a precaution.
- [procedure] 1) Find a hidden vantage point with a view of the target's location. 2) Make a small opening for observation. 3) Keep the watch running over several nights until the target's pattern is caught. When: a secret act must be witnessed.
- [procedure] 1) Move into a setting where observers have not seen you. 2) Adopt that setting's routines fully, not just its costume. 3) Keep every routine consistent with the new role. When: when a suspect must pass among people unfamiliar with them in the new setting.
- [procedure] 1) Keep one party talking while facing away from the likely hiding place. 2) Coordinate a second party silently to watch from cover. 3) Watch for the listener to move or reveal themselves. When: When a listener is suspected but a direct approach would alert them. Why: Speech that rewards approach makes an eavesdropper move into view without confrontation.
- [tradeoff] Hiding evidence in a visible spot can be safer than a concealed hiding place, but both depend on the culprit controlling who passes the location. When: Choosing how to dispose of incriminating evidence. Why: A concealed item looks more suspicious, while a visible one risks being noticed by an observer.
- [tradeoff] A resource-limited agent is easier to predict and search, while an agent that can restock anywhere is harder to bound. When: when designing an enemy or pursuer's movement. Why: Limits create predictable search areas but can be broken by restocking.
- [pitfall] A hiding place chosen from the owner's known habits is predictable, so anyone who knows those habits can find the object; assuming a careful person hides predictably can mislead investigators. Why: A hiding place is only concealed if it does not follow a pattern others can read.
- [pitfall] Objects moved or handled before investigators arrive alter traces, can drop out of the record, and mislead about the original scene and who was present. When: Investigating a scene others had access to first. Why: Prior handling alters traces, and an object's later presence then suggests the wrong people were on the scene.
- [pitfall] Searching a hideout can alert the person hiding there, so plan the approach first.
- [pitfall] Elimination of hiding places depends on assumptions about how the hider thinks; if the hider thinks differently, the deduction points to the wrong place. When: Reasoning about a hider's choice of location. Why: The constraints used depend on the hider's reasoning, which may differ.
- [pitfall] Containers and tools used to hide an item carry their own traces, such as scent, that can reveal what was hidden and where.
- [pitfall] A precaution against one trace can leave another, such as changing footwear to avoid soft-ground prints while hard surfaces still show prints.
- [pitfall] Once players learn that obvious objects can hide clues, every openly placed object looks suspicious and the concealment effect is lost. When: Plain-view hiding is used repeatedly across a game. Why: Players generalize the trick and begin searching obvious places.
- [pitfall] Ignoring masking factors such as engine noise, distance, or hidden injuries leads to misreading silence as meaning nothing happened.
- [pitfall] Assuming concealment is safe is a mistake: a nearby person reaching toward the hiding place can nearly expose the observer. When: the observer is close to the people being watched.
- [pitfall] A hiding place obvious to a searching NPC defeats its purpose, so search routines should check the most likely spots first. When: Designing NPC search behaviour for hidden items. Why: A search that checks likely spots first finds the obvious concealments.
- [fact] Valuables a person keeps hidden on their body reveal what they value and expect to need in an emergency.
- [fact] Concealment often targets what an object would reveal about its owner's identity, rather than the hider's own needs.
- [fact] Concealed spaces behind removed boards or panels can hide a person or object from search.
- [fact] Hidden items are often kept in clothing linings, sleeves and pockets, where the holder expects no one will search.

## search coverage
- [definition] Spatial deduction rebuilds a hidden layout from limited senses, using step counts, marked starting points, touch and sound when sight fails. When: a character or player must map a space without light.
- [mechanism] Give each zone or wall a visible cleared or completion flag, and mark each container as checked, empty or holding an item, so players track remaining coverage. When: a hidden target is searched for across a grid of zones. Why: each cleared zone narrows where a small hidden target can be. (x2)
- [mechanism] A search mechanic that appears to clear an area gives the player a false sense of safety, and a later disruption then creates suspense before the reveal. When: Designing a search or clear-the-area moment before a twist. Why: A sense of safety makes the later disruption more startling.
- [mechanism] A container that has not been emptied keeps items from earlier dates, so its contents bound when an item could have been placed there. When: When a housekeeper or other source reports when a container was last cleared. Why: Unemptied containers retain items for the period since the last clearing.
- [mechanism] Measurable cues such as step counts and sound differences can narrow a secret door's location to a short span, letting players search only that area. When: Designing a secret door that players must locate. Why: Quantified clues convert vague searching into a bounded search.
- [mechanism] An aerial observer flying low over a valley makes previously safe places less safe, so a fugitive must reassess which routes are hidden. When: an overhead observer is active. Why: height gives a view that ground routes do not.
- [behavior] Players and characters pass over plain packing materials and boxes on high furniture, because such things blend into the surroundings and go unnoticed. When: moving through a room without searching it. Why: they offend no eye.
- [rule] After a failed search, test where the object went by searching surroundings methodically, not assuming it is elsewhere; someone else may hold it, so suspicion should move on. When: a search comes up empty. Why: absence in one place does not clear the item's whereabouts.
- [rule] Search every kind of hiding place beyond the main scene: gardens, sheds, conservatories, pavilions, kennels, top branches of each tree, cloakrooms, sweepings, waste containers, lounges and restrooms. Why: clues outside the building are easily overlooked when searchers check only the inside.
- [rule] Record a negative result, since a fully searched area with nothing found narrows where a hidden object can be.
- [rule] Give counts and measures such as dimensions, door offsets and window numbers, so the reader can build an accurate mental map. When: when the layout matters later. Why: exact figures make the map checkable.
- [rule] Give each chamber with a corpse or wreck a fixed inspection list of posture, bedding and objects so players can search systematically.
- [rule] Make in-game ledgers searchable by date so players can narrow entries to the probable date of an event.
- [rule] Search a small group in one room with outer doors locked, and search people of the same gender together, so the search is seen as fair. When: A small closed group shares access and a search is socially acceptable. Why: Locked exits and same-gender searches reduce objection and keep the process credible.
- [rule] Let players search a group of NPCs in a room, set a remembered flag when someone refuses, and track reactions as behavior clues. When: Designing an interrogation or search mechanic. Why: Persistent flags let refusal and reactions affect later inference.
- [rule] Give NPCs searchable items, such as a key with a coloured tag, that point to a specific door or room. When: an NPC carries an item tied to a location. Why: searchable pointers turn NPC inventory into navigation clues.
- [rule] Let players stash an item in a high, partly hidden spot and retrieve it with a tool, so the item never needs to be held in hand. When: in stealth or puzzle levels.
- [rule] Route item searches through logical locations so players learn the layout while looking.
- [rule] Assume players and NPCs will recheck a stash, so give a hidden item a second hiding place. When: placing a hidden item. Why: a single hiding place is likely to be found.
- [rule] State the limit of search coverage, such as dim light, and do not treat one failed check in a partly seen area as ruling out a hiding spot.
- [rule] Escalate to a second, more thorough search when a first pass finds nothing and suspicion of hidden activity remains.
- [rule] When the number of actors is unknown, add coverage rather than assume a single actor, since a group may be present. When: the number of people involved is not known. Why: A lone-actor assumption misses accomplices who may act unseen.
- [rule] Give each character a fixed vantage point so its claims about what it saw can be tested against the level geometry.
- [heuristic] When obvious places fail, check overlooked containers such as bins, baskets and drawers, and sift mundane material and every fragment. When: an expected item is missing from obvious places, especially where the owner's habits explain its presence. Why: careless habits make items survive in neglected spots.
- [heuristic] Give a covert search a narrow checklist of specific items to find, rather than letting the player browse the whole space. When: a stealth mission requires locating information. Why: targeted search is faster and reduces exposure time.
- [heuristic] Make a blanket search of all staff low-value in the game, and reward players who narrow the search to those who knew the goods were there. When: Designing investigation rewards in a mystery level. Why: Rewarding targeted reasoning over brute-force searching teaches the intended deduction.
- [heuristic] Compare the effort needed to leave a scene with the effort needed to enter it, because the easier direction often shows how the person actually left. When: When an intruder's route in and out is uncertain. Why: Exits from a raised position can be a single step, while entry may require climbing.
- [heuristic] When a target could be in many rooms, wait at the exit instead of searching, if the target's route is predictable.
- [heuristic] Structure search phases so each area yields less than the last, pushing the player toward the next lead.
- [heuristic] Give the player a reason to examine a low-stakes side quest closely, since a main-plot item can be hidden inside it. When: Designing side quests that connect to the main plot. Why: Expectation of triviality makes players skip details that matter.
- [heuristic] Study soft ground at close range, lying flat, rather than surveying it from above. When: When traces are likely on soft outdoor surfaces; hard, dry ground holds tracks poorly.
- [heuristic] When an NPC is missing, prompt the player to search the expected locations first, since the search itself can reveal clues.
- [heuristic] Nested containers, such as a desk holding a pigeonhole that holds a small case, reward thorough searching and give players a reason to check every compartment. When: designing hidden items for a search-based puzzle. Why: players who learn that compartments hide items will check each one.
- [heuristic] A scripted search order can guide the player through rooms so the most urgent space is checked first.
- [heuristic] Place two observers with overlapping but different sightlines, so each covers the other's blind spot. When: one vantage point cannot cover every route. Why: the overlap closes the gaps each observer leaves.
- [heuristic] Treat a negative search result as conclusive only after every zone has been checked and nothing has been found.
- [heuristic] State a puzzle's search boundary in advance, saying the clue is in this room and visible and touchable, without naming the exact item, so the player searches within a fair limit. When: Designing a search puzzle where the player must find a clue. Why: A defined search area keeps the puzzle fair and the answer still open.
- [procedure] 1) Handle every ordinary object, not just look at it or read it. 2) Squeeze, shake and turn each one. 3) Notice any wrong give, sound, weight or crackle that marks a hollow. When: searching for items or messages hidden inside everyday objects. Why: looking alone misses hollows; concealment inside ordinary containers is invisible to visual checks.
- [procedure] 1) Open and search each container or drawer in turn, separately, since a later one may hold what an earlier search missed. 2) Record its contents. 3) Return every item to its original place so nothing looks disturbed. When: the room must stay undisturbed for later examination. Why: orderly searching keeps traces of the search hidden.
- [procedure] 1) Search every wall and corner for a second opening. 2) Divide the suspect area into wall and floor sections. 3) Test each section and record which are ruled out before concluding no hidden door exists. When: a room appears to have no exit. Why: hidden exits are often placed where the first search did not look.
- [procedure] 1) List every route into and out of the room: doors, windows, transoms, air-shafts, dumb-waiters, side doors, spare keys. 2) Check each at every height, including low door gaps, upper transoms and cracks. 3) Test each against physical evidence. 4) Conclude a route was used only when evidence supports it; treat sealing as proven only when every route is closed. When: a room looks sealed on first reading. Why: sealing one height does not rule out another; a locked door alone does not prove a sealed room.
- [procedure] 1) List every candidate who passed through the relevant area. 2) Question each one. 3) Follow up on every lead that comes out of the answers. When: Tracing a vehicle or person through a region. Why: Systematic coverage avoids missing the one candidate who matters.
- [procedure] 1) Narrow candidate locations using the target's likely habits and motives. 2) List every remaining candidate. 3) Check each in order and record its result. 4) Mark each candidate as checked. When: the target's trail is missing but the candidate set is finite. Why: an ordered, recorded check covers every option without repeats or gaps.
- [procedure] 1) Empty the container onto a clear surface. 2) Examine each item one by one. 3) Do not look only for the item already in mind.
- [procedure] 1) Visit a fixed list of sites in set order. 2) Justify each visit with a cover story. 3) Pay small sums for access. 4) Keep spare money so the canvass can continue. When: Searching many locations for one item or person. Why: A fixed route and cover make the search systematic and sustainable.
- [procedure] 1) Have the responsible official conduct the search. 2) Lay each item out in view. 3) Record every item as property as it is handled. When: when personal effects are searched as evidence. Why: a record of handled items supports later proof.
- [procedure] 1) Scan the edges of cover for odd shapes. 2) Check protruding parts such as limbs. 3) Mark the spot before anyone moves closer. When: Searching open ground for a body or hidden object. Why: Bodies often show as small irregularities at the edges of vegetation.
- [procedure] 1) Start at a stated end of the guide. 2) Feel along the guide step by step. 3) Follow a cord to the point where it enters a hazard. When: for clues that require a precise search method.
- [procedure] 1) Check each sense or channel in turn for anomalies. 2) Return in better light or conditions. 3) Find the spot that stays still against moving background. 4) Search that spot directly and repeatedly. When: a small clue is lost among moving or busy surroundings. Why: stepwise checks catch what one glance misses.
- [procedure] 1) Strike the surface with a tool. 2) Listen for a change in tone. 3) Repeat strikes in steps across the area to narrow where the hollow lies. 4) Measure from a known reference. 5) Compare the result with what is expected there. When: a cavity may lie under a floor or behind a wall.
- [procedure] 1) Strip or sound walls, floor and ceiling to detect hidden openings. 2) Inspect the door for removable panels or weak points. 3) Consider whether a person could pass under furniture near the door. 4) Check the window and adjoining space as a route of flight. When: A room seems sealed and the apparent seal is the central problem. Why: Each structure is a distinct possible exit that needs its own test.
- [procedure] 1) Lie low so the surface is seen at a grazing angle. 2) Sweep a light across the floor. 3) Look for seams between blocks. 4) Clear rugs away and check again. When: a floor may hide a concealed cache.
- [procedure] 1) Turn the handle slowly in small steps. 2) Stop when pressure goes beyond a point, which signals a bolt. 3) Restore the handle to its original position before releasing it. When: Testing whether a door is locked from the other side. Why: Gentle probing avoids noise and leaves no sign of tampering.
- [procedure] 1) Inspect every access point (door, window, roof) for marks of passage. 2) Check supporting marks such as ladder marks or footprints. 3) Search every place a hidden object could sit, including storage. 4) Complete the search before forming a concealment theory. When: when a scene appears closed but someone must have entered or left. Why: Missing a storage space or access point leaves concealment untested.
- [procedure] 1) Divide the area into enclosed spots by type. 2) Check each type in a fixed order. 3) Follow any sound from the target at once, ending the sweep early. When: The target could hide in several enclosed spots in a small, bounded area.
- [procedure] 1) Split the area into zones so no corner is left out. 2) Assign each searcher one zone. 3) Check every zone fully. 4) Recall the team once all zones are checked. When: A hidden target could be in any small space and helpers need a shared plan.
- [procedure] 1) Circle overhead and lower height to spot the target through optics. 2) Advance ground searchers in a line across the area, like beaters sweeping cover. 3) Keep the line moving until the area is covered. When: a target is hidden across open terrain. Why: height reveals figures and a line sweeps ground systematically.
- [procedure] 1) Fix a sightline using visible landmarks. 2) Hide where the view is clear. 3) Count time to measure the target's actions. 4) Wait a set interval before moving. When: a hidden action must be seen unnoticed or repeated at the same spot. Why: landmarks and counts let a later repeat be matched precisely.
- [procedure] 1) Observe the building from a public route. 2) Make repeated passes rather than approaching the entrance. 3) Note window coverings, smoke and movement. When: You need to learn occupancy without entering. Why: Repeated passes from outside avoid drawing attention.
- [metric] Mapping every surface on a fixed grid of about six inches, and sounding each square, ensures no panel or cavity is skipped.
- [tradeoff] Hide a clue or key item in a searched room so the search feels full, or report nothing so the player learns it is elsewhere; choose by intent. When: designing a clue in a room the player has already searched. Why: a place that seems searched is rarely searched again; the earlier search pattern explains why it was missed. (x2)
- [tradeoff] Full zone sweeps find small traces but are slow; skipping a few spots with a visible tell, hiding an item there, invites return. Choose by whether completeness matters. When: designing or searching a bounded room for a small hidden target. Why: full sweeps catch traces in corners; an incomplete search signals something remains and gives a fair discovery route. (x2)
- [tradeoff] Repeated failed searches raise pressure while the answer stays unknown, or flatten interest and tire a watcher into exploitable lapses when they add nothing new; choose by the effect wanted.
- [tradeoff] A methodical search keeps the room tidy but takes longer; a rushed search finds the likeliest spots sooner and leaves disorder behind. When: under a time limit. Why: speed and tidiness cannot both be maximised within one window.
- [pitfall] A careful or single-method search can miss items hidden outside its assumptions, and a finished search then falsely suggests absence; cover all likely places and check personally. When: a lost item is being searched for. Why: limited coverage produces false negatives.
- [pitfall] Failed checks, empty observation sessions or silent witnesses do not prove absence; the target may lie at the next location or appear on another day. When: a search produces repeated negatives, a stakeout is empty, or no witness reports seeing the person. Why: routines vary by day, so one null result is weak evidence; repeated failures do not show the item is absent.
- [pitfall] Attention on the most suspicious object, like a lock or obvious spot, misses surrounding frame and fittings, and thorough searching there wastes effort while decisive traces lie nearby. Why: attention settles on the most suspicious object.
- [pitfall] Checking only hidden nooks, or expecting containers to be empty, misses items in ordinary visible containers; check every container fully. When: the search space contains both hidden and visible spots. Why: expectation lowers attention; the search pattern assumes concealment.
- [pitfall] A failed register search may mean the name is false rather than that the person never stayed, so pair it with an alias check. When: A name is absent from every register checked. Why: A false name would leave no record under the real name.
- [pitfall] Checking a long candidate list in person is tedious and draining, so ordering and recording the work keeps it manageable. When: the candidate set is large. Why: repetitive legwork wears down the investigator.
- [pitfall] Choosing a search direction arbitrarily wastes time and invites regret, so an ordered plan should be set before the search begins.
- [pitfall] Judging a crowd only by close-up details misses the pattern that distance reveals.
- [fact] A full search of a large building can take months, making exhaustive search impractical for an investigation. When: a crime scene is a large building with many rooms.

## physical traces
- [mechanism] A late physical lead can overturn an earlier elimination, changing the whole inquiry and forcing earlier notes to be revisited. Why: Eliminations rest on facts that a new discovery may contradict.
- [mechanism] Items on a corpse or in a container can hold clues, such as food residue or unusual materials, that a player reads only with the right tool. When: When designing evidence in a game world. Why: Tool-gated reading turns minor objects into meaningful discoveries.
- [mechanism] A found object can lead the player through a sequence of stops back to its owner, turning one item into a multi-step quest. When: In investigation games with physical clues.
- [mechanism] Pre-placed markers, such as chalk or a dropped item, let a player recover a lost trail without breaking the chase.
- [mechanism] Wear marks on a lock, chest or door persist as world state, letting players infer which tool was used and who has been there. When: a world object is used repeatedly.
- [mechanism] Forged or removed items can carry physical marks, such as ruling, trim size or a count one short, that the player can match against a stack or set elsewhere in the level. Why: Matching physical details across locations turns scattered objects into a discoverable link.
- [mechanism] Residue such as clay or sawdust can link a suspect to a specific place, such as a pit or workshop.
- [mechanism] A grip that uses one hand where two would normally be needed leaves the other hand free for a second action, which is a usable physical clue. When: A restrained or held target shows a grip that is physically weaker than expected. Why: The free hand is available for another task, such as taking an item.
- [behavior] Hurried acts leave marks on surfaces that a careful examiner can read, so the rush itself becomes evidence alongside the tool. When: an act is performed in haste. Why: abrupt movement during haste causes the marks.
- [rule] Give horror scares discoverable mundane traces, such as a sound, moving object, injury, damp clothing or fresh tool mark, specific enough that players can choose them over the supernatural reading. When: designing anomalies in horror-leaning levels or haunted locations. Why: Specific clues give players a checkable alternative to the supernatural reading. (x2)
