- [rule] Leave wax, footprints, smudges or scattered objects along a route so players can trace the culprit's path and confirm they are on track before the final location. When: Designing a path the player must reconstruct or a tracking route in a level. Why: Traces let players reconstruct movement without a witness and verify they are on the right path.
- [rule] Let a witness's described route leave footprints, disturbed dust or other traces the player can revisit, so an inconsistent route exposes the lie. When: an NPC describes events along a route. Why: Visible movement traces let players verify stated routes and test claims directly.
- [rule] A puzzle room can leave bullets, broken glass or scraped floors as traces that reveal events without the weapon present.
- [rule] Store physical traces such as drag marks, scorch patterns, and broken railings in a level so players can rebuild what happened.
- [rule] Before accepting an identification, ask whether the witness saw the person up close rather than only glimpsing them. When: when a witness claims to recognise someone. Why: a recognition based on a glance is weak evidence.
- [rule] In a feast or potion mechanic, log who consumed each item, so the player can rule items out by checking who was unaffected. When: A game has shared food or drink that can cause harm. Why: Consumer records turn a shared item into a checkable clue.
- [rule] Log item movement through the level so a player can check whether an item left a room. When: Building a stolen-item puzzle. Why: A checkable movement history makes the route deduction fair.
- [rule] Place residue, scorch marks or stored raw material that contradicts an NPC's stated purpose so players can infer the real activity. When: Designing environments that reveal NPC lies. Why: Physical contradiction rewards inference.
- [rule] Give the room a physical signature, such as marks or stains, that matches the intended method. When: Designing a scene that implies a specific action. Why: Matching traces let players confirm the method from the environment.
- [rule] Place residue such as soot, blood, or putty as negative evidence that rules out a route.
- [rule] Record the player's own traces, such as disturbed dust or items left out, to create tension about whether they left evidence behind.
- [rule] Make burnt or half-destroyed items sift-able and partly recoverable, so players reconstruct content from remnants. When: Designing destructible or burnt clue items. Why: Partial recovery turns damage into a usable mechanic.
- [rule] Build rooms whose residue is readable, such as empty frames, missing toiletries and dated calendars, so players can reason about what happened and when. Why: authored traces give players evidence to build a timeline from.
- [rule] Leave physical remnants such as cellars as clues that can confirm or contradict a written record.
- [rule] In a locked room, plant one trace that breaks the impossibility, such as footprints ending at a threshold or a mark near an object, so players can deduce the route. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle with a solvable route. Why: A single clear trace gives the player a concrete lead without solving the puzzle for them.
- [rule] Leave visible residue such as scorch marks or footprints in a dark room so a player who searches afterward can reconstruct what happened. When: Designing a space where events occur in darkness. Why: Physical residue lets players infer hidden actions after the fact.
- [rule] Make a wrongly accused person's innocence provable only by one specific physical trace, so the player must search for that exact trace. When: Designing an accusation the player must overturn. Why: A single decisive object gives the search a clear target.
- [heuristic] Compare any time-stamped physical trace against each account, since physical records are harder to bias than recollection, which cannot be checked when no object survives. When: Physical evidence is available alongside witness statements. Why: Physical records are harder to bias than human recollection. (x2)
- [heuristic] Judge whether a mark is fresh before linking it to a timeline, since fresh residue, such as wet finger marks, shows something was touched recently relative to earlier events. When: a mark is found on a surface after an event.
- [heuristic] Test a witness's identification or account against physical details such as a ring, missing item, footwear or gear, which are hard to fake consistently. When: When a witness identifies a suspect or describes clothing or gear, and physical details may not match. Why: Physical details are harder to fake than general impressions.
- [heuristic] Check dust, cobwebs and rust, which signal long disuse, before judging a door, route or broken part as recently used or damaged, since a fresh-looking break can mislead. When: judging whether a route or object was used recently. Why: Slow-forming traces accumulate only when nothing has disturbed them.
- [heuristic] Test a stated journey or timing against a physical condition it implies; a contradiction can overturn a claim the group accepted. When: a witness's account of distance, time or effort conflicts with a physical state. Why: a physical condition constrains what could have happened.
- [heuristic] An absence of fog or condensation in a room can suggest it was kept closed through the night.
- [heuristic] A clean test result may mean the tested item was swapped before testing, so consider substitution as an explanation.
- [heuristic] Claiming an incriminating object as your own before others attach it to a crime can explain it away. When: when an object links you to a crime.
- [heuristic] Test a witness's stated need or motive against visible possessions, clothing or behaviour. A mismatch is a lead about hidden purpose, not proof of wrongdoing. When: A witness gives a reason that can be checked against visible wealth or behaviour.
- [heuristic] A locked container with no outward damage that was later relocked was probably opened with a key rather than forced. When: Inspecting a container that shows no sign of tampering. Why: Forced entry leaves visible marks, so their absence points to legitimate access.
- [heuristic] A genuine trace carries random individual imperfections, so a copy that matches the original, flaws included, is suspicious. When: when a trace is presented as strong proof. Why: random flaws do not repeat exactly across independent traces.
- [heuristic] Identify fibres by type and dye colour under magnification; a mix of furnishing-type fabrics points toward an indoor, furnished place. Why: furnishings are normally found in habitations.
- [heuristic] Let an item carry a trace such as a fingerprint that links two personas, so an inventory check can connect them.
- [heuristic] Food quality can reveal a person's means or tastes, and a mismatch with the rest of their appearance is itself a clue.
- [heuristic] Deliberately scuffed trails can plant a clue that a subject tried to hide movement, leaving players to judge how far to trust them. When: Designing a clue that involves concealment. Why: Players must weigh a disturbed trail, which keeps the clue ambiguous.
- [heuristic] Use a plain, low-contrast surface under a fine layer, so later disturbance is easy to read.
- [heuristic] Leave physical traces such as cut glass, trampled objects or dropped items that players can read to show the entry route and the thief's knowledge. When: Designing scene evidence for a break-in. Why: Readable traces let players reconstruct the method.
- [heuristic] Wear on objects, such as edges rubbed from handling, shows how often something was used and supports claims that it was kept and read. When: a document or item must show past use. Why: Physical wear records handling that testimony cannot.
- [heuristic] Give rooms door seals that the game checks on return, and flag a breach as a clue. When: When designing a room that the player may leave and revisit. Why: Persistent state changes let the player detect unseen entry.
- [heuristic] Use a small clothing or equipment detail, such as mismatched footwear, as a physical clue, and hold back its explanation until later. When: Creating a clue that gives an identity or timing contradiction. Why: A detail noticed early but explained late rewards careful observation without giving the answer away.
- [procedure] 1) Compare the timing of each discovery against prior communications. 2) Ask whether objects could have been placed after those communications. 3) Test the evidence before accepting the clue.
- [procedure] 1) Look at the body for pallor, glassy eyes, and stillness. 2) Touch a hand and note coldness or stiffness. 3) Listen at the mouth for breath. 4) Feel for a pulse at the heart. When: When a person appears unconscious or dead. Why: Each check is more decisive than appearance alone.
- [procedure] 1) Note each fold, seal and crease on the object. 2) Establish the order in which these marks were made. 3) Check whether that order fits the owner holding and dropping the object, or someone inserting it later. When: An object was found beneath a body or in a disturbed place. Why: A consistent sequence supports the owner's use, while a conflicting one suggests later placement.
- [procedure] 1) Present the physical item. 2) Ask whether it could fit the witness's account. 3) Record any agreement as a checkable fit, not as confirmation of the claim. When: when a witness's memory concerns an object. Why: a physical match turns vague memory into testable evidence.
- [procedure] Lie flat on a waterproof sheet and use a magnifier to inspect the ground closely. When: Traces are small or faint. Why: Low, steady viewing reveals faint detail.
- [procedure] 1) Compare a witness's account with physical signs such as marks or injuries. 2) Where they contradict, identify the exact points of conflict. 3) Press on those points directly and factually. When: A witness's account is smooth but a physical sign suggests otherwise. Why: Targeting the conflict draws out what the witness has left unsaid.
- [procedure] 1) Read any name or mark on the item to establish ownership. 2) Count the remaining rounds or uses to check whether it was fired.
- [procedure] 1) Examine the door frame where a bolt is claimed to be shot to confirm it. 2) Unbolt and reopen the connecting door repeatedly, examining the bolt each time. 3) Collect any tiny residue from the bolt and seal it. 4) Test adjoining doors, relocking each after inspection. When: when a closed-room claim is made.
- [procedure] 1) Compare the object with its original record, such as purchase or inventory details. 2) Look for mismatches in shade or material. 3) Check whether adhesive or residue dates the replacement. 4) Ask why the object was changed. When: An object has been moved, replaced or cleaned and the reason is unclear.
- [procedure] 1) Take a finding from one source. 2) Look for the same trace at a second, independent location. 3) If they agree, the finding gains support. 4) If they disagree, reconsider the finding. When: when a finding needs verification. Why: a mismatch shows the finding may be misread.
- [pitfall] Absence of powder marks does not prove no shot was fired; poorly fitted cartridges can spurt back and many shots leave no sign, so a clean weapon proves nothing either.
- [pitfall] Reading meaning into an object before checking how it came to be there leads to false conclusions. Verify its origin first.
- [pitfall] Restricting the search to an area already inspected can miss the evidence that matters most.
- [pitfall] A closed door does not mean an empty or safe room, since fumes can linger and become deadly in a small enclosed space.
- [pitfall] A clean inspection of a letter does not prove that no one read it.
- [pitfall] Assuming a near-fatal wound was deliberately aimed, without checking whether the victim's movement changed where it landed, can mislead an investigation. Why: a small turn can shift a shot's path.
- [pitfall] Reading a clean fogged surface as proof of no handling is an error, since wiping and no handling look identical. When: interpreting a breath-test result. Why: Both leave no ridge pattern, so the result cannot separate them.
- [pitfall] Generic traces such as common dust may be too general to identify a particular owner. When: when residue is common across many places.
- [pitfall] Squeezing through a narrow opening risks breakage and injury, and can nearly fail the entry altogether. When: entering through a small or upper window. Why: tight gaps and fragile glass make the route dangerous.
- [fact] Chloroform signs found at post-mortem are mostly negative, so the body alone often under-reports the cause and the scene carries much of the proof. When: When a death may have been caused by an agent that leaves little trace. Why: Volatile agents evaporate and leave few physical marks on the body.
- [fact] Soot or burn marks on skin around a wound show that the muzzle was close, so their absence suggests the shot came from a distance. When: Judging whether a gunshot wound was self-inflicted. Why: Close-range discharge leaves residue on nearby skin.
- [fact] Hairs lifted with tweezers can show a person was present in a place; coarse fibres can suggest a material such as a sheet used for an improvised rope.

## concealed items and hiding
- [definition] A concealed method can use an ordinary object delivered through a normal social action, so the mechanism stays hidden until the object is found.
- [mechanism] Concealed, locked objects whose key is carried by a suspect often hold the decisive evidence about the suspect's secret. When: A suspect hides an object or mail that is central to the secret. Why: Locks and custody control access, so the contents reveal what the suspect is hiding.
- [mechanism] An object hidden in an ordinary storage place can survive a search, so a search that misses it must be explained rather than assumed complete. When: when an object has been concealed in household storage. Why: searchers may skip places they do not expect to hide an object.
- [mechanism] A room search that requires opening containers and moving objects defines hiding spots by what the player can disturb.
- [mechanism] A concealed release such as a hidden panel or keyhole, set into ordinary architecture like wainscot or angled walls, stays hidden until found by testing, not sight. When: when a room's exit must be hidden but reachable by someone who knows the mechanism. Why: Hiding the trigger in ordinary architecture makes discovery depend on interaction.
- [behavior] A physical sign shown at a critical moment, such as a change in skin colour, may be misread at the time and only understood later. When: During a tense scene. Why: Urgency leaves no time to interpret the sign.
- [behavior] A person hiding evidence tends to choose a place that searchers cannot reach without being told where to look. When: when concealing a physical trace from a search. Why: Keeps the trace outside the house, where only an informed person can find it.
- [behavior] Observers believe only what they see, so an object moved out of their view before a reveal stays hidden from them. When: a character hides an item in plain sight of others. Why: Watchers judge by what is visible to them.
- [behavior] A hider who believes a threat to a hidden item is real and imminent will retrieve it early, exposing themselves before the deadline. When: when the hider thinks discovery is about to happen. Why: the deadline removes the option of waiting.
- [behavior] Small signs, such as a handle that turns and returns without opening, suggest someone inside is listening.
- [rule] Place a key item or clue inside a mundane or unexpected container, such as a drawer in an already cleared room, that the player must deliberately open and inspect. When: Designing a clue or key item. Why: Thorough searching and deliberate inspection reward attention.
- [rule] Do not declare a place empty until each floor and room has been checked. When: When deciding whether a location is unoccupied. Why: A quick look at the main area misses rooms where occupants or clues remain.
- [rule] Place a single breaker or gas meter in a room, since switching it off can hide player movement and NPC sightlines.
- [rule] Build a pleasant or dreamlike area that hides a hazard, and give a clear warning cue before the danger appears.
- [rule] Place a concealed item in a room that an NPC's patrol schedule avoids, so players must learn the routine to find it. When: Designing a hidden collectible or clue. Why: Makes the schedule itself the way to discover the item.
- [rule] Gate concealed spaces behind specific tools, such as probes for soft fillings, a light for dark gaps, or a lift for fixed fittings.
- [rule] Design NPC scenes that draw attention, and give the player a cue that reveals when the real goal is being hidden. When: an NPC stages a scene to cover another action. Why: the player can then notice the misdirection.
- [rule] Set a covert meeting point out of sight of the main building, using back routes, small gates and tree cover that block line of sight. When: Designing a secret contact location. Why: Layout determines which watchers can see the meeting.
- [rule] Place small trace objects where a larger object would be overlooked, so careful players can find them. When: Designing clue placement in a level. Why: Small clues reward close inspection without being obvious.
- [rule] Hide the stolen item inside an object the player has already searched, so the search itself is what conceals it.
- [rule] Plant a visible concealment, such as a wig or mask, as an early passing remark, so the player does not treat it as the solution but later connects it to the reveal. When: Using appearance as a clue to an enemy's or NPC's identity. Why: A casually stated clue is noticed but not overvalued, so its later payoff feels fair.
- [heuristic] A concealed space's size, shape and construction suggest what it held: a small flat pocket points to a flat, light object such as paper, and a cramped gap to small or forbidden objects. When: Interpreting a concealed space. (x2)
- [heuristic] Objects players dismiss as set dressing, such as chairs or cabinets, can conceal a trigger; placing it early among decor lets a later targeted check reveal it fairly. When: Designing a room with a potentially dangerous or functional item. Why: Early, unobtrusive placement makes later recognition fair and satisfying. (x2)
- [heuristic] Concealed items are often hidden in bulky, decorative or dismal everyday objects and places that others dismiss, so careful inspection of mundane objects finds what a broader search misses. When: When a room or set of belongings with many ordinary objects is searched. Why: Everyone sees such objects and few examine them, and people overlook what looks unimportant.
- [heuristic] Hide clues among ordinary contents of storage like costume chests or wardrobes; they draw little suspicion and are searched least, so the clue surfaces only when moved or collided with. When: Placing a hidden item among household or everyday objects. Why: Routine objects draw little suspicion and get the least inspection.
- [heuristic] Check whether a concealed surface has been disturbed by comparing it against the surrounding material, since matched repairs still differ from the original. When: Investigating a suspected hidden space. Why: Disturbance leaves differences that a careful inspection can detect.
- [heuristic] Disturbed soil or a glint within it can signal that someone buried an item to keep it out of sight.
- [heuristic] A culprit who cannot destroy evidence may place it in an ordinary, visible spot and rely on others overlooking it, if the culprit controls who passes the spot. When: Concealing an item that cannot be destroyed. Why: Visible objects tend to go unnoticed when nobody expects them to be connected to a crime.
- [heuristic] A concealed object is usually kept close to its owner and under the owner's control, not entrusted to a third party. When: The object is small and the owner is its only custodian; less applicable if a third party holds it. Why: Owners prefer to keep valuables within reach and personal control.
- [heuristic] Objects thrown into water or otherwise removed may have been the former contents of a container, disposed of to hide evidence. When: Recovered objects do not fit the scene's purpose. Why: Removing evidence is a common way to conceal a crime.
- [heuristic] Evidence concealed inside ordinary objects and places is found by searching the spots where it could plausibly have lodged.
- [heuristic] A careful hider avoids eye level and places a concealed object above or below it, since a loosened item at eye level is noticed in a close search. When: Judging where a hider would conceal an object. Why: Eye level is the zone most often inspected closely.
- [heuristic] Look for changes that contradict what was known earlier, such as a room now open that was known closed, and ask what the change removes. When: A scene differs from its earlier recorded state. Why: A culprit often alters the scene to erase traces of the method.
- [heuristic] An object turned to face the wall and covered in dust suggests someone wanted it out of view.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can use a concealed item that the player must learn to operate, with a limit on what the item can move. When: Designing a puzzle built on a hidden tool. Why: Learning the tool and its limits makes the puzzle satisfying to solve.
- [heuristic] Place a hidden location under the player's own resting spot to create dramatic irony that rewards careful inspection. When: Designing a secret near a safe area the player frequently uses. Why: The player's proximity to the secret makes discovery feel earned and tense.
- [heuristic] A mundane facility such as a salvage or construction site can hide a different function, such as military defence, so judge its purpose by what it could conceal. When: when a commercial site appears to have an innocent purpose. Why: a cover activity can conceal a real purpose.
- [heuristic] Hide a device among objects that suggest disuse, and disguise its container with a familiar label, so close inspection tends to pass over it. Why: Signs of disuse and familiar labels discourage a closer look.
- [heuristic] An unused room with its door open is a natural hiding and ambush site when NPCs walk past. When: Designing opportunistic hiding places in a level. Why: An open empty room gives a place to step aside and wait.
- [heuristic] A pursuer can hide under a stopped vehicle to see where the target steps out, rather than following into the open.
- [heuristic] Make a late reward container empty, and plant a small trace, such as a fresh scent or a missing key, that suggests it was emptied on purpose. When: Placing a chest or stash the player expects to hold loot. Why: The trace lets the emptiness read as deliberate concealment rather than a broken reward.
- [heuristic] Hide a substitution behind a visible disguise that the player sees but is not told to examine, then make searching candidate locations the objective.
- [procedure] 1) Obtain a search warrant. 2) Open the claimed trunk with a locksmith if needed. 3) Open it in the presence of an official. When: a suspect says belongings were left in a container. Why: An official present makes the search verifiable.
- [procedure] 1) Remove or weaken a corroborating trace. 2) Make a duplicate of the item before any inventory check. 3) Keep the original locked away out of view for later use.
- [procedure] 1) Test several candidate spots. 2) Choose one that is easy to reach but hard to see from the usual path. 3) Place the object out of view. 4) Grip it with a tool kept in the same spot. 5) Before use, check by feel that the cache is intact. 6) After use, return the tool to restore the cache. When: when an object must be hidden and later retrieved without direct hand contact. Why: retrieval without touching the object avoids leaving new traces on it.
- [procedure] 1) Empty each pocket in turn, not just the first one felt. 2) Examine and record each item. 3) Return each item after checking it. When: when searching a body or container.
- [procedure] 1) Search large furniture first. 2) Open locked containers. 3) Inspect compartments inside each container, such as pigeonholes and small cases, before moving to the next object. When: a scene holds furniture and containers that may conceal evidence. Why: hidden items often sit inside other objects, so a surface check misses them.
- [procedure] 1) Replace the real document with a blank or decoy before a search. 2) Hide the real pages inside an ordinary object. 3) Move them to a place searchers are unlikely to take apart, when watchers are least alert. When: when a captive must keep a document safe under observation.
- [procedure] 1) Check the item's last known location. 2) Check its usual container. 3) Check the people and bodies involved. 4) Record which places were empty. When: searching for a missing item. Why: ordered checks reveal each empty place in sequence.
- [procedure] 1) Search the person carrying a sealed object for keys 2) Try each key found against the lock 3) Force the lock only if no key fits. When: a sealed object is held by someone.
- [procedure] 1) Stage a disturbance that draws the target's attention away. 2) Exchange the object during that moment. 3) Leave a convincing substitute in its place. Why: Each step covers the next so the exchange goes unseen.
- [procedure] 1) Check the searched character's clothing and footwear. 2) Check waistband and pockets. 3) Check the enclosing space, including bedding. 4) Test fixed fixtures one by one by force, noting any that loosen. When: a searcher must leave no part of a confined space uncovered. Why: A fixed order makes the search systematic so no hiding place is skipped.
- [procedure] 1) Stay still until the approach is clear. 2) Slip into a side room out of sight. 3) Secure the entry so it cannot be opened. When: a visitor approaches a room the player occupies.
- [tradeoff] Key items in the most obvious nearby spot reward players who check close by; when hiding from pursuers, avoid routes and places searchers check first. Why: Players search nearby spots quickly, but hiding where pursuers expect to search gives them an easy find.
- [pitfall] Hiding evidence makes the person who hides it look guilty and harms the inquiry, such as when a witness hides an object to protect one person.
- [pitfall] Each concealment step, and concealing an object's route, creates traces such as scraped surfaces, fresh copies, dust, damage or handling habits that a careful observer can notice. When: When concealing an object or hiding from pursuers.
- [pitfall] Attempts to hide traces by rubbing, rolling or re-roughening paper can leave signs of their own. When: Someone tries to conceal an alteration on a document. Why: Physical concealment damages the surface in detectable ways.
- [pitfall] Hiding that evidence was damaged is itself a risk to the case; report the disturbance openly so others can judge what remains.
- [pitfall] Hiding the empty container in the same spot as the loaded one is a natural move that can also reveal the swap. When: When concealing a swapped container. Why: Keeping the substitute beside the original makes the swap easy to detect.
- [pitfall] Assuming objects remain where you last left them can fail when someone else has entered the room.
- [pitfall] A hidden eavesdropper can be noticed by a second, silent character present, so concealment must account for every observer. When: a stealthy listener is in a shared space. Why: other present characters can perceive the watcher.
- [pitfall] Searchers who follow their own impulses leave gaps in coverage; without a shared division of the area, some corners are never checked.
- [fact] A personal desk can hold records of wealth its owner hid from others, so documents kept in furniture can reveal concealed income.
- [fact] A concealed object may show as a bulge or stiffness in clothing; a single such sign is only a lead.
- [fact] Open flat ground gives little concealment, so a visible tail tends to fail; hedges and fields provide cover.
- [fact] In busy, open spaces an intruder can pass unnoticed, so routine inattention by staff excludes few people as suspects. When: A location is crowded and open to many people. Why: Activity and openness hide individual movements among ordinary traffic.
- [fact] Open moor with no trees or shelter offers almost no cover, so a hidden person there depends on hollows and crouching rather than structures. When: a level is open and flat. Why: cover needs height or obstacles to block sight.
- [fact] Clothing such as gloves or sleeves can disguise a body part, and a refusal to remove it can be concealment rather than an innocent excuse. When: A character's apparel or refusal to remove it conflicts with the rest of the evidence. Why: Re-reading earlier details in light of a reconstruction can reveal that the item hid the true means of action.

## level and route layout
- [mechanism] Fog, dim light, weather and endless or unfinished streets build unease during an approach; a lit entrance and a briefly blocked key door add hesitation before the reveal. When: designing the journey to a destination; staging an approach to a boss or reveal. Why: Atmosphere, disorientation and pacing make the approach feel threatening and shape anticipation.
- [mechanism] A region graph can tag each edge with a required capability, such as a vehicle, climbing or a key; the player removes edges by finding the matching gate or witness. When: Generating a level whose routes are gated by abilities. Why: Capability tags turn traversal options into a checkable elimination space.
- [mechanism] A lookout whose line of sight is blocked gives a built-in, in-world reason for a witness account to be incomplete.
- [mechanism] An angular error at the start of a bearing grows with distance along the line, so a small starting miss can put the endpoint far off. When: measuring a line of direction. Why: the error scales with the length of the line.
- [mechanism] Colour-coded rooms and coloured light steer the player's eye through a layout and can discourage entry into particular areas. When: A layout needs to guide or deter movement without signage. Why: Colour draws attention and signals which spaces are open or dangerous.
- [mechanism] When every object has a fixed place, a moved or missing item stands out at once, so departures from the layout reveal tampering without a full search. When: a space's expected arrangement is known. Why: a planned baseline makes deviations obvious.
- [rule] Map every route and mark which are visible to NPCs; guard routines should contain predictable gaps, with patrol schedules defining time windows the player can exploit. When: designing level routes, guard patrols and locked areas guarded by NPCs. Why: Visibility and timing define when a route is safe; predictable routines create openings that reward observation.
- [rule] Make a visible sign, such as a forced or broken door or a cleared shelf or tidied area, indicate a second or concealed exit the player must find.
- [rule] Generated dungeons can place waypoint markers at each junction to support tails and escorts.
- [rule] Provide a directory or map of candidate locations and let a note's constraints, such as floor count, distance or landmark, eliminate all but one. Why: Explicit constraints make elimination a solvable puzzle.
- [rule] Build a dead-end chamber with the same mechanism to confirm a solution, rather than making the first hidden door the answer. When: Designing hidden door puzzles. Why: A repeat confirms the route.
- [rule] Apply a consistent supply-path rule to generated items so that trace clues remain coherent across the game. When: Generating a set of items that players may trace. Why: Contradictory supply paths make trace clues unreliable.
- [rule] Define region boundaries with landmarks such as bridges or roads, and make a dispute depend on which landmark the player finds.
- [rule] Keep the full solution of a generated mystery as a fixed data record while the player-facing layout varies between runs. When: Generating mysteries procedurally. Why: A stable solution keeps clues consistent while the presentation changes.
- [rule] Place a marker item the game can check on return, so players can confirm their own route through a dark area.
- [rule] Let an NPC's directions reference physical landmarks, such as an arch, steps or a name plate, that the player can verify in the layout. When: A game NPC gives navigation help. Why: Verifiable landmarks let players test claims.
- [rule] Mark access lines such as paths and their bordering features on the map, connecting the map layout with the physical traces found on site. When: when preparing a site survey.
- [rule] Give players a way to map a level and record doors and sightlines so that navigation in darkness remains fair. When: in levels where light is limited.
- [rule] Use a visible lure, such as a rumored vault, while keeping the true route in plain sight.
- [rule] Make the necessary route through the level geometry visible, so a careful player can reconstruct how an event such as a disappearance happened. When: when an event's solution depends on movement through space. Why: Players can only solve what the layout lets them trace.
- [rule] Make back lanes and service routes look like background while connecting rooms the player is not expected to reach. When: designing hidden connections. Why: plain routes can be found by careful players.
- [rule] Give each possible exit of a sealed puzzle room a visible, testable state, such as latch, window or flue, so players can rule options out. When: designing a sealed room puzzle. Why: players can only eliminate options they can check.
- [rule] Build a finale room where suspects converge, and let each evidence piece the player assembles unlock the next step of the reveal.
- [rule] In stealth levels, offer a visible route that guards watch while a hidden route serves as the real exit.
- [rule] Make hidden routes visible only from certain angles, so players can test their reasoning about exits.
- [rule] Build branching paths where one option is blocked, so the player's choices narrow naturally. When: When designing routes where players choose a direction. Why: Blocked branches reduce options and point the player toward the remaining route.
- [rule] Build entry routes as separate access points with different risk levels, so the player must weigh them. When: Designing ways into a restricted area. Why: Players then make a meaningful risk-versus-timing choice.
- [rule] Use exits and sightlines to block a visible culprit's route, so players who test the obvious theory against the layout find the real path. When: when designing a decoy suspect. Why: Testing the decoy reveals the true route.
- [rule] Treat a witness's claimed route or landmark that does not exist on the map as evidence that the account is false.
- [heuristic] Let shared pipes carry sound so NPCs in adjacent spaces can overhear the player. When: Designing connected rooms or cells. Why: Overheard speech creates tension and new information paths.
- [heuristic] Not every hole in a wall should be a passage; some openings can lead to dead-end shafts, so players learn that openings need testing. When: generating wall breaches or openings in rooms. Why: a mix of real and dead-end openings teaches players to verify rather than assume.
- [heuristic] Scatter personal items along approach paths so players can prove a character passed by, even when the room looks untouched.
- [heuristic] A chance encounter can open a new area, but the game should also give the player a way to reach it through search.
- [heuristic] A floor plan showing where each room sits and who sleeps where indicates which lit opening deserves attention. When: choosing which opening to observe.
- [heuristic] Let a location change over in-game days through repairs, new structures and new occupants, so early visits preserve evidence that later visits lose. When: designing an investigation location. Why: Time pressure rewards prompt visits.
- [heuristic] Design an NPC's unusual arrival so a player watching the wrong angle misses it, while one watching the right angle catches it. When: Placing a sightline-dependent event in a level. Why: Rewarding good observation makes the event feel earned.
- [heuristic] Use a fixed landmark such as a beacon or lit tower as a navigation anchor while the rest of the map grows darker and more dangerous.
- [heuristic] A route that looks impossible for one person may work with a helper, so consider whether an accomplice could make it possible. When: Only one person seems able to reach the route. Why: A second actor can complete what one cannot.
- [heuristic] An approach builds tension only when it leads to a clear end point; a long approach without landmarks or pressure drags. When: when pacing a long journey. Why: suspense needs a destination and interim pressure.
- [heuristic] Compare two maps made for different purposes; a feature drawn on one but absent or different on the other can expose something hidden. When: when one map may omit features another shows. Why: the gap between maps can reveal hidden works.
- [heuristic] Give a target location a second approach that avoids the main alerting point, such as a side entrance that bypasses a doorbell. When: Entry at the usual point would give the party away or is guarded. Why: A silent alternate route keeps the approach unnoticed.
- [heuristic] Expect unmapped branches in a pursued passage; a plan that assumes one route fails when a second route is used. When: pursuit enters terrain that has not been mapped. Why: an unknown branch can carry a second party into the encounter.
- [procedure] 1) Start from one verifiable fixed point. 2) Place each boundary from that point using bearings and distances. 3) Cross-check against deeds naming neighbours. 4) Confirm with surviving features. 5) Combine independent plans so two fixed boundaries locate a third. 6) Mark each site's confidence. When: the exact position of a site matters to the sequence of events. Why: A single anchor and measured offsets fix positions, while cross-checks and confidence marks limit errors.
- [procedure] 1) Anchor the level to one landmark. 2) Place rooms by bearing and distance from it. 3) Check the layout against scattered reference notes. 4) Adjust any room that conflicts with a note. When: building a level from scattered reference notes. Why: A single anchor keeps the layout consistent, and reference notes catch placement errors.
- [procedure] 1) Make every visible approach fail the visibility test. 2) Leave one concealed route. 3) Let the player deduce that route by elimination. When: When designing an ambush or apparition puzzle. Why: Elimination of visible options guides the player to the hidden route without explicit hints.
- [procedure] 1) Note the building's road frontage. 2) Note its orientation. 3) Match these against old map records. 4) Confirm against present land plots. When: Locating a building from historical records.
- [tradeoff] Waiting at an earlier point or changing elsewhere may reach the same connection with less risk, at a different time cost. When: when a planned connection is at risk. Why: each alternative trades time against safety.
- [pitfall] If an area's boundaries are never made clear, players cannot test claims about who passed through them.
- [pitfall] Assuming a clear sightline when foliage, a curtain or other cover blocks the view leads to wrong judgements about who was seen.
- [pitfall] Proposing a route, such as a chimney or roof, before the window and other routes are checked leads to unsupported answers; check routes in order. When: Reasoning through a sealed room. Why: Guessing skips the evidence.
- [pitfall] Accepting a single witness's assurance that no exit exists, without checking the room plan, can hide the route that was actually used.
- [pitfall] A checkpoint's records cover only its main route, so a visitor who enters another way passes unrecorded. When: a guarded area has more than one entry. Why: the log only captures traffic through the watched gate.
- [pitfall] Stacked floors and open shafts make it hard to track who was where, so investigators easily lose the sequence of events. When: Scenes with vertical layouts such as lift wells or multi-storey buildings. Why: Vertical separation hides positions.
- [pitfall] A layout that seems too familiar may be skipped by players, so the designer should make the key constraints explicit.
- [pitfall] A layout is useful only when its features are recorded accurately; approximate plans can mislead the investigation. When: a plan is drawn from memory or rough notes. Why: Errors in the plan propagate into every test.
- [pitfall] An unguarded exit can look like the obvious escape route while its lock state is never checked, so verify it physically.

## lighting and shadow
- [mechanism] Light one person's face while the observer sits in shadow: the lit person's reactions stay readable, and the observer watches every movement while staying partly hidden and unnoticed. When: Arranging a room for questioning, confrontation, or a scene where some characters must watch unseen. Why: Light makes the lit person's reactions visible, while shadow conceals the observer; darkness hides observers while light draws attention to one area.
- [mechanism] Light sources decide what the player and NPCs clearly see and what sits in shadow; a lit corridor exposes a figure while cluttered dim space lets it hide. When: Designing lighting for stealth or investigation levels, or deciding where characters can be seen. Why: Light sets what can be perceived and shows who could observe whom in a room.
- [mechanism] A caller on unlit steps cannot be seen through glass, so the occupant must recognize them by voice. When: Judging whether an occupant could identify a visitor visually. Why: Poor lighting and sight lines limit what can be seen.
- [mechanism] A figure that draws back into shadow as others approach stays unidentified, and it is revealed only when it steps into light.
- [mechanism] Placing an act where a visible light source makes it possible lets players infer the actor's needs from the location. When: designing where an event happens in a level.
- [mechanism] A single controllable light source for a room lets one person change what everyone in it can see.
- [mechanism] Low raking light across a surface makes shallow depressions visible that flat lighting hides. When: when inspecting a floor, rug or carpet for impressions. Why: shallow relief casts shadows at a low angle.
- [behavior] Dim light and distance can reduce a viewer's perception to a silhouette, so identification may rest on a single cue such as voice. When: Viewing a person in low light or at a distance. Why: Features cannot be made out, leaving only partial cues.
- [behavior] A mechanism shown in full inside a playful or fairy-tale frame is easy to read past, even though it is fully disclosed. When: a key detail is presented within a whimsical story or game fiction. Why: the light frame lowers attention to the detail.
- [behavior] A shadow cast across a lit path is an early visual trace of an intruder who is still out of view. When: a lit area is watched at night. Why: Light casts a visible shape before its source is seen.
- [behavior] In a dark space, one small lit detail can reveal a hidden person's presence through its movement and a distinguishing feature. When: an unlit area with a moving light source. Why: motion draws attention and a lit detail stands out against darkness.
- [behavior] Lights seen in rooms known to be empty are read as proof that someone is moving about and that something is wrong. When: when a place expected to be unoccupied shows activity. Why: the contradiction with what is known signals danger.
- [rule] Light sources and angles determine what players see: place an NPC so lighting favours the player's view of their face; a lantern behind the player lights a suspect's face. (x2)
- [rule] Changing light, such as a moved torch, can expose details or figures that earlier lighting hid, so a scene can be re-read once conditions change. When: When a reveal depends on what the player can see, or when designing visibility-dependent clues. Why: Lighting changes let players re-observe a scene and find what was hidden. (x2)
- [rule] Light tense rooms with one shaded lamp so the player sees only what their position reveals; partial visibility delays understanding, raises tension and makes the audience fill gaps. When: Designing tense or secretive rooms, or scenes that are dark or lit by a single source. Why: Limited light adds tension and controls information.
- [rule] Tie a hidden item's or body's visibility to lighting so it stays unnoticed in dark or cramped spaces and is found only when lighting changes. When: Designing dropped, hidden items or bodies in dim, dark or crowded areas. Why: Visibility depends on light and sightlines; scheduled lighting changes can reveal concealed content.
- [rule] Shadows from moving objects, such as branches or swinging lanterns, can resemble a figure; tag such ghostly sightings as lighting effects so players learn to discount them.
- [rule] Map limited perception onto limited vision or fog: give sound, touch, duration and landmark cues instead of full view, so players narrow location from partial information. When: Designing fog-of-war, blinded, dim or dark sections. Why: Partial cues let players deduce location and keep the search active.
- [rule] In a dark level, limit light sources such as torches or lamp fuel, so that light becomes a scarce resource the player must manage. When: when designing a dark area. Why: Scarce light forces deliberate choices about where to look.
- [rule] Give players a level map showing sightlines, access lines and nearby buildings, so they can plan routes and identify who could have seen what. When: when designing a level for stealth or investigation.
- [rule] Place mirrors, glass or water so players can see hidden NPCs or objects indirectly, and make clear which surface reflects what. When: Designing a space where key information should be visible but not directly accessible. Why: Players can only use reflections if they understand the surface's geometry.
- [rule] Offer a hidden vantage point with a limited view, so the player needs a second angle to read the full scene.
- [rule] Signal hidden occupants with indirect traces such as gloved marks and rubber-soled prints, so players infer presence without seeing NPCs.
- [rule] Let sunlight or lamp position act as a clue, so a changed shadow reveals that a door or hatch was opened.
- [rule] Store line-of-sight data for a level so designers can verify which approaches a player could have been seen on. When: When building stealth or ambush encounters. Why: Verified sightlines prevent unfair or unintended exposure.
- [rule] Make a clue that shows only under a certain light, such as a lantern or torch, depend on a light source placed in the scene. When: a hidden trace is meant to be seen by an observer. Why: without a plausible light source the clue cannot be seen.
- [rule] Use night or low-visibility scenes so the player must read traces and sounds without full sight; avoid them when evidence should be easy to inspect. When: Designing investigation scenes. Why: Limited sight shifts the challenge to reading indirect clues.
- [rule] Do not assume an open or lit room means an accomplice was present; several explanations are usually possible. When: Interpreting a room left open or lit. Why: Ordinary reasons can explain the same state.
- [heuristic] Use light and weather to set mood; they shape what players notice and how a room feels, so soft light can make a scene dreamlike or ominous and support suspense. Why: Lighting controls what is visible and how the space reads.
- [heuristic] Scan to both sides of a search path, since unexpected evidence may lie off the predicted route.
- [heuristic] Items lit but barely used suggest abandonment, while items burned down suggest a longer stay.
- [heuristic] Seed background facts lightly early, such as a financial position or a rumour, so that a motive can be picked up later. When: establishing characters before the plot turns. Why: early seeds become usable motives without needing exposition later.
- [heuristic] A performance can cover a simple fact, so look through the performance to reach the core. When: when a character's behavior seems staged.
- [heuristic] A killing at an hour when the scene has no light implies darkness or a light source the actor carried; if neither is plausible, the scene is probably wrong. When: Time of death and lighting conditions are known. Why: A violent act needs enough light to be done as described.
- [heuristic] Keep a hidden inscription invisible until a light source touches it, and let the reveal lapse quickly so players must time their action.
- [heuristic] Trigger a resemblance puzzle only when the player passes a specific light source or window, with an NPC crossing the view on a timer. When: designing a recognition puzzle. Why: position and timing control when the clue appears.
- [heuristic] A lit window usually signals that someone is inside, so a visible light can serve as a presence cue.
- [heuristic] Build a junction so a short time window shows two NPCs without guaranteeing what the player will see. Why: brief windows create suspense while keeping outcomes uncertain.
- [heuristic] Do not assume a sightline is complete; partial views and angles change what can be seen.
- [heuristic] Light levels that change as the player moves toward a key site can increase tension in a confrontation. When: Staging a confrontation in a level. Why: Shifting light changes visibility and the mood of the encounter.
- [heuristic] Use dim lighting at a doorway or archway so an NPC's identity is not clear until it is close. When: When designing a reveal of an approaching NPC. Why: Poor light delays recognition and heightens the moment of reveal.
- [heuristic] Gate detailed inspection behind a lighting or time-of-day condition to reward patience. When: Designing environmental clues.
- [heuristic] A room can have a power or lighting system the player can short out, changing enemies' ability to see, and a puzzle can rely on a fault visible in a separate box. When: designing a room with controllable lighting. Why: tying visibility to a controllable system creates tactical options.
- [procedure] 1) Observe whether a glowing object lights the nearby floor and walls. 2) If it does not, treat it as a non-emitting appearance rather than a true light source. When: Deciding whether a glowing object is a physical light source. Why: A real light source spreads light onto surrounding surfaces.
- [procedure] 1) Place the clue in a niche the light does not reach. 2) Provide a movable light source. 3) Let the moved light expose the clue to the player. When: hiding a clue in plain view. Why: a clue in shadow is missed on a first pass but found once the light changes.
- [procedure] 1) Pace along a wall and count steps as a rough size. 2) Mark the start with a fragment of cloth. 3) Probe floor and walls by touch, arms extended, before moving. 4) Drop an object and listen for it landing. When: reconstructing a layout in darkness.
- [procedure] 1) Assign a watch at a likely meeting point. 2) Add an observer who can make the identification. 3) Record what is seen separately from what is inferred. 4) Resolve the figure before acting. When: Following a suspect whose identity is uncertain. Why: Structured observation separates confirmed identity from assumption.
- [pitfall] Dim light or moonlight can hide a key detail, so give the audience enough light to notice the clue needed later. When: when lighting a scene that holds a later clue. Why: A clue the audience cannot see cannot be used when the reveal comes.
- [pitfall] Colors that look identical under artificial light can differ in daylight, so test shades under the lighting the original witnesses had.
- [pitfall] A changed lighting condition can reveal a difference without explaining its cause, so the cause must still be identified separately. When: When varying conditions makes a detail visible or invisible. Why: Revealing a difference is not the same as identifying what produced it.
- [pitfall] Shadowed areas and places a follower cannot enter let a tailed target slip away, so these gaps need covering or accounting for. When: Pursuit crosses dark or restricted terrain. Why: Line of sight breaks down where light is low or access is blocked.
- [pitfall] Searching only in lantern light misses small items; a magnifier and a slow careful sweep find what a quick pass overlooks.
- [fact] Light exposure gradually changes fabric colour, so a shade difference between matched items can reflect where each one stood. When: comparing fabric or padding of matched objects. Why: Sunlight and lamplight fade materials unevenly over time.

## timeline and logged events
- [model] Store each NPC's movements as timestamped events against a game clock, so players can assemble timelines from several accounts, find contradictions and check who could have heard an event.
- [mechanism] A game timeline built from timestamped events and NPC schedules lets players reconstruct a crime window themselves.
- [mechanism] Matching torn edges of paired stubs shows whether the holders were issued or presented them together; mismatched edges indicate separate entry, even if arrivals were close in time. When: Paired physical tokens such as ticket stubs are recovered and the question is whether two people entered together. Why: Tickets torn in one act share matching edges, while tickets torn at different times do not.
- [mechanism] A half-finished action, such as a drink or meal left unfinished, shows the action was interrupted and gives a cue for dating events.
- [mechanism] A sudden stop in a visible signal, such as a lantern flash or whistle, can tell the player that an NPC has spotted them or is about to act. When: in a level where NPCs signal each other. Why: A break in a readable pattern carries information the player can reason about.
- [mechanism] Timing a bribe so that an interruption cuts off the reveal creates a ticking-clock encounter that rewards quick action. When: Designing a reveal that a player must race to hear. Why: The interruption forces the player to decide quickly.
- [mechanism] A recurring trap that fires at a fixed interval can be made discoverable from a log of firing times, so players infer the interval rather than reading it as a stated rule. When: When designing periodic hazards or events in a level. Why: Players learn the pattern by comparing timestamps side by side, which makes the discovery feel earned.
- [behavior] Idle occupants who stay in a building still restrict movement, so a time when people seem inactive is not automatically an empty window. When: occupants are idle but indoors. Why: their continued presence limits what an intruder can do.
- [rule] Timestamped NPC movement logs with room entries, exits, sightings, carried items and patrol times let players disprove alibis by finding a suspect elsewhere or a claim conflicting with the log. When: designing a world where players check NPC alibis or timed NPC movement. Why: A shared timestamped record gives players a neutral reference for testing timing claims. (x2)
- [rule] Timestamp and source every game event, including NPC movements, door openings, purchases, trap triggers, alarms and state changes, so players can rebuild who was where, when and in what order. When: designing a quest log, journal or mystery's event history where order and timing matter. Why: Ordered, sourced timestamps let players compare accounts, test consistency, find contradictions and recover where a sequence breaks. (x2)
- [rule] Timestamp and place events such as calls, messages, sleep requests, departures, arrivals, deliveries and sightings, with carried items, so each NPC's claim can be checked against independent sources. When: for timed events and sightings recorded in a game. Why: Timestamped sightings let players test whether a watcher could have seen what they claim. (x2)
- [rule] Store each NPC statement as a timestamped, fixed record so players can review it, catch contradictions when new clues appear, and test it by confronting the witness. When: designing dialogue systems that track NPC testimony. Why: Timestamps let contradictions be discovered later, and a stored claim becomes checkable through live questioning. (x2)
- [rule] Log door and gate timestamps, sounds, consumed resources, delivery crates and guard patrols, so players can rebuild who entered and spot gaps revealing a hidden visit or open path. When: designing a game where players reconstruct past events. Why: Logged traces give players checkable evidence for testing timing answers.
- [rule] Keep one shared, timestamped event log the player can query, so every character's account or testimony is cross-checked against one sequence.
- [rule] Timestamp room visits and events, such as lock states, item drops and guard patrols, so players can check when a victim was last alive or when a last-person rule breaks.
- [rule] Label clocks that run fast or slow; with several regional clocks or time skips, store each event's reference time so doubt about one source does not spread. When: a clock is used as evidence or several regional clocks or time skips exist. Why: Unlabelled inaccuracy makes all timings uncertain.
- [rule] Give placed or disturbed objects time markers, such as fresh soil, wet paint or fresh dust, so players can tell current traces from older ones. When: designing evidence that changes over time. Why: Timing lets players separate current traces from older ones.
- [rule] Timestamp found documents and in-world objects, such as logged notes, so players can place clues on one shared timeline and anchor reconstructed sequences. When: when clues must be ordered in time or in a timeline-based mystery. Why: Concrete timestamps let players place clues on one timeline and anchor sequences.
- [rule] Anchor timed events to in-world references players can observe or verify, such as bells, landmarks, shift changes, patrol times or posted schedules, so they can order events. When: designing timed events or story sequences the player must reconstruct. Why: A verifiable shared reference gives players fixed points for ordering events without a written log.
- [rule] A crime-scene clock or watch can be set to a false hour, and checking whether its mechanism still runs can reveal the tampering. When: Designing a timing clue in a scene. Why: Players can detect tampering through physical inspection.
- [rule] Use a witness arrival or outside pressure to pause a search and force the player to decide what to reveal. When: Ending a scene or raising tension during a search. Why: Interruptions create decisions and suspense.
- [rule] Give in-game events timestamps and distance estimates so players can assemble the sequence of a timing puzzle themselves.
- [rule] Give world events timestamps so players can check whether an NPC could be in two places, and let them compare clocks, dated letters and schedules for contradictions. When: designing a mystery or alibi puzzle. Why: Timestamps let the player find contradictions themselves.
- [rule] Log who proposed each plan in NPC conversations so that later clues can depend on attribution.
- [rule] Generate motives from a relationship history of timestamped events, so the player uncovers the layers in order.
- [rule] Learn a target's routine before placing a clue; if the routine is unknown, delivery depends on luck.
- [rule] Let items a character leaves behind show which exit was used and when, giving the player a timeline to reconstruct. When: Levels that reconstruct a past event. Why: Left-behind objects give the player concrete evidence that orders events.
- [rule] Stage an identity switch during a journey between two locations so neither persona is seen leaving its home.
- [rule] Check a door's security at the time of the crime, since the time it was secured may differ from the time it was last checked.
- [rule] Tie a missing item count to a character's earlier visit, so the player connects the loss to the right suspect. When: Designing a clue based on a missing item. Why: A visit gives the player a reason to suspect a particular character.
- [rule] Design a location that holds the record of an earlier event, such as a house where a past crime occurred, so the player reads its traces.
- [rule] Log purchases and item acquisitions as timestamped planning evidence that the player can cross-reference with other events. When: Building a game where earlier acquisitions can implicate a character. Why: Acquisitions that match later events let the player reconstruct intent.
- [rule] Log time-stamped actions so a game can show when a character was absent from a post during an event, and derive alibi checks from those logs. When: A game tracks character presence during events. Why: Timestamps let the game verify claims about who was where.
- [rule] Log ticket or key issuance as queryable data, so unclaimed slots near a crime become clues a player can check.
- [rule] Make an offered explanation checkable against the level's timeline, so an attentive player can catch the gap.
- [rule] Place ambient sound events from other areas as location and timing clues, so the player can order events. When: designing a sealed space where events occur out of view. Why: Sounds from elsewhere let the player reconstruct a sequence without seeing it.
- [heuristic] Test whether a timeline is genuine by asking who could have made a key call or action at the moment the event is fixed. When: when an anchor is a recorded call or a similar action.
- [heuristic] Use travel time across the setting to limit where a missing person can be, since a place crossable in a fixed time bounds the search. When: Reasoning about whether a missing person is still nearby. Why: Distance and travel time constrain possible locations.
- [heuristic] Record the questions asked in witness logs, so players can see how a statement was extracted.
- [heuristic] Track each location's traffic, including patrols, NPC routines and weekday schedules, letting players judge whether a stashed clue could survive.
- [heuristic] Include visitors who arrived too late, so the player learns that the site was used on a schedule.
- [heuristic] Set the intervals between a scream, a silence and an arrival as tuned triggers for patrols and guard movements. When: Designing timed reactions to in-world events.
- [heuristic] Use a dated backward trace on a case board, where each record found unlocks the next location in a fixed order. When: Designing investigation progression in a game. Why: Ordered records gate progress and keep the player on a clear path.
- [heuristic] A locked-room puzzle can log a door-state change caused by an NPC, giving a timestamp the player must reconstruct from other events. When: when designing a locked-room puzzle with NPC movement. Why: Logged state changes make the timeline a task the player can solve.
- [heuristic] Plant a timed object, such as a stopped watch, whose recorded time can be compared with other locations to test who was where. When: Designing a clue that tests alibis or movements. Why: A recorded time can be cross-checked against other locations and witnesses.
- [heuristic] Make an alibi a physical object or timed fact the player can check, such as a telegram or a timed event, so its falsity can be proven by a later clue. When: Designing a suspect's cover story. Why: An alibi that can be tested gives the player a real route to disproving it.
- [heuristic] Build a timeline puzzle around a single hinge event, such as a checkpoint, so the player must work out a character's state before and after it. When: Designing a timeline puzzle in a level or case. Why: A single hinge gives the player one clear point to test all other facts against.
- [procedure] 1) Count tickets sold from the sales record. 2) Count stubs recovered at the scene. 3) Subtract to find the unaccounted tickets. When: Sales logs and collected stubs are both available. Why: The difference reveals tickets that never returned to the scene, which can point to hidden or absent parties.
- [procedure] 1) Log each trap or event with its cause and trigger. 2) Store the order of occurrence. 3) Replay the log to show the solution step by step. When: a level needs a reconstructable solution. Why: Logged causes allow the sequence to be shown.
- [tradeoff] A single watcher over a long period becomes unreliable without a relief watcher or a written log. When: when a watch lasts a long time. Why: Attention fails over long periods.
- [pitfall] Leaving a post to chase a sound opens the gap that an intruder needs to pass unseen.
- [pitfall] Leaving the most enclosed spots for last means the hardest checks fall when the search has already run long.
- [fact] Common journey delays include a mechanical breakdown, a chance encounter, removed navigation signs, an interrupted conversation and a closing time.

## stealth cover
- [definition] A secret operation can be hidden behind a visible, ordinary activity so that observers take the hidden work for routine business.
- [mechanism] A hidden observer with a dark background, unlit face and silhouette, or light behind, is detected less and can watch and listen unrecognized; lit or pale features draw attention. When: choosing a stealth position near light sources, or in lit or dark areas. Why: Lighting and cover keep the observer's face obscured, while light-coloured or lit features contrast with the background and draw attention. (x2)
- [mechanism] A cover identity can require a skill check whose difficulty depends on how well the costume matches the local role.
- [mechanism] A search that yields nothing deflates expectation and resets the investigation, so the next lead starts from a clean slate. When: a planned excursion produces no evidence. Why: the failed effort lowers hopes, shifting focus back to open questions.
- [mechanism] If a post-holder leaves the post at any point, coverage has a gap, which opens a window in which access to the guarded point is possible. When: when a guard, watchman or attendant may step away.
- [mechanism] Sudden total darkness covers movement, because everyone loses sight at the same moment with no gradual fade.
- [mechanism] Street crowds, stalls, accidents and blocked vehicle lines give a fleeing target cover that breaks the pursuer's line of sight.
- [mechanism] Detection chances can scale with distance, cover and noise, so the player's position and actions shape the risk of discovery.
- [mechanism] A guard that checks for gaze exposure can detect a reflection-based peek and respond by redirecting the player's attention.
- [mechanism] Ambient noise that drowns out an observer's alert lets the player act unseen, but the observer's sighting can still be recorded for later investigation. When: Designing stealth with environmental noise. Why: Masking the alarm does not erase the NPC's perception of the event.
- [mechanism] A guard that re-checks line of sight after each turn of the target makes the player's timing of hiding and movement matter. When: a guard patrols with a stealth state. Why: the recheck forces the player to break line of sight quickly.
- [mechanism] Give stealth or eavesdrop mechanics a visibility range, so a hidden position lets the player hear dialogue unseen. When: designing eavesdropping. Why: A hidden position makes overhearing possible without detection.
- [mechanism] A route close to a building but hidden from its view suits secret meetings or concealment, because it is near enough to use yet unseen. When: choosing a path for covert movement. Why: proximity with no sightline offers cover.
- [mechanism] A cue such as a moving object or an animal's reaction draws attention to one spot, letting the real action happen unnoticed elsewhere.
- [mechanism] A stealth sequence can let the player appear to rest while preparing an ambush, with an ally who believes the cover story.
- [behavior] A bystander's reaction, such as a guard's tense posture, can be a designed tell that the player learns to read. When: reacting to a revealed name or secret. Why: consistent body cues become learnable.
- [behavior] Rivals who fear publicity more than suspicion tend to avoid overreacting and stop probing once a plausible explanation is offered. When: when the rival has more to lose from exposure than from uncertainty. Why: exposure risk makes quiet responses more attractive.
- [rule] Give stillness a concealment bonus, since a motionless figure blends into a background similar in colour or texture, while movement draws the eye; this rewards patient play. Why: Movement draws the eye while stillness merges with the surroundings, making stillness a meaningful choice.
- [rule] Give stealth spaces hidden vantages, like a pipe, balcony or window, with a clear view of the target and no sightline back, so players gain information without entering the room.
- [rule] Provide cover props or excuses, such as a forgotten item or a distraction, that the player can invoke to continue an action. When: designing interruption events for stealth.
- [rule] Have a guard issue a challenge whose answer the player can learn by eavesdropping on earlier dialogue, turning the gate into a deduction puzzle. When: Designing a gate guarded by a password. Why: Eavesdropping gives players a fair path to the answer.
- [rule] Make a scripted reaction detectable by letting it always stop on one specific action, so a careful player can spot the trick.
- [rule] For stealth, place vantage points close enough that a player can read faces or objects once in position. When: designing observation spots. Why: detail must be readable for the player to act.
- [rule] Place a watch point with a narrow but controlled view of the passage, and keep its openings clean enough to see through. When: when a player or NPC must observe a bounded point. Why: a controlled sightline lets the observer watch without being seen.
- [rule] In stealth areas, include shadows that turn out to be harmless, so players learn that movement is not always a threat. When: Designing stealth detection and enemy visibility. Why: Players calibrate their caution from what proves harmless as well as what proves dangerous.
- [rule] During a crowd event, make guards watching the spectacle less likely to notice a thief at a side door, so a scripted theft can succeed. When: Designing a stealth or theft scenario during a festival. Why: Guards' attention is pulled toward the event.
- [rule] Make observed detail depend on light, distance and observer state, so a witness in darkness or fog reports less detail or less consistent detail.
- [rule] Use a hedge or screen to limit a witness's view so the figure is glimpsed rather than fully observed. When: Designing line of sight for witness scenes.
- [rule] Add a harmless-looking nuisance, such as a recurring noise or scattered debris, that quietly hides a movement. When: when designing ambient hazards or distractions.
- [heuristic] Treat silhouette bulges larger than the hands could explain, and rigid hands with a fixed gaze toward the chest, as tells of a concealed object or threat; show them visibly. When: when judging a person's silhouette or posture. Why: so players can spot concealed threats before combat begins.
- [heuristic] Wait in a busy place, where loitering is less noticeable, though a cautious target can still spot the watcher.
- [heuristic] A courier who must deliver information in person can be intercepted at a known time and place, which makes a natural set-piece. When: when the enemy's information must travel by hand. Why: in-person delivery cannot be done remotely.
- [heuristic] Covert observation works best on a target in an open area with a known routine; unpredictable movement weakens it. When: Planning a stealth watch.
- [heuristic] Offer party-splitting as a designed choice: one character scouts while another holds an exit and waits for a signal to rescue.
- [heuristic] A covert signal should be a single, simple cue disguised as ordinary activity, since a signal that is long or repeated draws attention. When: when two actors must communicate under observation. Why: Simplicity lets the receiver decode it, while deniability keeps the watcher from registering it as a message.
- [heuristic] Give an actor a plausible reason for being in a room, such as a casual errand, so their presence and movement look natural to watchers. When: when an actor must act unnoticed in a location. Why: An explained presence lowers suspicion about activity there.
- [heuristic] A covert search suits a suspect who may be lying only when the premises can be reached unseen and the search fits within the available time. Why: a visible approach or short window makes the search fail.
- [heuristic] Stealth levels can use sightlines, lit windows and noisy gear as mechanics, giving players a lower-risk route with its own constraints. When: when designing stealth spaces. Why: Readable hazards let players plan approaches.
- [heuristic] A stationary object amid moving elements such as water, foliage or crowds stands out when attention is focused on it, so it can be made findable. When: placing a clue in a busy, moving environment. Why: motion contrast draws the eye to still items.
- [heuristic] A stealth level can place a dark alcove with a view of a lit hall, letting the player observe while remaining unseen. When: Designing a stealth space. Why: Light contrast gives the player concealment and a clear view.
- [heuristic] Use hollows and low movement to stay out of view when open ground offers no structures, keeping the profile low against the skyline. When: fleeing across open ground. Why: low profiles and dips break sight lines.
- [heuristic] Signs that a target is evading a watcher include dismissing a vehicle, passing through a transit hall without buying a ticket, leaving by a second exit, and checking around before re-entering. When: a target is followed through transit or buildings. Why: Each action tries to shake off a follower or conceal a destination.
- [procedure] 1) Pick a concealed spot with a full view of the target. 2) Watch it over time and record its routine. 3) Act only once the routine is clear.
- [procedure] 1) Pick a believable pretext for asking about an absent person. 2) Judge the witness's evasiveness at the door. 3) Watch from cover until the witness leaves. 4) Enter by the least conspicuous route. 5) Search quickly and exit before the witness returns. When: a witness may be lying and must stay unaware of the investigation. Why: the search depends on the witness not knowing about the inquiry.
- [procedure] 1) Watch from concealment before approaching. 2) Check for pursuers and onlookers. 3) Speak quickly. 4) Leave at once. When: when a person avoids being seen. Why: Checking for witnesses reduces the risk of exposure.
- [pitfall] Assuming a crowd will hide you fails when the target is cautious or has already left.
- [pitfall] Relying on fog or the sea to hide a disposal fails when tide and visibility change. When: when an actor uses weather or water to conceal evidence. Why: conditions that hide evidence now may shift later.
- [pitfall] Marks that the subject or bystanders can read, or that stand out, expose the pursuit.
- [pitfall] Moving to act while a still posture would hide the watcher exposes the watcher to detection. When: A watcher is near a target and needs to stay concealed. Why: Movement breaks the camouflage that stillness provides.
- [pitfall] Unseen observers can be present; an unexpected listener can change the picture, so any timeline must allow for hidden watchers.
- [pitfall] Relying on an excuse whose prop the checker can inspect is risky, because a mismatched or empty prop collapses the cover.
- [fact] Concealed actions, such as a figure bending low over a vehicle, are visible only to someone close.

## disturbance signs
- [mechanism] A brief stall or slowdown in a guarded transit masks an exit, because observers' attention and any alarm are overwhelmed by the ambient disruption. When: A transit route has predictable slow points such as junctions, tunnels or traffic. Why: Noise and the momentary halt drown out the signal that would otherwise expose the escape.
- [mechanism] A visible disturbance draws attention away from the action the disturber actually wants, so the real act goes unseen. Why: Observers focus on the most visible event.
- [mechanism] Recording when each surface was cleaned lets a trace's age become a usable clue for the player. When: designing time-based evidence.
- [mechanism] Residue left by a cleanup, such as powder grains lodged in glue, shows that handling happened after an object was used and can exclude some sources.
- [mechanism] A disturbed-room system that tracks displaced furniture lets players deduce a hidden item's location from which objects have moved. When: a room tracks object displacement.
- [mechanism] An object found in a different position after its owner was away shows that someone visited and handled it. Why: Physical state changes record access to a private space.
- [mechanism] Disturbed ground or a dropped object shows someone passed without entering a room, which narrows the list of suspects.
- [behavior] Fresh stitching or disturbed material at a hiding place shows that someone has tampered with it before the investigator arrived. When: Evidence has been hidden and may have been reached by others. Why: Disturbed concealment leaves physical signs of recent handling.
- [behavior] A person who tries to clean a stain has likely known about it. When: A stain has been partly removed or tidied. Why: Cleaning is a deliberate act driven by awareness of the mark.
- [behavior] An object disguised by dirt is usually noticed only by a searcher who is looking for that specific item. When: When searching a scene for a specific object. Why: Dirt makes the object look like ordinary debris.
- [rule] Leave damaged, moved or disturbed objects and room states, such as a moved chair or wet path, that players can read as signs of recent handling and search direction. When: when designing a scene that implies a recent event or placing clues. Why: Players reconstruct recent activity from disturbances, and disturbance implies someone touched or moved the objects.
- [rule] Design a ransacked room so its disorder shows what the searcher wanted, letting players infer the search goal from the scene. When: when designing disturbed scenes. Why: Disorder that reveals intent gives players a readable clue.
- [rule] Add detection risk when the player moves a curtain or screen that hides them. When: designing stealth cover that can be disturbed.
- [rule] Mark a shifted board or disturbed debris as a visible clue that guides the search, and let touch or a close look confirm what lies beneath. When: when hiding loot in a dark area. Why: Physical disturbance signals a hidden space without requiring full light.
- [rule] Give containers physical states such as resealed flaps, altered dates and stains that the player checks in a fixed order. When: Designing containers that carry evidence of handling. Why: Concrete physical traces give the player something specific to examine and reason about.
