- [rule] Make containers, props and cosmetics inspectable, with a turn or twist mechanic that reveals hidden items, so players learn to test objects rather than only read them. When: Designing props or containers in a dungeon or level. Why: Players then learn that objects reward manipulation.
- [rule] Give each disturbed area its own texture and scent, so a careful player can notice a mismatch with the ground around it. When: Designing hidden or disturbed areas in a level.
- [heuristic] Cleaning, repair, replacement or disturbance of a scene or object before examination erases or fakes the traces, so do not rely on them as reliable evidence. When: when an object or scene may have been cleaned, restored or altered before inspection. Why: Cleaning and handling remove or change the marks that would have been read; disturbance erases or fakes the traces the method depends on.
- [heuristic] Ordinary traces such as a stale smell, thick dust, unlatched case, open empty drawer, paint smear near a lock or lockless cabinet show a space has been used or disturbed. When: when deciding which spaces to search first or signalling disturbance. Why: Such traces reveal past or recent activity in a space that appears closed.
- [heuristic] Disturbed furniture, such as a rumpled couch, can show where something was hidden and later removed. When: an item may have been concealed in a room.
- [heuristic] An object lying out of place on the floor can point to something hidden behind nearby furniture. Why: Moving the furniture may reveal a concealed compartment.
- [heuristic] A room with dismantled shelves and open drawers shows that someone searched it hurriedly, and that search can be read as a clue.
- [heuristic] An overturned object beneath an exit, such as a chute, can be the only sign of how an escape was made.
- [heuristic] Cleared desks and neatly arranged papers suggest someone has already searched the room before you arrived.
- [heuristic] Before treating a disturbed trap as proof of intrusion, check whether an innocent cause could have moved the marker. When: A trap or marker shows signs of disturbance. Why: A disturbed trap shows that the place was touched, which can have an ordinary explanation.
- [heuristic] Objects left in shared transport are often cleaned the same night, so tracing must begin before that cleaning routine.
- [heuristic] Titles of disturbed books reveal the reader's interests, but such an inference must be tested before it is treated as evidence of a particular person. When: reading the titles of books handled in a room. Why: A title suggests interest, yet a single item alone does not establish who handled it.
- [heuristic] A bent or disturbed fixing beside an empty slot suggests the item was pulled out quickly rather than removed carefully.
- [heuristic] A scrubbed spot, moved rug or missing cloth can indicate that a killing happened elsewhere and the room was tidied afterward.
- [heuristic] A door found locked and later found open shows that someone passed through in the interval, which suggests an unseen movement. When: a room's entrance changes state between checks. Why: a state change records movement the witnesses did not see.
- [heuristic] Set markers at points an intruder would not expect to check, such as an item's position or a faint residue, and check those points rather than only where things are stored. When: Designing a space or object that players or NPCs may search or handle without authorization. Why: Intruders focus on hiding what they took and often restore visible order, leaving unexpected spots unchanged or altered.
- [heuristic] Debris such as shells or moss on an injured character or item can point players to a specific area, like a flooded passage or submerged structure. When: when designing environmental clues.
- [heuristic] Disturbed furniture, displaced cushions and crushed carpet show how a person sat and moved.
- [heuristic] Faint traces rarely survive in a room that has been heavily handled or cleared, so survey it before disturbance erases the marks. When: when deciding whether a room is worth a trace survey. Why: disturbance destroys faint marks.
- [heuristic] Retain sweepings from cleaned areas for sifting, since small clues such as ticket stubs can turn up in debris.
- [heuristic] Signs a concealment leaves behind, such as disturbed ground, scent and routine, can be read in reverse to expose a hidden act. When: A scene depends on whether a location shows signs of disturbance.
- [heuristic] Concealment depends on removing every sign of disturbance from the ground, not only the most obvious one, so the surface matches its surroundings. When: Hiding a trap, pit, or dug area.
- [heuristic] A visible change in the surface, such as freshly turned soil, can signal that a hidden cache has already been disturbed and moved. When: Designing hidden caches that players search for. Why: Disturbed ground betrays past activity and gives players a clue to the true location.
- [procedure] 1) Give the corpse items that suggest an identity. 2) Include a feature that cannot be altered. 3) Require the player to find and check that feature before confirming identity. When: Designing a body-identification puzzle. Why: The player must weigh misleading items against decisive evidence.
- [tradeoff] Disturbing a target's space can trigger the desired search but may alarm them into fleeing or destroying evidence. When: choosing whether to tamper with a target's environment. Why: the same disturbance can prompt escape.
- [pitfall] Handling the key book visibly can alert the suspect, so fetch it without any tampering he could notice.
- [pitfall] Items found away from a body do not by themselves show who discarded them. Many chances to dispose of them exist, so location alone leaves the culprit open.
- [pitfall] Assuming a sealed room must have been entered through its door hides the real access route.
- [pitfall] Searching after bystanders or first arrivals have moved things loses evidence of original positions.
- [pitfall] Items may be hidden by disposal in water or drains, and burned or torn papers leave fragments the player must reassemble to recover information. When: when designing hidden evidence in a scene. Why: Disposal and destruction create search targets and reconstruction puzzles.
- [pitfall] Locking a room behind you can keep others out of the evidence they need to examine.
- [fact] A departure from an expected layout shows that something was moved, but not who moved it. When: an investigator finds a disturbed item. Why: the layout reveals change, not the cause.
- [fact] In a concealed killing, disposing of the body is a harder problem than the means of death. When: When planning or analysing how a death is hidden. Why: Remains that are found or traced create the link that a killer most needs to break.
- [fact] An emptied container with a known opening time gives the player a clue about when a theft occurred.

## environmental storytelling
- [mechanism] A specific trace, such as a particular tobacco ash, narrows the search sharply when the lore maps it to few possible sources. When: when a trace is distinctive and its lore is known. Why: the fewer matching sources, the smaller the suspect pool.
- [mechanism] Props such as weapons, containers or animals can be placed so that they confirm or rule out each candidate explanation for a supernatural-looking event, rewarding careful checking. When: Designing a level with a mysterious event that has several mundane explanations. Why: Physical evidence in the environment lets players eliminate candidates.
- [mechanism] Furniture that makes a relaxed posture plausible supports innocent readings of a figure and can hide the dangerous one.
- [behavior] To outsiders, specialist knowledge seems trivial or like eccentric minutiae, even though it is the key that makes a trace meaningful. When: when a character's expertise is dismissed by others. Why: detailed lore appears unimportant until it solves a case.
- [rule] Give corpses, containers and drawers wear patterns, missing items or small leftover objects that reveal their user's habits or prior visitors, so players can deduce what happened. When: when designing level props. (x2)
- [rule] Give unusual observations or test results a visible in-world explanation, such as age or fear level, so surprise feels strange without being left unexplained. Why: Players can then interpret surprising results. (x2)
- [rule] Seed an ambiguous inscription or readable object early, without a prompt, so its meaning emerges when the player finds the matching object, location or NPC interaction. When: when seeding a clue for later payoff. Why: The player can notice it later without being told; a clue connecting to a later find rewards exploration and recall. (x2)
- [rule] Make lore documents' figures, claims, dates and authors checkable against other clues or world state, so a bad number, false claim or forgery can be exposed.
- [rule] Let players corroborate clues against in-world books, journals, archives or directories, so checking a hunch can itself reveal further clues. When: Designing clue sources for a mystery.
- [rule] Repeat a symbol or consistent staging style, such as a trap pattern, across locations so players link each encounter to one faction or recurring villain.
- [rule] Build a reconstructable sequence in a game scene from connected physical clues, such as a broken object, a dust mark and a stain, so players can deduce events without dialogue. When: When designing environmental storytelling for a staged event. Why: Connected clues let players reconstruct the sequence through reasoning rather than exposition.
- [rule] Use book titles and margin notes as environmental hints about a hidden mechanic, and mark each trace with enough detail that a player can link it to a later find.
- [rule] Record a lore note's chain of custody, such as who wrote it and who copied it, so players can judge how far to trust it. When: Placing written lore that players may need to weigh. Why: Provenance gives players the information to assess reliability themselves.
- [rule] Gate a puzzle behind lore, such as a poison, plant or dialect, that the player learns from in-world codex entries before a trace becomes meaningful. When: when designing a clue that requires specialist knowledge. Why: in-world learning makes the lore a discoverable key rather than an arbitrary requirement.
- [rule] Have an in-world character explain the solution, so the player can check their own reasoning against it.
- [rule] Place memorials, inscriptions, or reliefs that can trigger a visible NPC reaction, and use the reaction to hint at that NPC's past. Why: the environment can carry evidence that dialogue does not.
- [rule] Place props such as journals, half-made items or a turned-away portrait that encode motive without giving a single answer. When: When building environmental storytelling. Why: Props that hint without settling let players interpret and compare clues.
- [rule] Give found letters a typewriter defect or watermark, and found clothing embroidered initials, so the player can link each item to one sender or owner. When: when designing found documents and clothing.
- [rule] Give a game's backstory named documents and dates so the object feels real, and let the player's journal or an expert NPC confirm or deflate some claims. When: Designing lore for an object or faction. Why: Verifiable references reward investigation while keeping the world believable.
- [rule] Give rooms and objects checkable state, such as warmth, arrangement and disorder, and flag contradictions with NPC claims while leaving the player to decide what they mean. When: Designing environments that support dialogue checks. Why: Object states let players test claims and make environmental storytelling interactive.
- [rule] Build a game's past from in-game documents such as letters, court records and diaries, and from locations matching their descriptions, leaving the link partial. When: Designing backstory for an event the player must reconstruct. Why: Partial links make the player judge how well evidence fits, which rewards investigation.
- [rule] Place reversed inscriptions on blotters or rubbings so players can read a message from a damaged note. When: designing readable clues from damaged documents. Why: reading reversed text is a satisfying discovery.
- [rule] Give NPCs props consistent with their stated activity, since a wrongly rigged tool or badly cut herb can contradict a cover story. When: designing NPCs with a stated occupation or pretext. Why: mismatched props expose the lie to observant players.
- [rule] Make some props distinctive and others generic, so only a few traces point to a specific NPC.
- [rule] Build crime scenes whose staged method conflicts with the physics the game enforces so players can detect the mismatch.
- [rule] Give a lore NPC one fragment of a dead figure's history, told with a reason to shade it, so players must weigh the source. When: Designing lore delivery about a past figure. Why: Partial, biased sources make players reason about the history rather than receive it whole.
- [rule] A missing stretch in a character's recollection can signal that a place was visited but not remembered, serving as a level-design hook. When: Designing a character backstory that a level should reveal. Why: The gap invites the player to find the place that the memory lacks.
- [rule] Plant ominous signs, such as a warning or a fork the player only understands later, to set dread without revealing the outcome. When: Designing a dungeon that should build unease. Why: Signs that gain meaning later reward attention and sustain tension.
- [rule] Place an element whose purpose is not yet clear in one area, and explain its cause in a later area's notes or environment.
- [rule] Place a written warning at an entrance to set expectations and raise stakes without dialogue. When: Designing the entry to a dangerous area. Why: Text at the threshold communicates stakes efficiently.
- [heuristic] Vague testimony or uncertain recollection becomes useful when confirmed against a second source, such as a logbook, footprint or found journal, turning vagueness into a puzzle. When: When designing lore that pays off after a second find. Why: Partial information gains value when corroborated.
- [heuristic] Signs such as thieves' slang, a lookout, and gambling tables show who controls an area and who knows it. When: When designing environmental clues about a criminal network.
- [heuristic] Objects left at a scene, such as a dropped case or torn notebook pages, can carry a message and point to where further evidence is stored. When: A scene contains objects that seem out of place. Why: Deliberate or accidental markers often direct the investigator onward.
- [heuristic] Mud tracks, fresh breaks in surfaces and cold drafts signal recent activity, and players learn to read them as signs of presence.
- [heuristic] Spotless surfaces in a room can suggest an orderly, strict occupant, so environmental details can be used to hint at character.
- [heuristic] Local tradition is a valuable lead but can be wrong about location, so test it against terrain and records before accepting it. Why: Traditions often preserve events while misplacing where they occurred.
- [heuristic] A journal collecting several NPC accounts can be sorted by time, so the player can see gaps in the story. When: designing quest logs with multiple accounts. Why: chronological order exposes missing events.
- [heuristic] Schedule a key lore reveal behind a sealed item or timed delivery, so players anticipate the content before learning it. When: When a major lore reveal should build anticipation. Why: Waiting for a delivery builds expectation.
- [heuristic] Leave unanswered petitions and character witnesses as open plot threads that the player can revisit later. When: when writing a legal storyline. Why: unresolved requests create lasting narrative leverage.
- [heuristic] Keep room motifs consistent so they point the same way, and use single unrelated objects as red herrings.
- [heuristic] Give a location a mood that contradicts the public face of its owner, and let details such as cold rooms or bare walls hint at private trouble. When: designing a location whose owner has a public image. Why: A contradiction between surface and setting invites players to look beneath the facade.
- [heuristic] Encode a scene's story in props such as a dropped book, a clutched blanket or a lit lamp, so the player can read the sequence of events from them. When: when designing environmental storytelling. Why: Props reward observation and let the player infer what happened.
- [heuristic] A stopped object, moved chair or half-dressed character can convey a timeline, so players reconstruct events rather than read a log. When: Designing scene clues for a mystery or story-driven level.
- [heuristic] Let an unrelated lore text, such as a plaque or a building's history, hold the clue for a hidden passage.
- [heuristic] Place many odd events with mundane causes, such as drafts, loose boards or previous occupants, and reserve the truly unusual cause for a few rooms. When: Designing a level with mysterious events. Why: Keeps player trust in the investigation meaningful.
- [heuristic] Place a cult belief as an early room description or inscription, then let a later document recast it as a witnessed event so the same location reads differently afterward. When: When building a dungeon or location whose meaning should change after a late reveal. Why: A location's description gains new weight once the player learns the belief was a real event.
- [procedure] 1) Seed a region's lore with several stacked pressures. 2) Record them as dated chronicle entries. 3) Let the player build a timeline of unrest before the main event. When: Building background lore that explains local hostility. Why: A dated timeline makes the causes of suspicion discoverable and credible.
- [tradeoff] Put a secret's full history in optional lore such as letters or diaries, while main dialogue hints at it earlier, so progress is never blocked. When: Designing how a late-revealed motive is delivered in a game. Why: Optional lore rewards curious players without gating the main case.
- [tradeoff] Environmental clues such as cellars and overgrown roads can reveal a former settlement, and a surviving family name can tie a living household to a documented event. When: When designing environmental storytelling for an in-game world. Why: Physical details let the player infer history without reading a document.

## room state
- [mechanism] Objects that reset unless touched let a displaced item show the player that someone was there.
- [mechanism] An environment can change when the decisive clue lands, marking the turning point for players and separating it from the minor clues before it.
- [mechanism] Tracking a room's item state lets a gap prompt the player to ask where the removed item went, turning an inventory gap into a clue.
- [mechanism] Withholding information from players or NPCs can be modeled as a visibility flag, which changes what the player can learn and when. When: A game hides a fact until a condition is met. Why: Flags control which information becomes available to each actor.
- [rule] Timestamp room state changes, such as alarm raised, door opened or torch burned out, and record time since last disturbance, so players reconstruct timelines from residual lights or doors ajar. When: A world records the aftermath of actions. Why: Stored timestamps let players date events; leftover state persists after the actor leaves and reveals the sequence.
- [rule] Store room state such as fire level, window state, furniture position, lights, open or locked doors, noise gaps or cluttered ashtrays so players reconstruct earlier events and test accounts. When: when building a room or scene that players investigate or must reconstruct. Why: Persistent state lets past actions leave readable evidence and gives facts against which accounts are checked.
- [rule] Track dust, snow, mud, blood trails and object states over time, such as wiped handles or moved books, so visits leave readable changes players can date. When: when designing environments that record visits or movement. Why: Persistent, readable traces let players reconstruct sequences and infer who was present and when.
- [rule] Save a baseline or per-event snapshot of clue state so the game can diff against it and tell whether a clue existed beforehand or was added afterward. When: when clue timing matters to the mystery or rooms may be altered during play. Why: Without a stored baseline the game cannot check when a clue appeared or report what changed.
- [rule] Place a distinctive, harmless-seeming object or habit early, like a cloak, key or boots, so a later cue prompts recall and links it to the answer or an NPC. When: when a solution or clue chain depends on an earlier detail. Why: A later cue rewards attention without signposting; early mundane links point to a character later.
- [rule] Use dated in-world items such as newspapers, receipts, journals, inscriptions or photographs to bound when events occurred, what an NPC knew, or a household's history. When: when a game needs a player-reconstructed timeline or time-sensitive knowledge clues. Why: Dated items give objective time bounds; dated documents tie knowledge to specific moments.
- [rule] Record each room's openings, shared walls, doors or service routes with states such as barred, fastened or passable, so claims can be checked against the layout. When: when rooms must be reasoned about or sealed-room claims checked. Why: Explicit states let players rule out routes without trial and error.
- [rule] Place torn cloth, stains or missing items so players can rebuild what happened in a room. When: Designing a crime scene for the player to solve. Why: Physical remnants let players infer past actions.
- [rule] Use a level reset or replay to recreate a room's exact state, so the player can test a theory of how an event happened.
- [rule] Store the real heights of furniture, doors and props so an NPC's height can be deduced from where it leaned or reached. When: designing scenes for height deduction.
- [rule] Give sealed rooms environmental clues such as scent, sound or residue at the door, so players can judge danger before entering. When: Designing hazardous rooms in a generated dungeon.
- [rule] Place objects whose wear, debris direction and quantity imply an occupant's size or number, so players can infer creature details from the scene. When: generating environmental clues about occupants. Why: props can imply presence.
- [rule] Give a narrow hidden shaft a size clue, such as a matching object that fits only that passage, so the player can discover it. When: when a secret passage should be findable through logic. Why: a size clue links the hidden route to something the player can test.
- [rule] Let hotel or inn staff record room use, so the designer can hide relationship clues that the player collects.
- [rule] Generate murder scenes as closed with a fixed NPC cast or open with any wandering NPC possible, since this changes how many suspects the player must check. When: Generating a murder-mystery room. Why: Suspect count sets the deduction workload.
- [rule] Let disposable items such as wrappers, torn notes and bin contents persist in the world so that a player who searches bins can find them. When: Populating a dungeon or environment with clues. Why: Discarded items reward thorough searching and feel believable.
- [rule] Place props such as fuel, tar or tools so they imply a process, letting players infer events that no NPC describes.
- [rule] Encode a building's period in its construction details, such as hand-hewn beams, low ceilings and rough foundation stones, so players can date it from its walls. When: Generating historical buildings. Why: Visible construction lets players read history without text.
- [rule] Give a returning player a visible trace, such as a stain or dirt, so an NPC can recognize them on the next visit.
- [rule] Track which conditions the player has restored, and show when a missing object blocks the test.
- [rule] Let the player prove an enemy was inside before doors locked, using uncleaned blood or left-behind objects, so the sealed-room puzzle has a findable solution.
- [rule] Where rooms look alike, use their contents, such as an occupant's belongings, to correct a mistaken assumption about position.
- [rule] Place a crime inside a window between two known events, so the player can test the gap between them. When: When designing a timed mystery puzzle. Why: A bounded window gives the player a concrete interval to check against alibis.
- [rule] Record the state of each door and switch in a level, so the player can rebuild who had access and in what order. When: Designing a level where access must be reconstructed. Why: A recorded state history gives the player a timeline to reason from.
- [rule] Plant clues such as an open door, a left-behind key or an unlatched bolt early so the player can see them before the reveal. Why: Players need prior exposure to find a clue fairly.
- [rule] Build rooms as readable scenes, so leftover objects reveal who was present and what happened.
- [rule] A reset that restores props every cycle erases player disturbances; keep the state of objects that clues depend on. When: a level resets objects between sessions or cycles. Why: clues that rely on moved props break when reset.
- [rule] Store dust, ash and cleanliness as room state so the game can show the player when a document or fire was recent.
- [rule] Store a clearing of an accused character as an explicit world-state flag, so later dialogue and reactions reflect the reversal. When: A character's status changes after an accusation is withdrawn. Why: Without a flag, NPC dialogue can contradict the new record.
- [rule] Make two views of the same space share viewpoint and scale so that differences between them can be checked. When: Designing a discrepancy the player must compare, such as a before and after state. Why: Comparison fails when viewpoint or scale differs between the two views.
- [rule] Author a puzzle room or hub with defined zones so the player can enumerate every reachable location and every character inside it. When: Designing a bounded hub or puzzle space. Why: A finite, visible set of zones lets players reason systematically about who could have acted where.
- [heuristic] Cleaning, dust, fresh sand, new stock, footprints or disturbed objects reveal a recent visit or new occupant, letting players test claims, though the reading may be wrong. (x2)
- [heuristic] Show recent occupation via warm embers, half-eaten food or a freshly made bed; with a defined routine, deviations like a half-filled lantern or cold brazier reveal events. When: when designing rooms that should suggest a recent occupant or environmental storytelling. Why: Traces of recent presence prompt players to investigate; defined routines make deviations readable as evidence of recent activity.
- [heuristic] Let props change state or record past events, such as a container's level, rumpled rug or wedged block, so players reconstruct and test event sequences. When: when designing environmental storytelling. Why: Changed props let players infer off-screen actions and give them something concrete to verify.
- [heuristic] Tie item retrieval or evidence availability to a natural cycle such as a tide, day, patrol timing or dawn, so players must plan a timed follow-up. When: when a hidden item or evidence is reachable only at certain times.
- [heuristic] Plant an early schedule, record or classified notice whose meaning appears after a later event or whose check overturns the apparent answer; players must reread or verify it.
- [heuristic] Reopen previously cleared NPCs or locations as the story advances, so players who revisit them are rewarded.
- [heuristic] Let message origins encode the mode of travel, so the player must infer the mode to set a deadline. When: designing deadline puzzles. Why: inference from origin rewards attention.
- [heuristic] Give each room a material palette, such as rug fibres, ash or grit, so players can match a carried trace to the room it came from.
- [heuristic] Place a conspicuous note or an open door as a deliberate clue, where the player cannot miss it.
- [heuristic] A reconstruction scene can let the player replay positions and spot contradictions between NPC accounts and the physical layout.
- [tradeoff] A reset of a hiding spot can leave a trace such as a slightly raised stone or disturbed soil, which gives a checkable clue to a detective NPC or player. When: A hiding spot has been restored to look untouched. Why: Even careful restoration rarely leaves no evidence.
- [pitfall] A bill shows one room's occupancy, not where the occupant was at night. When: using a bill to place a person at a time. Why: Billing records track rooms, not the locations of people.
- [pitfall] A clue placed in shadow is overlooked until the player searches deliberately.
- [pitfall] A search during an empty window is still risky if someone notices the visit, so empty time does not guarantee safety. Why: observation can occur from outside the room.

## environmental traces and cues
- [mechanism] Naming how many others know a secret and a witness who will attend makes the recipient believe the matter is contained and cannot be quietly buried. When: when confronting a person who may try to evade or hide facts. Why: A visible, limited circle of knowers removes the recipient's hope that the matter will fade.
- [mechanism] Environmental objects can carry state, such as a crumpled note beneath a body or a burned scrap in a grate, encoding a hidden event the player can reconstruct. Why: Physical arrangement records what happened in a place.
- [mechanism] An environmental cue such as an animal disturbing soil can reveal a buried item, rewarding careful searching.
- [mechanism] Stride spacing and stumble marks can reveal a figure's state, such as fleeing or injured, through the physical record left in the level. When: a tracked figure moves through the level. Why: gait changes reflect the figure's condition.
- [mechanism] A vessel that grounds on a falling tide stays stuck until recovered, so grounding is a costly failure when water levels drop. When: when water level is falling during a crossing. Why: the water drops below the hull and the vessel cannot float free.
- [mechanism] Newer floor decals such as dust, blood or water overwrite older ones, so the visible layer shows the latest movement and players must infer the earlier sequence. When: Floor decals record movement in a dungeon.
- [mechanism] Marks left in the environment, such as footprints, dents and cut grass, record the direction and force of past actions.
- [behavior] A defensive position inside an isolated room protects its occupant but brings no outside help.
- [rule] Leave readable traces such as wet footprints, dust, missing keys, wear marks, debris or spill patterns whose placement records what happened, so players reconstruct the event. When: Designing puzzles or spaces that tell a past event based on physical evidence. Why: Readable traces let players reconstruct what happened, and consistent placement lets them infer the sequence. (x2)
- [rule] Give environmental inscriptions and damage marks at least two plausible readings, so player interpretation changes a route, key or door and can be tested against other clues. When: Designing puzzles in a game level. Why: Interpretation then has consequences in play.
- [rule] Add physical evidence such as casings, holes, scorch marks, disarray, damp clothing or fresh tracks that can support or contradict an NPC's stated account or location. Why: Physical evidence gives players a check on what they are told.
- [rule] Place environmental triggers such as heat, light, or movement so that the player's elimination of them leads to the hidden cause. When: Designing an unexplained effect, such as a sigil that appears in a level. Why: Players can rule out visible conditions and reach the hidden trigger by elimination.
- [rule] Let a damaged journal or inscription show gaps, and accept any completion that fits both the visible letters and the surviving sense. When: Designing a text puzzle built from fragments. Why: Multiple valid completions let players reach the same conclusion by different routes.
- [rule] Support player deductions from odd behaviour, such as a locked door or a startled reaction, with clear environmental cues. When: Players must infer something from an NPC's unexplained action. Why: Clear cues let players reason to a checkable conclusion.
- [rule] Place mud, dust or sand where creatures move so footprint sizes and strides reveal how many moved a victim. When: Designing environments that record movement. Why: Printed traces convey count and role.
- [rule] Make the environment leave expected traces, such as wet footprints or dust, so their absence becomes a meaningful clue when the setting should have produced them. When: A scene where the player is expected to read tracks. Why: A missing trace only tells the player something if the world normally produces it.
- [rule] Place one unexplained action in a level, such as a door opened for no clear reason, as a designed clue that the player must interpret. When: Puzzle or mystery levels. Why: An unexplained action invites the player to form and test hypotheses.
- [rule] Make a trap mechanism discoverable from the object itself, so a careful player can notice it before triggering it. When: Designing a puzzle item with a hidden danger. Why: Fair discovery makes the harm a lesson rather than an arbitrary penalty.
- [rule] Place small environmental tells, such as a curtain out of place or a figure angled wrongly, that a careful player can notice. When: Building a staged scene that should hint at deception. Why: Subtle tells reward observant players and make staging discoverable.
- [rule] Offer a disguised survey of a location before the main puzzle, so the player learns the layout as a different character.
- [rule] Offer a staged-accident explanation that careful players can disprove by checking floor layouts and NPC positions. When: Designing a misleading accident in a fair mystery.
- [rule] Place disguise tells such as ill-fitting clothing or a spotted false feature in the environment for observant players to notice. When: When a level contains a disguised character. Why: Rewards observation with a clear, discoverable clue.
- [rule] Show footprints in mud, snow or dust, and let an object in the wrong spot reveal recent movement.
- [rule] Use countable features such as steps, doors or windows as puzzle keys, so a count that matches a clue identifies the right location.
- [rule] Make a prop's residue purpose discoverable from the game world, such as a workshop tool or lubricant, so players reason from it rather than guess. When: a prop carries residue that links to a puzzle.
- [rule] Make ambient sounds diagnostic: a lever click or a dropped object in an adjacent room should be reproducible by the player to test a hypothesis. When: designing environmental audio clues. Why: reproducible sounds let players test their reasoning.
- [rule] Let environmental objects carry meaning the player can work out from the room layout.
- [rule] Place environmental inconsistencies, such as light levels, patrol routes, or blood placement, so that only together do they point toward the real scene. When: Designing a crime scene the player must reconstruct. Why: Each clue alone is ambiguous, so the player must combine them.
- [heuristic] Expose variables like water depth, temperature or flow as readable numbers, so players reason whether an event could have occurred and can test conditions to isolate its cause. When: Designing an effect that varies with the environment. Why: Testing conditions lets players isolate the cause. (x2)
- [heuristic] A container with mechanical features such as chains or a hasp can be used to sink an object and retrieve it later, so its features hint at how the item was hidden. When: Assessing an object's function from its physical features. Why: Mechanical features reveal the intended method of hiding or recovery.
- [heuristic] Keep creature reactions consistent so players can reinterpret them when new information arrives.
- [heuristic] A hidden cache behind a wall can leave signs such as fresh mortar, texture mismatch or dust that a careful player can find. When: Designing concealed objects in walls or floors. Why: Recent alteration usually differs from the surrounding surface.
- [heuristic] Plant an object or unexplained sound early in a level so a later reveal feels fair to the player. Why: clues seen earlier make a reveal feel earned rather than arbitrary.
- [heuristic] Let players find disguise pieces in a shop or costume chest, so the impersonation becomes a discoverable trail they can follow. When: Designing a mystery where players must reconstruct a disguise. Why: Traceable supply sources let players infer the impersonation.
- [heuristic] In a locked-room puzzle, snow or mud tracks serve as a fixed record that narrows the possible escape routes. When: designing locked-room or sealed-location puzzles.
- [heuristic] Give weapons or tools a handling trace, such as grip wear or unusual prints, that reveals how the act was done.
- [heuristic] Environmental interruptions such as fog or a bell can delay a reveal and control its pacing.
- [heuristic] Plan leads that deliberately fade and return later, since a paused thread keeps suspense alive between active discoveries. When: When designing the sequence of leads in a mystery or quest chain. Why: Lulls between discoveries give tension a second wind when the thread returns.
- [heuristic] Place a single piece of furniture in front of a locked connecting door so that a careful player can discover the listening spot. When: Designing a level with adjoining empty rooms.
- [heuristic] Earthworks, old paths and worn ground can show how long a site was used and by whom, without any written record. When: Designing historic sites. Why: Physical wear communicates use.
- [procedure] 1) Collect the times of every check on a door. 2) Order them with the last sighting of the occupant. 3) Bound the period in which the door's state changed using the nearest checks on either side. When: When a door's state change must be dated. Why: Bounding the window between checks narrows when the change could have occurred.
- [procedure] 1) Mark each faction victim's body with that faction's symbol. 2) Add the symbol and its meaning to the player's journal lookup. 3) Let players match the symbol to the victim and group.
- [procedure] 1) Sort footprints by owner using size, sole pattern, patches and wear. 2) Eliminate candidate owners whose known prints do not match.
- [procedure] 1) Give a disguise a visible oddity that makes it seem trivial. 2) Let the odd trait be inconsistent in private. 3) Let attentive NPCs have a way to see through it. 4) Reveal the true role at a scripted story beat. When: Designing infiltration or stealth disguises. Why: Trivial appearance grants access, while the tell gives attentive observers a route to suspicion.
- [tradeoff] Disorder can signal haste or struggle, yet may be innocent once the room's purpose is known; the most dramatic reading is not always the real one. When: When interpreting debris, disturbance or scattered objects after an event: treat disorder as evidence of urgency, but check the room's purpose before concluding a struggle. Why: Disorder signals urgency and can point to what happened, but ordinary disorder may have an innocent explanation.
- [pitfall] Rain, trampling and routine use can blur or erase prints, or create false marks, so examine the scene quickly before evidence is lost. When: When evidence depends on weather, elapsed time or foot traffic. Why: Environmental change removes the traces an investigation needs.
- [fact] Dry, flaky snow can make it impossible to tell whether a line of prints was walked inward or outward, so the doubt should be recorded explicitly. When: reading direction from prints in dry, flaky snow. Why: The surface may not preserve the orientation of the walker's steps.
- [fact] The pattern of studs or prints pointing inward or outward shows the direction someone moved, so footprints can indicate entry or exit. When: Footprints are visible on a surface. Why: Print orientation records the direction of travel.

## ambush design
- [mechanism] Narrow passages with several doors opening onto them let anyone entering be intercepted from several sides, making them natural ambush points. Why: Multiple entrances converge on one path, limiting escape options.
- [mechanism] A trap where the enemy has already moved into position when the player enters a room makes the room feel deliberately staged. When: Designing encounters where the enemy is hidden or pre-positioned. Why: Prepositioned enemies reward players who anticipate what the room conceals.
- [mechanism] Waiting in concealment lets a target act on the bait, which reveals its intent without alerting it.
- [mechanism] A bounty notice can lure a culprit NPC, and a timetable-based NPC route can set up a controlled meeting or ambush. When: Designing NPC behaviour around rewards or schedules. Why: Rewards draw targets to predictable places at predictable times.
- [rule] Give an ambush encounter set positions, a covered exit and a wait timer, then start the confrontation with one clear trigger such as a sudden light. When: designing an ambush encounter. Why: a single trigger gives the encounter a clear start.
- [rule] Use a rehearsed signal and assigned positions as spawn and trigger conditions, so the ambush fires only when the planned sequence completes. When: Scripting an NPC ambush. Why: Tying triggers to sequence completion keeps the encounter coherent.
- [rule] Display a physical item and ask the NPC to explain it, so an unprepared response can reveal knowledge. When: Designing confrontation prompts in a mystery. Why: Unprepared reactions expose what the NPC knows.
- [rule] In a viewing puzzle, make the hidden feature vanish when players move, so they must record bearings at the viewing point. When: designing a telescope or viewing-point puzzle.
- [rule] Design a chase through rooms whose exits close one by one, and always keep at least one risky route open. When: when building an escape sequence. Why: a fully closed path leaves the player no real choice.
- [rule] Give each armed participant a firing condition tied to the other side's action, so no one fires early. When: an ambush has armed participants.
- [rule] Use a crowded doorway as a choke point where NPCs and the player slow each other down.
- [rule] In levels with an aerial scout, provide cover that places the player out of the scout's line of sight, so hiding is possible despite the height advantage. When: designing levels with overhead patrols. Why: a height advantage otherwise removes all safe positions.
- [rule] Do not stage an ambush where the designer cannot control the room's exits. Why: Uncontrolled exits let the target escape the trap.
- [rule] Let a branching hidden corridor bring a second party in from another branch, changing the encounter partway through.
- [rule] Treat the room layout as a fixed constraint and ask who could pass each choke point.
- [rule] Link a trap to a hidden compartment by a cord or similar connection so it fires when the player opens that compartment. When: when designing a trap that the player must trigger through exploration. Why: the connection makes the trigger an in-world action.
- [rule] Hide enemies near a meeting point and trigger reinforcements from both ends of a corridor at the same moment. When: Designing an ambush encounter. Why: Simultaneous approach from both sides cuts off the player's escape and raises tension.
- [rule] A meeting room that closes off the exit, with an early drink offer from the host, makes an ambush setup readable to the player.
- [rule] A chase can plant a partial glyph, dial or code fragment that players glimpse in passing and must recall later. When: designing clues in a fast-paced sequence. Why: Brief exposure during action rewards later recall.
- [rule] Let the player plant a second observer or wait at a likely destination, creating a scheduled ambush point in a surveillance system. When: when designing a tailing or surveillance mechanic.
- [rule] Choose a meeting point where all pursuers converge, so no one is separated from the group during a struggle. When: planning a multi-agent ambush or capture. Why: separated pursuers can be overpowered one at a time.
- [rule] Give enemy AI a trigger that checks whether the player has already been identified, so it can detect a counter-trap.
- [rule] Place an ambush in a dead-end area where the attacker vanishes quietly, so the player must search for other traces such as dropped items or footprints. When: When designing an ambush encounter in a generated level. Why: A quiet escape removes the obvious trail and forces the player to look for secondary evidence.
- [rule] Build an ambush that is itself a staged event the player can reread, so a trap that looks like a performance rewards careful observation. When: designing a combat encounter. Why: A rereadable trap connects the threat to a clue the player already saw.
- [heuristic] Station a watcher at a chokepoint that every route to the area or target must cross, so one post covers all approaches. When: when one passage or point is crossed by every route to the area or target. Why: All movement in or out must pass that point.
- [heuristic] Add a countdown that forces the player to act before time runs out, so that pressure to reveal or accuse builds. When: Designing a mystery or accusation scene in a generated level. Why: A deadline turns a slow investigation into a decision under pressure.
- [heuristic] End a stealth segment with a surprise NPC encounter so the player's next action responds to a changed situation. When: designing stealth sequences. Why: A changed situation forces an immediate response and sustains tension.
- [heuristic] Set detection odds so that a clever antagonist leaves few traces and a careless one leaves obvious ones, shaping player expectations about how hard the culprit is to catch. When: Designing antagonist evidence trails. Why: Trace density signals the antagonist's skill to the player.
- [heuristic] Arrange encounters in a ladder where each hazard is more severe than the last, separated by a short safe room, with the next threat hinted in the preceding room. When: designing a sequence of hazards in a dungeon.
- [heuristic] Use a controlled encounter, with a lure and a single prepared person in a private room, when direct questioning yields only denials. When: the target refuses to speak openly. Why: A staged setting can make the target speak more freely.
- [heuristic] Stage an ambush in a graveyard or ruin by showing the enemy at a distance and identifying it only when close.
- [heuristic] End an encounter on an unanswered question and deliver the answer in a later area or conversation.
- [heuristic] A timed public event, such as a crowd absorbed in a spectacle, marks a window in which the player can act without being noticed.
- [heuristic] Do not assume the target's route; keep alternative directions covered until the target actually moves. When: when the target could leave in more than one direction. Why: Taking the direction for granted can leave the ambush on the wrong path.
- [heuristic] An eerie event can be built as a human plan using a trained creature, letting the player move from fear to a solvable cause. When: When designing a mysterious event for a game. Why: A plausible cause turns dread into a puzzle the player can solve.
- [procedure] 1) Stage backup in a nearby empty space well before the event, out of the target's notice. 2) Give it a set waiting time. 3) Have it enter only after the target's specific action or a hidden signal. When: When hidden reinforcements or ambushers should respond to a trigger in a stealth scene. Why: A fixed delay stops early action, and triggering on the target's action keeps intervention responsive to play.
- [procedure] 1) Choose a stimulus that is safe to trigger, such as a noise, draft or light. 2) Apply it at the suspected hiding place. 3) Let the hidden occupant leave on their own. 4) Avoid forcing every wall to be searched. When: A hidden character or item is suspected in a concealed space. Why: A safe stimulus draws out the occupant with less cost than a forced search.
- [procedure] 1) Place ambient cues such as sounds, moving shadows and a nervous NPC. 2) Add a false alarm. 3) Reveal the real threat after a timed delay.
- [procedure] 1) Lock the room's other exits and verify each one in person. 2) Hide watchers in an adjacent space, leaving a deliberate opening toward them. 3) Wait for the target to reach a set spot at a set time. 4) Trigger the ambush. When: The target is expected to act at a known time and place.
- [procedure] 1) Predict the channel a hidden enemy would use to reach the target. 2) Place a trusted intermediary there, visibly and at a predictable time. 3) Watch who approaches and record the contact and its terms. When: A hidden enemy is isolating a target from allies. Why: Watching the channel exposes the enemy's approach and aims.
- [procedure] 1) Isolate the opportunity the suspect needs, such as an empty room, a locked door or a moved NPC. 2) Give helpers precise instructions. 3) Wait out of sight. 4) Watch which NPC reacts. When: A suspect's action depends on an opportunity the investigator can control. Why: Controlled conditions make the suspect's response observable and attributable.
- [procedure] 1) Place the false lead in a channel the target reads. 2) Position a concealed watch where the target would act on the false belief. 3) Observe whether the target acts. When: When the target will act on a belief that appears safe. Why: Setting a trap at the expected action point turns the false belief into observable behaviour.
- [procedure] 1) List each enemy's known habits and codes. 2) Identify the response each habit predicts. 3) Design the encounter so those predicted responses lead into the trap. When: Scripting an ambush that depends on enemy behaviour.
- [procedure] 1) Place a decoy that draws the enemy's attention. 2) Wait for the enemy to commit its shot at the decoy. 3) Spring the ambush on the enemy from concealment.
- [tradeoff] Letting a suspect escape at a planned moment can be a designed branch with recorded consequences, at the cost of losing the immediate resolution. When: A suspect may flee before the accusation. Why: The branch adds consequence and replay value but delays closure.

## staged reveals and set pieces
- [definition] A staged reveal shows a scene in parts over time rather than at once, using fog, a winding route or a gate to control what is visible. When: the reveal should build gradually.
- [mechanism] Landscape, weather, season and architecture set a mood that shapes how players read events, building dread or suspicion before facts are known. When: at the start of a case when facts are few, or when designing the atmosphere of a crime scenario. Why: Mood influences expectations, which affect how players read ambiguous events.
- [mechanism] A scripted NPC action can expose a hidden chamber, so the player learns its location before deciding whether to enter.
- [mechanism] A forced lock is suspicious when a simpler entry route, such as a key, would be available, because forcing it then serves to display a break-in rather than achieve one. When: Evaluating visible damage to a lock or container. Why: A rational actor with easy access gains nothing from a noisy method.
- [mechanism] A staged collapse can reverse the apparent roles of attacker and victim, so a 'dead' actor may still be armed and the one who approaches to help may be the target. When: A scene presents a fallen character as a victim and a nearby character as the culprit. Why: A feigned collapse exploits the watcher's expectation that a motionless body is harmless and that a helper is safe.
- [rule] Stage the final reveal inside a location the player has already visited, such as the crime scene, a trap or stakeout, so they can return to the evidence. Why: Familiar space makes the climax readable and ties back earlier exploration.
- [rule] Include one prop in a staged scene that contradicts its stated purpose, so attentive players can spot the staging without it being spelled out. When: designing a fake robbery, planted camp or similar staged set-piece. Why: players who check purpose against details can detect the deception.
- [rule] Stage a search or excavation with empty results, a disturbance, and partial finds before the main reward, and defer the puzzle explanation until after the reward is seen. When: Designing an excavation or hidden-vault sequence. Why: Staged finds raise expectation and make the eventual solution feel earned.
- [rule] Show the public performance and the private reality side by side so attentive players can compare them. When: Designing contrasting behavior for the player to notice. Why: Direct comparison makes the discrepancy visible to attentive players.
- [rule] Release information in stages, so each partial answer narrows the search without solving it.
- [rule] Give shocking set pieces an in-world explanation that clears them while still rewarding curiosity.
- [rule] Let players inspect items in stages, with hidden details appearing only under magnification or at close range. When: Designing examinable clue objects in a game world. Why: Progressive disclosure rewards careful inspection and keeps casual players from solving the clue instantly.
- [rule] Stage disturbances such as a broken window or spilled goods to lead guards and players along a chosen path. Why: Visible disruptions give players and guards a readable direction to follow.
- [rule] Make empty areas risky: a lone character should be more visible to patrols, so solitude carries a cost rather than offering safety. When: designing quiet or empty zones in a level. Why: patrols notice isolated characters more readily than crowds.
- [rule] Plant a staged battle scene in which a bound prisoner's knots or a tool mark reveal the truth to a careful player. When: When designing a staged combat scene as a misdirection. Why: A physical detail rewards players who examine the scene closely.
- [rule] Give the antagonist a hidden escape route, such as a locked gate with a concealed mechanism, so a trap has a second phase after it springs. When: designing a confrontation that ends in a trap. Why: a second phase keeps the climax from ending too early.
- [rule] Give a scripted scare a checkable tell, such as residue on a costumed character's hand, so a careful player can expose the staging. When: designing a fake supernatural event.
- [rule] Before a final reveal, give the player a short confirmation step, such as checking a route or record, so the answer rests on facts they have verified. When: Designing the approach to a puzzle's final solution. Why: Verified facts make the eventual conclusion feel credible rather than arbitrary.
- [rule] Let the player deduce the culprit from how a scene was staged, using several clues, rather than from a single item. When: Designing a deduction puzzle around a crime scene. Why: Multiple converging clues make deduction feel fair and reduce guesswork.
- [rule] Release exiting groups in sequence so each area empties before the next is searched.
- [rule] Let a journal entry or cutscene give the player the answer while the detective NPC still searches, then return control to the player at the NPC's point of confusion. When: Creating dramatic irony in a game. Why: The player acts on knowledge the NPC lacks, which creates suspense.
- [heuristic] Make a staged room detectable through inconsistent details, such as a made bed lacking sleeper's habits or uncut pages and unused objects, rewarding observant players. When: when designing environmental clues in a mystery level, or making a room feel staged or lived in. Why: Inconsistent details reward observant players; detail states communicate how the space is used.
- [heuristic] End a level section at the moment of recognition, so the reveal becomes a cliffhanger.
- [heuristic] Visible marks of an unseen past add atmosphere and suggest that a clue may mean more than it appears. When: Designing a setting with history. Why: Hints of history invite players to look closer.
- [heuristic] Let players infer planned traps from setup clues, such as a guard swapped at a set time or a tool kept in an unusual place. When: designing a trap or puzzle. Why: visible preparation rewards players who notice it.
- [heuristic] Pair a serene location with a hidden danger so the setting itself gives the player a hint that the calm is misleading. When: When designing a peaceful area that hides a threat. Why: A mismatch between appearance and history prompts the player to look closer.
- [heuristic] Use staged scenes, such as a notice or a board, to give players a sense of place and show which details matter to a character. When: Designing scenes that carry memory cues. Why: Environmental detail signals relevance without explicit exposition.
- [heuristic] A level can carry a secondary map, such as a surveyor's record, showing a changed feature; hide the difference so careful comparison reveals it. When: when designing a discoverable change to a location. Why: careful players are rewarded for comparing sources.
- [heuristic] Separate level sections so that a player's theory about one area is disproved when they reach the next.
- [heuristic] A schematic map shows routes with conventional symbols that omit exact positions and minor detail, so players must learn precise paths through observation. When: when designing a simplified map. Why: symbols give a guide, not a complete record.
- [heuristic] Stagger reveals across rooms and use ambient sounds or clock objects as timers to pace the player's progress. When: Designing the pacing of a multi-room area.
- [heuristic] Arrange objects so that a forced grip or misplaced item signals a staged scene, prompting the player to question the setup.
- [heuristic] A recorded admission triggered by a hidden event can serve as a reversal in a multi-stage investigation. When: Structuring a multi-stage mystery. Why: A late reversal rewards players who followed the earlier clues.
- [heuristic] A final set-piece room can gather NPCs under a timer with an evidence display, and lighting or a decisive player action can trigger the reveal. When: Designing a finale encounter. Why: Environmental timing and staging concentrate tension.
- [heuristic] Show the final clue last and summarize the rest, giving the player a satisfying payoff. When: Structuring the reveal of a mystery. Why: Withholding the decisive clue preserves tension until the end.
- [heuristic] Show a first answer early, then reveal the true location after a later region or document unlocks, so the first answer becomes a stage.
- [procedure] 1) Start with a bright public area. 2) Move into dark, narrow lanes. 3) Cross a hazardous passage. 4) Reach the hideout. Pace the reveal through these spaces. When: When designing a visit to a dangerous location. Why: Each step increases tension and reveals more.
- [procedure] 1) Agree a signal in advance that the target will trigger. 2) Place a hazardous object where the target will be seen handling it. 3) Position waiting responders to move on the signal. 4) Warn responders to handle the object carefully, since it may later be evidence. When: The target will act at a known place and time. Why: The signal tells responders when to move and the object becomes evidence.
- [procedure] 1) Order the locations from small to large and central. 2) Assign each location one new kind of evidence. 3) Let the picture grow in layers as the player moves through them. When: Evidence is revealed gradually across several spaces. Why: Layered reveals build understanding while keeping information withheld.
- [procedure] 1) Summon the culprit for a set time. 2) Lock the room. 3) Lay out the evidence together. 4) Allow the culprit to flee, turning the escape into a tactical moment.
- [pitfall] A single tone can mislead, so repeated strikes in a pattern are needed before trusting a reading.
- [pitfall] A garden's appearance reflects the season as well as care, so judging neglect from an empty-looking lawn is unreliable. Why: Season and weather change how grounds look.

## systematic search
- [mechanism] A visual sweep does not test a surface's structure, so items hidden inside walls survive it and need a targeted, structural check.
- [mechanism] One observable condition can rule out most candidate locations on a map, shrinking a radius-based search dramatically. When: a search is bounded by travel distance from a point. Why: a condition implying limited travel excludes far locations at once.
- [behavior] A person whose tool or pipe is blocked will search for a replacement, drawing them toward where that replacement is likely kept. When: a habitual need is blocked or missing. Why: an unmet need drives search behaviour.
- [rule] Examine items closely and cover walls, floor and ceiling systematically rather than relying on a quick glance that finds everything at once. (x2)
- [rule] Search a room's corners and directly behind and beneath furniture, since a quick glance misses small traces and hidden compartments there.
- [rule] Search padded or lined objects and their seams by touch as well as sight, since a surface-only look misses linings and a trained hand can find what the eye misses. When: An object could hold a concealed item.
- [rule] Search interiors room by room, moving furniture, opening cupboards and drawing back hangings, working from upper floors down to the cellars.
- [rule] Hide traces such as a scrubbed patch under a rug, and reward players who check beneath movable objects.
- [rule] Give a player search action per-room completion flags, with an optional companion NPC who rechecks finished rooms.
- [rule] Plan a level as a sequence of rooms, each unlocking one evidence type, with a timed event at the end of each act. When: Structuring an investigation level. Why: Each room adds a layer and the timed event sustains pressure.
- [rule] Let a target's possible locations widen after a set number of in-game days, expanding the area the player must search. When: designing a missing-person or fleeing-target search. Why: elapsed time makes the target harder to find in a fixed area.
- [heuristic] A room can be searched while its owner is away for a set time, forming a timed window whose outcome stays open; an empty result still counts as a step. When: Looking for traces of a suspect who is out for a set period, or designing a room search with a suspect away.
- [heuristic] Gate each search step with timed events, such as a train departure or deadline, and sort collected leads by priority so players weigh them by urgency. When: Designing a time-limited investigation or a search in a mystery level. Why: A deadline forces the player to prioritise checks, and time pressure with priority ranking reflects how investigators triage leads.
- [heuristic] Let walls or floors give hollow or responsive sounds when struck, so a player's test of a surface can reveal a concealed space. When: Designing hidden areas that players might search by probing. Why: Audible feedback rewards testing and hints at hollow spaces.
- [heuristic] A fixed-order room search suits answers hidden among many small containers in one enclosed space. When: The target may sit in one of many small containers in an enclosed room. Why: Systematic order ensures no container is skipped.
- [procedure] 1) Walk from object to object. 2) Inspect containers such as basins and dishes. 3) Check gaps, folds, and cracks. 4) Bring small items close and examine them up close. When: Searching a room for traces that may be small. Why: Small traces collect in ordinary containers and gaps that a casual look skips.
- [procedure] 1) Empty drawers and closets. 2) Move furniture to check beneath and behind it. 3) Log each object in order with where it was found.
- [procedure] 1) List the places the owner says they already searched. 2) Search systematically in places outside that list, including rarely used containers and clothing. 3) Pair results with when and why the item was last used. When: An item is reported missing. Why: Repeating the owner's search repeats the owner's blind spots.
- [procedure] 1) Keep the room exactly as found. 2) Post a lookout on the occupant. 3) Go through luggage and papers. 4) Arrange questioning so the occupant is unaware of the search. When: the occupant may return during a discreet search. Why: A fixed order protects the search from discovery.
- [procedure] 1) Separate the suspect from others present. 2) Keep the room in view while searching. 3) Examine each garment piece by piece with hands as well as eyes. 4) If nothing is found, redirect suspicion to the next likely holder. When: when a small concealed item must be found quickly. Why: Systematic search finds hidden items and avoids missed areas.
- [procedure] 1) List the named target objects. 2) Search the likely surfaces first, such as writing tables. 3) Move to other rooms in sequence. 4) Check pockets of hanging clothing. 5) Record what is found and what is absent. When: When searching a room for specific physical evidence. Why: Expectation guides the search and an ordered sequence avoids gaps.
- [procedure] 1) Predict how the item was concealed. 2) List plausible locations. 3) Check each in order and record results. 4) Observe the found target before moving in. When: when a missing object is among many similar possible locations. Why: An ordered, recorded search avoids repeats and covers every candidate.
- [procedure] 1) Check the obvious spots, such as drawers. 2) Check the room's ordinary structure, such as floor boards and pipe access points. When: Searching a room thoroughly. Why: Searches limited to obvious spots miss hiding places built into routine structure.
- [procedure] 1) Count the barriers between the character and the goal, in order. 2) Note routine visit or patrol times. 3) Read sounds and sights for unseen geography. 4) Infer hidden routes from unexplained movement. When: planning an approach before acting. Why: A survey turns a closed space into a plannable route.
- [procedure] 1) Fix a reference object and note its position. 2) Sweep surrounding surfaces at low height, then raised overhead. 3) Repeat the sweep around the reference. 4) Split heights between searchers if reach is limited. 5) Test each element for looseness. When: a hidden object is believed to be in a confined space with a known reference point. Why: Height-separated sweeps cover surfaces one searcher cannot reach.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the likely approach path from the layout. 2) Sweep rough ground in parallel lanes. 3) Search dense cover such as vegetation along the inferred path. When: searching for a lost object. Why: a structured sweep finds items a casual look misses.
- [procedure] 1) Divide the scene into zones and search them in a fixed sequence. 2) Mark each find at its exact position. 3) Move to the next zone only when the current one is complete. When: a scene is large or has several areas that may hold evidence. Why: a fixed order and recorded positions prevent missed areas and preserve find locations.
- [procedure] 1) Survey each object in turn and form a clear picture of it before moving on. 2) Examine the floor with a lens, eyes close to the surface. 3) Record every faint mark found. When: when a room may have held an object that has since been moved. Why: objects are easier to test against traces when each one is understood first.
- [procedure] 1) Search every closet and waste space in order, starting at the lowest level. 2) Move upward through the building. 3) Do not skip spaces that look empty. 4) Check rooms unused for years and test whether they are locked. When: searching a building for concealed evidence. Why: Hidden compartments often sit in spaces that look unremarkable.
- [procedure] 1) Finish one area before starting the next, without going back. 2) Open every container, locked or unlocked. 3) Test soft objects by feel and sound for hidden items. 4) Check room structure: under rugs and furniture, inside blinds, window ledges. 5) Check unusual spots such as jars, atomizers, drains and cisterns. 6) Record negative results. When: A small object may be hidden in a confined space and the searcher has time and a plausible reason to be there.
- [procedure] 1) Start in the place the person was last known. 2) Search adjacent spaces in a fixed order, moving outward. 3) Check each candidate hiding spot. 4) Check exits last. When: when a person has gone missing from a known place. Why: A systematic order covers the likeliest spots first.
- [procedure] 1) Divide the room into its walls. 2) Search one wall, then its opposite, covering all furniture on each. 3) Open every container, including drawers, bowls and spaces under cushions. 4) Examine objects closely, checking each card and shaking books. 5) Reach high places with a ladder. 6) Mark empty containers as checked before moving on. When: Searching an enclosed room for a hidden item. Why: A fixed order ensures no container, surface or object is skipped.
- [procedure] 1) Decide whether the player searches for an NPC or an object. 2) If searching for a corpse or hidden item, open containers and under-furniture spots. 3) If searching for a person, check rooms and visible spots. When: when designing a search action in a game. Why: the search method must match the target to be fair.
- [tradeoff] Systematic sweeps find items hidden in containers, while a thorough search can still miss items in plain sight, so a failed search does not prove the item is gone. Why: Full coverage removes the advantage of plain sight.
- [tradeoff] A thorough search costs time, which rewards careful players while penalizing hurried ones. When: Giving search actions a time cost.
- [pitfall] A search that tests only surfaces or easy-to-reach spots can miss small or hidden evidence, so an empty result may reflect the method rather than the evidence's absence. Why: Surface searches miss small or hidden evidence.
- [pitfall] Searching by genre tropes can waste time when the real release is a simple mechanism.
- [pitfall] Searchers tire, so late searches are the least careful unless the work is kept under time pressure.
- [pitfall] A single visible trace is enough to redirect an entire search toward one spot.
- [pitfall] A quiet search is not unobserved, because a sound made during it can draw a checker to investigate.

## ciphers and signals
- [mechanism] A prearranged signal expected on fixed days can be set to mean trouble when it fails to arrive, so its absence becomes the trigger for action. When: A plan depends on a routine message that should arrive on schedule. Why: A missing expected event is treated as evidence that the prearranged condition has occurred.
- [mechanism] A pre-agreed call among characters announces an incoming threat to anyone who knows the code, and can warn that an ambush is starting.
- [mechanism] A fragment placed at a second scene links it to the first, so the player connects two events through a shared physical object.
- [behavior] A short phrase with two senses can mislead a listener, since the speaker may expect the intended sense while the listener picks the other. When: wording admits two meanings. Why: listeners resolve ambiguity with their own reading.
- [behavior] A writer may argue with themselves in a double sense, so apparent honesty and evasion can sit side by side in one text. When: Interpreting a text that both concedes and evades. Why: The reader must decide from context which reading holds.
- [rule] Offer dialogue options with an innocent and a plan-revealing double meaning; players signal knowledge without revealing it, observe the NPC's response, and their follow-up decides which reading surfaces. When: Designing conversations with suspects or informants. (x2)
- [rule] Write a single clue, such as a torn letter, ledger entry or symbol, to support two plausible interpretations, so players must use other clues to choose. When: designing ambiguous evidence.
- [rule] Record the same events through two observers, one keeping a main log and another adding brief notes, so each event is seen twice. Why: a single narrator's word is easy to doubt.
- [rule] Agree a visual code in advance so a signal can confirm a sender's identity and safety without speech. When: when characters cannot safely talk.
- [rule] Arrange a backup arrival in advance so it can be announced as a surprise at the right moment; an NPC's arrival can serve as a staged reveal.
- [rule] Split a clue across two copies of a document so the answer appears only when the copies are compared. When: Hiding a passage or code. Why: Comparison reveals what each copy hides.
- [rule] Let one NPC appear as an apparently independent second NPC, revealed later as the same actor, so one character fills two roles.
- [rule] Hold two working theories in a quest log, each listing its supporting evidence, until one is confirmed. When: Designing investigation journals or quest logs. Why: Parallel theories let players reason about ambiguous evidence without committing early.
- [rule] Agree a signal in advance so a trusted insider can summon help without delay.
- [rule] Store two readings for each clue, attaching the favoured reading to the faction that holds it, so players can challenge the bias. When: clues feed factional beliefs. Why: competing readings let players contest a shared conclusion.
- [rule] Encode short messages in a level's symbol set, reuse a known name as a crib, and anchor at least one message in context to keep it fair. When: designing solvable puzzles. Why: a known anchor lets careful players succeed.
- [rule] Plant the answer to a puzzle lock in an earlier area so the player has a fair route to it.
- [rule] Build two plausible readings into a dying fragment; the wrong reading should lead to a dead-end area or an innocent suspect. When: Designing a clue from a dying character. Why: Ambiguity makes the player weigh evidence instead of accepting the first reading.
- [rule] Create torn notes with glyph fragments that combine into several candidate words, and accept only the one matching the speaker's known vocabulary. When: Designing a document puzzle in a game. Why: Vocabulary matching makes one reading correct and others checkable.
- [rule] Use an agreed signal, such as a whistle, to announce a brief departure, and have the partner answer to confirm.
- [rule] Agree marker signals, such as chalk marks, in advance so a follower who has fallen behind can recover the route.
- [rule] Give an on-site observer a prearranged signal that every team member understands, so the whole group acts at the same moment. When: when team members cannot see the target directly. Why: Without a shared signal, the group cannot coordinate timing of the move.
- [heuristic] Give each ambiguous suspect or NPC action or reaction a benign and a suspicious reading, with a later event revealing the true one so players reinterpret earlier behavior. When: Designing suspect or NPC behaviour that supports a mystery. Why: Players then reinterpret earlier actions when the truth arrives, and must test which reading holds.
- [heuristic] Read an ambiguous riddle word in the sense used by the specialist group that would use it, such as sailors using a word for a telescope.
- [heuristic] Give each suspicious element two explanations, a sensible cover and a true cause, so the audience must choose which one to test. When: Designing a hidden element that should not be resolved instantly. Why: Two candidate explanations keep the investigation active instead of closing it with the first plausible answer.
- [heuristic] Build two evidence tracks, one from motive and one from the scene, so the player must find where they agree before concluding. When: Designing investigation structure. Why: Convergence of independent tracks makes the answer earned rather than assumed.
- [heuristic] Test each phrase in a written message against both an innocent and a sinister reading before judging it. When: a written clue could be harmless or incriminating. Why: A phrase can carry either meaning.
- [heuristic] If the cipher was agreed in advance, test the parties to the agreement rather than who could have observed the situation. When: a code was arranged before the events. Why: a prior agreement explains the message without close observation.
- [heuristic] A puzzle room can require the player to check every gap, vent and transom before concluding that a route is sealed.
- [heuristic] A haunted-room puzzle can hide a trick behind mirrors or sliding panels, leaving clues like a sound or sightline that an attentive player can use to reconstruct it.
- [heuristic] Build a puzzle from several partial fragments, each ambiguous alone but jointly solvable, such as an overheard term, a letter key and numbers.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can offer a plausible false sequence built from partial clues, rewarding players who test it against physical evidence.
- [procedure] A double book cipher encodes text as page and word-position numbers. 1) Both parties obtain the same edition of an agreed book. 2) The sender opens it at random and writes the page number. 3) Each word is written as its position on that page, counted from the top. 4) The receiver reads the words at those positions. When: Two correspondents share a secret book and the sender can choose a new page per message. Why: Random page choice and word positions hide the text without a separate code table.
- [procedure] 1) State the impossible claim clearly. 2) List every plausible rational explanation. 3) Give the player a test or evidence to check each explanation. 4) Let the test eliminate options until one remains or the question stays open. When: Designing a mystery with an apparently impossible event. Why: Testable alternatives let the player reach a conclusion through reasoning rather than accepting a declared miracle.
- [pitfall] Secrecy at both ends of a case compounds confusion, since an unknown client and an unknown intermediary leave too little to test.
- [pitfall] A second correction written over the first can be a deliberate attempt to make the alterations seem to cancel out, so a forger expecting one check may be hiding a second. When: A document has more than one overlapping correction. Why: Observers often look for a single alteration and stop.
- [pitfall] A puzzle answer built from a guessable pattern, such as a date or a name from the world, lets players skip the puzzle even when the mechanism itself is strong.
- [fact] A cipher that depends on a prearranged rule can be solved only by knowing that rule or by finding the pattern within the text.

## trap design
- [model] A timed sting has five parts: a hidden listener, a concealed response team, a visible trigger cue, a decoy who stalls the target, and a standby exit with open comms. When: Designing a covert ambush or sting event. Why: Each part supplies one need: information, surprise, timing, delay or escape.
