- [mechanism] A prepared object catches only a suspect who touches it; one who avoids contact leaves no trace, so a trap's proof comes from the target's own act. When: The trap relies on the target acting freely. Why: The target, not the investigator, controls the decisive action.
- [mechanism] A marker placed on a hiding place, such as a hair on a drawer lip, moves or falls when the place is opened, signalling that it was searched. When: A hidden object must be guarded and the owner needs to know whether it was touched. Why: The disturbed marker acts as a silent alarm that needs no witness.
- [mechanism] A trap that fires when triggered can signal the trapper, who waits nearby and times a return to the sound of the trigger.
- [mechanism] A light-sensitive trigger hidden behind ordinary objects completes a circuit when light reaches it, sending a signal to a remote point the target cannot see. When: a confrontation is staged in a room the target controls. Why: concealment keeps the target from knowing the signal exists.
- [mechanism] A hazard disguised as an accident depends on the victim's expected mistake, so a small out-of-place container or item near it can reveal the true intent. When: when designing a trap that looks accidental. Why: Disguises rarely remove every trace; the leftover anomaly lets careful players detect the hazard.
- [rule] Give a trap a clear trigger condition so the player knows what they have set in motion. When: Designing player-triggered traps or ambushes. Why: Players can plan only when the outcome of their setup is legible.
- [rule] Place a watcher or trigger at the expected exit so the player's observation of the reaction advances the plot.
- [rule] Let a trap or elevator be set in one room while its effect appears in another, so the timing of that effect becomes the evidence. Why: Separating cause and effect in space and time creates a deducible puzzle.
- [rule] Place trip-wire alarms on routes into a base, and let the player detect and avoid them through careful movement. When: A base has known approach routes. Why: Alarms that ring on contact reward a defender who knows the approaches.
- [rule] Link a trap's trigger to an everyday object that players are tempted to reposition. When: When designing traps in rooms players will search and handle.
- [rule] A trap room that leaves a body behind can be designed so the player's exit looks suspicious to NPCs who did not see the entry.
- [rule] Place a trigger inside an ordinary object and route its wiring along walls, so the target neither notices the components nor trips over the wire. When: a hidden trap or alarm must stay undiscovered. Why: discovery or accidental disturbance makes the trap fail.
- [rule] Stage a supposedly cleared suspect's final confrontation in a room the player has already explored, as a trap. When: when planning a late-game confrontation. Why: Familiar space makes the trap feel fair and lets the player recall earlier clues.
- [rule] Structure the final confrontation as a trap sequence, such as a locked door and smoke, that forces the culprit to confess. When: when staging a climactic confrontation. Why: Physical pressure leaves the culprit few options except a confession.
- [heuristic] Give a hidden or lethal hazard a subtle visible tell, such as a misplaced item, an exterior flaw, or residue on a lamp shield, so careful inspection can reveal it. When: Designing hazards that should be fair to careful players, including in closed rooms. Why: A visible tell rewards careful inspection and keeps the hazard from feeling arbitrary. (x2)
- [heuristic] Create a sealed room with a timed hazard so the player must make decisions under pressure, and count down the shrinking space to make the threat visible. When: Designing a trap or escape encounter that needs urgency. Why: A visible countdown turns an abstract timer into concrete tension.
- [heuristic] Time a trap to the moment an earlier event is believed to have occurred, so the expected event becomes the trigger.
- [heuristic] If a lure is suspected, letting the target leave on it can remove them from the scene while you prepare a counter. When: When you expect the lure to be followed by an attack.
- [heuristic] An ambush prepared by an opponent can be turned against it when the defender anticipates it and surrounds the waiting attackers. When: When the defender knows the opponent is setting an ambush. Why: The opponent's waiting becomes a trap for the opponent.
- [heuristic] A trap built on a belief about how a watcher behaves works only if that belief holds. Test the belief before committing resources, because a false one leaves the trap empty. When: Designing a trap or ambush for an observing agent. Why: The trap's outcome depends entirely on the single assumption it rests on.
- [heuristic] Show a trap's components as ordinary decoration, such as inscriptions or altar fixtures, so careful inspection lets players deduce the mechanism early.
- [heuristic] Send the player to a location as a decoy, and trigger a trap, such as gas or a shock, through a signal the player can hear or see. When: Designing an ambush or trap encounter. Why: The player feels the trap was set by a clue they could have noticed.
- [procedure] 1) Hold an independently verified timeline. 2) Ask about a detail the target could only know by being present. 3) Compare the target's answer, including any substitute detail, against the timeline. When: A target claims to have been somewhere or seen something, and dates or times can be checked.
- [procedure] 1) Approach slowly. 2) Operate a suspected switch with an improvised tool. 3) Scan the room for signs of danger. 4) Examine common everyday objects first. When: When an enemy has had access to a room. Why: An improvised tool keeps the hand off possible hazards, and everyday objects are the ones people are most likely to touch.
- [procedure] 1) Stretch fragile traces, such as threads, across the room at the height and route a visitor would cross. 2) Place a marker on a door or lock. 3) Check the traces and markers afterward. 4) Compare disturbances with the expected routine to confirm an entry and its route. When: When you need proof that a room was entered and the route of entry matters. Why: Traces disturbed in an unexpected pattern reveal movement that bypassed the door.
- [procedure] 1) List every candidate container. 2) Order them from oldest to most recent. 3) Inspect each in turn, checking for recent handling. 4) Do not guess which one matters. When: An investigator must search many similar containers for hidden evidence. Why: Methodical ordering prevents missed containers and avoids guesswork.
- [procedure] 1) Build a trap with a visible flaw in its mechanism. 2) Let a careful player notice the flaw. 3) Make the confession or later dialogue reveal the trap's concealed purpose. When: Designing a trap that serves as a murder disguise and a clue. Why: A visible flaw rewards observation and lets the reveal be checked against the environment.
- [pitfall] A lure that a careful reader can see through fails, so the message must hold up under scrutiny. When: when writing a coded notice or false lead. Why: suspects who examine the message will recognise the trick.
- [pitfall] A disturbed trap proves that someone touched the hiding place, but it does not reveal who did it. When: A trap has been triggered and a suspect is being named. Why: The trap records the event, not the identity of the person who caused it.
- [pitfall] A trap whose timing depends on uncertain transit can fail when delays, such as weather, upset the schedule. When: the target's arrival time is set by travel.
- [pitfall] A decoy that never moves gives itself away as fake; movement is needed to keep the bait believable.
- [pitfall] A signaller may be left stranded and hesitate about entering danger if the expected follow-up never appears. Why: no instructions cover the case where the expected event does not happen.
- [pitfall] Entering a venue whose activity holds you in place can trap a follower, who then becomes the one pursued. When: Following a subject into a shop or room that requires participation. Why: Required participation keeps the follower stationary while the subject moves on.
- [pitfall] Moving or handling a concealing object can set off a hidden trigger by accident, so its placement must be checked before the trap is relied on.
- [pitfall] A hidden trigger depends on its power source and signal timing, so a failure in either silently disables the whole response.

## contradictory physical evidence
- [rule] Place an item or independent record, such as a receipt or gate log, that contradicts an NPC's testimony or stated story, so checking it exposes the lie. When: Designing clues that test an NPC's testimony or a character's claims. Why: Physical items and records are harder to rehearse away than speech; discovering the contradiction rewards attention. (x2)
- [rule] Keep a clue that contradicts the obvious answer, such as clean cut debris far from its source, so checking players can reject the decoy. When: Building a fair mystery where the decoy can be disproven. Why: Fair clues let observant players reach the truth.
- [rule] When a new observation or custody account contradicts a prediction or working theory, revise the hypothesis rather than treating it as an investigation failure.
- [rule] Use a mismatch between a room's disorder and its contents as a designed clue that the apparent motive is false. Why: valuables left behind contradict a theft reading for careful players.
- [rule] Give a wrong suspect a plausible motive, a missing object and a suspicious manner, and include enough contrary evidence that a player can detect the flaw. When: when designing a red herring NPC case. Why: a fair red herring rewards correction without giving away the true solution.
- [rule] When a physical state contradicts a reported event, such as a door found unfastened after being reported locked, explain the contradiction before accepting either account. Why: Either the report or the physical evidence is wrong, and the contradiction points to where the story fails.
- [rule] Build a sighting that conflicts with expectations, such as a child where an adult was expected, as a clue pointing to the true occupant.
- [rule] Confirm that a withdrawing party has actually left before continuing, so no one can reverse the decision and reclaim the scene.
- [rule] Design clues so rival explanations predict different observable results, letting players test and separate them. When: designing mysteries with several plausible causes. Why: distinct predictions allow evidence to decide.
- [rule] Provide two or three sources that can be cross-checked, so players must reconcile conflicting records to proceed. When: When designing investigation puzzles with record sources. Why: Requiring reconciliation tests player reasoning rather than rewarding a single lookup.
- [rule] Include a scene object that contradicts a naive reading, such as a neatly placed container, to prompt players to revise their first explanation. When: designing a crime or event scene. Why: a contradicting detail rewards players who question the obvious story.
- [rule] In a staged room, place a small trace that contradicts the staging, such as a half-eaten dish or unexpected light, that careful players can match against a character's habit. When: Designing a room whose arrangement should be questioned. Why: Small contradictions reward observant players without making the staging obvious.
- [rule] Place traces such as drag marks or blood pools that are consistent with the action that supposedly occurred in the scene. Why: Consistent traces make the scene's story checkable by the player.
- [rule] Place dated objects so the player can find one whose date contradicts an NPC's testimony, giving them a lead to pursue.
- [heuristic] When pieces refuse to fit one attractive theory, examine the misfits before accepting it; they usually signal a wrong assumption, not a missing piece.
- [heuristic] When stolen goods do not fit the thief's apparent aim, or valuable items were left untouched, consider that the random haul is a disguise hiding a targeted motive. When: When valuable items are ignored or the haul makes no sense together. Why: Random additions can make a targeted crime look like an ordinary burglary.
- [heuristic] Objects that conflict with a household's stated routine reveal what its occupants do not admit. Ask what each unexpected object would be needed for. When: an object's presence or position conflicts with stated routine. Why: the conflict shows an activity the occupants hide or do not mention.
- [heuristic] Note a mismatch between two traces, such as a broad mark and a set of prints, before trying to explain it. When: two traces differ in shape.
- [heuristic] A strong mismatch between a suspect's capacity and an act's demands should lower their rank, not prove innocence. When: A capacity check reveals a clear mismatch. Why: The suspect may have had help performing the physical part.
- [heuristic] An item on a body that does not match the victim's own kit suggests the kit was kept elsewhere and the victim was prepared there. When: Cosmetics, tools or supplies on the body differ from the victim's usual set. Why: The victim's own items would have been used if the preparation had happened at the scene.
- [heuristic] Behaviour that does not fit a stated motive is a clue; contradictory signals, such as generous offers from someone claiming poverty, suggest a hidden purpose or a played role. When: a character's words and actions point in different directions. Why: a true motive usually produces consistent action.
- [heuristic] Give the player a copy and an original; a layout mismatch, such as a blank line on the wrong page, becomes a checkable clue. When: when a document must be verified. Why: Comparison turns a discrepancy into a fair, testable clue.
- [heuristic] Use small stains, such as a few drops of blood, to confirm that a trail is correct and to show which person was injured. When: A trail needs confirmation from a second trace. Why: A minor physical clue corroborates a trail without requiring a larger scene.
- [heuristic] Place a clue that contradicts a convenient theory later in the investigation, so the player's own hopes are tested. When: a mystery encourages an appealing wrong theory. Why: late contradiction forces the player to revise.
- [heuristic] Plant deliberate contradictions in clues and let a case board flag them, so players can resolve them. When: Designing a mystery's evidence. Why: Visible conflicts give players a clear target for deduction.
- [procedure] 1) Confirm that two traces that should align really do not. 2) Propose a rearrangement that would restore agreement. 3) Check whether a second trace fits that rearrangement. When: A scene was kept in position and then altered.
- [pitfall] Not every discrepancy is a lie; memory, fear and accomplices can all produce differences between accounts. When: Judging a witness whose accounts differ. Why: Innocent causes produce mismatches as readily as deception.
- [pitfall] A recreated test may give a false result if its conditions differ from those of the original occurrence.
- [fact] A trace mismatch proves an object was moved but not why; a rearrangement narrows the question without answering it.
- [fact] The detail that contradicts a story is often a small, ordinary object rather than a dramatic clue.

## testimony and dialogue
- [mechanism] Store witness statements as dialogue lines so players can compare two NPC accounts side by side and discover contradictions themselves. When: when building NPC testimony. Why: side-by-side versions expose differences. (x2)
- [mechanism] If a player alarms a contact on the trail, the contact can vanish or change route, so the trail goes cold. When: A player is too visible while following a contact. Why: Noticing the watch causes the contact to act to avoid it.
- [mechanism] A talkative NPC can yield extra dialogue that the player may need to verify later, with a noted word triggering a follow-up question. When: Designing dialogue-heavy NPCs. Why: Flagged words create optional follow-up threads that reward attention.
- [mechanism] An NPC can store a remembered sound or sighting that unlocks only when the player asks the right question at the right in-game time.
- [mechanism] A character's vague answer can unlock a dialogue branch when the player finds a location that matches part of it.
- [mechanism] A voice heard through a door may not belong to the expected character; reveal the true speaker only through sight or a second clue. When: Designing audio-only clues. Why: Audio alone can mislead players.
- [rule] Flag testimony as unreliable, such as a dying NPC's last words or claims about a missing item, until a second witness, physical clue or player confirmation verifies it. When: A quest or puzzle relies on what an NPC says. (x2)
- [rule] Include bluff dialogue options, such as false claims or pretending to already know, where NPC responses reveal whether it knew the fact and whether it talks or shuts down. When: designing dialogue systems with deception or bluffing. Why: Lets players test NPC knowledge and unlock or close disclosure. (x2)
- [rule] Tag each testimony with its source (access, witnessed, heard directly, or secondhand), distance from the event, and witness condition such as drunk, fearful or self-interested, so players weigh reports by tags. When: designing NPC testimony systems. Why: tags make source reliability visible.
- [rule] Store each NPC's testimony with timestamps and positions so the game can flag contradictions with scene state and players find the account that does not fit the timeline. When: designing a mystery level.
- [rule] A tavern NPC who gossips after being bought a drink can reveal a quest clue, and the player's pretext or timing before a rumour spreads can change what they learn. When: Designing gossip-based information gathering. Why: Gossip content and willingness depend on approach and timing.
- [rule] A thief NPC can offer to return stolen goods for safe passage, giving the player a choice between confrontation and a negotiated outcome. When: designing a theft quest.
- [rule] Write an NPC who insists on a false explanation as an unreliable witness, so the player must test the claim.
- [rule] Let a hired informant deliver a report after a set in-game time, and log the evidence it delivers as a timeline entry.
- [rule] Offer dialogue options that let the player probe an NPC with what-if questions, so the NPC's reaction sets a flag without a full confession. When: Designing dialogue systems for suspect interrogation. Why: Partial reactions let the player learn without forcing a full admission.
- [rule] Let witness NPCs supply testimony that changes an outcome, such as a lighter sentence or faction trust, rather than a simple yes-or-no verdict.
- [rule] Give the player two accounts of one boundary dispute, one from each neighbor, so the gap between record and memory becomes a clue. When: when designing a dispute as a discoverable mystery. Why: contrasting accounts make the difference itself informative.
- [rule] Let players question a survivor about route and timing, so the answers change which room is safe to enter. When: designing NPC information that guides exploration.
- [rule] Use a journal entry or NPC remark as a recall trigger that lets the player sense a missed detail before the game names it. When: Building suspense toward a puzzle solution in a game. Why: A remark can bring a forgotten detail back to mind without stating the answer.
- [rule] Give a refusing witness a pressure point, such as a relationship or secret, that players can use to reopen the dialogue. When: When designing witness NPCs who refuse to talk. Why: A pressure point gives players a path forward without forcing the answer.
- [rule] In a puzzle, give each testimony a checkable prediction, such as a broken object, a hole or a footprint, that players can verify. When: when building clue-based puzzles. Why: verifiable predictions let players test claims themselves.
- [heuristic] Track which NPC observed which detail, so a player who hears a secondhand description has a reason to doubt it. When: Generating multi-witness investigations. Why: Knowledge provenance lets players judge reliability.
- [heuristic] Host the first step of a scheme in a public space or town event, leaving the player to trace how the contact develops. When: designing a scheme that spans locations. Why: public settings make early steps look incidental.
- [heuristic] Let a partly open door or vent carry an NPC exchange to the player, so overheard talk changes what the player knows before entering. When: Designing a room where the player can hear NPCs talk. Why: Overheard fragments create anticipation and shift the player's expectations.
- [heuristic] Give a suspect NPC a dialogue option that changes tone when the player presses a contradiction, offering an apology and promise in return for dropping the topic. When: Scripting a suspect's reaction to a challenged statement. Why: A tonal pivot gives the NPC a believable escape route and keeps the scene dynamic.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC who answers a question with a counter-request or refuses until a condition is met gate a clue, requiring a favor before the truth is given. When: Designing a dialogue tree that withholds information. Why: A conditional refusal turns the clue into a reward for a favor, adding a choice to the scene.
- [heuristic] Script a deflecting NPC so the player must ask the same question again to get the real answer, and let repeated attempts unlock different dialogue. When: Designing an NPC that avoids a topic. Why: Repeated attempts reward persistence and reveal the boundary the NPC will hold.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC's refusal state a conditional or hinted boundary, telling the player what remains possible even when the answer is withheld. When: Writing an NPC who refuses a question. Why: A stated boundary keeps the player informed about the path forward.
- [procedure] 1) Ask how a secured route came to be secured again. 2) Ask who changed its state and when. 3) Treat the answers as reopening the entry problem. When: when a single-route conclusion depends on a lock's state.
- [fact] Overheard speech in a language the player cannot follow yields only partial data that the player must infer.

## search and investigation
- [mechanism] A string of empty results builds tension before a turn, so it works as a deliberate pacing device. When: When pacing a mystery or investigation sequence. Why: Repeated dead ends raise expectation for the next reveal.
- [rule] Re-test each eliminated route before treating it as closed, since an elimination can rest on a mistaken time or witness.
- [rule] Station a watcher inside the location during the danger period under a plausible pretext, so the watch stays where the threat lies. When: a further crime is expected and suspects are confined to a known location.
- [rule] Describe a place from a stated vantage point, and say why it was chosen, since the viewpoint determines what the reader can see.
- [rule] Leave a trail leading to a lucky find so the discovery feels earned rather than arbitrary.
- [rule] Build indirect leads, such as asking an associate first, as a branching route that reaches the same record. When: When a direct route to a record is locked or unclear. Why: Alternative routes let players reach the same information in different ways.
- [rule] Let a quest require the player to revisit an earlier area and search for details missed the first time. When: Designing a reopened-case or revisit quest. Why: Returning with new knowledge rewards attention and reinforces the reopened question.
- [heuristic] Let players reveal item properties or a sealed container's contents through non-destructive checks such as weight, feel, colour or a scanner, so the object survives. When: Designing item inspection that should yield hints, or when the answer is inside a container the player must not break. Why: Non-destructive checks reward attentive players while preserving the item; the player proves the theory without the level being destroyed.
- [heuristic] When evidence disappears, resume the search where the hypothesis says it should reappear.
- [heuristic] Repeated advice from characters to seek a particular person sets up a thread the audience expects to follow. When: opening a story or case. Why: An expected thread gives players a goal to pursue.
- [heuristic] Look an odd object up in a reference source to find a known practice that explains it, making the object make sense.
- [heuristic] Knowledge of a hiding place narrows suspects only if it can be shown to be restricted. If many people saw it, the suspect pool stays wide.
- [heuristic] When a theory fails, drop its conclusions and return to the established facts before building a new one. When: An investigation's working theory is contradicted. Why: Clinging to a failed conclusion distorts how new evidence is read.
- [heuristic] Restrict observers' access to the accused, so the player must find another route to the truth. When: designing an investigation with a controlled interrogation. Why: Blocked access forces alternative investigation.
- [heuristic] Trace responsibility by asking who could have stopped a process with a word, and whether anyone with standing demanded delay. When: assessing who is accountable for a process.
- [heuristic] Wet floors, dust, or mud can record NPC movement, letting players reconstruct a route through the space. When: A level has surfaces that hold impressions. Why: Visible marks turn environmental detail into a traceable record of movement.
- [heuristic] Ledgers kept in a level can be cross-checked against each other, such as a supplier list against a detailed book, to reveal a chain of transactions.
- [heuristic] Include plausible but empty leads on a quest map, such as an address, shop or contact, that resolve quickly and point the player back to the real clue. When: When designing an open quest map with multiple leads. Why: Quick resolution keeps the player moving and reinforces the true path.
- [heuristic] Let the environment confirm a claim during a short wait, so suspense builds before the payoff.
- [heuristic] Reward cross-checking, for example where a missing key proves a witness's claim about a lock wrong.
- [heuristic] Tag each district with a particle profile, such as mill dust or coal soot, and let players match residue on an item to a district. When: when designing location-finding puzzles.
- [heuristic] Do not sweep an entire area at random when geography already suggests a direction; search the likely path first.
- [heuristic] A dormant site such as an abandoned workshop can be inferred from ownership change combined with remaining physical traces. When: a site's use ended without a recorded reason.
- [heuristic] A distant fire, smoke or light seen from one spot can have several sources, so the player should check the map before settling on one. When: Designing an ambiguous distant visual cue. Why: Multiple plausible sources keep the cue open until the player investigates.
- [heuristic] State the limits of help the NPC will give during a search, such as no assistance or hints, so the rule of the search is explicit. When: Designing an NPC who stands aside while the player searches. Why: An explicit rule prevents players from expecting help that will not come.
- [heuristic] Design a reconnaissance phase in which players gather identities, codes or records before the final entry, rewarding patient observation over direct force. When: Designing a protected location with a final entry challenge. Why: Information gathering makes the entry feel earned and lets players succeed through observation.
- [procedure] 1) Visit every residence with a view of the scene. 2) Record who saw nothing as well as who saw something. When: start of an investigation with few leads and many possible witnesses. Why: negative results narrow the field.
- [pitfall] Assuming a room is empty after a first search found nothing can lead past the real hiding place; check its contents, since an absent occupant still leaves traces. Why: absence of a person does not erase what they left behind.

## npc movement scheduling
- [mechanism] A barrier that changes state on a schedule, such as a bridge raised at dusk, defines when access is possible.
- [mechanism] A timetable giving daily routes, weekly calls and arrival times narrows when a party could have reached a given place. When: travel routes must be checked against schedules. Why: Fixed calls and arrival times bound the possible times of presence.
- [rule] Build timetables of scheduled departures, including conditional entries like weather-dependent stops, and tie NPC exits to them so players test alibis against a second time source. When: designing alibi checks. Why: A shared schedule makes claimed movements checkable.
- [rule] Store container, door and object states so a later visit reveals changes; an object appearing since the last visit becomes a lead. When: when designing rooms that change after events. Why: Persistent state makes changes detectable on return.
- [rule] Script patrols, NPC routines or moving platforms to slow at known points or after a diversion, opening a short window telegraphed by sound or animation. When: designing timed stealth or action sections. Why: Visible, readable windows let players plan and time moves, so timing becomes a skill rather than luck.
- [rule] Anchor generated NPC routines to a fixed event time, so each suspect's movements can be checked against that moment. When: when generating suspect schedules for a mystery. Why: a shared fixed time gives the player a common reference point.
- [rule] Give a level a shared clock, bell or scheduled light change so the player can place events on one common timeline. When: a level contains events the player must order in time. Why: A visible shared time reference lets the player sequence events without guessing.
- [rule] Use a broken routine as a clue, such as an NPC who always carries a key but has none, or a usual object missing from its room. When: when designing puzzle clues.
- [rule] Script an offender's route through the level and place a trace at each step, so the player can rebuild the sequence. When: designing a past event the player must reconstruct. Why: one trace per step gives the player a complete chain to follow.
- [rule] Let players build timelines from overheard reports, and use windows when staff are away as a mechanic for unobserved action. When: Designing investigation or stealth mechanics around NPC schedules. Why: Scheduled absences create safe moments for player action.
- [rule] Give the player a social entry route, such as an introduction from a known NPC, that unlocks a building, and let the host resist until a persuasion check succeeds. When: when a locked building is controlled by an NPC. Why: A social route offers an alternative to force or stealth.
- [rule] Shop opening and closing hours constrain when a player can make a purchase inquiry, so purchase time is a gameplay constraint. When: When designing timed inquiries in a town. Why: Inquiries are only possible while the shop is open.
- [rule] Script an NPC's route so it passes the player's trap while the player watches from a fixed vantage point. When: Designing an ambush or trap the player must set. Why: A fixed vantage point lets the player observe the NPC's path without being seen.
- [rule] Tie a hidden item's location to an NPC's daily schedule so the player finds it by working out when and where the routine opens a window.
- [rule] Schedule timed events from a discovered pattern so the player learns when to act. When: Designing timed events tied to clues. Why: The pattern tells the player when the window opens.
- [rule] Use scheduled events, such as a guard arriving at a set time, to give the player a deadline that explains a hidden character's choices. When: A hidden character acts under time pressure. Why: A fixed deadline makes the character's timing understandable.
- [rule] Let a fleeing NPC carry a key item out of the level, so the player must decide whether to pursue.
- [heuristic] Place clue objects where the character who hid them would logically keep them, such as under cushions, in a purse, or in a locked room, so searches follow character habits. When: designing hidden items in a level. Why: plausible hiding spots make discovery feel earned.
- [heuristic] Use a recurring in-world sound, such as a bell or gong at fixed intervals, to mark elapsed time and trigger NPC or enemy behavior on a schedule. When: Designing a timed event in a level. Why: A steady audible cue lets players and characters know the time from sound alone.
- [heuristic] An NPC sent to a hidden place can be scheduled to appear when the player needs them, so the player finds them at the right moment. When: Controlling when a witness becomes available. Why: Scheduled availability paces the investigation.
- [heuristic] Stage the first contact between an NPC and a target as a seemingly chance event, so the player sees only the opening step. When: designing an NPC plot. Why: an innocent-looking start hides the scheme that follows.
- [heuristic] Scheduled openings of a service door can serve as the intended way for a player to bypass a locked area.
- [heuristic] When many people gather, absences, seating and gifts become readable clues, so a feast or wake can serve as a staging area. Why: small social signals become meaningful in a crowd.
- [heuristic] Give each crowd member a defined exit route so the space empties in a readable order, letting the player notice what was left behind. When: When a space must be cleared of many NPCs in a visible way. Why: Ordered exits make the clearing legible and reveal remaining objects or clues.
- [tradeoff] Fixed routines leave findable unobserved windows and let impossible alibis be exposed via modeled distances and vehicle stats; varying schedules makes learned plans fail and need rebuilding. When: timing-based stealth or investigation puzzles; fixed routines suit deduction, varied schedules suit learnable stealth levels. Why: Predictable schedules let players deduce gaps; varying them forces players to re-check assumptions.
- [pitfall] Mistaking an old removal for a recent one happens when a witness's memory predates the window being investigated. When: dating when an item went missing. Why: Old memories can attach to the wrong time window.
- [fact] A brief sighting of a servant near a building confirms that staff are present, though not whom they serve.

## surface trace evidence
- [mechanism] Surface material decides which traces survive: footprints show on bare gravel or soft carpet but not grass or bare hard floors, so sweep and examine loose debris instead. When: When choosing where to look for impressions. Why: Traces are readable only on suitable surfaces.
- [mechanism] Environmental change such as a rising tide, lifting fog or shifting weather can erase footprints or clues on a timer, forcing action before evidence fades. When: Outdoor levels with weather or tide states that change over time; designing timed evidence destruction. Why: Environmental change limits how long clues stay available.
- [mechanism] Mud, dust or snow tiles can record footprints, so a tracking puzzle can let the player match tracks to a boot or shoe found elsewhere. When: designing a tracking puzzle. Why: soft surfaces store impressions the player can compare.
- [mechanism] When traces degrade over game time, investigating early yields more evidence than investigating late. Why: Degraded traces are harder to read, which rewards prompt investigation.
- [mechanism] A hidden mechanism can be made discoverable through a chain of traces, such as a smell, a pipe, a control tap and a concealed outlet, each leading to the next. When: Designing a hidden hazard or device that players must uncover. Why: Each linked clue gives players a reason and a path to the next one.
- [rule] Place readable wear, dust, disturbed furniture, half-finished drinks or fresh scents against stale air so players learn how recently a room was used, even with no one present. When: A place must reveal recent activity without characters on stage. Why: Physical traces imply recent use; players who check surfaces learn it.
- [rule] Let players compare mark spacing with each candidate tool's dimensions to identify which tool made the crossing.
- [rule] Let scene traces encode the number of actors, such as one drag trail versus several footprint sets, and add a later clue when a lone actor is mistaken for a group. When: Designing a crime scene for players to reason about. Why: Players can infer who was present, and a second clue can correct a wrong assumption.
- [rule] Leave a crime scene unchanged, treating its lighting and furniture as affordances that let the player read the room's history. Why: Placement and light carry the room's story without text.
- [rule] Encode an NPC's height and mood in stride and weight, and design investigations that reward players for cross-checking two traces. When: Designing a footprint-based investigation. Why: Cross-checking tests whether players verify evidence.
- [rule] Place timing traces such as dropped embers, melted wax or muddy steps so players can estimate how long an NPC waited.
- [rule] Place destroyed or hidden papers in a room whose traces point toward a motive, and make the player confirm that motive in another location. When: Designing clues that suggest a hidden motive. Why: A single trace should not settle the question on its own.
- [rule] Give marks a clear sequence so players can tell which event happened first.
- [rule] Let a small, specific trace such as a material or residue identify a creature or room type, and let the player confirm it elsewhere.
- [rule] Let a plain object carry a trace matching an NPC's record, so the player can link the object to that suspect. When: designing clues for an identification puzzle. Why: the match turns an ordinary item into evidence.
- [rule] Pair each physical trace in a level, such as footprints or dust trails, with a question about purpose, so a match alone does not resolve the puzzle. When: Designing evidence that records movement. Why: Purpose questions keep trace-matching from solving the puzzle without reasoning.
- [rule] After an ambush, leave physical traces such as a broken lock, broken window or a scratch that identifies an item, so players can reconstruct the attack. When: Designing evidence in an attack or theft scene. Why: Traces give players concrete evidence to reason from.
- [rule] Place surviving traces such as foundations or walls in the scene as evidence that a building once existed.
- [heuristic] Let footprints or blood drops lead players into concealed areas; a trace ending at a spot points to a hidden area, so do not treat dead ends as final. When: Tracking or exploration levels; closed spaces holding traces that lead into hidden areas. Why: Traces point to places the player has not yet searched; a trace ending at a spot directs the player to search there.
- [heuristic] Place boot wear, soot or staining on a corpse or ruin so players can work out who was there and how recently.
- [heuristic] Removing items from their settings leaves empty fittings that betray the theft. When: A stolen item was set in a mount. Why: Missing pieces show what was taken.
- [heuristic] Rule out floors and ceilings with practical risk or visible seams. A floor covered wall to wall in carpet shows no seam where a hidden access panel could be. When: Narrowing where a concealed access point could lie. Why: Hidden access leaves physical traces that can be ruled out.
- [heuristic] Ask how a culprit would obtain a material and whether they could avoid leaving a trail; the answer shows where to look for its source. When: Tracing where a rare substance came from.
- [heuristic] Encode clues as traces, such as scorch patterns, residue or missing marks, that players check with the right tool. When: Designing rooms that hold evidence.
- [pitfall] A good-looking surface can hide poor condition, so only close inspection reveals the truth and distant views mislead.
- [fact] Worn or repaired surfaces keep traces of disturbance after a room has been tidied.
- [fact] Marks such as scratches and dents, plus displaced objects, can rebuild what happened in a scene nobody observed.

## sealed room puzzles
- [mechanism] Seal a setting with visible barriers, such as collapsed passages or locked gates, cutting off outside help so only intended suspects or threats remain and the setting bounds the puzzle. When: Designing a self-contained area for an encounter or mystery, or when the people involved should be confined. Why: Visible barriers and cut-off conditions remove outside options and focus events on those present.
- [rule] Define each barrier separately, such as a locked door, inside bolt, barred window, fastened shutters, light source and witness, so the player can verify no visible route exists. When: Defining a sealed room, vault or locked-room puzzle. Why: Explicit, checkable barriers make the impossibility verifiable rather than asserted. (x2)
- [rule] Build sealed-room puzzles with a stated time window, a clear last-seen timeline and NPC positions logged at fixed moments, so players can test who could have acted. When: Designing a sealed-room puzzle. Why: Players can then check claims about who was where and when against the room layout. (x2)
- [rule] Build sealed-room puzzles with several openings, such as door, hatch, ladder and empty neighbouring building, plus keys and scheduled events, so players can enumerate, test and deduce the route out. When: Designing a sealed-room puzzle. Why: Enumerated components give the player a complete set to reason over. (x2)
- [rule] Use a self-relocking lock to force the player to carry or find a key to re-enter a room.
- [rule] Link two separated scenes through a shared object the player can recognise, so they connect the scenes without explicit explanation. When: A scene jumps in time or place. Why: A recognised object bridges the gap.
- [rule] Make a sealed room an optional mechanic that the player can choose to enter, rather than a fixed barrier.
- [rule] Let a sealed room open through a mechanism tied to its occupants or contents, so players test people and objects instead of walls. When: Designing a sealed vault puzzle. Why: Checkable routes that are ruled out move attention inside the room.
- [rule] In a room with no forced-entry marks, offer hints toward an NPC holding a key, a servant's route or a hidden passage.
- [rule] Pair a secret with a simple lock so that keys the player has found are rewarded. Why: A concealment that relies on secrecy can be opened by any found key.
- [rule] A sealed room should still have at least one route the player can discover through clues. When: designing a sealed or locked space. Why: A sealed space with no discoverable route frustrates players rather than challenging them.
- [rule] Use a sealed grave or container as a forced escalation point: opening it at the player's decision triggers a new scene. When: a hidden object is needed to advance the story. Why: Player-initiated opening turns pressure into a concrete action with consequences.
- [heuristic] A room with no visible disturbance is a useful clue only if obvious exits are visibly closed and shown; unrevealed gaps feel like cheating. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle or clue. Why: Obvious exits must be closed before a hidden route is plausible, and gaps the player never saw make the solution unfair. (x2)
- [heuristic] Hold a reveal behind a sealed door or item that opens on a deadline or checkpoint; record the solution in advance and let an NPC note promise the explanation. When: Designing a delayed reveal in a quest. Why: A fixed record guarantees the reveal happens at the intended moment.
- [heuristic] Physical security does not stop an attack via a utility shared with other rooms, such as a lever or pipe network, so a trap there can be operated from another room. When: Analyzing or designing a locked-room situation. Why: The attack path runs through shared infrastructure, so a locked room can be solved without breaking its door.
- [heuristic] Place a trap or poison on a shared object such as a chest, potion, or gift, so whichever character picks it up can be struck, and model the handover chain so players trace it. When: Designing a trap that can hit an unintended NPC. Why: Handover chains give players a route to deduce the intended target.
- [heuristic] Build a sealed chamber so the player checks each exit in a fixed order, and each failed check narrows the options. When: Designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: A fixed order makes elimination systematic and fair.
- [heuristic] Make a sealed room less certain by leaving a second-person trace inside, such as a half-eaten object whose state changes over time.
- [heuristic] A sealed room can look empty on first inspection, with its hidden route revealed only after the in-game timeline is checked. When: when designing a locked-room puzzle. Why: Timeline checks reveal what visual inspection misses.
- [heuristic] Place a sensor on each door in a multi-door level so a player who hears a noise can work out which room it came from. When: a level has several doors near a hidden event.
- [procedure] 1) List every exit, such as doors, hatches, chimneys, windows, roofs, hidden spaces and adjacent buildings. 2) Test each against a checkable physical property. 3) Rule out routes with stated evidence. 4) Accept a surviving route, even if improbable, only once any proposed mechanism is confirmed by experiment. 5) Re-examine nearby features for overlooked routes. When: A room seems sealed or a crime seems confined to it. Why: Stated reasons show which exits were ruled out; a locked door hides other openings.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible entry and exit. 2) Check each for a physical trace. 3) Rule out exits with no trace. 4) Weigh the remaining explanations. When: a death or event seems to have no explanation.
- [procedure] 1) Rule out ordinary exits by checking doors and windows for disturbance. 2) If none show it, infer a hidden route. 3) Search for wear, oil or scuffs that a mechanism leaves. When: A room is sealed and no forced entry is visible. Why: Elimination narrows the search to concealed routes.
- [procedure] 1) List every exit and access point. 2) Check each against the guard posted there and its physical state. 3) Bound the escape window using observation times. 4) Cross-check with an independent question. 5) If all exits are closed, the culprit is still inside. When: A crime occurs in a space with a limited set of controlled exits and a known observation window. Why: Each exit is ruled in or out by evidence, so closed exits leave the culprit inside.
- [procedure] 1) Establish each seal condition, such as locks, checks, light and witnesses, before breaking it. 2) Let the player verify each condition. 3) Break the seal in a scripted event showing the room empty and undisturbed. When: Designing a fair locked-room impossibility. Why: Verified constraints make the impossibility fair and invite deduction.
- [fact] A spring lock relocks when its door swings shut, so entry requires a key even when the door is closed behind you.

## clue verification
- [rule] Encode sightlines, partitions and stop points so blocked routes, failed timing or the side of a room an action came from refute wrong theories or accusations, not written text. When: Designing deduction puzzles in a space where an accusation or theory can be tested against the environment. Why: Level geometry gives a fixed, checkable answer that the world state, not text, can check. (x2)
- [rule] State every assumed input, such as speed or angle, and present model results only as plausibility, since a missing input can change every conclusion. When: Running a physical model where an input such as speed or angle is unknown. Why: Unstated inputs make conclusions look firmer than they are, and conclusions depend on all inputs.
- [rule] Plant a small verifiable number in an alibi record, so a player who notices it can unlock the contradiction. When: designing an alibi puzzle. Why: a precise detail gives the player a concrete check to make.
- [rule] Tag found letters as unlinked until the player finds a matching clue elsewhere. When: Designing clues that may be irrelevant. Why: Players should not treat a fragment as significant before a connection is shown.
- [rule] Keep the candidate list visible to the player so each filtering step is fair and verifiable, and give partial clues such as an initial, a color or a limp that narrow it. When: Designing a deduction puzzle over NPCs. Why: A visible list lets the player check each attribute rather than guess.
- [rule] Design a second source that can confirm or break a character's alibi during play, so the alibi is tested rather than simply accepted.
- [rule] Separate witness-only claims from claims backed by objects or positions, so players can tell which evidence they are able to verify. When: when placing clues in a level. Why: Players need to know which claims they can check against the world.
- [rule] Store each self-report with a verification status so that later evidence can confirm or contradict it without overwriting the original. When: designing a data model for NPC claims and player knowledge. Why: Keeping the original claim lets later evidence change its status.
- [rule] Let players check identity through faction or guild records, so a fictitious character's absence from the records becomes a discoverable clue. When: a game has organisational records.
- [rule] Matching ballistic or print evidence across scenes can confirm a link between crimes, which strengthens a case without proving identity alone. When: comparing evidence from multiple crime scenes. Why: Shared evidence connects scenes even when the culprit remains unknown.
- [rule] Flag any log claiming a confession that the scripted process could not have produced as false.
- [rule] Verify every advertised or stated claim about a location against physical reality before trusting it, since posted notices and NPC statements can be wrong. When: A player must act on a sign, notice or NPC's description of a place. Why: Stated locations can be false or outdated, so verification is needed.
- [rule] Offer the player two candidate explanations, human action or environmental change, and let a material test such as heat, time or moisture decide between them. When: designing a clue with two possible causes. Why: A test the player can run turns a guess into a checkable choice.
- [rule] Let a player compare identifying marks on a corpse's head with its body, so a substituted head can be detected. When: sealed scenes with a discovered body. Why: A visible mismatch gives the player a checkable way to detect a substitution.
- [heuristic] Leave one ordinary-looking element unchecked so the player's elimination leads to a real discovery. When: When designing a sealed room with a hidden solution. Why: The remaining untested element is where the player's reasoning pays off.
- [heuristic] A dull, solid tap suggests masonry behind a surface. A hollow or different tone may signal a space, but a dull sound alone does not prove a passage exists. When: Tapping walls to locate a hidden cavity. Why: Sound reflects material density, which is ambiguous.
- [heuristic] Let an object's label or appearance match a character's sensory report, such as taste or smell, so the player can cross-check two sources. When: designing object clues. Why: Two independent sources make a clue more convincing.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the location named in the claim. 2) Repeat the witness's position and view the room yourself with a line-of-sight check. 3) Record what is seen. 4) Compare the result with the claim, not the witness's word alone. When: Testing a witness's claim, such as that they saw nothing, or a rumour such as a patrol or sound.
- [procedure] 1) Fix one route through the space. 2) At each door, note what it connects. 3) Describe contents in the same order you moved.
- [procedure] 1) Walk through the rooms and grounds. 2) Understand how the building fits together. 3) Only then draw conclusions about movement. When: Reasoning about movement in a multi-route building. Why: Understanding the layout first prevents wrong conclusions about routes.
- [procedure] 1) Place a matching fragment on the victim. 2) Hide the matching item in a diggable location. 3) Let the player discover both and decide whether the fragment and the item agree. When: When designing a frame-up in which the player must check whether evidence was planted. Why: Pairing a visible fragment with a hidden match tests whether the player verifies the link.
- [procedure] 1) Place a journal entry in the player's space that foreshadows a later location. 2) Add the evidence found at that location to the player's inventory once it is reached.
- [pitfall] Fatigue and slippery footing change step length, so a count taken under those conditions cannot be trusted as a measurement.
- [pitfall] Failing to examine an object already in use can waste the evidence it holds. Why: the clue sits in plain view but goes unnoticed.
- [pitfall] Accepting a witness's description of an object without identifying its true function can mislead, so the function must be confirmed. When: a witness names an object. Why: Witnesses can misname objects, hiding what the object really did.

## npc statements and witnesses
- [mechanism] A guardian NPC who offers to waive access to some rooms steers the player toward other areas.
- [behavior] An NPC's alibi can be checked against a shop's opening hours or a patrol schedule, so a false claim is exposed when the player checks. When: Designing NPC alibis in a game world. Why: Fixed schedules kept by other parties provide independent records the NPC cannot control.
- [rule] Turn an NPC's claim, such as a hidden partner or having locked up, into a checkable world state like a scar, tool, record or locked room players can inspect. When: Designing NPC testimony that the player must verify. Why: Verifiable details let players separate truthful claims from fabricated ones.
- [rule] Write a hint-giving NPC to challenge the player's assumptions rather than confirm them. When: Designing advisors in a mystery game. Why: Challenged assumptions push players to re-test their reasoning.
- [rule] Give a sensitive area's NPC a reaction or hidden item that supports both an innocent and a guilty explanation, so the player must weigh both. When: The player approaches a sensitive area or object. Why: Two-sided evidence keeps the player's conclusion open until confirmed elsewhere.
- [rule] When a guard describes only a cloak, the player should match the description against other clues such as height, voice or gait before accusing anyone. When: Designing evidence that offers only partial identification. Why: Partial evidence narrows suspects without naming one, so further clues are needed for a fair accusation.
- [rule] Offer a bluff option whose success depends on facts the NPC has already learned, and let a second NPC verify the claim so a failed bluff becomes a failure state. When: Designing dialogue challenges with consequences. Why: Tying success to prior knowledge makes the bluff a fair test of what the player has learned.
- [rule] Give a sighting an ambiguous location, such as an adjacent room or the target room, so the player must resolve the doubt.
- [rule] Give NPC storage a canonical orientation, so a moved item betrays a visitor who did not know the room.
- [rule] Make NPC predictions checkable by recording whether each warning came before the harm, so players can verify the claim against the timeline. When: when NPCs make predictions about future events in a game. Why: Checkable predictions let players judge whether a prophecy is genuine or reconstructed.
- [rule] Give two NPCs separate memories of one event that include a detail also present in the level, so players can verify the link. When: Two NPCs share a past event. Why: A world detail lets players confirm or refute the memory.
- [rule] Make an item an NPC remembers, such as a coat or towel, a checkable clue rather than background detail. When: Designing NPC dialogue that mentions possessions. Why: A remembered item lets players verify its absence.
- [rule] Build a sequence of NPC statements that each get retracted, so every retraction opens a new area or ability for the player.
- [heuristic] Give NPC sources such as reporters or informants a friendship, rivalry or agenda that biases what they share, so players must weigh or cross-check the information. When: Designing NPC summaries of events, or NPCs who provide clues or story information. Why: Omission by a biased reporter is a source of uncertainty; biased sources make players verify rather than accept at face value. (x2)
- [heuristic] Keep NPC statements ambiguous so players must judge which were meant for an audience and which carry the true meaning. When: Writing dialogue for NPCs in monitored scenes. Why: Ambiguity makes the player weigh the speaker's motives.
- [heuristic] Give captured NPCs a fear or temperament trait that changes how they answer threats, so players must choose their approach. When: Designing captive NPCs who may be interrogated. Why: Different traits call for different approaches, making the choice meaningful.
- [heuristic] Give attendant NPCs visible clues, such as grooming reactions or leftover objects, that the player can read as evidence. When: When designing servant or companion NPCs who share private spaces with another character. Why: Physical traces from private moments give players evidence without dialogue.
- [heuristic] A child NPC who repeats overheard lines can supply true but incomplete clues, and an adult who interrupts them signals hidden influence to pursue. When: When designing a child NPC who overhears adult conversations. Why: Children repeat what they heard, and adult control over them points to a further lead.
- [heuristic] Let a feverish or disturbed NPC produce fragments that hint at secrets and mark them as unreliable, so the player must verify them elsewhere. When: Designing dialogue for a disturbed character who holds clues. Why: Fragments that are unreliable keep the player searching for proof rather than accepting the hint as fact.
- [heuristic] Give the player a letter or journal item that resolves a location's mystery in an NPC's voice, leaving one moral choice unresolved.
- [heuristic] Place an observer in a hidden spot so a confession is overheard rather than told.
- [pitfall] Blocking onlookers' view of a scene hides who was watching and how they reacted, removing witness information.

## puzzle items and hooks
- [mechanism] An unopened pouch keeps its contents unknown until opened, so whether someone checked it becomes a puzzle for the player.
- [mechanism] Keep a fragment unusable until it is paired with a second location, so the player must connect two areas.
- [mechanism] Props such as a loose sheet, a wrecked toy or a rigged fitting can be placed so an attentive player can connect them to a method.
- [rule] End each level or reveal scene on an unresolved item or beat, such as a stray item in a cleared room, half-burned note, collapse or shut door, pulling the player onward.
- [rule] Show the premises of a deduction puzzle clearly, so the player can locate a weak assumption and reason about its effect on the answer. When: Designing a puzzle that requires computing a route or time from in-world figures. Why: An unstated chain reads as arbitrary and cannot be challenged.
- [rule] Place an item with no obvious use in a room and resolve it later, rewarding attentive players.
- [rule] Open a level with unexplained hooks, such as lights switching off or a silhouette at a window, before the main objective is revealed. When: designing the opening of a level. Why: hooks pull attention and create questions before the goal is stated.
- [rule] Place a clue object in one room and its matching document elsewhere, so that matching them becomes the unlock for a later step. When: designing a multi-part identity puzzle.
- [rule] Hide a key in an unexpected spot in a locked room so players can solve the puzzle by checking the room's props. When: Locked room puzzles with a vanishing or trap element.
- [rule] Let the key proof item look like a collectible or quirk, so players pick it up early without treating it as clue-bearing, then reuse it as the final proof. When: designing a puzzle or secret whose solution depends on items found earlier. Why: Players do not expect a decorative item to become the answer, so the later payoff feels earned rather than arbitrary.
- [rule] Make the solution a physical object already on the table and used in the open, so its role is in view but easy to overlook.
- [rule] Place a solution's key object in an early, unremarkable location so the final confrontation pays it off. When: Designing the layout of a puzzle or mystery level. Why: An object seen early becomes a meaningful payoff when revealed later.
- [rule] Use a missing item or body part as a puzzle lock, so the player recalls a prior fact such as an unremovable ring to see the link. When: designing a puzzle around an absence. Why: Requiring recall of an earlier fact makes the solution feel earned rather than arbitrary.
- [heuristic] Use an object's physical detail, such as a symbol, inscription or broken pattern, as a key matching a door, chest or empty slot elsewhere in the level. When: Designing a puzzle where the player must link an item to a lock, container or empty slot. Why: A matching physical detail gives the player a concrete link to follow.
- [heuristic] A ledger or shop book can be a puzzle object in which the player notices a missing charge and infers who stayed there. When: designing a record-based puzzle. Why: Players deduce from absence and pattern without dialogue.
- [heuristic] A puzzle item can carry a pattern impression that the player must cast or copy and match to a character's trait, such as a bite or glove mark. When: When designing evidence-matching puzzles. Why: Pattern matching turns physical evidence into a checkable clue.
- [heuristic] An ordinary object such as a coat or lantern can serve as a trigger that the player must notice moving, rewarding attention to small changes in the scene.
- [heuristic] Place a carved inscription behind debris or a removable panel, and store the fuller account elsewhere, so players must visit both to assemble the story.
- [heuristic] Hold back a found object's full story until the player has physically recovered it, and let its use become clear through application in a set order. When: Designing a puzzle room or item whose purpose is revealed through use. Why: Tying the explanation to possession and action makes the discovery feel earned.
- [heuristic] Place an inspectable artifact in a public space, such as an item implying a missing person, to open a quest whose cause is discovered later.
- [procedure] 1) Place a readable codex in a single room that explains the cause of the scene. 2) Link the final interaction to the completion of a gameplay object.
- [pitfall] Withholding clues for too long leaves the player unable to track the evidence already gathered. When: Pacing a gradual reveal. Why: Too much unresolved information overwhelms memory.

## route and sightline geometry
- [mechanism] A mirror angled at a target's window lets a watcher learn when the target is asleep, with no direct view into the room. When: Watching a target without line of sight. Why: Reflection carries the view around an obstacle.
- [mechanism] Reflective or repeated spaces make one figure seem to appear many times, so witnesses lose track of who is actually present.
- [mechanism] What a person can see or reach from a spot depends on room geometry: door placement, furniture position and distance set what is visible and within arm's length.
- [mechanism] Random events such as a patrol changing route can break a player's plan and add variance to level play. When: Designing level events that are not fully scripted. Why: Unplanned changes force the player to adapt.
- [mechanism] Different ways into a blocked room, such as force, persuasion or another route, change what the player learns from it.
- [rule] Give a physical guide, such as a stick laid along the bearing, so the player can follow the line exactly. When: for hidden locations reached along a fixed line.
- [rule] Design corridors and gardens with few, readable entry points so players can reason about who could reach a point.
- [rule] Place routes and landmarks so the player can infer the likely approach path, and seed search zones where items are likely to be dropped.
- [rule] Plant climbable escape routes, such as ivy beside a window, as options when the player must leave unseen. When: Designing a location the player may need to exit covertly. Why: Visible exits let the player choose a route without being caught.
- [rule] Give players a room map with sightlines, so a locked-room puzzle can be solved by checking windows and walls.
- [rule] Create a hazard area, such as a bog or fog zone, where visibility is reduced and players must rely on audio cues and a direction tool. When: designing a dangerous traversal area.
- [rule] Use exterior cues such as blinds, smoke and staff movement as readable hints about which interior rooms are in use before the player enters. When: Designing a building the player views from outside first. Why: Exterior signs prepare the player for what is inside.
- [rule] Place identifying signs such as house numbers and painted names on doors, so a follower can confirm a destination by reading the environment. When: designing a location the player must find by tailing. Why: lets the player confirm a room without entering it.
- [rule] Use the map to limit which routes a prisoner or cart could take, and let players deduce the route from it.
- [heuristic] Check whether an access route would be seen by patrols or a watchful street before accepting it as viable.
- [heuristic] Model a player's view through a doorway or window as a limited sightline, so clue text shows only what that viewpoint could reveal.
- [heuristic] A physical route counts only if it actually connects to the destination; check each route's geometry, such as whether a ledge reaches the target room.
- [heuristic] Plant a physical route and a social cue early as ordinary detail, so they read as harmless until later used for access. When: when a route or cue will matter in a later beat. Why: ordinary detail is not questioned until its use is revealed.
- [procedure] 1) Let the player visit a location once. 2) Record its layout, stair counts, sounds and occupants. 3) Reward the player's recall of those details on a later visit.
- [tradeoff] A roof route carries its own risks, while a ground-floor approach exposes the listener to far more danger; the lower-risk option is usually preferred. When: when two approaches to a listening spot are available. Why: Each route has a different cost of detection.
- [fact] A ring of trees on a rise can hide the interior from outside observers even when the ring is not solid.
- [fact] Physical features such as walls and dense vegetation constrain where events could plausibly occur, and so limit which stories a scene can support.

## clue placement in scenes
- [mechanism] Marks lined up across terrain can act as a breadcrumb trail leading the player to a hidden site.
- [rule] Address a quest item to a similarly named NPC so players must inspect the item's reverse to find its true recipient. When: Designing a discoverable item tied to a specific character. Why: Inspection rewards attention and reveals the real target.
- [rule] Position overheard lines and reflections where the player can watch unseen, and tie each one to a later place or character.
- [rule] Use a new area or act to mark that the investigation's premise has changed. When: a reveal changes the nature of the investigation. Why: a structural break signals the turn to the player.
- [rule] Place a physical trace such as dust, cobweb or rust at each exit so the player can rule routes in or out. When: a room has several exits or hiding places.
- [rule] Split a cache's contents across several items, each a partial clue that the player must combine to open the next area. When: When designing a multi-step clue chain. Why: Combining fragments gates progression and rewards synthesis.
- [rule] Chain document clues across rooms, with each found item naming the next location, so layout is revealed through reading as well as exploration. When: When designing a level revealed through found documents. Why: Reading directs the player through the space and reinforces the story.
- [heuristic] Place a key object in plain sight among ordinary container or room contents; searchers expecting concealment skip it, and its meaning becomes clear only when tested. When: When placing a key item that must be hard to find but fair, or a reveal object for a puzzle. Why: Searchers model the target as hidden and skip obvious objects; players dismiss familiar objects, so the test creates the moment of discovery.
- [heuristic] Keep an inventory of each container's contents so that a missing item becomes a clue the player can notice. When: When designing containers that the player can search or revisit. Why: A known baseline lets the player detect absence.
- [heuristic] Give containers hidden compartments and log the player's first discovery in a journal so later accusations can refer back to it. When: When designing containers that hide evidence in a mystery. Why: A recorded discovery gives the player a reference point for later reasoning.
- [heuristic] Stamped marks or initials on items can connect a found object to a named character, letting players link evidence to people. When: Designing evidence items for a mystery. Why: Identity tags turn a found object into a lead.
- [heuristic] Place a clue in the environment, such as a dated record that stops abruptly, so the player infers a mechanism without the game stating it. When: Hinting at a hidden system or transformation. Why: Environmental clues let the player make the connection, which feels more rewarding than direct exposition.
- [heuristic] Show an item's origin through packaging or workmanship differences, giving the player a route to its source. When: Designing items that lead to a location or maker. Why: Visible craft differences let the player trace an object to where it was made.
- [heuristic] A recurring sound or symbol can be planted as a clue and later revealed as misdirection or as a true sign.
- [heuristic] A player can recover a damaged document and identify its material by matching it to stationery or items found elsewhere, so puzzles can compare properties rather than readable text. When: Designing document-based clues in a game world. Why: Readable content may be lost while material properties remain.
- [heuristic] Plant small physical evidence such as wet prints, smell or disturbed dust, so the player can check a trick against what they observe. When: When designing a trick or hidden mechanism. Why: Observable traces let players verify a suspected solution.
- [heuristic] Leave one distinctive item in a cleaned-up scene that players can recognise from an NPC's routine, so it links the scene to a person. When: Designing a crime scene the culprit has tidied. Why: Players connect the item to a character they have already observed.
- [heuristic] Use environmental physics such as tides, currents or water flow to move a clue to a place the player must trace backward from. When: Designing a clue that must be found by reasoning about movement. Why: Players reconstruct the route from the clue's final location.
- [heuristic] Place a symmetry-breaking object, such as an empty bracket or displaced statue, in a mirrored room where the player sees it on entry, so it is planted in view and interpreted later. When: Designing a room with mirrored decoration that should signal something was taken or hidden. Why: A visible anomaly on entry is noticed but not understood until the player gathers further clues.
- [procedure] 1) Place atmosphere and omens early. 2) Place NPC testimony in the middle. 3) Place the key document or test late. When: Distributing clues across a long investigation. Why: Escalating clue types match the player's growing ability to interpret them.
- [pitfall] A cache made only of riddles frustrates players, so include at least a few concrete, testable items. When: When filling a cache with clues. Why: Players need some firm footing to reason from.

## key custody tracking
- [model] Model keys as an ownership graph with holders, copies and past uses, so players deduce who could enter and when from key state. When: Designing keyed access or a locked room the player must investigate. Why: Graph and key history give the player concrete evidence to reason from.
- [mechanism] A key that opens a container passes custody between holders; if carried away after each use, it limits who can open the container at any moment, learnable from NPC dialogue. When: when a restricted container is designed. Why: the key's movement controls when access is possible.
- [rule] Attach each key to a specific NPC and log every handover; track door states like locked, unlocked or bolted, recorded at intervals so players see each carrier and read changes. When: Designing key custody and locked doors for an investigation game. Why: Logged handovers and door states let players reconstruct access and reason about who could have entered. (x2)
- [rule] Track each key as an owned item in game state, so removing or moving it changes who may enter and when a theft could have occurred. When: Designing locked-room puzzles, lock-and-key puzzles or theft-based mysteries. Why: Custody state keeps access logic consistent and gives the player a basis for timeline deduction. (x2)
- [rule] Move a key through NPCs and containers in a fixed order across scenes, requiring two characters to agree before it opens, and log each transfer so players can trace its path. When: Designing a key item's route or a quest item that passes between NPCs. Why: A visible, defined trail lets the player reconstruct the key's history and sequence.
- [rule] Model lock states and missing keys as a set of routes the player can rule out one at a time.
- [rule] Turn an item's route through staff routines, such as cleaning schedules, into a tracing puzzle the player can follow.
- [rule] Require a paired item to open a two-part lock, such as a double key with two ends, and provide a diagram that matches the level layout. When: when designing locks that need two parts to open. Why: Pairing items creates a dependency the player can verify against the plan.
- [rule] Make a key held by an NPC who recently left an area a traceable item, so players can reconstruct where a captive was taken. When: Designing an item trail that leads to a hidden location. Why: Item possession ties an NPC to places and times.
- [heuristic] Tie each lock to its known keyholders so players narrow suspects by method and intruders stay few when entry to a sealed space must be restricted. When: Designing a locked-room or break-in clue, or a room that must be kept from unauthorised entry. Why: Method-based filtering turns a lock into a deduction tool. (x2)
- [heuristic] A key's lock type can identify which entry it opens, linking an item to a specific door or route. When: an item looks like a key with no known lock. Why: the lock type narrows which entry the key belongs to.
- [heuristic] Place each object where its owner could reach it, so a missing item can be traced to who had access. When: designing object placement in a scene. Why: Location links an object to a set of possible people.
- [heuristic] Place a sealed door's key with another character or in another room, so entry becomes a locked-room puzzle with an intended bypass. When: Designing access puzzles. Why: Separating the key from the door forces the player to find the key or a different route.
- [heuristic] Place keys in unexpected locations so that access to a locked container changes depending on who holds the key. When: When designing locked containers and their access rules. Why: Misplaced keys shift suspicion to people who could not have been expected to reach the container.
- [heuristic] Label each locked container with the key that opens it, and place the keys where the player can find them, so no lock is a surprise. When: Designing locked containers and their keys in a level. Why: Identified locks let the player plan their search instead of being blocked by hidden obstacles.
- [heuristic] Place a copy of a key elsewhere so a key the player believes is unique can be duplicated. When: Designing key-based access puzzles. Why: Hidden copies reward players who check whether apparent exclusivity holds.
- [heuristic] When one person holds the only key to a locked room, treat that person as the natural suspect and build the mechanism within the room's fixed fixtures.
- [tradeoff] A key ledger works well when entry is central, but it is weak when keys can be copied or the lock can be bypassed without a key.
- [pitfall] A single lock can fail and be retried repeatedly, so a door and key alone buy only seconds of protection.
- [pitfall] Verify who actually held each key, since key ownership may be shared or disputed. When: an access claim depends on who held a key. Why: A recorded owner may not be the person who actually had the key at the time.
- [pitfall] A lock that opens too easily wastes tension, so the key should cost the searcher some effort to find. When: When setting how hard it is to open a container. Why: Effortless access removes the sense of discovery.

## physical trace evidence
- [mechanism] A wound on a creature can be mirrored on a linked NPC, letting the player verify the connection by inspecting the mark or attacking and observing the result. When: When a creature and a character are linked in a game. Why: Checkable mirrored injuries let the player confirm the link through inspection or direct action.
- [mechanism] Neglected features such as an idle well handle or rotten rope show that nobody visits, so objects hidden there can stay unseen for a long time. When: a location shows signs of disuse. Why: disuse means fewer visitors who might discover concealed items.
- [rule] Build a death scene with a deliberately absent struggle, and let players check the verdict against physical traces such as footprints and servant accounts. When: When designing a death scene that appears self-inflicted. Why: Absences invite the player to test the apparent explanation.
- [rule] Reward players for inspecting objects before moving on, since decay-based room-state puzzles depend on that inspection. When: Designing puzzles that use object state as a clock. Why: Clues are only usable if the player examined them.
- [rule] Let a broken object's rust or wear show how old the damage is, rewarding careful inspection.
- [rule] Keep at least one reliable physical feature in a deceptive space, since a place that misleads on every detail becomes arbitrary. When: Designing a setting whose appearance is partly misleading. Why: A fixed reference lets players judge which other details to trust.
