- [rule] Use footprint trails as tracking evidence that leads toward hidden rooms or a locked door that has been opened. When: Designing clues that point to secret areas. Why: Trails turn a visual trace into a navigation lead.
- [heuristic] Give remains or disguised NPCs permanent marks such as rings, scars or tattoos that the player can check against a missing NPC's known features or an unmasked identity. When: When designing bodies, remains or disguised enemies as clues. Why: Checkable permanent marks turn identification into a player inference, since costumes can be changed. (x2)
- [heuristic] Let a player inspect an NPC's wound with a light or magnifier, and make its pattern contradict the NPC's stated cause to point to a suspect. When: Designing clue-based wounds on NPCs. Why: Inspection with a tool turns a mismatch into a discoverable clue.
- [heuristic] Let players order events from body signs such as pooling, temperature or stiffness, and give an NPC medic a time range they must narrow with other clues. When: When designing a corpse or collapse puzzle. Why: Signs the player can read make the timeline a solvable inference rather than a guess.
- [heuristic] Let physical states that change over time, such as stiffening or moved furniture, reveal the real cause of an event and reward players who inspect late.
- [heuristic] A stealth or tracking mechanic can use intermittent signs such as ripples, ash, footprints and sounds that the player must chain together. When: when designing a pursuit or stealth encounter. Why: Chained clues reward attentive players.
- [heuristic] When a key witness cannot speak, leave physical clues they left behind, giving the player another route to the answer. When: A witness is unable to give testimony. Why: Environmental evidence can substitute for lost testimony and keep the investigation going.
- [heuristic] Place a clue on a surface the player inspects with a tool that reveals invisible traces, and let a skilled actor's technique leave a recognisable residue. When: Designing hidden-trace clues. Why: Missing traces then point to method, which the player can learn to recognise.
- [procedure] 1) Find traces at each affected site. 2) Connect them in order of occurrence. 3) Check for sites with no traces. 4) Read the line of traces as the actor's path and target selection. When: Reconstructing where an unseen creature or NPC moved through a space. Why: Sites with no traces show what the actor avoided or ignored.
- [pitfall] A smooth surface can look like a protective covering; examining only one side of a residue fragment can lead to a wrong identification. Why: Both faces of a fragment must be checked before deciding its origin.
- [pitfall] Trails that lead into corners and back again suggest a search or an unknown intruder rather than a single route. When: Interpreting a messy footprint trail. Why: Looping paths indicate searching, not one clear passage.
- [fact] Scorch marks, ash rings, warm or damp objects, residue, smells and stains reveal recent activity, giving a timeline or contradicting a claim.
- [fact] Physical traces such as dust and cobwebs are less reliable when others may have disturbed them. When: others may have entered the scene.
- [fact] Tracks in loose ground show that someone has moved through a space even when no one is seen.
- [fact] Disturbed wrappings and a missing object at a body show that it was touched and what was taken.

## scene clearing
- [mechanism] An NPC's partial, subjective description of a stranger can be matched by the player to a physical marker placed in the level. When: Designing NPC clues that point to a level object. Why: Incomplete descriptions reward the player for linking clues to the world.
- [rule] Protect a scene with a covering, barrier or marker so repeated foot traffic does not destroy or confuse its evidence. Why: Repeated foot traffic can destroy or confuse evidence.
- [rule] Let witnesses give partial descriptions, such as height or build, so the player can narrow suspects without a full identity.
- [rule] Let a distinctive but partial NPC description drive a search across a bounded area, such as one town block.
- [rule] Record each object's position and state when a scene is first examined, so later disturbances can be detected against the original.
- [rule] Track the time since each scene was last inspected, so the player can judge whether a trace would still be present. When: Levels where traces decay, such as footprints fading or water drying. Why: Players can reason about trace survival only if they know how long ago the scene was examined.
- [rule] Let players examine how a scene was arranged, such as tie patterns or disturbed objects, to detect a false trail. When: When a level includes a staged scene the player can inspect. Why: Inspectable arrangement turns the scene into a detectable false trail.
- [rule] Offer a secure-the-scene action that seals rooms and designates a key custodian; each choice carries a time or reputation penalty.
- [rule] Flag a scene as altered when someone disturbs it without authority, and record that it was disturbed.
- [rule] Search the floor with a lens and a close crawl for small or faint traces before the scene is disturbed further.
- [rule] Design scene evidence whose meaning depends on a second observation the player can make, such as checking the weather.
- [heuristic] Examine a scene before others alter it, since traces degrade when characters walk through or handle objects, such as footprints being walked over. When: when a scene is at risk of contamination by other characters; designing investigation scenes with fragile evidence. Why: Handling by others can destroy traces the player needs; time pressure adds stakes to first inspection.
- [heuristic] Reward players who cannot see a room but can hear it by letting them learn plans from sound before entering. When: Designing rooms where sound carries information. Why: Sound gives players advance knowledge that changes how they approach the room.
- [procedure] 1) Measure distances between marks with a tool. 2) Inspect written or marked text closely, character by character. 3) Collect loose material samples into a container for later analysis. When: when examining a disturbed or contested scene. Why: Measured and preserved evidence can be analysed after the scene changes.
- [procedure] 1) Clear the empty room first. 2) Search the room where the disturbance was heard. 3) Search the place where the injured person lay.
- [procedure] 1) Read posture, object positions and lighting as traces of events. 2) Propose a sequence of events for each victim. 3) Mark each reading as a hypothesis and test it against other traces. When: when reconstructing a scene from physical evidence. Why: A single reading can fit posture yet miss the actual route taken, so cross-checking is needed.
- [procedure] 1) Control all exits so people leave only through checked routes. 2) Search each person and parcel at the checkpoint. 3) Sweep every zone for small items. 4) Collect and log all fragments before releasing anyone.
- [procedure] 1) Partition the space and assign each searcher a side or set of containers. 2) Work toward the middle. 3) Compare notes where the sweeps meet.
- [pitfall] A framed character who never resists weakens the story, so show their attempts to defend themselves.
- [pitfall] A suspect is not cleared when only one condition has been tested; every condition must be checked before dropping them.
- [pitfall] Dusting, cleaning or relighting a scene before inspection destroys evidence and makes later observation unreliable.

## barriers and obstacles
- [mechanism] A barricade that holds for a limited number of hits and cracks before breaking gives the defender an audible warning of the threat.
- [behavior] When people close to a victim refuse to help, enquiries stall and the official explanation becomes easier to adopt.
- [rule] Do not treat a barricade or bolted door as proof that nobody entered the room.
- [rule] Keep an unavailable alibi witness blocked until the player finds another route to reach them.
- [rule] Place artificial barriers only where terrain fails to block movement, so walls appear only where nature leaves a gap.
- [rule] Make pursuit obstacles look either random or planted, so players cannot tell which at first and tension stays high. When: Placing obstacles in a chase. Why: Uncertainty about intent keeps the player watchful.
- [rule] Check the bolt type on a door that looks usable: a solid turn-bolt cannot be worked from outside, while a slip-bolt can. When: when assessing whether a door could be opened from outside.
- [rule] Use a blocked door as a locked-room obstacle that requires finding the blocking object before it opens. When: Designing a level with blocked passages.
- [heuristic] A deliberately misleading clue or false detail is evidence of motive: its design reveals what the staging party or witness wanted to hide or protect, unlike an accidental error. When: Evaluating a clue that seems deliberately misleading, or a witness description that would make identification difficult. Why: Staging and chosen falsehoods serve a purpose that accidental errors do not.
- [heuristic] A bolt being shot does not by itself rule out entry; test the bolt for wear, residue or recent movement before accepting the claim.
- [heuristic] Re-check each barrier's timing. A barrier may have been set at a different time than its usual schedule, creating a gap to weigh.
- [heuristic] Do not assume a barred or bolted route is closed without checking whether it can be opened from the other side.
- [heuristic] A measured warded boundary with a clear edge gives the player a checkable rule about what may cross it. When: When designing magical or protective barriers. Why: A precise boundary turns a protection into a testable rule.
- [procedure] 1) Identify every barrier of a sealed space. 2) Test each from the near side. 3) Test each again from the far side. 4) Inspect adjoining spaces for physical traces of use. When: Checking whether a sealed room has a hidden route. Why: A single-side check leaves open routes undetected.
- [metric] Measure barrier heights; a gate's height decides whether a person could climb over it without opening it.
- [pitfall] Treating a partly blocked observation as complete gives false confidence in what was actually seen.
- [pitfall] Assuming a barricade is sturdy. Combined force can break an old door at once. When: When a barrier blocks access to a room. Why: Barriers that look solid may give way under concentrated effort.
- [pitfall] A witness who found a bolted door silent may be right about the door, so reconcile their account with physical traces of how the room was entered or left.
- [pitfall] A locked or barricaded door can mislead investigators into searching for a hidden mechanism when the real route lies elsewhere.
- [fact] A genuine trace can still lead nowhere, so keep alternative explanations open when following a lead. When: Pursuing a single physical lead. Why: A real trace does not guarantee that it connects to the culprit.

## fixture state and change
- [mechanism] A feature seen earlier in neutral form changes meaning when it reappears under new conditions, becoming a route or a warning. When: when the same environment element returns later. Why: the changed context gives the old detail new function.
- [mechanism] Failed forced-entry attempts can leave persistent scratches as world state that guards can later discover. When: a lock or object is tampered with and not repaired.
- [mechanism] A found object that raises a new question can serve as the trigger for the player's next goal.
- [mechanism] A fixture that changes state, such as a phone marked out of order, redirects where people go and what they do in a space. When: When a fixture's state affects movement or access.
- [mechanism] A switch can be operated from beneath the floor by a line through a floor hole, cutting power without any visible wiring.
- [rule] Give each room or character a known set of expected items, so a missing item such as a key, tool or cup becomes a clue about who moved it. When: Designing a space or character with possessions, or rooms with clue objects. Why: Known sets make absence readable.
- [rule] Test whether a fitting performs its apparent job; a mismatch between construction or position and function may mean it is a dummy or a conduit to another space. When: A fixture's appearance suggests a plain use. Why: Appearance alone does not reveal whether a fitting works.
- [rule] Show the player a partial trace of a hidden change, such as a missing item or fresh mark, so it can be deduced.
- [rule] Hide a key or document in a pocket or torn seam on NPC or player gear, and let a tear show that an item was pulled out in a fight.
- [rule] Connect a fixture to a route, such as a vent into an adjacent room, so players must reason about what could pass through it.
- [rule] Place injuries, fixtures and furniture so the player can measure their relation, such as a wound matching a lamp bracket's height.
- [rule] Name locations so the label hints at their function, such as a name fragment that suggests a sluice, so players can reason from the name.
- [rule] Make a recorded or automated sound suggest a room was occupied at a time when it was not, giving players a false timeline to test. When: designing a timed clue for players. Why: players must test the timeline against other evidence.
- [rule] Plant a device that fails under certain conditions, so the player's trial and error reveals how the mechanism works.
- [rule] Hold a ranged device on a fixed line with a clamp or fixture so its shot travels in a predictable direction. When: when a device must hit a specific point without a person aiming it. Why: a steady mount keeps the trajectory consistent.
- [rule] Give hidden-object puzzles one partial change that fits no obvious purpose, to signal an unfinished plan or a second actor. When: designing a hidden-object clue. Why: A purposeless partial change invites the player to question the obvious explanation.
- [heuristic] Show a routine, such as a steady wind, water level or guard rotation, before changing it, so the change reads as a clue.
- [heuristic] Equipment such as a hidden-lining helmet or a traceable loot item can reveal who was prepared for a fight. When: designing items that carry clues about their owner. Why: Preparation shown through gear tells players who anticipated conflict.
- [fact] Furniture clamped to the floor keeps a constant position relative to nearby fixtures, giving players a stable reference for planning paths or traps.
- [fact] Physical features carry meaning, such as a line of floating barriers marking a tidal approach to a settlement.

## measurement and access checks
- [mechanism] A route whose required reach or height is a measurable value gates access, so players deduce it from their character's stats; confirm feasibility by measuring rather than assuming. When: In level design with climbing or reach-based traversal, when designing or evaluating routes requiring a specific height or ability. Why: Measured values turn a plausible claim into a testable one, and tying access to a measurable value lets players deduce the route from their character's capabilities.
- [rule] Log exact measurements alongside notes; dimensions may either confirm or exclude a match.
- [rule] Use measured physical evidence, such as a demonstrated height or reach, to test whether a suspect fits the act. When: When the act depends on a physical attribute. Why: Measured fit is more reliable than impression.
- [rule] Build impossible-seeming access, such as a moving vehicle or high window, that players can measure and test. When: Designing a location with an apparently inaccessible entry or exit. Why: Measurable access lets players reason their way to a confirmed solution.
- [rule] Tie a hidden stash to a routine the player can observe, such as when keys move, so players learn the access pattern before using it.
- [rule] Make a thrown object's landing spot or a wound's position reveal who acted and how, so players can measure and compare them.
- [rule] Show a discrepancy the player can measure, such as a feature differing slightly between two states, rather than a vague change, so it can lead to a hidden mechanism like a secret door. When: Designing a clue that hints at a hidden door or moving wall. Why: A measurable difference gives the player a concrete lead to follow.
- [heuristic] Small measurement errors compound over distance and can miss the exact target; verify a bearing-and-distance result with a physical check before committing. When: When locating a spot by paces, bearings or projected shadows, or a search depends on a measured offset from a reference point. Why: Each small offset compounds over a long measurement, so an unverified bearing can miss the target though it may confirm the general path. (x2)
- [heuristic] Judge objects and traces by measurable properties, such as weight or how far a stub was smoked, rather than appearance or brand, since surfaces can hide differences. When: When two objects look identical or a trace is judged by a surface feature. Why: Appearance can hide a difference in material or construction.
- [heuristic] Check a witness's measurements against the place itself, since a claim built on dimensions can be tested directly. When: when an account depends on measured quantities such as distances or heights. Why: Measurements can be verified on site where memories of events cannot.
- [heuristic] Where an object contacts the body, its width and placement suggest body measurements, such as nose-base width or eye spacing.
- [heuristic] Measured distances, such as how far a car went before a look back or how far a body lay ahead, act as checkable constraints on the timeline. Why: Distances turn vague recollections into values that can be tested against travel times.
- [heuristic] When a witness recalls a physical state, ask for a measurement, since even a rough estimate gives a checkable value.
- [heuristic] Tie examination rights to a permission token such as a badge, and let partial permission unlock only limited tasks. When: when designing access to evidence or locked areas. Why: partial grants give a sense of progress without full access.
- [heuristic] Give the player a small set of observable tests, such as a handle pull or keyhole check, so a locked-room deduction has footing. When: Designing locked-room puzzles. Why: A deduction needs tests the player can actually perform.
- [heuristic] Whenever a witness claims to have seen something across a space, verify the geometry, including distance, lighting and reflection, against the plan. When: a witness reports a sighting across a shaft or room. Why: Lighting and reflection can extend what is visible beyond what a plan suggests.
- [heuristic] Let containers hold a countable amount, so a player can deduce how much was used by measuring what remains. When: designing consumable containers as clues. Why: A measurable quantity lets the player check a theory numerically.
- [heuristic] Make a substitute's description almost match the target but differ in one detail, so the mismatch becomes a clue. When: When designing impersonation or substitution clues. Why: Near-matches are plausible at first but reveal the fraud on closer inspection.
- [heuristic] Let the player replay a room's noise levels to check whether a reported event was possible. When: Designing an investigation of a reported event. Why: Replaying conditions lets the player test witness claims against the setting.
- [pitfall] Measured distances given in old or unfamiliar units can be misread, so the searcher must confirm the units before digging. When: Distances are recorded in historical or unfamiliar units. Why: A unit mismatch places the search at the wrong distance.

## stealth and surveillance
- [mechanism] A stealth level where one NPC can change outfit or role makes the player's sightings conflict until they find where the swap happened. When: Designing disguise mechanics in a game. Why: Conflicting sightings drive the player to discover the swap location.
- [mechanism] Track whether a character is present in the scene versus visible, so a scripted alibi can be broken by a hidden route. When: A character's alibi relies on a headcount or witness observation. Why: A person can be counted as present while being out of sight.
- [behavior] On a quiet street, a watcher hears an approaching footstep well before the figure is visible, so sound gives early warning of a match. When: when ambient noise is low and sightlines are blocked. Why: Small sounds carry in quiet surroundings, so they arrive before sight.
- [rule] Give a non-human agent observable limits, such as one item at a time or no speech, so players can deduce its role from its behavior. When: When designing a creature or automated agent that players must reason about. Why: Clear, observable limits let players infer what the agent can and cannot have done.
- [rule] Avoid a stakeout when the target has an independent way in, such as a key, because the watcher cannot stop the entry. When: when the target can enter without passing the watched point. Why: Watching a fixed point cannot intercept an alternate route.
- [rule] Include deliberate distractions such as a character who changes the subject, and mark them so players can tell them apart from real clues. When: when placing red herrings in a mystery. Why: Clear marking keeps the distraction fair while still misleading careless reasoning.
- [heuristic] Use shifting light and line of sight to hide a threat until the player moves into position, so a harmless-looking shape becomes an enemy. When: Designing lighting for a stealth or reveal moment, or placing enemies in low-visibility areas. Why: Changing light alters what the player perceives, and delayed visibility creates surprise and rewards careful movement.
- [heuristic] A stealth or infiltration mission with a costume or alias disguise, where NPC reactions depend on identity and a reputation check fails if the disguise is too well known, adds replay and tension. When: Designing stealth or infiltration missions where the player takes on a false identity. Why: Identity-dependent NPC reactions and a recognition check make the disguise consequential and tie it to the player's notoriety.
- [heuristic] Place cover objects such as graves or hedges near meeting points so the player can watch unseen and follow after participants leave. When: Designing a meeting space for stealth play. Why: Cover supports watching, and the player can then track the group's movement.
- [heuristic] Create patrol windows and chokepoints where the player can wait, with sound cues that reveal approach, and vary guard route timing so the player must watch at different hours to find the pattern. When: when designing guard routes and waiting spots. Why: Sound reveals approach before sight, and varied timing forces the player to observe over time.
- [heuristic] Use light, sound or fire as timed checkpoints in a level so the player can build a timeline, and give NPC alibis that the player can check against them. When: when designing time-based clue systems. Why: Environmental timers let the player verify alibis independently.
- [heuristic] A hidden device placed in furniture or a floor seam and discovered late can produce a dramatic payoff in a stealth or setup puzzle. When: Designing a stealth or planting puzzle where the player places a hidden listening device. Why: The delayed discovery turns concealment into a payoff rather than a dead end.
- [heuristic] A disguise or pretext item can unlock a guarded area, with a guard who may check it. When: Designing a guarded area that the player must enter. Why: A verification step creates tension and a risk of failure.
- [heuristic] Let players map sightlines and choose where to place a tool, rewarding them for noticing the one hidden spot. When: Designing a stealth or surveillance puzzle in a fixed room. Why: Reading the room becomes the skill being tested.
- [heuristic] Hidden surveillance suits events whose location is known in advance and where responders can wait close by; it fails when the sensor can be found or destroyed. When: Choosing whether a covert sensor fits an event.
- [heuristic] Place a key event in a blind spot, such as off-screen or behind an obstruction, and reward the player for returning to examine it. When: Designing a critical event the player must later discover. Why: Events outside the player's view are easy to miss on first pass.
- [heuristic] Let a hidden character watch a room the player thinks is empty, revealed only after the player has acted on a wrong theory. When: Designing a reveal in an enclosed location. Why: A late reveal of a watcher reframes the player's earlier actions.
- [procedure] 1) Pick a hidden post with a clear view of a fixed destination. 2) Watch at varied times of day so no period goes uncovered. 3) Stay out of the line of sight. 4) Wait until the target arrives. When: when the target has a fixed destination and the watcher cannot approach directly. Why: Covering all times and staying hidden keeps the watch effective.
- [procedure] 1) Count the doors between the listener and the unseen source. 2) Mark each door so any movement shows. 3) Note which marked door moved to locate the event. When: a sound is heard but its source cannot be watched.
- [pitfall] A watcher who is seen loses the advantage, so a watch post must stay concealed. When: when the player or guard holds a surveillance position. Why: Discovery turns the watcher from observer into target.

## testimony and suspect logic
- [mechanism] A physical trace that contradicts an NPC's testimony can prompt the player to revisit a locked door or check a second route the NPC denied. Why: The conflict between evidence and account gives the player a reason to re-examine the scene.
- [rule] Resolve conflicting NPC accounts with a verifiable environmental mechanic, such as a door, stair, weather, a lever's pivot or a reflection, so players need not decide who lies. When: Two NPC accounts of the same event disagree. Why: A verifiable physical cause lets players resolve the contradiction without deciding who lies, and rewards careful observation. (x2)
- [rule] Tie a confession opportunity to a place or schedule the controlling figure rarely leaves, so the player must time the approach.
- [rule] Give each suspect a fixed trait, such as temper or hobby, so players can rule out suspects whose traits do not fit the crime. When: Building a murder-mystery scenario with several non-player suspects. Why: Fixed traits let players eliminate suspects by reasoning rather than guesswork.
- [rule] Use a trusted authority figure with a visible motive and a physical link to the crime, so a late reveal recasts earlier clues.
- [rule] Build puzzles that combine a character trait with a scene feature, such as a non-swimmer and a pond, so the player can rule out a route.
- [rule] Time a withheld name, shown only in a later scene or record, to match the player's readiness to act on it. When: A key identity is revealed in a game. Why: Revealing too early or too late reduces the impact of the reveal.
- [rule] Let the player control the order in which suspect NPCs are presented during the reveal.
- [rule] Plant a found item in an NPC's inventory, and place a witness NPC who saw the act where the player can find and question them to clear the accused.
- [heuristic] A social hub can host several NPCs whose versions of an event differ, so the player must cross-check their accounts.
- [heuristic] Keep tells ambiguous so a faint or tears could be innocent, which keeps the player assembling a case rather than reading a flag. When: When designing NPC tells that signal guilt. Why: Ambiguous reactions require the player to combine evidence rather than accept a single signal.
- [heuristic] Require a final accusation to rest on several linked clues, and make each link visible so the player can see which inference is still missing. When: Designing a final deduction or accusation mechanic. Why: Visible links let the player judge their own reasoning and find the gap.
- [heuristic] Track whether the player accepts an NPC's pivot, and if they do, mark the contradiction as unresolved so it can return later. When: A player lets a suspect's emotional pivot end the questioning. Why: Keeping the contradiction open lets it resurface as a later hook.
- [heuristic] Reveal motive through a sequence of NPC conversations, letters and locations visited in order, each adding a piece. When: When designing a backstory the player must assemble. Why: Ordered pieces let the player build the motive progressively.
- [heuristic] Opportunity alone does not prove guilt, but it reduces the set of candidates who could have acted. When: physical access limits who could have committed an act. Why: Excluding people who could not reach the scene shrinks the suspect pool without proving anyone's guilt.
- [heuristic] An NPC interrogation can offer a timed question with a short window, and a companion's glance toward the speaker can serve as a visible tell the player must notice. When: Designing an NPC dialogue with multiple speakers. Why: Timed prompts and visible tells reward attentive players.
- [heuristic] For a mystic NPC who claims magical sight, give the player a test, such as whether the NPC could actually have seen the object, to separate showmanship from real knowledge. When: A mystic NPC claims knowledge of an event. Why: A checkable test exposes whether the claimed power matches the actual access.
- [pitfall] Accepting that a door or window is found fastened without checking its mechanism, or dismissing a route as impossible because it looks awkward. Why: appearance and difficulty are not evidence that an exit is closed.
- [pitfall] A misfit noticed from one angle may vanish from another, so note a tell at the moment it is first seen.
- [pitfall] Interpreting facial expressions as a specific emotion is subjective, since the same expression can reflect several different emotions. When: when reading a victim's expression as evidence. Why: Expressions are ambiguous and open to several interpretations.

## npc behavior and reveals
- [mechanism] Tie an NPC's decline to a visible change in a gameplay object, so the player can track progress without relying on dialogue. Why: A visible state conveys progress.
- [rule] Set window heights, ledges and reach distances so that the player can infer which character types could have entered a location. When: When designing a location that restricts which characters could have acted. Why: Physical limits let the player eliminate suspects using only the scene.
- [rule] Let gossip NPCs seed suspicion about a faction, and require the player to find a record such as a ledger or arrest entry to turn rumour into proof. When: Designing a faction that the player must investigate. Why: Rumour motivates investigation, while records supply verifiable proof.
- [rule] Place a helper NPC inside a sealed room whose timed action reveals the hidden access route.
- [rule] Make hidden contact messages refer to real location details so players can verify them against the game world. Why: Verifiable details let players judge whether a message is genuine.
- [rule] Hide a key character in a location the player has already cleared and labelled a dead end, then expose them through a small domestic clue. When: designing a character who must be found later.
- [rule] Show an ally's disappearance first as a clue, then reveal its cause once the player reaches a specific location.
- [rule] Have a captive NPC give the final clue after the player frees her, unlocking the last room of the reveal.
- [heuristic] Give NPCs physical tells, such as a dropped object, pause or pet's retreat, tied to sensitive topics, readable as clues rather than verdicts. When: When designing NPC reactions for confrontation scenes or sensitive subjects. Why: Visible tells let players read stress without relying on dialogue, and add information while leaving room for ambiguity.
- [heuristic] In any room where a private talk happens, check large cupboards and similar enclosures for space to conceal a listener. Why: Concealed observers can hear conversations that seem private.
- [heuristic] A rustle outside a locked room suggests someone is listening, so check the hall and consider what an eavesdropper would want to learn. When: A noise is heard outside a room where a conversation takes place. Why: A listener in the hall may be watching the investigation.
- [heuristic] A hidden access point lets a player overhear targets who believe they are alone, and their unguarded talk can reveal plans. When: a hidden access point lets the player stay unseen near the targets.
- [heuristic] Give a fallback NPC instructions to reveal the solution if the player fails to return in time. When: Designing a time-limited reveal that depends on the player's return. Why: A fallback keeps the reveal from being lost if the player misses the window.
- [heuristic] In a gathering scene, each character's reaction to a shared event, including tone and omissions, can expose motives.
- [heuristic] Hide an acquaintance between two NPCs, revealed only when the player asks the right question or cross-checks a record such as a guest list. When: Designing social connections the player must uncover. Why: Hidden links reward investigation and make cross-checking records meaningful.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC show several cues, such as untidy dress, a half-finished meal and nervous speech, that the player combines into a guess. When: When designing NPC behavior that lets the player infer recent activity. Why: A set of cues supports inference, while no single cue gives the answer outright.
- [heuristic] Show a character locking a door as a visible tell that they are concealing a state from others. When: When designing a character who keeps others out of a room. Why: A visible lock signals concealment to the player.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC's sudden movement toward a key object signal that the object matters, so the player notices it. When: Designing NPC behavior around important items. Why: Physical tells direct player attention without exposition.
- [heuristic] Give a non-player character one habitual behaviour that players learn to read, so they can predict how it will act in an unscripted encounter. When: Designing NPCs whose behaviour should be learnable. Why: A consistent habit lets players form and test predictions.

## pacing and suspense
- [rule] End each level area on an open detail, such as an unexplained locked door, that becomes the next objective and gives the player a reason to keep moving. When: Structuring a sequence of level areas or a level meant to sustain forward motion. Why: An open question the player carries keeps the level pulling them forward.
- [rule] Pair every stall point with a new stimulus, like an alarm, a visitor, or a fresh clue, so players keep moving. When: designing paced puzzle or investigation sections.
- [rule] Alternate open exploration with confined interview spaces so attention narrows gradually toward key rooms and witnesses. When: When structuring the pacing of an investigation area. Why: Shifting space types steers focus.
- [rule] Show a deadline on screen alongside reversals, such as a secret found already gone, to keep pacing tight.
- [rule] Introduce a noisy or joke item, such as a bell or chime, early with recorded properties, so a later puzzle can refer back to them.
- [rule] End a scene at the peak of danger with the outcome withheld until the player makes the next choice. When: Designing a scene's ending. Why: Withheld outcomes keep tension and force a decision.
- [rule] Use a scheduled gathering as a suspense gap, and let players pursue other investigation threads during the wait. When: A main objective is temporarily blocked by a timed event. Why: Parallel threads keep players engaged while the blocked objective resolves.
- [heuristic] Calm stretches before or between threats, like scenery, small talk, exploration lulls or quiet rooms, make the next danger or ambush land harder through contrast. When: Leading players toward danger; designing encounter rhythm or a sequence of dangerous spaces. Why: Contrast with calm moments makes threats sharper; a lull builds expectation that the strike releases; recovery sharpens the next threat. (x2)
- [heuristic] Pause or remove ambient sound, such as dripping water or a clock, just before a reveal or threshold opens, so the stakes register and the next sight or sound stands out. When: Designing a threshold or major reveal moment. Why: Delay makes the opening feel consequential; silence makes the next sound or sight stand out. (x2)
- [heuristic] Reveal events out of chronological order through found notes or journal fragments, and control when each actor appears, so information reaches the player in a shaped sequence. When: Pacing a scene or investigation that reveals clues. (x2)
- [heuristic] Use NPC interruptions in dialogue as a pacing lever that delays a reveal without removing it.
- [heuristic] Let an NPC warning that the player ignored return as the means of escape, rewarding players who heeded or noted it. When: Designing NPC dialogue that foreshadows a later event. Why: Delayed payoff makes earlier choices feel meaningful and rewards attention.
- [heuristic] Environmental triggers such as sudden stillness or stirring leaves before an ambush can warn players of nearby danger. When: Designing an ambush or hidden threat in a level. Why: Players learn to read such cues as signs of an imminent encounter.
- [heuristic] Delay revealing a link between events until the player has enough evidence, so the connection feels earned. When: When designing a multi-event mystery or quest chain. Why: A connection revealed too early feels arbitrary; one earned through evidence feels satisfying.
- [heuristic] Rotating suspicion through NPCs, each shift triggering a set-piece event, can pace a level.
- [tradeoff] Obstacles such as forced doors or embedded projectiles add urgency and keep players moving, but a run of delays without progress makes them impatient; alternate obstacles with progress. When: Pacing a long route, investigation, or sequence of obstacles leading toward a hidden outcome. Why: Each obstacle costs time so pressure builds before the outcome, but consecutive delays turn eagerness into impatience.
- [pitfall] Letting a wait run too long makes its cues stop feeling meaningful, so the buildup loses its force. When: a suspense sequence holds the player in waiting. Why: Cues depend on the player still associating them with an approaching event.
- [pitfall] Ending the mission cleanly right after a reveal can leave the player feeling the solution was hollow; withhold or undercut the reward to keep consequences meaningful. When: a reveal is the climax of a mission or quest. Why: A reward taken or a culprit released makes the outcome feel unresolved and memorable.
- [pitfall] Too many false alarms dull the audience, so each should lead somewhere or be verifiable later. When: Pacing ambiguous cues in a level. Why: Unresolved false alarms lose their tension over time.

## darkness and low light
- [mechanism] In a dark room, an attack from behind leaves only a partial account, so the investigation depends on physical traces and the victim's limited perception. When: A victim is attacked where vision is limited. Why: Limited sight produces a narrow witness account.
- [mechanism] Removing or breaking a light source creates a darkness window in which a concealed actor can move unseen.
- [behavior] In dim light a victim close to an attacker may still have seen him, so if she never names him, her silence becomes evidence to weigh. When: a victim or witness saw an attacker at close range in low light. Why: Low light still gives some visibility, so darkness alone does not rule out recognition.
- [behavior] In darkness, a witness's eyes adjust slowly, so they may believe they see more than they do or mistake faint light for a clear view. When: the witness has moved from light into dim or dark conditions. Why: eye adaptation lags behind the change in light.
- [behavior] A dim or shaded room limits what a witness can see, so their account depends on where they stood. When: The scene is lit by a single lamp or otherwise darkened. Why: Darkness hides identity and detail.
- [behavior] In darkness, witnesses may report only what they could touch or hear, not what they could see.
- [behavior] A person groping toward a switch suggests the room was dark, and their route should match where the switch sits. When: a witness describes moving through a dark space. Why: Reaching for a switch reveals the lack of light.
- [rule] Let dark or fogged areas be mapped through counted, paced movement, paired with map fragments or wall features so players cross-check and correct step-count estimates. When: dark-area mapping or puzzle.
- [rule] Make lighting a gameplay variable: in dim areas, detection is less accurate and objects can look like something else until the player moves closer. When: Designing detection in dim or dark areas.
- [heuristic] Darkness is not a clean alibi when many people saw who was near the controlling switch.
- [heuristic] Contrast a lit, crowded public area with narrow dark lanes; the change of light and noise marks the approach to danger. When: When designing a route into a dangerous place. Why: Light and sound shifts build suspense.
- [heuristic] A dark observation post with a view into a lit space lets the observer watch while staying unseen, because light contrast hides the watcher. When: Placing a watcher's position in a level. Why: Lit areas are visible from outside while dark areas conceal the watcher.
- [heuristic] Use a low-light room with an unclear figure to hide a body or trap until the player commits to looking closer. When: Designing a dark room with a hidden threat. Why: Players must commit to inspection before the threat is revealed.
- [procedure] 1) Use the scene to infer the most likely hiding spot. 2) Spend light only on that spot. 3) If nothing is seen, feel the surface by touch. 4) Lift any loose piece and check beneath. When: when light is scarce and the search area is large. Why: Targeted light and touch conserve a limited supply.
- [procedure] 1) Switch off the room light 2) Show the object with its faint distinguishing mark 3) Ask a witness to name the object. When: a witness must identify an object in poor light.
- [tradeoff] Darkness hides an act well but is fragile: one light source or lucky glance can break it, while it also hides the actors' own movements. When: Choosing darkness as the mechanism for a hidden event. Why: Concealment strong enough to hide acts is also prone to sudden exposure.
- [pitfall] Brief, dim lighting makes it easy to swap two similar objects and return the wrong one.
- [pitfall] A map made in good light can miss details that matter in darkness, such as which doors were closed.

## forced entry and damage
- [mechanism] Forcing a joint during rigidity leaves it loose afterward, so a limp joint amid set joints signals tampering. When: A body is rigid except for one loose joint. Why: Forcing breaks the joint's stiffening.
- [mechanism] Forcing a barrier can destroy the traces of earlier tampering, so the act that seems to confirm the lock hides the trick. When: the barrier is forced open by those trying to help.
- [mechanism] A fragile prop left in a covert entry path can add a penalty, rewarding players who clear it before entering. When: designing covert entry routes.
- [mechanism] Forcing a locked door takes several attempts and leaves damage, and the broken bolt becomes a physical trace of the entry. When: when a key is missing and the lock must be defeated.
- [mechanism] A locked obstacle can resist a weak first attempt and yield to a stronger second attempt, signalling the player to find better means. When: designing locked doors. Why: Graduated failure teaches the player that the obstacle is passable.
- [heuristic] A lock opened without tool marks or damage suggests a genuine key or copy, since forcing or picking usually leaves visible marks. Why: Visual evidence tracks how the lock was opened.
- [heuristic] A forced lock is evidence in itself: an intruder who accepted a visible risk of discovery probably valued the object inside. When: when a sealed space or locked container shows signs of entry. Why: the intruder accepted a visible risk.
- [heuristic] Someone already inside has no need to force a barrier, so signs of forced entry suggest the person came from outside. When: A barrier that could be opened from inside shows signs of forcing. Why: Forcing is needed only by those without access.
- [heuristic] A tool made for forcing locks points to illegal entry and narrows the set of people likely to have used it.
- [procedure] 1) Record the lock and frame condition before forcing. 2) Force the door. 3) Lock the affected rooms and secure their keys to keep the scene closed. When: when a door must be broken open to enter a sealed room. Why: forcing destroys the original state of the lock and frame.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the tool that could make the mark. 2) Confirm the mark matches that tool. 3) Confirm the suspect possessed the tool. 4) Confirm the suspect needed to force the entry. When: A barrier shows signs of forcing and the question is who forced it. Why: Each link must hold, so a weak link breaks the whole inference.
- [procedure] 1) Listen at the door to judge the threat. 2) Plan the breach so one person forces the door while others wait ready nearby. 3) Enter, then search the scene in order. When: when delay could cost a life. Why: coordinated entry keeps the breach controlled.
- [procedure] 1) Check every access point to the locked room, such as keyhole and second door. 2) Announce identity and the discovery, and give the occupant a chance to open. 3) If there is no reply, force the door. When: A locked room may hold a witness and all other access has been tried. Why: Escalating in order gives the occupant a fair chance and confirms that force is needed.
- [procedure] 1) Concentrate force on the bolt and a nearby bar until they weaken. 2) Drive a heavy movable object into the door as a ram. 3) Repeat until the fastening gives. When: When a door is fastened and no other exit exists.
- [tradeoff] Forcing a sealed room gains access and information but destroys the original scene, so breaking in trades speed against evidence. When: when designing a barrier that players can break.
- [pitfall] Forcing a door can injure people, destroy evidence inside, or make the scene look different from how the intruder left it. When: Deciding whether to break into a locked room. Why: Force is destructive and irreversible, so it must be weighed against what the room may hold.
- [pitfall] Forcing a lock or door is loud and dangerous, alerting those inside, so bystanders must be kept clear. Why: Force produces noise that alerts those inside.
- [fact] Evidence of forced entry, such as a broken window, can reveal a route that the visible layout alone did not show. When: when a hidden route must be discoverable. Why: Physical damage signals a passage the map did not mark.

## puzzle solution testing
- [mechanism] A test-and-result step, such as growing an old seed to check viability, can unlock a later area while the environment reacts visibly. When: Designing a puzzle that gates progress behind an experiment.
- [rule] Give each candidate cause its own test in the room with a distinct result, such as sounding a wall or measuring a gap, so players eliminate options by evidence. When: Mystery levels or puzzles with several possible answers or hazards. Why: Separate tests let players narrow candidates rather than guess, so the solution feels earned. (x2)
- [rule] Make exits that look blocked testable, such as a barred window to force or a window too small to pass, so each claim can be confirmed or refuted. When: When designing blocked exits in a puzzle room. Why: Testing and failing a route teaches the player why it is closed; a test turns a looks-blocked claim into a checked result.
- [rule] Require a hidden mechanism to respond only to specific contact points, not any force, so brute force fails and testing each point in turn finds the correct one. When: When designing a sealed entrance or hidden door with an unknown trigger. Why: A puzzle is only a puzzle if the correct input is distinguishable from wrong ones; a specific-response mechanism only reacts to a particular pressure point, so only methodical testing finds it.
- [rule] Make props that look operational testable, so a player can check whether they work; otherwise they serve only as misdirection.
- [rule] Encode a hidden route through testable features such as slope, dryness and species, so players can verify their deduction.
- [rule] Plant false leads that players can test by checking the named suspect's location and the speaker's history. When: When designing red herring content. Why: Testable false leads reward verification without being unfair.
- [rule] Keep each tested object within the player's reach, so substitution becomes an option the player can investigate.
- [rule] Let the player test a reconstructed sequence by checking staff answers or measuring a bed. When: designing deduction puzzles.
- [rule] Give planted clues a test the player can run, such as a handwriting comparison or a check of who held a key, rather than accepting them at face value.
- [rule] Place a submit-your-answer moment before the reveal so players can test their deduction against the designer's answer. When: Designing a mystery ending. Why: Players can compare their reasoning with the actual solution.
- [rule] Build room puzzles from dust, disturbed items and handedness so the player can test which side an object would fall from. When: designing an environmental deduction puzzle. Why: physical habits and layout give checkable answers.
- [heuristic] Let the player compare a written theory with an observed mechanic, such as a vortex or rising water, so the theory's failure is found through play.
- [heuristic] Place a mirror clue in a nearby room so the player can test the reflection explanation before the reveal.
- [heuristic] Make an apparent solution feel too easy so players examine the objects that do not fit it.
- [heuristic] Give small feedback, such as a shift or sound, for each tested point, so the player can tell whether a point is correct. When: When a hidden mechanism responds to specific contact points. Why: Without a signal, a systematic search gives the player no way to make progress.
- [procedure] 1) Collect earlier items that each hold part of the answer. 2) Place them in a final challenge room. 3) Require the player to match them against each other to reach the conclusion. When: designing a climactic reveal built from items the player already holds. Why: Assembling known evidence rewards attention and turns the finale into a deduction rather than a surprise.

## scattered clues and traces
- [mechanism] Scatter small traces such as scents, wood shavings or dropped items, each tied to a room and time, so the player can link an NPC to a location. Why: Each trace provides a partial connection that the player assembles into a chain.
- [mechanism] An object bouncing down stairs leaves repeated scrapes on consecutive treads, then a stretch with no contact, then a dent. When: reading marks on a staircase.
- [mechanism] Searchable refuse piles can hide items, with later layers making some clues harder to reach than others. When: When designing searchable sites in a level. Why: Depth of covering layers sets the effort needed to reach each clue.
- [mechanism] A cache of mixed partial clues forces the player to compare items and see how they connect, which turns the cache into a pointer to further sources. When: When a container holds several kinds of material. Why: Comparing fragments produces understanding that no single item provides.
- [rule] Place a key clue visible from only one window or balcony, so the player's choice of position becomes the puzzle.
- [rule] Scatter disturbed objects around a room to signal that an enemy searched it, and let the player compare against the undisturbed state.
- [rule] When several events occur at once, let the player reconstruct them one at a time from scattered traces such as dropped items and sounds. When: A level contains overlapping simultaneous events. Why: Scattered traces let the player separate sequences that overlap in time.
- [heuristic] Split clue parts across separate locations, pages or fragments, such as ledger, bank record and order book entries, so players must combine them. When: Designing a deduction puzzle, written clues in a dungeon, or fragments that form one usable clue. Why: Players who gather and combine sources feel the reveal was earned; combining parts makes the clue require attention. (x2)
- [heuristic] Partial traces such as visible stairs, footprints toward a back door or a moved mat can point to an entrance or hidden passage the player has not seen. When: Designing a hidden passage, or a player must find an entrance from incomplete evidence. Why: Traces left by occupants point toward access routes.
- [heuristic] Scenes can record a sequence through footprints, dropped items and disturbed objects, so players can reconstruct events.
- [heuristic] Loose particles lying in a pile, such as spilled sawdust, can reveal what was spilled and where the container stood. When: when debris lies near a mark or impression. Why: spilled material falls from its source.
- [heuristic] A waste pile or abandoned site can hold a discoverable clue in its debris; scatter items the player must sort to infer what each implies.
- [heuristic] Scattered finds across separate sites can look connected; keep them unlinked until the player compares the exact items. When: when designing several finds that should form a pattern. Why: the apparent connection is only confirmed when the specific items match.
- [heuristic] A crime scene can hold several traces, each pointing to a different location, letting the player reconstruct a path from them. When: when designing a investigation space. Why: Multiple pointing traces turn a single room into a route the player must solve.
- [pitfall] A single unverified clue can mislead; confirm a hidden route by finding the same mechanism repeated in a second place. When: Accepting a solution. Why: A first lead may end in a dead end.
- [pitfall] Concealment fails if one sign is left, such as a smell or a broken track, because that one trace can expose the whole setup.
- [pitfall] Stacking too many traces makes them compete so that none stands out as a clue.

## elapsed time and dust
- [mechanism] As elapsed time since an event grows, its subject can be spread across a wider area, so a fixed local search succeeds less often. When: a subject must be found after an event. Why: the subject has more time to move away.
- [mechanism] Sand, dust or ash in a corridor can record when a hidden door was opened or which creature passed, and the prints can be read on return.
- [rule] Count elapsed days between dated events to measure how long a delay lasted. When: a sequence of events includes a delay. Why: Duration can change how events relate.
- [rule] Use stocked provisions and cooking equipment in a room to signal that someone has been living there for some time. When: designing environmental storytelling for a hidden room. Why: supplies and cooking gear are signs of extended occupancy.
- [rule] Use dust layers and freshly disturbed objects as flags that show a room was used recently, so players can infer activity. When: When dressing a laboratory, studio or workspace.
- [heuristic] Thick, undisturbed dust and cobwebs show a room has stood idle a long time, since footsteps disturb dust and the layer records absence of recent entry. When: judging whether a room was recently entered or how long it has stood idle. Why: footsteps disturb dust, so an undisturbed layer records absence. (x2)
- [heuristic] Report durations at a coarse grain, such as under a minute or under two minutes, so readers can judge how fast a danger closed in.
- [heuristic] Count elapsed time since an event in days or years, giving a second anchor that helps recall and check the event.
- [heuristic] Estimate the time since the last sighting, then combine it with known conditions such as tides to narrow where the person could now be.
- [heuristic] Test whether a claimed action could realistically take the stated time, which can expose a false alibi without any extra clue.
- [heuristic] A witness's judgement that they would have noticed an arrival before a given time sets the earliest possible bound on that event. When: Estimating how early something could have happened. Why: If the witness would have seen it, earlier arrival is unlikely.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a consumable trace at the place that burns, melts or builds up at a known rate. 2) Confirm nobody has handled or replaced it. 3) Count units used or judge its state. 4) Multiply by per-unit burn time, adjusted for conditions, to estimate elapsed time. 5) Compare that estimate with the claimed sequence of events. When: the time spent in one place, or a claimed sequence of events, needs a rough duration. Why: consumed items burn at roughly known rates, so counts give durations.
- [procedure] 1) Estimate how long the journey or task takes physically. 2) Subtract that from the witness's stated times. 3) Treat any unexplained surplus as a place where hidden activity may lie. When: when a stated timeline leaves a gap. Why: A surplus longer than the activity suggests concealed action.
- [metric] Measuring elapsed time for each leg of a journey shows where a timeline runs behind schedule.
- [pitfall] Dust shows only the surfaces you can see, not every route in and out; a visitor may have stayed outside or left another way.
- [fact] A permanent timed object such as a sundial keeps a steady record for decades, making it a durable reference for dating events.

## clue discovery and search
- [rule] Make observation conditions such as distance, light, and obstruction checkable so players can judge how reliable a clue is. When: Building a visual clue system in a level. Why: Players need to weigh a clue's reliability, which depends on the viewing conditions.
- [rule] Check the edges of a room and interactive fixtures such as vents, registers or closed openings, since clues often lie there. When: Searching a scene for concealed items. Why: Objects dropped during an act can fall into small gaps near the scene.
- [rule] Let a character's fear or stress state change what they notice, so some clues appear only under high tension. When: Designing clues that depend on player or NPC state. Why: Stress-gated perception rewards attention to character state.
- [rule] Gate the largest reveal behind dated or timed clues the player must assemble before it opens. When: When designing the final reveal of a mystery. Why: Assembling time-linked evidence makes the reveal feel earned.
- [heuristic] Model line of sight from windows and balconies, so witness reports match what the player can verify from a given spot. When: Placing observers in a level. Why: Sightlines determine what a witness can credibly report.
- [heuristic] Searching the obvious room first and skipping closets or cellars leaves routes unchecked, so cover every area in a set order.
- [heuristic] Lore or in-world references can state a rule with an exception the player must uncover by reading the source material. When: Designing in-world documents or diagnostic tools for a puzzle. Why: A result that looks conclusive can be overturned by one hidden condition the player must check.
- [heuristic] Place a small unexplained object or footprint that players can return to after learning the solution. When: Designing a level for a mystery with a late reveal. Why: Returning to the object turns it into confirmation of the solution.
- [procedure] 1) Measure the enclosed zone's length and width. 2) Divide the party into a line with fixed spacing and assigned lanes. 3) Sweep the zone and log each cover checked. 4) Do not declare it empty before searching; clear it only when every cover and exit, including water access, is checked. When: A small bounded area must be searched for a hidden entity, or before declaring it empty. Why: A coordinated sweep covers all hiding places only when lanes are fixed and exits are included; unchecked covers or exits, such as water access, leave escape routes open.
- [procedure] 1) Place physical signs of use in the hidden area, such as fresh straw, lantern light, or a worn ladder. 2) Let the player notice them. 3) Let the player conclude the myth is cover. Why: Players can discover the true use through clues.
- [procedure] 1) Describe the scene before touching anything. 2) Examine the body or key object for clues to cause. 3) Check each exit and hiding place in a fixed order. 4) Read traces of age and use at each. 5) Rule out escape routes before searching desks and papers. When: a missing person may have left by another route. Why: a fixed order keeps every exit and hiding place checked.
- [procedure] 1) Present a vague impression that fits several suspects. 2) Let the player narrow candidates only after finding one specific matching fact. When: when designing a deduction puzzle with multiple suspects. Why: The specific fact resolves the ambiguity the impression created.
- [procedure] 1) Schedule a late cluster of clues. 2) Unlock them when a new character arrives. 3) Let the second half of the investigation open after a pause. When: Pacing the back half of a mystery. Why: A new arrival gives the late clues a natural reason to surface.
- [procedure] 1) Plant a rumor that points to a region. 2) Add a boundary marker that narrows it. 3) Add an ownership record that confirms it. 4) Hide the target so all three clues must be combined to find it.
- [pitfall] A cue the player can see may also be seen by enemies or other observers, who can read it and exploit it.

## navigation
- [mechanism] Landmarks that persist while nearby features change let players reconcile old maps with the present.
- [mechanism] A returning player's map memory can disagree with a rebuilt layout; landmarks that shift in size become a puzzle the player must test. When: when a layout changes between visits.
- [rule] A room whose orientation or gravity flips is fair when the flip rule is stated in an earlier room or a readable note.
- [rule] Place distinctive landmarks along the route as navigation clues that reward players who notice them before reaching the goal.
- [heuristic] Give corridors fixed names and number each person, seat, exit and access point on the map so routes, sightlines and positions can be checked unambiguously. When: Designing a venue or building where movement sequence or position claims must be verified. Why: Fixed names and numbers keep route descriptions unambiguous and let claims about position be tested against a map.
- [heuristic] Omit one feature from a puzzle sketch or map; players who rotate the item, change light or examine it closely will find the hidden detail.
- [heuristic] Mail, telegrams and forwarding addresses can serve as location markers that lead the player from one place to the next.
- [heuristic] Have an NPC give step-by-step directions to a crawl route, then set a time limit on the search. When: Designing a timed passage through a tight space. Why: Directions reduce guesswork while the time limit adds pressure.
- [heuristic] A map feature noted during first exploration, such as a small window, can later become a stealth route on a return visit. When: Designing a level that is revisited. Why: Features the player noticed early reward them when they return.
- [procedure] 1) Start from named landmarks in old records that still exist. 2) Fix each lot by the described positions of its neighbours. 3) Match changed wording of the same bound across records. 4) Check the candidate against physical features in the description. 5) Grade the conclusion only after records and tradition agree. When: when records give relative positions and named bounds but no coordinates. Why: Several independent clues together narrow the location more reliably than one.
- [procedure] 1) Walk each room and note entrances, passages and fixtures. 2) Note which doors are closed or sealed. 3) Record sightlines from key positions. 4) Write the map down for later study. When: before acting in a dark or confusing location. Why: light may be unreliable during the action.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a fixed, known landmark as the reference point. 2) Set a compass bearing from it. 3) Measure the stated distance along that bearing. 4) Probe the marked spot to confirm the target. When: A hidden location is defined relative to a known landmark and measured with simple tools. Why: A fixed reference and measured offset locate a target that cannot be seen directly.
- [pitfall] A difference between maps alone is not proof, since features may be absent from one map for legitimate reasons. Why: maps differ due to purpose, age and scale.
- [pitfall] A bearing taken from the wrong landmark sends the search to the wrong place, so the reference point must be fixed and correctly identified. When: Using a landmark to locate a hidden object. Why: Errors in the starting point carry through every subsequent measurement.
- [fact] Reading a map can reveal what a site is for before anyone visits it.

## npc routines and access
- [mechanism] Fixed occupant routines leave rooms predictably empty or occupants busy, creating windows of unobserved access that players can time entries and searches around. When: a place with habitual occupant schedules. Why: predictable absence lets the player time an entry.
- [mechanism] A vault or guard gap can open only when an NPC's routine changes, such as a late request or an absence, so the player can trigger the window by influencing that routine. When: Designing stealth or heist encounters. Why: Routine exceptions give players a timing puzzle to solve.
- [rule] Give NPCs fixed, readable timed routines so players can reconstruct who was where and deduce when opportunities arise. When: When designing NPC schedules that create opportunities for events. Why: Readable routines let players deduce positions and timing from observable patterns. (x2)
- [rule] Protect a secret by paying informants to stay silent or by soundproofing its room, forcing the player to find another route to the truth. When: when a secret should be reachable only by indirect means. Why: blocked direct access rewards alternative investigation.
- [rule] Do not stage an opportunity where no routine has been established, since the encounter will feel arbitrary to players.
- [rule] Give a routine NPC role, such as a delivery worker or servant, a route out of a sealed area so players check roles rather than assuming guards are the only exit. When: Designing an escape or locked-room puzzle. Why: Players who notice ordinary roles can discover the hidden route.
- [rule] Give NPCs schedules with travel times between locations so players can detect impossible alibis by checking whether travel is feasible. When: Designing timeline puzzles with NPC movement. Why: Makes alibi checking a matter of reasoning from concrete constraints.
- [heuristic] Record fixed routines, such as a chest emptied at dusk or room lights and shades, as world state; players learn the baseline and treat deviations as clues. When: Designing containers, vaults, NPC schedules or timed level behavior. Why: A broken routine signals that someone with access arranged the change. (x2)
- [heuristic] Furnish rooms so worn stairs, scorched flooring near a hearth, unslept beds, half-packed bags or cold cups reveal routine and recent events without dialogue. When: Designing rooms that tell the player what happened recently. Why: Ordinary objects in disturbed states give time-stamped clues.
- [heuristic] Plan any search or observation around the target's routine, since routines set both who is present and which evidence remains. When: planning an entry or observation of a place with fixed habits. Why: routines determine occupancy and evidence survival.
- [heuristic] Gate NPC knowledge by role and timing: a new hire knows only recent events, and a veteran knows the full history but speaks only when given a reason. When: when designing NPCs who hold story information. Why: Gating makes each NPC a distinct, useful source.
- [heuristic] Make an NPC's stated schedule a timeline the player can verify by observing the level at the given times. When: An NPC describes when they or an event will occur. Why: Verifiable schedules let players test dialogue against the world.
- [procedure] 1) Place one unidentified NPC in several scenes. 2) Make each appearance plausibly innocent or purposeful. 3) Later either resolve the NPC's purpose or keep the NPC as a deliberate red herring.
- [pitfall] Routines are not perfectly fixed; absences and unusual days break the pattern, so a plan built only on routine can fail. Why: plans assume a regularity that exceptions disrupt.
- [fact] Access to a room can be limited by fixed schedules, such as cleaners and watchmen who open it at set times and no one else.

## visible clue cues
- [mechanism] Place visible but unremarked clues in rooms, which only an attentive player or a perception check will pick up. When: Clues should reward attention without being flagged. Why: Attention and skill checks separate observation from mere seeing.
- [behavior] A visible surface effect, such as trembling ground at the turn of the tide, signals a hidden danger beneath.
- [behavior] A setting visible from many windows makes a witness feel she could be seen and heard, yet it gives no protection against a quiet threat. When: a public location with many sightlines hosts a private conversation. Why: Visibility is felt as safety even when no one can intervene in time.
- [rule] Plant small visible traces, such as mud on boots, a missing button, fresh wear, dents, scuffs, dust disturbance or missing footprints, so careful players can deduce hidden mechanisms. When: Designing environmental clues. Why: Careful observation rewards players without needing text explanation; visible traces let players deduce hidden mechanisms. (x2)
- [rule] Trigger a hidden passage with a visible cue, such as a flash from a fixture, so attentive players can connect cause and effect. When: Designing hidden doors. Why: The cue links the action to the reveal.
- [rule] Make a hidden timed trigger fire on a visible NPC action, so the player must watch the same cue the witness watched.
- [rule] Avoid overloading an opening with description that no later scene uses.
- [rule] Hide a distinguishing detail that is visible only at close inspection, such as in an enlarged portrait, to reward players who examine carefully. When: Designing a clue for identifying a culprit. Why: Careful inspection then yields a clear and satisfying discovery.
- [rule] Show the final mechanism in plain view early, such as a fake or dummy element, so the eventual explanation feels fair and retrospective. When: Designing a trap, route or device that the player will later understand fully. Why: Players accept a late reveal more readily when the components were visible earlier and simply misread.
- [rule] Use cheap, durable environmental anomalies, such as a closed window or an object built after death, as tells the player can notice and revisit later. When: Adding clues to a scene for an investigation. Why: Persistent details let the player return to them once the context that explains them is discovered.
- [heuristic] Give a cover-up a visible but easy-to-miss mismatch, such as a replaced rug or fresh paint, spotted with a tool or good light, so careful inspection rewards.
- [heuristic] An unusual event in a level can serve as a strong opening clue that narrows the player's search. When: designing the first clue of a mystery. Why: an unusual event points to a smaller set of explanations.
- [heuristic] A fixture that looks decorative but is placed in an odd way may be the hidden mechanism, so treat no visible detail as trivial. When: placing secrets in a room. Why: players who inspect odd details are the ones who find the secret.
- [heuristic] Place a warning at a feature, such as a well, fountain or locked gate, with a casual reason, and make the real reason discoverable later. When: Designing environmental hazards tied to a feature. Why: A casual warning stays a live clue until its reason is found.
- [heuristic] Show a re-fitted or freshly scratched lock as a visible trace, placed alongside an innocent explanation the player can weigh. When: When designing evidence of tampering that should not be conclusive. Why: Ambiguous traces keep the player reasoning rather than receiving a flag.

## occupancy inference
- [definition] A change of occupant shows up as a mismatch between what a household expects and what it observes.
- [mechanism] A door fastened by a hook from inside clanks when pulled, showing someone is inside, while a key locked from outside shows the room is empty. When: Reading a locked door to infer whether someone is inside. Why: The fastening's side and sound reveal who set it.
- [rule] Leave countable traces such as cups, cigar ends or footprints so players can deduce how many characters visited a room. When: Designing a room that implies past occupants. Why: Matching counts let players reason about the number of visitors.
- [heuristic] Look for occupancy traces such as bedding, ash, empty tins and bottles, food and water supplies, and warmth from a heat source that leaves no smoke. When: Investigating a room or hideout suspected of being occupied. Why: Physical traces reveal use without anyone being seen.
- [heuristic] Combine several small physical signs, such as stains, smells and marks on the body, to infer a person's occupation. When: A person's job is not stated. Why: Several weak signs together point to a profession more reliably than one.
- [heuristic] Record objects lying near a victim, such as a weapon or a glove, since they may belong to someone who was present.
- [heuristic] Good muscle with clean, unworked hands suggests a person who did not do manual labor. When: when inferring a stranger's work from the body. Why: hands and build record how a person has used them.
- [heuristic] Half-used items such as cigars or cigarettes left behind indicate that people were recently present and left quickly.
- [heuristic] Mostly closed blinds suggest that much of a building is unused, so window coverings are a readable sign of occupancy. When: Judging whether a building you cannot enter is inhabited. Why: Visible signs carry information about use.
- [heuristic] A door left unlocked by habit can provide a second path into a locked building, though it remains bound by the same timeline.
- [heuristic] Track NPC occupancy and sightlines as data, so an NPC's presence can rule a route out. When: When designing sealed-area puzzles with NPCs. Why: Recorded positions let the game check whether a route was observable.
- [procedure] 1) Note disturbed dust and fresh footprints. 2) Check for swept corners. 3) Note consumables such as candle stubs and dated newspapers. 4) Infer recent occupancy and its rough timing. When: estimating recent use of a hidden place. Why: residual traces reveal use without naming the user.
- [fact] A small object found at a scene can prove a person was there even when that person denies it. When: a suspect's account conflicts with an item at the scene. Why: an item tied to a person places them physically in the space.
- [fact] Fixtures and decoration reveal who used a room, and a layout plan shows where those traces should appear. When: identifying a room's occupant. Why: Personal objects link a space to a person.

## hazards and traps
- [mechanism] A bait mechanic lets the player place a lure with the right properties to force a hidden enemy or clue to appear. When: when designing a puzzle around a hidden enemy or clue. Why: The player's correct setup produces a visible response they can act on.
- [mechanism] When a crowd is about to lock its verdict, give the player a timer to reach a specific witness, so exposure becomes a race against the crowd.
- [rule] Give players a prearranged visible cue, such as a light or sound, that tells them when a planned action is safe. When: A plan depends on a timed moment the player must recognize. Why: A clear cue lets the player act at a set moment instead of guessing.
- [rule] At a climactic door, make the expected boss absent and place a lesser enemy's corpse on the floor, reversing the player's threat model within one room. When: Staging a boss reveal that changes what the player believes. Why: A single room that overturns expectations changes the player's model of the threat immediately.
- [rule] State the lure's required conditions, such as freshness or strength, in the environment so players can work out the correct setup. When: when a puzzle depends on a lure with specific properties. Why: Explicit cues make the solution deducible rather than guessed.
- [rule] Escalate across encounters, using a false victory followed by a new threat, and plant the cause of the later threat in earlier rooms. When: Designing a dungeon's encounter sequence. Why: Returning players can reinterpret earlier rooms once the cause is revealed.
- [rule] Give players a brief warning cue before a danger, such as a sound or light change, and vary how reliable that cue is across the level. When: before traps or ambushes.
- [heuristic] Make a hazard's true source a separate actor, such as a hidden NPC or past event, that the player can find and identify, and let the player's first explanation be plausibly wrong. When: Designing a level hazard that hides a deeper cause. Why: A wrong first explanation drives investigation toward the real source.
- [heuristic] Build a sanctuary that is actually a trap, with locked doors and an NPC host whose manner shifts from welcoming to threatening when the player is cornered. When: Designing a safe-seeming location that becomes dangerous. Why: A shift in host behavior signals the change of state to the player.
- [heuristic] A hazard can be disguised as an environmental event, and the player must find the underlying mechanism to tell the two apart. When: Designing traps that resemble environmental effects. Why: Players who investigate the mechanism distinguish a designed trap from natural danger.
- [heuristic] Use a threatened frame as a mid-game escalation that forces the player to protect an ally before the main truth is available. When: Escalating stakes in the middle of an investigation. Why: Protecting an ally raises urgency before the core answer is reachable.
- [procedure] 1) Start with a watch on a door. 2) Force a breach. 3) Enter a cabinet or inner room. 4) Yield a new document at each stage that raises the stakes. When: Structuring a level that builds toward a reveal. Why: Each stage raises tension and gives a new piece of information, so the player's stakes grow steadily.
- [procedure] 1) Place a poisoned object the player must avoid or swap. 2) Let the player's deduction determine which object is dangerous. 3) Resolve the case when the culprit consumes it.
