When: Designing a climactic mechanic for an investigation. Why: Ties the final action to the player's reasoning rather than a cutscene.

## npc routines and witnesses
- [rule] Have NPCs react visibly to world events, so a missing reaction becomes a readable clue about where that NPC was.
- [rule] Keep two plausible culprits viable until the player's clues separate them, and give each rival its own gaps. When: designing a whodunit. Why: rivals that can be dismissed on evidence feel fair.
- [rule] Include a wrong-person arrival as a misdirection that forces players to change their plan.
- [rule] Give witness NPCs partial, confident memories that are useful but need confirmation, so they cannot simply hand over the answer. When: When designing witness NPCs who identify characters. Why: Partial memories keep players working to confirm rather than being given answers.
- [rule] Let a visitors' book or guild register produce a list of candidates for players to narrow with extra clues. When: When designing investigation content around registers. Why: A candidate list paired with clues creates a narrowing puzzle.
- [rule] Scatter unexplained vacant positions in crowded scenes so an observant player notices them. When: Designing crowded scenes with clues. Why: Gaps in a dense crowd draw attention.
- [rule] Place NPCs in adjacent rooms with thin walls so that overheard dialogue gives the player clues before any direct conversation. When: designing NPC placement for investigation. Why: overhearing offers clues without a formal conversation.
- [rule] Place information hubs such as kitchens, markets, or stables where scattered NPC chatter holds clues. When: Designing where players gather rumors in a level. Why: Hubs concentrate chatter that players can piece together.
- [heuristic] Give NPC directions that the player must verify on the level, so a wrong reading produces a visible, informative failure. When: Designing puzzles where an NPC gives spatial or relational instructions. Why: Verification turns the hint into a deduction the player can test.
- [heuristic] An NPC who relays a message can give a deliberately vague description, so players must check the account against other sources. When: Designing message-relay or testimony content. Why: Vagueness forces players to cross-check.
- [heuristic] Build a quest around a single witness with a route to them and a deadline, and provide alternative sources so the player is not blocked if the witness is missed. When: When designing a quest that depends on one NPC.
- [heuristic] Give the game world daily routines, such as cleaning schedules and nightly fires, that leave timestamps the player can use to order events. When: designing traces for a mystery. Why: scheduled activity makes traces datable.
- [heuristic] Have a secondary NPC report an absence that the player can verify through a phone call or message check.

## suspect identification
- [mechanism] Giving each candidate door its own marker turns an unlocated sound into a located event, though every added candidate adds another check.
- [rule] Model each suspect as an area chain: entering each new area unlocks a clue contradicting the previous suspect, and the culprit's signature items sit in the last-visited area. When: Generating or authoring investigation levels. Why: Late-visited areas hold the decisive evidence while early areas build a misleading case.
- [rule] Make two enemies that share a key or access route potentially the same entity in two states, and let the player discover the link on a second encounter. When: Designing encounters where the player sees a locked access point used by a second enemy. Why: The shared key creates a plausible reason to think two foes are different, so the later reveal rewards attention to the access clue.
- [rule] Let an NPC witness a transformation in a locked room and deliver the reveal, with the room's contents as physical proof, rather than having the protagonist discover it alone. When: Revealing a hidden transformation to the player in a level. Why: An outside observer provides a credible account, and the physical proof in the room backs up what the player is told.
- [rule] A familiar-looking enemy or NPC can plant doubt about identity, to be resolved later by an in-game check.
- [rule] End a level with a confession encounter whose proof is an object the player carried through the whole level.
- [heuristic] Raise a suspicion meter only on physical evidence, not on atmosphere alone. When: When designing a suspicion or threat indicator. Why: Atmosphere can mislead, so the indicator should track verifiable evidence.
- [heuristic] Make identity checks in a mystery level require two or three independent clues found in different rooms. When: Designing a mystery that depends on confirming who someone is. Why: Several separated clues prevent a single missed item from blocking progress.
- [heuristic] Each misidentification can unlock a new zone, so players must revisit earlier clues instead of moving strictly forward.
- [heuristic] A false suspect can carry a visible, damning item or mark that leads the player to a dead end. When: designing misleading clues.
- [heuristic] A suspect who is watched rather than confronted can produce a long sequence of clues and behaviour changes that the player follows. When: Designing a suspect who should be observed over time. Why: Ongoing observation yields a stream of reactive clues instead of a single confrontation.
- [heuristic] In a hub, let each character's suspicion rise as the player finds items, with the victim's effects updating the accusation. When: Designing a hub-based investigation with multiple suspects. Why: Item discovery gives the player a reason to revise accusations over time.
- [heuristic] Hide the true culprit behind an authority NPC's wrong theory, and give the player a physical check, such as a route or item, that exposes the error.

## environment and architecture clues
- [definition] An architectural anomaly is a part of a building that its layout or function does not account for, such as a fireplace with no flue. When: inspecting a building for hidden spaces. Why: unexplained elements can signal concealed structures.
- [mechanism] Chained mirrors can relay an image so that a person appears far from where they actually stand.
- [rule] Absence is negative evidence: a passage missing from the blueprint eliminates its location; a 'nothing unusual' NPC report points to a constraint like a wall, distance or closed door. When: when a hidden passage is absent from the plan or an NPC reports no observation of a sound or event. Why: A null report still narrows where the event could have occurred. (x2)
- [rule] Make a room's most anomalous feature the intended key, so the puzzle does not rely on spectacle alone.
- [rule] Read level architecture as evidence: a moved chimney or a foundation with no building above can signal a later addition or a lost structure for the player to investigate.
- [rule] Use boundary deeds with exact measured lines as physical clues that can settle a land argument.
- [heuristic] Objects and rooms that reflect a location's history carry implicit evidence, so an atmosphere matching a family's past supports the story. When: when designing a location tied to a backstory.
- [heuristic] A climbing puzzle can leave handprints at a height so the player can deduce the size or reach of whoever climbed there. When: When designing a climbing sequence in a level. Why: Marks at height reveal the climber's reach.
- [heuristic] Make the environment's orientation a readable clue so the player can resolve the geometry before solving the mechanism. When: When the layout of a sealed space is ambiguous. Why: Unresolved orientation blocks the search itself and frustrates the player before the puzzle begins.
- [pitfall] A bright spot draws the eye and can hide what lies at the edges of the room. When: Lighting a key area of a level.
- [pitfall] A tree screen hides a site only while its canopy stays intact.
- [fact] A smoking chimney shows that someone is present in a building, even when no one is seen, but not how many people are inside.

## forged letters and records
- [rule] Build item puzzles in which a forged letter's handwriting does not match a sample the player has found, so the mismatch is discoverable. When: Designing document-based puzzles for a game. Why: A discoverable mismatch rewards the player for comparing evidence rather than trusting the document.
- [rule] Give an npc's handwriting a distinctive letter form that players can spot across several documents, so forged notes can be matched against it. When: Designing forged-note clues. Why: A recurring distinctive form lets players compare documents.
- [rule] Let the player send an NPC a forged message with scripted reactions such as pausing, looking around, reading or hiding, so the reactions reveal the NPC's allegiance. When: Designing a loyalty or deception check in a game. Why: Observable reactions give the player evidence about hidden allegiances.
- [rule] End a dungeon with an in-world record giving a false official explanation, and let the player's findings contradict it so the player decides which account to trust.
- [rule] Give the player a fake trail, such as a footprint or item, plus a second object that exposes the forger, such as a shoe or tool the suspect holds. When: Designing a puzzle where a clue has been planted. Why: The second object gives the player a way to detect the forgery.
- [rule] Give a forged in-game letter a correct hand but wrong directions; players who visited can catch the errors, and their pattern hints at the writer. When: designing a forged document players must evaluate. Why: checkable errors let players test the letter against what they already learned.
- [rule] A quest can deliver a forged letter from an NPC to lure an enemy to a set location and time. When: Designing a lure or trap quest. Why: A trusted-looking letter gives the player a reason to set a trap and the enemy a reason to walk into it.
- [heuristic] Build some forgeries into the game with a detectable mismatch, so a careful player can catch them. When: Designing deceptive in-world evidence. Why: A detectable flaw rewards attention and keeps deception fair.
- [heuristic] In a forged-letter puzzle, the player should compare the document's contents, not only its seal or handwriting, to find the forgery. When: Designing a document-forgery puzzle. Why: Content can reveal inconsistencies that surface imitation hides.
- [heuristic] A lock or code can reject a forged key by checking for impossible perfection, such as an exact copy of a seeded random flaw. When: when designing anti-forgery checks.
- [heuristic] Build a fake death from checkable pieces such as a grave, register entry and body, and let the player check each piece. When: When designing a staged death in a mystery. Why: Each verifiable piece gives the player a way to expose the lie.
- [heuristic] Let a found letter's physical details, such as trimmed margins or mismatched paper, lead the player to a room in the level where it was made. When: Designing a document puzzle in a level. Why: Physical traces give the player a concrete route back to the source.

## timing and scheduling
- [mechanism] A timed gate such as an arrival date or opening day forces the culprit to act before a fixed deadline. When: When designing time-limited actions in a mystery. Why: The deadline constrains the culprit's options and the player's window to intervene.
- [rule] Plant a false lead, such as a letter pointing to a rival faction, and let its timing reveal the trick to players who check it. When: designing a false lead in a level.
- [rule] Give a guard or servant who tends a shrine, cache or prisoner irregular patrol times, so the player can catch tampering and feel the suspense of a surprise check.
- [rule] Add an event in which the target NPC relocates before the player arrives, so the player's reconnaissance becomes outdated and the trap hits an empty room. When: Designing a stealth or assassination quest. Why: Stale information forces the player to re-scout, adding tension.
- [rule] Time a planted cache to appear when the player expects it, and let a later clue reveal who placed it.
- [rule] Give a poison or trap an onset time, so players can deduce that dosing occurred near the victim within a fixed window. When: When designing a poison or trap as a clue. Why: A known onset lets players infer proximity and timing from the effect.
- [rule] Make a timed object such as a clock or candle readable in two ways, and reward players who test whether it was moved. When: Designing a time clue in a locked-room puzzle. Why: Checking the object's state reveals a falsified timeline.
- [rule] Make a locked gate open on a set day and let players learn its schedule from in-world information. When: Designing an access restriction that should be predictable. Why: A learnable schedule turns the gate into a timing puzzle rather than an arbitrary block.
- [heuristic] Accumulated residue such as fallen cigar ash gives a rough duration for how long someone stood still.
- [heuristic] Time covert actions for a quiet window, and use ordinary routines such as staff departures or cleaning as cover. When: a player must act unobserved.
- [heuristic] End a level phase on a breach of an area the player was told is secured, creating a turn in pacing. When: the player has just been assured that an area is safe.
- [heuristic] A timed challenge can keep its rule limit hidden, so players must track the limit through the events of the level. When: Designing timed challenges.

## concealment and hiding spots
- [mechanism] Lighting controls what observers can perceive, so darkening a scene removes the evidence needed to see an act. When: A hidden act must happen unseen in a scene. Why: Observers can only perceive what light makes visible.
- [rule] Size hidden alcoves to match the objects they conceal, so a player can infer what was stored there.
- [rule] Let neglected features such as dry wells or overgrown shrubs mark places where items can be hidden and later found.
- [rule] Make the plan specific enough to measure; a vague plan cannot guide a physical search. When: Designing a hidden feature that players must locate from clues. Why: The search method depends on measurable distances.
- [rule] Use fog, lamp coverage and footstep audio to hide and reveal enemies, letting sound warn of approach before sight. When: when designing enemy visibility in a dark or foggy space. Why: Sound preceding sight gives the player warning while concealment preserves tension.
- [rule] In a room whose lighting can change, place a light source the player can reach at a critical moment so the reveal is earned rather than purely random. When: Designing a hidden enemy action or trap trigger in darkness. Why: Player-accessible light makes discovery a matter of skill and attention.
- [heuristic] Place hiding spots by function: drains, laundries and unlit alleys each support a specific kind of concealment and item type. When: Designing concealment locations in a level. Why: Matching location to function makes hiding plausible and discoverable.
- [heuristic] Place hidden items where a plausible action would send them, and make the hiding place explainable afterwards. When: Designing where a key item is concealed in a game world. Why: A player who searches beyond the obvious floor finds the item and can understand how it got there.
- [heuristic] Key clues can be hidden in ordinary, conspicuous objects that searchers overlook because they look first at obvious hiding places. When: Designing a clue that must be discoverable but not obvious. Why: Experienced searchers check secret or unusual spots first and skip mundane items.
- [heuristic] In a bare room, few hiding spots exist, so every fixture becomes a suspect and any hidden spot is itself a clue. When: Designing a sparsely decorated room. Why: Limited cover concentrates suspicion onto the few features that could conceal something.
- [procedure] 1) List every fixture, opening, shadow and bare surface in the room. 2) Identify which features could hide an object. 3) Choose a placement that uses a shadow or fixture for concealment. 4) Test visibility from the observer's position. When: A site must be covertly monitored and its layout limits hiding options. Why: Mapping features before placing equipment reveals the few viable concealment spots.

## misplaced and missing items
- [rule] Make a vanished key item the clue: end a section with it missing from its expected place, so the player's next task is finding where it went. When: designing a search puzzle or closing a section on an open question. Why: an absence gives the player a concrete question to pursue.
- [rule] Make an expected item missing from a victim's or NPC's inventory or equipment a clue, so players must find where it went, who had access, and reason toward the culprit. When: designing inventory-based clues or missing equipment. Why: absence prompts a search that leads to new suspects and reasoning from absence.
- [rule] Put a misplaced object, such as a ladder, in an unexpected outdoor spot so players must infer an entry route, linking an interior puzzle to the open map. Why: A misplaced object signals a route the player has not yet explored.
- [rule] Place small anomalies, such as misplaced or dropped items, loose planks, patched walls, or mislabelled crates, that careful players notice and trace to a hidden actor, route, or routine change. When: when hinting at a hidden actor, event, or route. Why: Players connect distant but similar anomalies as a trail; noticing a mismatch rewards the player.
- [rule] A found page's high number or a ledger's missing page implies missing entries; players must find another copy to recover them and finish the story, rewarding exploration and cross-referencing. When: designing a document-based clue. Why: Recovering a missing entry rewards exploration and cross-referencing.
- [rule] Plant a weapon where it suggests a different room or owner, so players must test where it was actually used, which can uncover a second, unrelated crime.
- [rule] Let fragments of an unseen object turn up in another location, creating a new clue that leads the player to a new area.
- [rule] A garment or item left by mistake can lead the player to the real owner of an item that was taken.
- [rule] Place a document in an unexpected spot to signal that someone visited after it was written. When: Designing clues about a character's movements. Why: Players infer later visits from displaced evidence.
- [heuristic] Missing items can show that someone cleared evidence from the scene.
- [heuristic] Design each absence so the player has a checkable question, such as whether its cause is accidental, natural or deliberate. When: building a clue around something missing. Why: A specific question gives the player a route to a verifiable answer.

## npc testimony and dialogue
- [mechanism] A sealed note carried by a servant or errand boy can be watched or intercepted along its route, and misread if the carrier is untrusted. When: a message must pass through a third party.
- [mechanism] A language barrier limits what players can learn from certain NPCs until a translator or tool is found, narrowing information flow. When: Designing information gates in a world. Why: Access tools control information.
- [rule] Plant an NPC's odd gesture in an early scene and reward the player when the gesture later becomes meaningful. When: Designing character-driven reveals. Why: Players who notice early details feel rewarded when they pay off.
- [heuristic] An NPC's willingness to reveal a secret can depend on how much the player has paid, insulted or threatened them. When: When designing NPC dialogue that gates secrets behind player actions. Why: Past treatment shapes how willing an NPC is to disclose.
- [heuristic] Give an informant NPC dialogue options at different confidence levels, with a specific question unlocking a more exact answer. When: When designing an NPC with partial knowledge. Why: Graded answers reward precise questioning and let the player build knowledge step by step.
- [heuristic] A masked NPC can demand a player's oath before naming a target, so the player commits before learning the secret. When: when designing festival or costumed NPCs who hold secrets. Why: The commitment creates tension and later accountability.
- [heuristic] Place a sealed note or journal in a container that opens only after a major fight, so it carries the level's explanation at the moment the player can absorb it. When: Delivering lore or explanation tied to level progression. Why: Gating the explanation behind a fight rewards progress and gives the player the answer when it has the most impact.
- [heuristic] Vary an NPC witness's report confidence by distance, lighting and duration of observation so players learn to weigh testimony. When: when designing NPC reports for the player to evaluate. Why: Observation conditions teach the player to judge reliability.
- [heuristic] Plant an NPC who claims credit for the player's work, then reveal in a late scene that this NPC is responsible. When: Designing a dungeon or quest with a deceptive ally. Why: The player retroactively reads earlier interactions differently once the reversal lands.
- [procedure] 1) Have the NPC hold a key fact back. 2) Place matching physical evidence elsewhere. 3) Trigger the scripted reveal when the player finds that evidence. When: A boss or NPC holds information that the player must earn. Why: The reveal feels earned because the player supplies the link.
- [pitfall] Fog, darkness or a cut sensor feed can hide information players need to act; critical information needs a backup channel. When: when atmosphere is used heavily in a play space, or when sensor feeds may cut out. Why: Concealment that blocks needed information prevents informed decisions.

## stealth and guard routes
- [model] A sequence of barriers, namely baize doors, curtains and a small circular hall, separates the approach from the inner room and controls who can reach whom. When: when designing access to a protected room.
- [mechanism] A guarded locked room can be entered through an approach the guards do not watch, while a distraction draws them to a different area. When: A protected room has guards watching one approach. Why: Guards respond to the most salient threat, leaving other approaches open.
- [rule] Place a small object such as a torn cloth or rope end on a high ledge or hook that links a room to a route the player thinks is sealed.
- [rule] Require the player to choose a disguise and backstory that stay consistent with any reference the game can check. When: Designing an infiltration mission with identity checks. Why: Consistency with checkable details makes the player's choices meaningful and testable.
- [rule] Lead players along a winding, poorly lit route to a guarded area, and place reusable landmarks along it so the route still supports later deduction. When: Designing an approach to a protected area. Why: Disorientation adds tension while landmarks keep the route usable for later reasoning.
- [heuristic] After leaving an observation point, listen for footsteps to check whether anyone is following before continuing. Why: A watcher may be watched in turn.
- [procedure] 1) Map guard and servant schedules. 2) Identify sleeping or distant observers. 3) Pick the weakest barrier as the route. 4) Time movement to the quiet window. When: Designing a stealth or heist route for the player. Why: Schedules and weak points define where the player can pass unseen.
- [procedure] 1) Display guard sight cones and patrol timings. 2) Let the player compare coverage across exits. 3) Reward finding the one route no guard watches. When: when designing a stealth level with an unwatched route. Why: visible coverage data turns the route search into deduction, not guesswork.
- [procedure] 1) Have the player wait in a vault for a threat. 2) Capture one enemy. 3) Let a second enemy escape through a hole the player can later revisit.
- [tradeoff] A noisy lock or dense plant cover at an exit adds risk, since audible tells can alert others, so sneaking out costs both time and sound. When: when designing a stealth exit route. Why: Noise and cover impose costs that make the exit a choice rather than a free escape.
- [pitfall] Routes along a narrow ledge above water carry the risk that one misstep ends the whole plan.

## traces and cleanup evidence
- [mechanism] Cleaning or restoration erases handling marks, so players must notice what is missing as well as present; a spotless space may hide tampering. When: The environment can be altered by characters or events, or repeatedly reset to a known state. Why: Missing evidence carries information as much as present evidence, and restoration removes traces that show interference. (x2)
- [mechanism] Cleaning routines that skip some surfaces let dust-based traces persist, so which clues survive depends on which tasks are done on which days. Why: a cleaner who skips dusting leaves dust evidence intact.
- [rule] Let a hidden item leave a detectable trace, such as a scraped surface or missing match, so a careful player can uncover the concealment.
- [rule] Make a removed body or object leave no drag marks, blood trail or dropped item when it was not moved by force, giving players a deduction hook. When: Designing a room where a body or object is apparently taken. Why: Missing traces reveal that the removal was voluntary, rewarding inference.
- [heuristic] Destroy a dangerous document before the player reaches it, leaving visible residue such as ash or a half-burnt scrap, and hide a copy elsewhere so players can recover what was lost. When: Designing a room where an NPC or event destroys evidence. Why: Visible residue gives the player a clue, and a hidden copy rewards searching. (x2)
- [heuristic] Give each ambiguous clue, such as a smell, sound or trace, two possible sources, so players must test it with a second clue before committing. When: Designing a clue that could mislead if taken at face value. Why: Requiring confirmation makes careful reasoning pay off.
- [heuristic] Cleaning leaves traces: a patch cleaner than the surrounding dust, or an unusually wiped object, shows someone wiped it, so tidy scenes can hide disturbance. When: Inspecting a dusty surface or a cleaned room. Why: Wiping removes marks but leaves contrast.
- [heuristic] Place a chain of NPCs along an item's path, each holding a readable record, so every handler leaves a clue that narrows the next search. When: Designing an item-tracing investigation in a level. Why: Each record gives the player a concrete lead toward the next link.
- [heuristic] A change in stride or trace pattern at one point along a route can mark where a person's behaviour changed.
- [heuristic] Give physical traces such as footprints, dropped objects or scuffs properties comparable to a suspect's gear so the player can match them. When: designing traces in a level. Why: matching requires the player to have a comparable attribute.
- [heuristic] Marks left on a body or object can be stored as checkable facts that confirm or contradict a testimony.

## enemies guards and threats
- [definition] A watch post is an observer stationed at a single passage who records who moves through it during a set period while the investigator works elsewhere.
- [rule] Give sensory warnings, such as a smell, sound, hiss or touch, before a hidden threat appears, so the player has time to react. When: a threat is about to appear in a level, or a dark room holds an enemy the player cannot yet see. Why: Early warnings let the player prepare instead of being surprised.
- [rule] End a dungeon section or encounter on an unresolved state, such as an ajar door, an unreadable message or a low-health fall, to pull the player into the next area. When: designing the end of a section or encounter. Why: Unfinished encounters prompt the player forward.
- [rule] Let a close pass identify a watcher who can later reappear in the level. When: Designing recurring pursuers.
- [rule] Tell the watcher the purpose of the watch so they know what to notice. Why: A watcher unaware of the purpose misses relevant events.
- [heuristic] Show the player an antagonist's preparations, such as a changed meeting point or a waiting figure, that the protagonist cannot see, so the coming encounter carries known risk. When: when setting up an encounter. Why: visible but unseen-by-protagonist threats raise tension without revealing the plan.
- [heuristic] Move covert work to a spot where any approaching footstep gives warning. When: designing hiding places for stealth play.
- [tradeoff] Visible watchers make threats readable so players can react, but when a trap needs a subject to enter, visible guards may frighten the subject away. When: placing watcher NPCs at a hub's entrances, or guarding a location where a trap relies on a subject entering. Why: Noticing a watcher lets the player respond to the threat themselves; a visible threat causes the subject to flee rather than enter. (x2)
- [pitfall] When a guard is drawn away, the location is left unwatched, so any unexpected arrival creates risk for the person inside.
- [pitfall] A hidden watcher behind a slightly open door can be noticed by the people being watched, so concealed observers may give themselves away. When: placing hidden NPCs who observe visitors. Why: the watched party can notice the door moving.

## hazards and timed windows
- [mechanism] A periodic hazard that publishes its cycle lets the player plan a crossing inside a safe window, so timing itself becomes the puzzle.
- [mechanism] Irregular timing of an effect, such as lights failing at random intervals, prevents anyone from catching the method while keeping the effect mechanically fair.
- [mechanism] A timed hazard, such as fog rolling over the route during a climactic encounter, closes the map's path and forces the player to act before it disappears.
- [mechanism] A drugged or altered state inside a sealed room can serve as a time-gated hazard. When: dungeon or locked-room hazard design.
- [rule] Use a moving hazard as a clock. Estimate the minutes left before it covers the route, and act early to keep a safety margin. When: when an environmental hazard closes an escape path.
- [rule] In a stealth room, place two sensor points with overlapping sightlines and tie a visible world cue, such as a lamp going dark, to a shared alert. When: designing a room with multiple guards or detection points. Why: the shared cue tells the player the alert is linked.
- [heuristic] Seed a mundane hazard, such as a lamp, oil spill or flammable drape, early in an NPC joke, complaint or note so the player recalls it when it becomes relevant. When: when a later event depends on an earlier hazard. Why: A casual mention hides its significance until the player needs it. (x2)
- [heuristic] Place dangerous tools in plain sight in a room and let them become relevant in a later scene, while attention stays on other things. When: When designing rooms with potentially lethal objects. Why: Visible but unremarked items become meaningful once the player needs them.
- [heuristic] Attach a hazard to retrieving or reading the final document, such as a collapsing stack or timed trap, so the reveal itself becomes a risk. When: When the final reveal is placed in a physical location. Why: Making the reveal dangerous to reach ties the narrative payoff to the gameplay tension.
- [fact] A dropped flame can ignite nearby flammable material, so a light source used carelessly near fuel can start a fire during a search. When: when light is used near fuel or fabric. Why: Flammable material catches fire from an open flame.

## soft ground tracks and prints
- [mechanism] Soft ground, dust or snow can record footprints whose shape and depth vary with footwear and weight, letting the level identify which character passed. When: A level includes receptive surfaces. Why: Impression differs by foot and load.
- [mechanism] A sign of unknown authorship invites guesses, and a later discovery can either settle those guesses or reverse them. When: A world element has no known author or origin. Why: New evidence changes the meaning of old marks.
- [rule] Record footprints on soft ground such as mud or grass, but let them vanish on paved surfaces; a path leaving no prints becomes a cue to deduce rather than track. When: When designing ground surfaces that affect tracking. Why: Tracking has natural limits, and surfaces that hide tracks shift the challenge from tracking to deduction. (x2)
- [rule] Record tracks as persistent terrain marks, with tread or wheel width identifying which route was used, so players can trace movement. When: Designing a level where movement should be traceable. Why: Readable marks let players reconstruct routes and vehicle types.
- [heuristic] Use soft surfaces such as sand, mud or dust, which keep the shape of each step, so routes can be reconstructed and players can deduce who entered and when. When: Designing traceable movement or an entry or exit puzzle on a surface that holds impressions. Why: Recorded traces let players reason from evidence rather than guess. (x2)
- [heuristic] Scent, residue or disinfectant can mark a hidden room or NPC as a discoverable clue for the player.
- [heuristic] Record footprints in soft ground so overlapping marks reveal the order in which the player or NPCs passed. When: When designing environmental storytelling in a level. Why: Layered tracks create readable history.
- [heuristic] Inspect objects for recent repair signs: a glue line still tacky, a patch with mismatched colour, and dust or powder caught in fresh bonding. When: an object may have been handled after an event. Why: Fresh repairs leave traces that aged, intact material does not.
- [heuristic] Dust, mud or snow on a level can record player movement, and a designed trail can end at a hidden exit the player must notice. When: When designing tracking puzzles on surfaces that can hold impressions. Why: Visible traces let the player infer routes and hidden exits.
- [heuristic] Footprints leading to each closed door and under it show a route, and the last print can reveal where movement ended. When: Tracing an intruder's path through a space. Why: Physical trails record movement across the floor.

## timeline and schedules
- [mechanism] Events that draw occupants away reduce the number of people who could observe an intruder, opening unusual access windows. When: a community event pulls people out of a building. Why: fewer observers lower the risk of being seen.
- [rule] Model patrols and transit as fixed timetables that players can check against suspects' claimed routes and use to predict locations and plan an intercept. When: When designing NPC movement that players should be able to anticipate. Why: Predictable schedules make interception a skill rather than a matter of chance.
- [rule] Give NPC couriers a fixed role, such as a servant who cannot read, so a message can be carried without its contents being revealed. When: when designing NPC message delivery. Why: A set role makes the courier's knowledge predictable.
- [rule] Require a timed investigation to be checked in fixed order, with each check unlocking the next lead while a clock runs toward a deadline.
- [rule] Give an escape puzzle a fixed deadline and a fixed meeting point, so the challenge has a clear finish and a clear test of success.
- [rule] Give NPC routines such as rent collection that run on a schedule, so a missed payment prompts the player to ask where it should have come from. When: designing a town economy that can carry a clue. Why: A break in a scheduled routine is noticed only when the player knows the routine exists.
- [heuristic] Let players build a timeline from NPC routines, delivery times and key-holder notes to find when an item was swapped. When: Designing a schedule-based investigation puzzle. Why: Combining routines and access records lets players deduce the window of opportunity.
- [procedure] 1) Anchor each planned action to a fixed day or event. 2) Order actions so that the target is acted upon before a suspect can respond. 3) Verify facts before the schedule forces action. When: Designing a timed investigation sequence. Why: A deadline creates pressure, and acting before the suspect can react preserves the advantage.
- [procedure] 1) Record the timing of each event in the case. 2) Lay the accounts against one another. 3) Mark where accounts conflict. 4) Let the player compare accounts against that timeline. When: Building a timeline for player-facing investigation. Why: A shared timeline makes conflicts between testimonies visible and gives the player a structure for deduction.
- [pitfall] A timed check is only as reliable as its starting time; if the starting point is an estimate, the computed window may be wrong. When: The start of a timed chain is not precisely known. Why: Errors in the first timestamp propagate through every later calculation.

## decoy routes and events
- [mechanism] A decoy event or sound in the same timeframe absorbs traces or draws guards' attention, letting the real actor or intruder act unobserved. Why: A plausible alternative cause diverts attention from the real one.
- [rule] Include a decoy item or route that looks like the answer, with a clear test, such as a record, a skill check or a reagent, that eliminates it. When: Designing false leads in a level. Why: Players learn by testing and eliminating decoys.
- [rule] Place a loud, harmless event such as a vendor, festival, or fight near a quieter clue the player must find. When: Designing a scene where a clue must be noticed. Why: The distraction draws attention while the clue stays available nearby.
- [rule] In locked rooms, place a decoy object that appears to solve the room, so the player is misled until the real mechanism is found. Why: A convincing wrong answer delays the solution and rewards close examination.
- [heuristic] Place harmless-looking decoy faction, quest line, or staged evidence on the map's far side, away from the true objective in a less noticed area. Why: Staged props draw the search away from where the event happened.
- [heuristic] Offer two plausible culprits or routes, with only one carrying the real clue and the other a convincing decoy. When: When designing a mystery or branching investigation space.
- [heuristic] A decoy that is partly true and explains only half the event can lead the player toward a second clue needed to complete the solution. When: When designing misleading clues in a mystery. Why: A partial explanation satisfies the player temporarily and motivates continued search.
- [heuristic] Place valuable-looking loot to suggest a theft motive, and leave decoys unlinked to the solution so the player must check each one against the scene. When: When designing misleading objects in a level. Why: Unlinked decoys force the player to verify rather than assume.
- [procedure] Lure a guard off post with a fake errand or decoy NPC quest, with the player's cover identity as the key to the approach, for a set window to take a route or search or swap an item. Plant earlier the dependency that makes the guard's departure plausible. When: Designing a stealth or distraction mechanic that pulls a guard away from a protected area. Why: A controllable absence gives a timed opportunity; players and guards need a believable reason to move, or the decoy feels arbitrary. (x2)

## locks and access control
- [rule] Let a key location fade from the map over time so the player must search for it using clues from locals. When: A quest depends on finding a place whose location is no longer known. Why: Searching for a lost site turns lore into gameplay.
- [rule] Let an NPC interrogation offer a legal or social pretext, such as a warrant, so the player gains access to a locked room. When: Designing a locked room that the player must enter legitimately. Why: A pretext gives a plausible in-world reason for the search.
- [rule] Build locked thresholds with a physical mechanism such as a bar or bell, and make its operation a clue to who controls the exit. When: Designing exits that a player cannot easily leave. Why: A visible mechanism gives the player a lead on the controlling party.
- [rule] Seal doors and stairs with a fragile mark; an unbroken seal shows the space was not entered. When: Tracking whether a room was entered while unobserved. Why: A broken seal is a clear, checkable record of entry.
- [rule] Use a timed journey, such as a train or boat schedule, as a lock: the player must know the schedule to see that a claimed location was impossible. When: when a claim depends on travel time. Why: The schedule is the missing knowledge that turns a claim into an impossibility.
- [heuristic] A locked door the player first assumes belongs to an enemy can later be revealed as the protagonist's own access point, with the key planted early and its owner changed late. When: Designing a locked door whose owner is hidden. Why: Reassigning ownership late recasts an earlier obstacle as a personal link, adding a twist without new geometry.
- [heuristic] Locked doors and a limited set of usable rooms define an investigation area's scope, so the player knows what remains to check. When: Defining the boundaries of an investigable area. Why: A defined scope lets the player track what has and has not been searched.
- [heuristic] Set a lock's difficulty by how much force its weakest point requires, and let players test tools to see which leave the matching marks. When: Designing lock or safe puzzles. Why: Designers then control how plausible a break-in is and players learn from tool marks.
- [procedure] 1) Fix a precise moment using several witnesses' statements. 2) Check each account against independent sources. 3) Establish who was within reach of the victim. 4) List every entry route. 5) Search walls, floor, ceiling and roof systematically. When: a crime seems to occur with no one nearby in a sealed room.

## mechanisms and triggers
- [mechanism] A test action, such as adding water, can reveal a hidden state like residual heat that confirms or breaks an NPC's statement. When: puzzle that checks a claim. Why: residual heat is held in material.
- [rule] Let a trivial action, such as moving an object or interrupting dialogue, trigger a hidden record, and make the cause traceable so the player can link the result back to it. When: When designing a hidden discovery triggered by a small action. Why: Players can only learn from a result they can trace to its cause.
- [heuristic] Let the player remove one component at a time from a machine to find the part that triggers a hazard. When: Designing a puzzle around a malfunctioning machine. Why: Isolating components gives the player a logical path to the cause.
- [heuristic] Break a hidden mechanism in front of the player, then explain it, placing every clue earlier so the explanation feels fair. When: Designing a game mechanism that is revealed to the player. Why: Physical evidence before explanation makes the reveal believable.
- [heuristic] A handheld detector that reacts to hidden magnetic or electrical triggers lets players test a suspicion before acting. When: Designing investigation tools for hidden mechanisms. Why: Players can verify a suspicion through a tool rather than guessing.
- [heuristic] Include a replica room the player can build or walk through to test a sound or path hypothesis. When: A mystery depends on a sound or route heard from another location. Why: Letting players rebuild the setting turns a heard clue into a testable one.
- [heuristic] A supernatural-seeming effect can be traced to a device with a signature and a hidden emitter, rewarding players who look for patterns. When: Designing a puzzle with an unexplained effect. Why: Pattern-seeking turns the effect into a solvable mechanism.
- [pitfall] A mirror shows a reversed, indirect view, so identities seen in it must be confirmed directly rather than trusted from the reflection alone. When: Observation through mirrors, glass or windows. Why: Reversal and indirection make a reflection easy to misread.
- [pitfall] Concealed lines remain visible to anyone who traces them back along their route.

## reach and location testing
- [rule] Give players runnable tests, such as checking line of sight from outside or walking a claimed route, to confirm or reject a theory. (x2)
- [rule] Compute, or let players time, travel between locations so a claimed location or time window is tested as physically possible. When: designing alibi or movement puzzles, especially when the claimed location depends on route length. Why: reachability turns a claim into a testable fact; a timed route makes the claim testable.
- [rule] Check any assumed reach, such as a ledge height or stretch needed to climb, against the actual geometry of the scene before accepting it. When: When a conclusion depends on whether someone could reach a point. Why: Reach estimates that ignore real dimensions can rule out the true route or accept an impossible one.
- [heuristic] Test reach from each tile or position a person could occupy, since a control may be unreachable from a resting spot yet reachable along an unintended route. When: when verifying who could operate a control, or when a puzzle must be solved only along a designed path. Why: a control may be out of reach from a resting spot; unintended reachable positions let players skip the intended sequence.
- [heuristic] Let players test an object's path by throwing or dropping it down a designed slope to see whether it reaches a target ledge. When: designing a physics-based puzzle. Why: trial and observation make the solution discoverable.
- [heuristic] Give the player an in-game measuring tool, such as a ruler or stick, to test whether an upper route is physically reachable. When: Designing entry-route puzzles with height-based access. Why: Measurement lets players verify routes through evidence rather than guesswork.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the suspected feature from the map comparison. 2) Physically inspect it, measuring depth or width. 3) Accept the feature only if the measurements match what the map difference implies. When: when a map difference suggests a hidden feature. Why: measurement separates genuine features from map artefacts.
- [procedure] 1) Map every door, switch, window and balcony. 2) Mark where the victim or subject was found. 3) Check which door links to which room. 4) Test each account of movement against this geometry. When: an account of movement in a room must be checked. Why: Physical space reveals which routes were possible.
- [procedure] 1) State who could reach the object and from where. 2) Physically recreate the route, including clothing or obstacles that alter reach. 3) Check whether the impossible result is possible from the other side. When: An event appears to defy physical limits, such as an impossible reach through a gap. Why: Rerunning the act with real positions often shows that the apparent impossibility rests on a wrong assumption about who could reach where.

## alibi and timeline checks
- [rule] Give each NPC a schedule and log each sighting with time, route, distance and light, so the player can assemble a timeline and check whether two accounts fit. When: when designing NPC movement or routines as investigative clues. Why: Timed sightings give the player discrete points to order.
- [rule] Let a false culprit's alibi be checkable in an accessible side area, so the player can disprove the error without relying on the antagonist's word. Why: A verifiable contradiction lets the player overturn the suspicion independently.
- [heuristic] Give NPCs daily schedules and treat a missing, changed or pattern-breaking routine as a clue that something has happened. When: when designing NPC behavior as investigative evidence or populating a setting with characters the player should watch. Why: Routine breaks signal change the player can detect, and a schedule gives a baseline against which deviations are measured.
- [heuristic] Anchor timeline puzzles on fixed events such as trains, guard shifts, market days or timed power cutoffs so the player reconstructs who was where and when. When: Designing a time-based deduction puzzle. Why: Scheduled events give a fixed frame for reasoning about movement.
- [heuristic] Model alibis as NPC dialogue branches giving different accounts of the same time window, and let the player catch contradictions. When: when designing suspects who give accounts of their whereabouts. Why: Contradictions between branches reward careful player comparison.
- [procedure] 1) Log each character's timed events and positions. 2) Map every room's doors and who holds keys. 3) Find windows where a suspect was absent. 4) Check each room for alternate entrances before accepting it as sealed. When: When the player must reconstruct who could have reached a location or victim. Why: Opportunity and access are only visible when movements, keys and routes are recorded together.
- [procedure] 1) List every exit from the area where the figure was last seen. 2) Measure the distance to each exit. 3) Compare the fastest route with the elapsed time. 4) If no route fits, state the impossibility plainly. When: Testing whether an apparent disappearance can be explained by physical layout. Why: Measuring exits against time separates a real impossibility from a missed route.
- [procedure] 1) Give NPCs distinct idle behaviours. 2) Repeat one behaviour at a key moment. 3) Let the player connect the repeated behaviour across scenes to infer intent. When: Designing NPC behaviour that doubles as a clue. Why: A repeated, recognisable gesture becomes evidence only when the player links it to a timeline.

## chase and escape design
- [mechanism] A chase mission can add decoy vehicles, crowd density and second exits, so the player must track the right target.
- [mechanism] A corroded trigger can make a hidden clue look unreachable, though a different approach may still open it. When: A hidden compartment's mechanism has degraded over time.
- [rule] Define a closed space by its exits: state precisely which doors lead out, since many internal doors can still leave a space effectively locked. When: Designing a puzzle arena whose constraints matter. Why: Vague exit descriptions weaken the logic of an impossible-event puzzle.
- [rule] Script the cordon as a visible event so the player sees it enforced and learns that suspects cannot leave the area. When: When a lockdown is part of a scenario. Why: Showing the restriction makes the closed suspect pool credible.
- [heuristic] Place a visible escape route above the player, such as a skylight, roof or ladder, that is found only by checking elevation. When: When designing a sealed room with a vertical exit. Why: Players tend to search at eye level and miss overhead routes.
- [heuristic] Exits with dangerous drops need no guard, while an exit with a safe drop needs a watcher. When: a room has windows or ledges at different heights. Why: a hazardous drop discourages escape, so only safe exits need coverage.
- [heuristic] Define each trigger and its expected reaction before building a scene, so every sound or movement has a known meaning when it occurs. When: designing scripted reactions for NPCs. Why: predefined meaning keeps feedback readable.
- [procedure] 1) Give the space many internal doors. 2) Allow only one guarded outer exit. 3) Block all other boundaries with walls or spikes. 4) State these constraints before the event. When: Creating an enclosed space for a closed-circle puzzle. Why: A readable boundary lets the player know exactly where people can and cannot go.

## deliberate gaps and missing items
- [mechanism] A destroyed item can leave a permanent gap that the player must reason around, rather than being restored by the game.
- [mechanism] Withholding one body part, such as a skull, blocks one line of deduction until the player finds it. When: when designing a remains puzzle with staged deductions. Why: the missing part removes the evidence that depends on it.
- [rule] Place missing elements, such as a blood trail or a dropped key, as deliberate clues that point toward a hidden route.
- [rule] An inventory puzzle can present a set with one deliberate gap, so the player notices the absence and traces the item to another room.
- [rule] Let a common-looking item, such as scrap or chips, hide a rare material, and reveal it only when the player tests it with a tool or second opinion.
- [rule] Plant two identical items as a trick that tests whether the player checks what is real. When: Designing a clue or item that tests verification. Why: Players who assume identical objects are equivalent miss the difference.
- [rule] Give a lab set of clean tests with one container out of its usual place, so placement rather than test results reveals it. When: designing a forensic room. Why: placement is a clue the tests cannot cover.
- [heuristic] A conspicuously absent key item can be placed deliberately, leaving the player to infer it from wounds, terrain and remaining traces. When: When designing a puzzle or mystery where the answer or key object should be inferred rather than found. Why: The absence itself becomes a puzzle the player solves by working out why the item is missing from indirect clues. (x2)

## false solutions and decoys
- [mechanism] Arrange areas so each yields a plausible culprit and a fresh discovery in the next area breaks it, forming a chain of false solutions. When: Structuring a multi-area investigation level. Why: Each new area overturns the previous suspect, sustaining progression.
- [rule] Use an inconsistent loot choice as a counter-clue that signals a non-obvious actor. When: Designing evidence that conflicts with a default culprit profile. Why: Odd choices of items raise doubt about an obvious suspect.
- [rule] Make the easiest reading correct for early events and let it fail only when a later event arrives. When: when designing a sequence of clues that should mislead before revealing the truth. Why: The player commits to a plausible model that a later event must then correct.
- [heuristic] Place a decoy item or sign initial at a suggestive location as a blind the player must test rather than trust; it misleads until a later clue exposes it. When: Designing environmental clues or placing hidden objects in authored or generated levels. (x2)
- [heuristic] Hold an arrested companion until the player finds a witness who contradicts the physical evidence, so the false solution can be overturned. When: A false solution has been reached but the real culprit is unknown. Why: A gate tied to contradicting evidence rewards continued investigation.
- [heuristic] A false trail can lead out of a location while the decisive object that disproves it is hidden on an inside floor of the same location. When: Designing an outdoor or multi-floor level with a misleading path. Why: The player follows the outward trail and only finds the inside evidence by searching thoroughly.
- [heuristic] Place a decoy objective that looks like the real goal but resolves to nothing, to test whether players check their assumptions.
- [procedure] 1) Place a decoy clue such as a glowing object or a set of tracks that points to a dead end. 2) Let the player's failed search expose the decoy. When: when building a mystery or dungeon that should mislead before revealing the true source. Why: The decoy is believable enough to be pursued, and its failure teaches the player to test explanations.

## quest objectives and rewards
- [rule] Treat a missing body as a possibly living victim, and let the player's search reveal the victim's own movements, such as missing clothing or cleaned traces. Why: A living victim who left alone is a plausible answer to a vanished body.
- [rule] A missing object from a body or altar can be a deliberate puzzle state that the player must restore.
- [heuristic] A timed quest with a conditional penalty, such as an item sent to an authority if a vault is damaged, uses the same structure as narrative deadlines. When: Designing timed objectives with consequences in a game. Why: The same deadline-and-consequence structure creates pressure in both stories and quests.
- [heuristic] Covert collection, such as taking a coat or cup unseen, can be a stealth objective whose results are sorted by owner. When: Designing a stealth objective involving evidence gathering. Why: Unseen collection creates tension while producing ownership-linked evidence.
- [heuristic] Present an apparent dead end or closed quest, then reopen it when a previously seen item or NPC reveals the truth, with every prior clue available to the player. When: Designing a quest that the player believes is finished. Why: Reopening through known clues rewards attention without cheating.
- [heuristic] Let a hidden human faction emerge from a location the player has already cleared, so the earlier search is rewarded.
- [procedure] 1) Hide the nature of a past offense behind a locked record or a reluctant NPC. 2) Show the offense's consequences first. 3) Reveal the offense only after the player has seen those consequences. When: Designing a quest built around a past wrong. Why: Consequences shown before the cause create suspense that the reveal then resolves.

## rumour and npc claims
- [mechanism] A place with a supernatural reputation deters visits, so it can serve as an unwatched hiding spot even though the rumor is false. When: A location is avoided because of a rumored danger. Why: People stay away from a feared place, leaving it unobserved.
- [rule] Give players a second, physical source, such as an object's state or a door's state, to confirm an NPC's report of a voice or movement. When: when an NPC reports sounds or movement. Why: A physical source lets players verify a partly true report.
- [rule] Give rumour clues a source label so players can judge reliability, as a careful investigator would weigh each account by its origin. When: when players receive clues from several informants. Why: Source information lets players decide which rumours to trust.
- [rule] Place rumored hidden passages in lore or room descriptions so the player's search can reveal them later. When: Designing a locked room with a possible secret route. Why: Foreshadowing makes the eventual discovery feel earned.
- [heuristic] Let a location carry a legend, but tie it to real NPCs, rent, ownership or hidden passages so the player can find a mundane explanation. When: when designing a haunted or mysterious location. Why: Concrete links give the player a path from myth to ordinary cause.
- [heuristic] A supernatural message can be tied to a living person with knowledge of the location, which then becomes the next lead for the player. When: Designing a mystery where an apparent spirit message has a human source. Why: Linking the effect to a person turns it into a traceable clue.
- [heuristic] A public notice can draw out the holder of a traced item, turning a dead end into a direct interview. When: Tracing an item whose current holder is unknown.

## scene reconstruction puzzles
- [mechanism] A room or NPC carrying two readings, such as a sleeping figure that may be a corpse or an ally, can be settled by a later event like a noise or light change. When: Designing a space with an ambiguous figure. Why: A dual reading holds tension until a later cue resolves it.
- [heuristic] Plant early staging clues, such as a blood-free or wet trace or an odd light or lock detail, so a staged obvious site misleads until close inspection exposes it. When: Designing a culprit-arranged scene where the most visible location is not where the event actually happened. Why: Careful players notice the inconsistency that a casual look misses, and the staged site draws investigation away from the real site until the clue exposes it. (x2)
- [heuristic] Encode a scene's story in body position, disorder radius and lock state, making each clue reachable by the player. When: When designing a crime-scene room. Why: Reachable clues let the player reconstruct the event.
- [procedure] 1) Stage an impression or dent in the scene. 2) Let the player place a matching item on it. 3) Confirm the event sequence when the impression reproduces on a second surface.
- [procedure] 1) List each actor's position and weapon at the moment of the first fall. 2) Note which weapon is dropped, hidden or still in hand. 3) Check the timing of the fall against the shot. 4) Compare the result with the apparent cause. When: A scene's apparent cause of harm conflicts with the physical evidence. Why: Positions, weapons and timing expose a reversal that the first impression hides.
- [procedure] 1) Let the player reproduce the original conditions of the event, such as dose, deprivation or timing. 2) Use the reproduction to unlock a hidden object. When: The final puzzle must reveal a hidden object tied to a past event. Why: Letting the player recreate the conditions turns a past event into an interactive solution.
- [pitfall] Finding no hiding place does not prove a crime was impossible; re-check witness reliability and who held the key before concluding.

## space layout and volume
- [rule] Treat gates and doors as deliberate thresholds that change who enters and what they face, since choke points concentrate tension and clues. When: Laying out a level's passages.
- [heuristic] When a measured space seems larger than expected, or a feature looks like a secret entrance, first check structural projections and partitions, since they often explain the apparent hidden space. When: a measured gap exceeds the visible room, or an element seems to conceal an entrance. Why: structural elements often account for the extra dimension, and many features serve only a structural role, so a suspicious element is not proof of a passage.
- [heuristic] Key layout constraints should be stated before they matter, since a reader or player needs the map in advance to check each route. When: Access routes are central to a puzzle. Why: Constraints revealed only at the reveal break fair play.
- [heuristic] Test walls and moldings by tapping for hollow sounds, and lift loose fittings, since hidden items can sit in cavities behind surfaces.
- [pitfall] A sealed room with no physical trace gives the player nothing to reason from.
- [pitfall] A trap that depends on exact fit fails if the passage and trigger component differ even slightly in size. When: when a mechanism relies on a component passing through a narrow route. Why: small size mismatches block the component or change its path.
- [pitfall] Sounds carry unevenly through a building, so check any claim that someone could hear an event from another area. When: a witness claims they could hear a distant event. Why: Walls, floors and distance change what is audible, so earshot claims can be wrong.

## building layout planning
- [mechanism] A building's placement, such as near a road with open ground and a nearby fort, shapes where people gather and where travelers are received.
- [rule] Describe walls, corners and doors precisely in early room descriptions so the player can later deduce hidden spots. When: Designing rooms that will contain concealed observation points. Why: A surprise with no earlier setup feels like cheating, while a described layout is fair play.
- [rule] Make the plan and physical layout match, so the player can verify a concealed mechanism before relying on it. When: when a level includes a hidden route described in a map or document. Why: Mismatched plans make hidden routes unusable or unfair.
- [procedure] 1) List every door of each room. 2) Mark which doors open outside and which connect to other rooms. 3) Note which doors stay shut during a given conversation. When: reconstructing who could see, hear or reach a scene. Why: Door access determines possible approaches and eavesdropping points.
- [tradeoff] Publish a labelled plan view marking sightlines, entrances and all routes so players can reason about unseen approaches; a single-room scene with one entrance does not need one. When: Deciding whether to provide a plan. Why: Route complexity determines whether a plan adds useful information.
- [fact] A building can record planned growth; a large chimney placed at one end can show that fireplaces were meant to open into a later extension.

## hidden passages and entrances
- [mechanism] Hidden routes such as trapdoors, screens or ceiling openings can be found only by reasoning from sounds or damage, which makes them discoverable through deduction. When: Designing a sealed room with a concealed entry. Why: Indirect clues reward reasoning and keep the route from being trivially visible.
- [rule] Avoid hiding a clue in a place the story marks as secret, because searchers check those spots first. When: Choosing a hiding place for a key clue. Why: A hidden drawer or similar marked spot is the first place investigators search.
- [rule] When no physical route fits the time available, signal the gap with a hidden passage or an explicitly unexplained effect rather than leaving the player to assume a route exists. When: Designing a space where a figure can vanish. Why: A player who cannot find a route needs a clear cue that the impossibility is intended.
- [heuristic] Give players a partial map and require them to pace out distances before probing walls for a hidden feature. When: Designing a concealed door or secret puzzle. Why: Measuring turns the search into a skill rather than a guess.
- [procedure] 1) Wait for darkness. 2) Enter through a rear barrier. 3) Stay out of sight of windows. 4) Ambush whoever opens the door. When: Forced entry carries noise risk and the approach must stay hidden until the occupant comes out. Why: Concealment keeps the element of surprise and avoids alerting the occupant before the door opens.
- [pitfall] Assuming a secret entrance explains a crime is a mistake when the attack itself could not be carried out by an ordinary person present in view.

## investigation pacing and reveal
- [rule] Design an ending that releases the agent with partial consequences so the level state persists after the reveal. When: Concluding a supernatural encounter in a level. Why: Lasting changes make the resolution feel consequential.
- [heuristic] Tune the distance and sound volume of an approaching entity so its reveal lands on the chosen beat of an encounter. When: when scripting an approach-and-reveal sequence. Why: Distance and volume control when the threat becomes visible.
- [heuristic] A recap screen that lists each clue the player found makes a final reveal feel earned. When: Designing a game's final-act reveal. Why: The player sees the evidence chain assembled before the conclusion.
- [heuristic] Leave a visible loose end at the close of a resolved mystery, such as an NPC escaping with a clue, so the player can follow it later. When: Ending a level or case that should lead to further content. Why: An unresolved thread carries the player's interest forward.
- [procedure] 1) Define the required linked clues. 2) Allow a partial accusation that leaves one inference open. 3) Show the player which link is missing. 4) Offer an ending that accepts the partial solution with uncertainty. When: Designing a mystery whose final verdict can be incomplete. Why: A partial ending respects what the evidence supports without forcing a false certainty.
- [procedure] 1) Place a trap room where a suspect's reaction to a false clue exposes guilt. 2) Make the player's deduction the trigger. 3) Provide a hazard the player can swap or neutralize as the climax. When: Designing the final encounter of an investigation level. Why: Letting the player act on their own deduction turns the reveal into an interactive decision.

## competing explanations
- [rule] Give a strange event both a mystical label and a checkable natural cause, so the player decides between explanations by checking evidence. When: when designing an encounter that appears supernatural. Why: The checkable cause rewards attentive players without removing the mystery's atmosphere.
- [rule] Make the competing theory testable at the same time as the supernatural one, so the player can disprove both. When: when a scenario offers several explanations for one event. Why: Players need a path to eliminate each explanation, not only the favored one.
- [heuristic] Keep one or two effects deliberately unexplained, so a mechanical solution does not close the entire mystery. When: When resolving a haunted-level puzzle. Why: Leaving residual mystery sustains player curiosity.
- [procedure] 1) Show the player a scripted illusion or vision. 2) Reveal the true state of the room. 3) Let the player learn to check perception against the environment. When: Designing a puzzle built on deceptive perception. Why: The contrast teaches the player to verify what they see.
- [procedure] 1) Present two competing explanations. 2) Require the player to choose one before a door opens. 3) Lead each choice to a different witness. When: Designing a gate that forces a theory choice. Why: Each explanation yields distinct evidence, so the choice shapes what the player learns.

## level layout and navigation
- [mechanism] A layout whose rooms lead back to places already passed leaves no one able to reconstruct who went where. When: visitor movement must be hard to trace. Why: revisited spaces erase the sequence of movement.
- [rule] Design disorienting layouts that conceal routes, and require the player to work out a path back.
- [heuristic] Fixed landmarks such as carpets, pillars or tiles can serve as measuring references for a puzzle that requires counting movements. When: Designing a puzzle that depends on counting movements. Why: Visible fixed references let the player measure and compare movements.
- [heuristic] Limit the player's first visit so that the real test becomes a return visit. When: Designing a location the player must investigate covertly. Why: A first visit is constrained, and the return visit is where the test pays off.
- [pitfall] Overloading a setting with detail, including decoration that serves no purpose, makes the one feature that matters later hard to notice.

## single route conclusion
- [heuristic] When a crime seems to have only one route, confirm every other route is closed before accepting that conclusion.
- [heuristic] A map with one main approach gives the player a clear point to watch and gives the game a clear point to spawn arrivals. When: Designing a location that the player defends or monitors. Why: A single entrance concentrates both player attention and enemy or NPC arrivals.
- [heuristic] A late-arriving document can gate the final reveal room.
- [procedure] 1) Design each mystery with at least two entry points. 2) Let a blocked player approach from a known event elsewhere. 3) Set the cost of a fact request to match the question's importance. When: Building investigation content that a player may get stuck in. Why: Multiple entries prevent a single blocked location from ending progress.
- [pitfall] A reveal that depends on a single light source fails if that source changes unexpectedly. When: Designing a reveal lit by one source. Why: Without a backup light, the intended moment can be lost.

## haunting and dread
- [heuristic] Build haunted-seeming effects from hidden mechanisms, such as a creaking panel, wire, secret stair, or an actor moving objects on a scripted path, that players can find; track who could reach each spot. When: Designing a haunted, trick-based, or ghost encounter in a location. Why: Discoverable mechanisms let apparent supernatural events resolve into traceable physical causes.
- [heuristic] Use weather, lighting and changes to landmarks to signal dread without dialogue. When: Setting a tense mood in a location. Why: Visual and environmental cues convey threat efficiently.
- [heuristic] Use a ritual or performance as cover for a character's exit, and reward players who watch the performers rather than the spectacle. When: Designing a scene where a character must leave unnoticed. Why: Spectacle draws attention, so a watchful player can catch the departure.
- [procedure] 1) Give the haunted effect a hidden human or mechanical cause. 2) Offer rival explanations, each leaving distinct checkable traces, such as a hidden door versus a sound effect. 3) Let an NPC offer a supernatural excuse the player can test against the traces. When: Designing a supernatural encounter or haunting with an unclear or rational cause. Why: Distinct traces let players test which explanation holds and reach the truth through testing.

## mechanisms and wear clues
- [rule] Timed mechanisms such as clocks or pressure plates can be set by a character, so let the player learn who set one before trusting its reading.
- [heuristic] Oil or worn stone on a passage floor suggests a mechanism was worked from the far side and needed lubrication. When: Finding wear near a sealed wall. Why: Use shows where a mechanism is.
- [heuristic] A missing refill or maintenance step can serve as a clue, letting the player infer an NPC's presence from what the NPC failed to maintain. When: designing environmental clues about an NPC's activity. Why: Omitted maintenance reveals that a specific NPC was present or absent, which players can deduce.
- [heuristic] A sensor or indicator in the level can act as an independent check, letting players confirm or reject a reconstructed mechanism. When: Designing puzzles that depend on a hidden mechanism. Why: An independent readout gives players a way to test their hypothesis.

## scene state and resets
- [mechanism] A physics puzzle can require players to rig a weight and line to reproduce a scene clue, and a matching result confirms their theory and unlocks the next step. When: Designing a puzzle where players must verify a theory about a scene. Why: Players gain confidence when their own setup reproduces the evidence.
- [mechanism] A room whose state changes over time, such as a missing object already gone, forces the player to reason backward about who acted first.
- [mechanism] Require players to preserve a scene and summon an examiner before the body can be examined, then fix the time-of-death estimate at that point. When: Designing a discovery mechanic. Why: Correct handling ties evidence to the timeline.
- [tradeoff] Encode events in body positions, scorch decals and footprints for reconstruction; hide traces only when a trap or secret must leave no visible evidence, e.g. via spring-hinged resetting panel. When: Deciding whether a scene should show evidence of past events. Why: Visible traces let players reconstruct events; a reset removes visible evidence of the action. (x2)

## secondary evidence use
- [mechanism] Giving an evidence object a secondary use, such as a scrap that resembles a tool, makes the player explain why it was placed where it was found. Why: An object with a plausible alternative function creates a question about its actual purpose.
- [rule] Make the final confrontation depend on evidence the player collected several zones earlier, not on a new key item.
- [heuristic] Give each trace one consistent source condition so that a rival explanation fails under a check the player can perform. When: Designing physical clues that players must test. Why: A rival explanation then becomes testable and can be ruled out.
- [heuristic] Make an object examination change meaning once the player reads an in-world text, so the same object reads as a clue after reading. When: Environments with inspectable artifacts. Why: Reading supplies the cause the object lacks.

## statement and document clues
- [mechanism] Give players a discoverable inheritance record so they can infer who benefits from a missing item. When: Designing a missing-item or theft quest. Why: Players reason from benefit to suspect.
- [rule] Letters or ledgers can carry dated instructions that supersede one another, letting the player choose which instruction is valid.
- [rule] Mark reconstructed or partial text in the game so players can tell which parts are inferred. When: When a clue is assembled from incomplete fragments. Why: Clear marking keeps the player's understanding accurate.
- [heuristic] Tag each NPC statement as lead, background or proof, so players learn what each one actually supports. When: Designing an interview or deposition system. Why: Labelled statements teach players how to weigh them.

## disconnected test fixture
- [mechanism] A communication line cut by a hidden object can appear dead before its cause is known, so the dead line itself becomes a clue to investigate.
- [rule] Place a visibly disconnected pull or lever so a player who tests it discovers that it is a dummy. When: Designing puzzle rooms with false clues. Why: Testing lets players learn the fixture is non-functional.
- [heuristic] A lab puzzle can let the player test residue or blood against a control subject, using labelled items and a numbered key. When: Designing a forensic or lab-based puzzle. Why: Controlled comparison gives the player a traceable route from evidence to source.

## tide-gated routes
- [mechanism] A tide or water-level variable determines which routes are passable at a given moment, so a puzzle or claimed event can depend on timing. When: when an event or puzzle depends on timing of the environment. Why: water level controls whether banks are covered and whether a vessel can float free.
- [mechanism] Some routes stay covered at every tide while others dry wholly or partly at low water, so timing decides which routes can be used. When: when a route depends on water level. Why: a route passable at one tide can be impassable at another.
- [rule] Gate a route with a tide or patrol schedule so players can deduce an NPC's destination from its time windows.

## condition clue to method
- [heuristic] Let a victim's condition point the player back to an earlier action, such as a drink taken at dinner, so the clue reveals the method. When: Designing a poisoning or drugging clue. Why: Symptoms can link the outcome to a prior event.
- [heuristic] Hidden changing spots, such as an empty waiting room, can serve as clues that reveal the disguise method to a careful player. When: Designing clues for disguise mechanics. Why: A discoverable location lets careful players work out the method.

## ruins and site reconnaissance
- [rule] Use ruins and foundation outlines to confirm that a location existed before the player arrives, as a check on a rumor or map.
- [procedure] 1) Scout the site when its structure is most plainly visible. 2) Take bearings and note what matters. 3) Keep the notes so the route can be followed later in worse conditions. When: before acting in unfamiliar or dangerous terrain.

## safety versus speed routes
- [procedure] Let the player's hypothesis be tested by a cheap, safe check before the expensive or dangerous one. When: Designing investigation sequences where the player tests a hypothesis. Why: Ordering checks by cost lets players rule out causes before risking more.
- [tradeoff] Show alternative routes with different time costs, so the player must trade safety against speed. When: when designing route choices. Why: a visible time cost makes the choice meaningful.

## secret holder deduction
- [heuristic] Let a locked object's secret be known to a small set of NPCs and plant clues so players can build the holder list and work out who could have opened it.
