When: Designing a locked object opened by a secret. Why: A bounded holder list turns the solution into a deduction rather than a guess.
- [procedure] 1) Record a baseline measurement of an identity early as a routine habit. 2) Record the same measure later. 3) Compare the two records; a mismatch exposes the hidden identity. When: a character's true identity must be proven from records kept at different times. Why: Comparing a fixed early record with a later one gives a checkable proof that a player can reproduce.

## secret meeting signals
- [heuristic] Soundproofing, restricted locks and fixed seating can signal a room set up for private, planned gatherings.
- [pitfall] A visible parked cart, carriage or known route can expose a hidden meeting, since observers infer it and a bystander can later confirm or contradict accounts. When: Players or NPCs approach a hidden meeting, or a meeting site with parked transport is designed. Why: Observers infer the meeting from peripheral signs.

## single controlled crossing
- [definition] A chokepoint is a location with a single practical approach, which limits who can arrive and gives a clear point to watch. When: Designing a map or location with a limited entrance. Why: A single approach concentrates arrivals and visibility at one point.
- [rule] Use one controlled crossing, such as a single bridge, to fix the player's route into an enclosed area.

## bypassable check-in device
- [heuristic] A guard post can use a timed check-in device the player can bypass, leaving a readable record behind.

## concealed inspection risk
- [pitfall] Inspecting a hidden spot with a light or by moving a panel is risky, because the light and movement can expose the observer.

## likely destination ranking
- [heuristic] Rank a missing person's likely destinations by both plausibility and risk, using local geography; hazard zones such as docks can be probable endpoints.

## list-based absence search
- [rule] Search for missing items against a written list of expected objects rather than doing a general search. When: checking a space for absences. Why: Without a list, missing items are easy to overlook.

## rusty fittings
- [pitfall] Rusty bolts and worn fittings can stick or make noise, so they need patient handling. When: Opening an old or worn door. Why: Age and corrosion increase friction and sound.

## safe stimulus design
- [rule] A staged stimulus meant to flush a hidden occupant must be safe; one that risks fire or harm must not be used. When: Designing an alarm or trap to draw out a hidden person. Why: Harm to the occupant or surroundings makes the method unacceptable.

## sailing chart coverage
- [fact] A sailing chart shows only features useful to mariners, so inland features are often missing or simplified. When: when comparing charts with land maps. Why: each map is drawn for a different purpose.

## save and state testing
- [rule] Hide layered history in searchable objects, such as luggage labels and letters, that the player unlocks by searching. When: designing an investigation space. Why: Searching rewards careful inspection with backstory and clues.

## secret height placement
- [rule] Place secrets above normal reach so the player must find a ladder, climb, or use a tool to reach them. When: When designing hidden areas in a 3D level. Why: Height adds a deliberate search cost without hiding the secret from a determined player.

## self-closing covers
- [mechanism] A spring-hinged cover that snaps shut after use leaves no visible sign that it was opened, erasing evidence of the route. When: when a hidden mechanism should leave no trace after use. Why: the automatic closing resets the visible state.

## separate construction and analysis
- [heuristic] Keep construction tasks, such as building a device, separate from analysis tasks, such as reading a scene, and judge each on its own terms. When: designing multi-step investigation challenges.

## separate dump site
- [rule] Place the murder room and the dump site apart, and leave clues on the route between them so players can trace how a body was moved.

## smooth but not staged access
- [heuristic] Balance access so progress is not fully blocked, but is not so smooth that it looks staged; vary route smoothness to keep progress feeling earned. When: Designing progression through a space with opponents or obstacles. Why: Overly smooth paths reveal the designer's hand and undermine the sense of earned progress.

## step and distance counting
- [tradeoff] Step counts estimate size well in simple spaces but diverge from true distance in spaces with many angles or turns, misplacing markers at a circuit's end. When: choosing between counting and other mapping methods. Why: the true extent exceeds the assumed size.
